About the Authors 
Dr. Joel D. Wallach, Bs, DVM, ND 
Dr. Wallach has been involved in biomedical research and clini- 
cal medicine for more than 47 years. He received his B.S. degree 
(agriculture) from the University of Missouri with a major in animal 
husbandry (nutrition) and a minor in field crops and soils; a D.V.M. 
(veterinary medicine) from the University of Missouri; three year post 
doctoral fellowship (comparative pathology/medicine), Washington 
University, St. Louis, MO - The Center for the Biology of Natural 
Systems (The St. Louis Zoological Gardens, Shaw’ Botanical Gar- 
dens, Botany Department, Washington University); lowa State Uni- 
versity, Ames, lowa; Natal Parks Board, Umfolozi and Hluehluwe 
Game Parks, Republic of South Africa; St. Louis Zoological Gardens; 
The Chicago Zoological Gardens (Brookfield Zoo); Shedd Aquarium, 
Chicago, IL; Yerkes Regional Primate Research Center, Emory Uni- 
versity, Atlanta, GA; a N.D. (naturopathic physician), the National 
College of Naturopathic Medicine, Portland, OR; Harbin Medical Uni- 
versity, Harbin, Hei Long Jiang and the Shanghai Medical University, 
Peoples Republic of China. 
He was a member of NIH site visit teams for facilities using exotic 
animal models for the study of human disease for four years and was 
a member of the 1968 National Science Foundation ad hoc commit- 
tee that authored the 1968 Animal Welfare Act (humane housing, 
nutrition and care of laboratory and captive Exotic Species); a pro- 
fessor of nutrition at the National College of Naturopathic Medicine, 
Portland, OR; and a Consulting Professor of Medicine, Harbin Medi- 
cal University, Harbin, Hei Long Jiang, People’s Republic of China. 
Dr. Wallach has appeared as an expert witness in State and Fed- 
eral Courts as a comparative pathologist. 
Dr. Wallach attained the rank of Lieutenant Colonel in the Mis- 
souri Air National Guard (131st Tactical Hospital), known as 
“Lindberg’s own,” and the Alaskan Air Reserves with responsibilities 
in public health and prevention and clean up of nuclear, biological 
and chemical attacks in North America and Europe. 
V

Dr. Wallach was a recipient of the Wooster Beach Gold Medal 
Award (Association of Eclectic Physicians) for a significant break- 
through in the basic understanding of the cause and pathophysiol- 
ogy of Cystic Fibrosis. Dr. Wallach was nominated for the 1991 Nobel 
Prize in medicine (Association of Eclectic Physicians) for his work in 
the understanding of the genesis and pathophysiology of Cystic Fi- 
brosis. 
Dr. Wallach received the 2004 Guardian of the Constitution Award 
(Emord & Assoc.) for his proactive and successful litigation efforts in 
obtaining nutrition claims from the FDA for Selenium and Omega 3 
essential fatty acids. He also received the 2004 James Lind Scientific 
Achievement Award (Emord & Assoc.) for “brilliant discoveries that 
have paved the way for greater health and longevity” for Americans. 
Dr. Wallach has appeared frequently on local and national net- 
work and public radio and television (including a special on Cystic 
Fibrosis with ABC's 20/20), regional and national talk radio programs 
as an expert on trace mineral and rare earth deficiency diseases. Dr. 
Wallach is the host of his own syndicated talk radio show programs 
(Let's Play Doctor and Dead Doctors Don’t Lie). Because of his free- 
wheeling style of humor and ability to zero in on the basic truth in 
health problems and medical politics he is widely known as the “Rush 
Limbaugh” of alternative health. 
Dr. Wallach has given an average of 300 free health lectures per 
year for more than 15 years, literally reaching millions of enthusias- 
tic fans. His tape, Dead Doctors Don’t Lie (translated into eight lan- 
guages) has sold more than 62 million copies world wide. 
Dr. Wallach has more than 70 peer review publications to his 
credit including a major reference, The Diseases of Exotic Animals - 
published by W. B. Saunders. This 1,000 page illustrated text is listed 
by the Smithsonian Institute as, “A recommended reference for all 
professional zoological parks and aquariums.”

Ma Lan, MD, MS, LAc 
Dr. Ma Lan was educated in the People’s Republic of China. Dr. 
Ma Lan received her MD from the Beijing Medical University, took 
her residency at the People’s Hospital, Beijing and was a staff surgeon 
at the Canton Air Force Hospital; she received her MS (Master of 
Science) in transplantation immunology from Zhongshan Medical 
University, Canton, Peoples Republic of China; as with all Chinese 
doctors, Dr. Ma Lan was trained in Traditional Chinese Medicine 
(i.e. - acupuncture, herbs, manipulation, food medicine, massage and 
hydrotherapy) prior to entering the Western-style medical schools. 
Dr. Ma Lan’s research credits include being an exchange scholar 
at Harvard School of Medicine, Boston, MA; a research fellow in laser 
microsurgery at St. Joseph Hospital, Houston, TX; the Department 
of Orthopedic Surgery, the Medical College of Wisconsin, Milwau- 
kee, WI; and the Department of Pharmacology, University of Califor- 
nia, San Diego, CA. 
Dr. Ma Lan has 10 peer review publications to her credit in the 
fields of transplant immunology and laser vascular surgery. Dr. Ma 
Lan attained the rank of Lieutenant in the Chinese Air Force with 
primary responsibilities as a general surgeon in the Canton Air Force 
Hospital, PRC.

Introduction 
Despite extraordinary efforts by the federal government (U.S. De- 
partment of Agriculture, U.S. Surgeon General), various associations 
(American Heart Association, American Diabetes Association, Ameri- 
can Cancer Society, American Medical Association, etc.) and private 
companies and authors (Atkins Diet, South Beach Diet, Zone Diet, 
Dr. Wallach’s See Food Diet, etc.), the rates of diagnosis of overweight 
and obese Americans and diabetic Americans (in fact, the problem 
includes peoples of all industrialized nations) continues to go up at 
an ever increasing and an ever alarming rate - a metabolic pandemic! 
The actual cause of obesity (we call obesity the Feedlot Syndrome), 
diabetes, the Metabolic Syndrome and other chronic degenerative 
diseases have eluded the “best” efforts of all - unfortunately there are 
no laws requiring doctors to cure people when there are cures avail- 
able so they don’t even look for the cure - result, 65 percent of Ameri- 
cans are overweight and 40 percent are obese; we Americans have an 
epidemic of 1 million new cases of type II diabetes each year; we 
rank 26th in the world when it comes to health and longevity (there 
are 25 other countries whose peoples are healthier and lived 
longerthan we); we rank 29th in the world in live births and first 
year survivability of our babies - even though we spend more money 
annually for health care than all other countries combined ($1.6 tril- 
lion for the U.S. alone out of $2.7 trillion for the entire world). 
Obesity, and the metabolic diseases associated with it, is a symp- 
tom of our doctor’, our health care system’s and our government’ 
abysmal failures or purpose. Obesity is not the disease, obesity is not 
the enemy, obesity is but a symptom of a deeper nutritional defi- 
ciency, just as a fever is not the cause of pneumonia, but rather it is a 
symptom of an infection - and we all know that just lowering the 
elevated body temperature (the fever) back to normal with aspirin 
does not kill the bacteria or virus - the cure for an infection requires 
a viable immune system and sometimes a wound disinfectant or an 
antibiotic. 
Obesity is an anthropological problem, not a medical problem, 
therefore we can only solve the obesity problem by studying man, 
man’s successes and failures throughout the years. 
Vill

We looked back beyond written history and looked for clues that 
would show us what events, what technology, what people set us on 
the terrible path to the world pandemic of obesity - as a result we 
have found and identified the events millennia by millennia, century 
by century, decade by decade and year by year that have led up to 
our modern health crisis - we have identified the men, the technol- 
ogy and the time and date, Monday September 4, 1882 at 3:00 p.m. 
that set us on the “Road to Hell” and we have determined exactly 
how we got into “HELLS KITCHEN.” 
We chose HELLS KITCHEN as the title for this book because we 
wanted to illustrate all of the side effects of the technology that led to 
the epidemic of obesity, diabetes, Metabolic Syndrome and their com- 
plications. We chose HELLS KITCHEN because the name conjures 
up the borough in New York that “cooked up” all of the known evils 
of gang warfare, drugs, prostitution, organized crime, petty crime, 
pickpockets and graft. 
Knowing the basic, bottom line root cause of obesity and its as- 
sociated diseases has allowed us to focus in on the cause, to elimi- 
nate the cause and approach it with all of our strength and resources. 
As a result, we have found the basic root cause of obesity - over a 
short 50 year period we (all modern people) willingly gave up a ba- 
sic practice that humans from Europe, Asia, Africa, South America 
and North America have depended upon for tens of thousands of 
years for the advancement of mankind and for long healthy lives. 
We as a people have literally discarded our health inheritance 
and heritage, as we would grandma’ accumulations in the attic, the 
health and life supporting practices of throwing our wood, peat and 
kelp ashes (minerals left over when we burn carbon fuel) into our 
gardens, and cutting our valuable table salt with wood ash, and us- 
ing ashes for food preparation “culinary” ashes - practices that we 
had used on a daily basis for thousands of years. 
Mineral deficiencies result in cravings a.k.a., the munchies, that 
ultimately, produce obesity, diabetes and the Metabolic Syndrome. 
We can simply eliminate obesity, diabetes, the Metabolic Syndrome 
and other degenerative diseases in three to six months in America 
and in the world for that matter, if only we would all adhere to simple 
principals and supplement properly and cut back on most forms of 
ix

dietary sugars and carbohydrates. 
We gave up the free byproduct of heating and cooking (wood 
ashes) that we humans had employed as a source of nutritional min- 
erals before written history when we began to use electricity, natural 
gas and propane. Instead of replacing the lost source of nutritional 
minerals, we were convinced by the medical profession that we could 
replace nutrion with pharmaceuticals, surgery and health insurance 
- we chose poorly and we are paying the price in epidemics of nutri- 
tional deficiency diseases, higher medical costs and shorter lives. 
Unfortunately, there is too much profit ($750,000 per diabetic, 
and $117 billion annually for treating obesity in America alone) in 
treating obesity and diabetes medically; there are no laws requiring 
doctors to cure people when there are cures available; therefore, 
medical doctors will never employ a cure even though the cure was 
available in 1957! If it is to be that we eliminate obesity in all of its 
metabolic forms and complications, it is up to each of us to employ 
the prevention and effect the cure - doctors are simply not going to 
do it. 
Experts will attack our observations and conclusions as coming 
from the untrained, unworthy of an opinion, however, we would 
point out that Columbus, Franklin, Newcomen, Faraday and Edison 
were all simple men who made critical observations and in one way 
or another took action against the sea anchor of the dogma and com- 
mon thinking of the day and as a result catapulted the capabilities of 
humanity forward beyond the dreams of the “learned” but ignorant 
scholars of the day. 
We truly hope you will read HELLS KITCHEN and employ the 
lifestyle and supplement suggestions in the book and pass on the 
information to those you love and care about. Only by taking re- 
sponsibility ourselves can we solve the obesity (Feedlot Syndrome), 
diabetes and Metabolic Syndrome pandemics - just another diet will 
not solve the problem. 
Dr. Joel Wallach and Dr. Ma Lan

Benjamin Franklin 
1706 - 1790 
American Longevity 
To the PRINTER of the LONDON CHRONICLE. 
SIR, I have often heard it remarked, that our Colonies in North 
America were unhealthy and unfavourable to long life; and more 
particularly so upon their first settlement. In opposition to this 
groundless notion, I here send you two paragraphs taken from the 
Pensylvania Gazette of July 16, and the New-York Gazette of August 
27, giving an account of the deaths of the first-born of the city of 
Philadelphia, and of the province of Pennsylvania: 
PHILADELPHIA, July 16. "At Kennet, in Chester county, the 5th in- 
stant, died John Key, in the 85th year of his age, and the next day was 
interred in the burial place belonging to the people called Quakers, 
in that township, attended by a large number of reputable people, 
his neighbours and acquaintance. -- He was born in a cave, long 
xl

afterwards known by the name of Penny-Pot, near Race-street; and 
William Penn, our first proprietor, gave him a lot of ground, as a 
compliment on his being the first child born in this city. -- In the 
early part of his life his conversation was very engaging, and his com- 
pany much sought after by those of his own age; in his decline he 
was much esteemed for his peaceable disposition. His constitution 
was very healthy till about 80, when he was seized with the palsy, 
and continued weakly till his death. -- About six years ago he walked 
on foot from Kennet to Philadelphia in one day, which is near 30 
miles. -- It has been said that people in this province are short-lived; 
but when we consider how few children were born here 75 years 
ago, and observe how many old people there are still alive among us 
(who were born here) we shall rather think no quarter of the world 
can shew a greater number of aged persons then this province can, in 
proportion to the children born." 
PHILADELPHIA, Aug. 24. On the 10th instant, at Brandywine Hun- 
dred, in New-Castle county, died Emanuel Grubb, in the 86th year 
of his age, and the next day was interred in St. Martin's church-yard, 
at Lower-Chichester, in Chester county, attended by a large number 
of his relations, neighbours, and acquaintance. -- He was born in a 
cave, by the side of Delaware river, not far distant from where he 
always lived, and died, and was the first child born of English par- 
ents in this province. His constitution was remarkably healthy dur- 
ing his whole life, having never been afflicted with any sickness till a 
few days before his death. His strength and activity were surprizing 
in a man of his age; he could mount and ride a horse with as much 
dexterity as a lad of 20. -- A few months ago, he rode from his own 
house to this city, and back again, which is upwards of 40 miles. - 
His memory was equally surprizing, and not in the least impaired, 
till his death; he could remember transactions of a late date equally 
well with those which happened in his younger days. He was ex- 
ceeding temperate in his living, seldom making use of spirituous 
liquors, and for his peaceable and friendly disposition, was highly 
esteemed by all that knew him. This instance, among many, is a proof 
of the longevity of people born here. 
It is worthy of observation also, that Governor Hutchinson, in his 
xii

valuable History of the Massachusett's Bay, Vol. II. pp. 148 and 216, 
says, 
"July 20, 1704, died at Marshfield, Peregrine White, aged 83 years 
and eight months, the first-born in Plymouth colony. 
"Jan. 14, 1716, died at Salem, Elizabeth Patch, the first-born female 
in the old colony of Massachusett's Bay, so that she must have lived 
86 or 87 years; and April 14, following, died at Newport, in Rhode- 
Island, Mary Godfrey, aged about 87, being the first child born there. 
-- The longevity of the first-born in each of the three colonies, is 
worth noting." 
As a well-wisher to mankind in general, I thought it worth while to 
send this to your useful paper, in order to allay the apprehensions of 
those, whose inclinations, business, or necessities may induce them 
to settle in that part of the British Empire. I must add at the same 
time, that I have myself lately travelled over the greater part of that 
extensive continent; and can with truth say, in spite of all that hath 
been boldly and positively asserted to the contrary, that 1 could not 
discover among that whole people, one grain of disaffection to their 
brethren in Britain, nor did I meet with a single person who had ever 
formed the most distant idea of throwing off their allegiance to the 
mother country. This it is doing them but bare justice to declare; and 
is all the return I can at present make them for the kindness and 
hospitality with which, purely on account of my being an Old En- 
gland-Man, I was universally treated. I am, Sir, your constant reader, 
Eas: 
The London Chronicle, December 15, 1767 
Benjamin Franklin

Chapter 1 
“And the Lord God formed man of the dust of the ground, and breathed into his 
nostrils the breath of life; and man became a living soul.” 
- Genesis 3:7 
“Iam a brother to dragons (dinosaurs), and a companion to owls” 
- Job 30:29 
“Praise The Lord from the earth, ye dragons, and all deeps.” 
-Psalms 148:7 
“And he laid hold on the dragon, that old serpent, which is the Devil, and Satan, 
and bound him (in fossils) a thousand years.” 
-Revelation 20:2 
Fire Power 
It is often said that, “The road to hell is paved with good inten- 
sions” and that “A kingdom was lost for want of a nail!” Both of these 
statements are true when it comes to the world wide pandemic prob- 
lem of obesity and all of its terrible consequences. 
There have been so many political, medical and dietary approaches 
devised to solve the problem of obesity, yet they have all failed, be- 
cause all of our historical approaches to obesity focused on the symp- 
tom - fat - fat in the food and fat in our bodies - none of the weight 
loss programs were focused on the cause. To date, without excep- 
tion, all attempts at dealing with obesity, diabetes and the Metabolic 
Syndrome are focused on the most obvious symptom (the body fat) 
and the simple concepts of exercise and caloric restriction (CR) diets. 
Believing that an obese person is fat because of a simple over

2 
consumption of fat, carbohydrates and total calories would be the 
same as saying that wildfires are caused by ashes - because there are 
always ashes found after every fire! 
This book will focus on the basic cause of obesity, thence on to 
the prevention and cure. As a result this book will feel and read dif- 
ferently than any other weight loss book that you have ever flipped 
through, read or used.To find out where we went wrong, we must 
begin at the beginning of the self constructed road to Hell. We will 
have to travel back in history - way, way back to before the begin- 
ning, and then methodically retrace our steps to find out exactly 
where we exited the highway to health that we had traveled relent- 
lessly for millennia, where we suddenly veered from our safe, tried 
and true path and took the wrong fork in the road, reverse our course 
and get back on the right road - the right road to fitness, health and 
longevity! 
Like an alcoholic, we must first admit we are lost and in 
trouble before we can begin to hope to extract ourselves from 
our downward spiral in health and our increase in weight. If we 
continue doing what we are presently doing in the world of weight 
loss and control, we will continue to get what we now have and 
we and our very cultures will be crushed by the health and fi- 
nancial cost of our own weight. 
So to begin at the beginning, we will start by using the time line 
of anthropological and geological dating, not because we necessarily 
totally believe in this system, but because that’s how the scientific 
clues to our obesity questions and problems are laid out. 
For those wanting to use biblical dating, they can use the named 
geological periods rather than the geologist’s chronological dates. 
The Road to Eden 
From the beginning, for “millions of years,” every species of plants 
and animals on earth depended on a daily source of “solar income” 
or fresh supplies of energy from the sun or consuming another or- 
ganism of some form that had temporarily stored the sun’s energy. 
Plants are like solar panels, their light sensitive chlorophyll laden

HELLS KITCHEN 3 
leaves collecting the sun’s radiant light energy as it arrives and con- 
verts it into chemical energy that humans and animals could eat, 
store and metabolically “burn.” 
Plants not only convert “solar income” into burnable chemical 
energy, they also store it for later use as cellulose, starch and sugar - 
storing it in their cells until they either use the energy themselves, 
decompose, burn or get eaten. Coal originated as plants that became 
buried deep within the earth, they were compressed under millions 
of tons of pressure and heated to thousands of degrees and ultimately 
became a fossil fuel. 
Before man appeared on earth, before the dinosaurs (dragons), 
before the continents slipped into their 21st century positions, a liv- 
ing fossil covered the earth. They were members of a swampy plant 
culture, literally forests of strange trees and enormous ferns - “mon- 
sters of the vegetable world.” 
Before the Jurassic Period, before the Triassic Period, before the 
Permian Period, there was the Carboniferous Period, a long period of 
primitive earth’s history named after the coal that was then being 
created. 
According to geologists, for most of the earth’s four and a half 
billion geological years, its land masses were starkly naked, while the 
cradle of plant and aquatic life unfolded unseen (except for the eyes 
of God) in the depths of the oceans. 
In March of 2004, the International Union of Geological Scien- 
tists added the Edicaran Period to the Pre-cambrian Eon, an enor- 
mous time span that covers ninety percent of the earth’s geologic 
history. The new period takes its name from Edicaran fossils, remains 
of the most complex animal life, that were found in enormous num- 
bers in the Edicaran Hills of South Australia. 
The period begins around 620 million years ago with the rapid 
end of the global Marinoan glaciation - the catastrophic environmen- 
tal calamity that entombed earth in ice for several million years. It 
ended 543 million years ago with the appearance of the Cambrian 
Period and the “explosion” of diverse forms of animal life. 
The first toehold gained on the earth’s beaches by plants occurred 
about 425 million years ago, and then ever so slowly, one could even

4 
say in the beginning “shyly;” after a time, they “figured out” how to 
convert from an aqueous life of light filtered through water to an 
atmospheric life collecting and employing solar incomes directly from 
the sun, and they eventually flourished and covered the once bare 
land with a green tidal wave of plant life. 
By the Carboniferous Period - about 360 to 290 million years 
ago, there was nothing shy about the growth and tangle of plants on 
earth - they dominated the earth! 
Verdant jungles of a density unimagined today sprung up, at first 
they crawled and then they raced over the formerly barren earth, 
ravenously taking in the carbon dioxide from the atmosphere, using 
the new process of photosynthesis and the sun’s “solar income” to 
fuel their growth; the new “trick” was that plants “learned” how to 
store the excess energy produced in the form of cellulose (wood), 
plant starches and sugars. 
Most coal beds were derived from the first waves of plants to 
leave the oceans and cover the land - animals soon followed. Coal is 
the concentrated remains of extinct plant life forms that once flour- 
ished and covered the planet. 
Most prehistoric coal forests were dominated by the Lepidodendron, 
a huge tree six feet in diameter that could reach the dizzy height of 
175 feet, the maximum growth potential of height that photosynthe- 
sis had ever attained to that date. Photosynthesis, a relatively new 
“discovery” had taken the single cell algae surrounded by the nutri- 
ent rich soup of the oceans, to an awesome dry land behemoth that 
converted the solar income of radiant energy into growth power and 
stored energy. 
At its crown, the Lepidodendron branched into short stubby 
branches covered with narrow leaves three feet long. Unlike modern 
trees, the Lepidodendron’s heart wood was soft, pulpy and pithy; as 
long as water was in good supply the inner cells would remain turgid 
and kept the tree erect; during draught periods, these monster trees 
dehydrated, wilted, drooped and fell into the swamp. 
The prehistoric jungles also boasted another botanical giant, the 
Sigillaria, a tree that had a long trunk that was characterized by a 
single fork at the crown; each fork was adorned with a large bundle

HELLS KITCHEN 5 
of long strap - like leaves. 
The ancient jungles seethed with vegetarian and carnivorous in- 
sects. With little or no competition or predators, these insects reached 
gigantic sizes; some cockroaches attained lengths of 12 inches; drag- 
onflies’ wingspans reached almost three feet; and millipedes grew to 
six foot lengths. 
Early in the Carboniferous Period, small newt-like vertebrates, 
not much different from fish, scuttled through the dense swampy 
undergrowth. By the end of the Carboniferous Period, fifteen foot 
long amphibian-like creatures were pulling their bellies through the 
primeval mud. During the same Carboniferous Period, quietly, and 
without much notice, some amphibians changed from laying gelati- 
nous strings of aquatic eggs and began to lay hard shelled eggs incu- 
bated in nests built of decomposing plant material. 
The shelled egg laying amphibians were followed by reptiles (some 
of which laid eggs and some gave birth to live young), dinosaurs 
(dragons - it is interesting to note that dragons are mentioned in the 
Bible thirty five times), birds and eventually mammals. 
The earliest known and dated reptile (340 million years old) is 
called Casineria, it was six inches long and looked like a cross be- 
tween a newt and a lizard; it had five toes, simple claws, a vertebral 
column and a foreleg anatomy that is characteristic of lizards. 
At first, mammals were small shrew-like creatures that ate insects 
and worms. About 130 million years ago, a twelve pound fanged 
marsupial, Repenomamus robustus, devoured small asian dinosaurs, 
psitasaur, and the eggs of larger dinosaur species - now that mam- 
mals were beginning to eat dinosaurs, dinosaur populations began 
to decline. 
Almost all of the plants of the Carboniferous Period wound up as 
coal because of low atmospheric oxygen levels (remember bacteria, 
algae and land plants had just begun to produce oxygen as a byproduct 
of photosynthesis) and they failed to decompose in the way that 
modern plants do in a twenty percent oxygen atmosphere. 
Typically, when a plant dies today, oxygen penetrates its meta- 
bolically dead cells allowing bacteria, fungi, amoeba and small in- 
sects to digest and break down the wood. By contrast, as the dense

6 
mass of the plants of the Carboniferous Period died, they fell into 
oxygen-poor water or mud, or were covered by additional layers of 
dead plants or anaerobic sediment. 
Sometimes this process of coal building was part of a great ebb- 
ing and flowing of the sea level along the coasts. During the Carbon- 
iferous Period, glaciers grew and alternatively shrank making the 
oceans rise and fall. As the glaciers melted, the rising oceans would 
flood the tropical forests with salt water and sediment. As the gla- 
ciers built up again, the sea levels would drop and drained the salt 
water that had been drowning and choking the forests. 
Because significant levels of oxygen were not yet available to sup- 
port the decomposition process in the flooded forests, the plants only 
partially decomposed, leaving an accumulation of black carbon that 
contained huge quantities of stored “solar income.” This spongy mass 
of carbon-rich “solar income” first became peat. After being squeezed 
and simmered by the tremendous pressure and heat of global geo- 
logical forces, the peat ultimately hardened into coal. 
In addition to the forest's carbon that wound up trapped in the 
coal, the energy that had accumulated in the plant’ “solar savings 
account” over millions of years was also trapped. 
The Taming of Fire 
Fire is far older than man; it was set loose in the forests and 
tangles of brush by volcanic eruptions; lightening bolts; spontane- 
ous combustion generated by the heat of decomposing plant mate- 
rial; and the friction of dry branches rubbing against each other at 
the pleasure of the wind. These early fires laid mineral rich ash onto 
the surface of the early “soil” which mixed a fine small particle sized 
mineral nutrient into the deep organic mulch that had built up over 
the years, decades, centuries and millennia. 
Humans are one of the most efficient converters of “solar income” 
found in our food to mechanical energy. Humans need only about 
half the calories that a horse needs to exert the same level of physical 
energy. Our human metabolism is very efficient. Anthropologist’s 
believe that our large brains are the result of a constant stream of

HELLS KITCHEN 7 
high protein, high fat food supplies, “as we ate more meat our brain 
got larger and it allowed us to figure out how to get more meat”; our 
large brains allowed us to develop other methods to tap into the 
endless stream of “solar income” trapped by plants - fire! 
By burning plants that we couldn’t eat, such as trees and briar 
patches, humans and their cultures rapidly moved far beyond the 
creative limits of their digestive and metabolic systems. This revela- 
tion - fire, was the step that took humans farther than our earthly 
competitors - humans controlled fire, something that no other spe- 
cies would ever do! This new method of tapping into stored energy 
or “solar income” protected us from the harsh environment of the Ice 
Age and allowed us to control the temperature of our microenviron- 
ments. Humans were no longer flotsam on the sea! We had “fire 
power” in the broadest sense! 
Early human remains from nine individuals were found in an 
Ethiopian fossil cache dated from 4.3 million years ago. The prehis- 
toric humans, Ardipithecus ramidus, were found with a treasure trove 
of the oldest dated stone tools. Plant and animal fossil remains indi- 
cate that these early humans lived in low lying woodlands and grass- 
lands teeming with monkeys, mole rats and wild cattle. 
Anthropologists long believed that early humans “tamed” fire 
about 500,000 years ago, however, a recent analysis of burned ante- 
lope bones from caves in Swartkrans, South Africa, confirms that 
early man (Australopithecus robustus and Homo erectus) had built camp- 
fires and cooked meat roughly 1.5 million years ago - as many as a 
million years earlier than previously thought. 
The cooked antelope bones were first described in 1988 by pale- 
ontologist, Bob Brain at the University of Cape Town. Brain suspected 
they had been burned by humans because the degree of charring 
indicated temperatures of more than 1,100 degrees F - heat levels 
that could be produced by campfires - heat produced by natural grass 
fires does not exceed 700 degrees F 
Anne Skinner, a chemist at the Williams College, Massachusetts, 
used Electron Spin Resonance, to examine the measurements of the 
free radicals left in the charred bones. Skinner said, “I’m very confi- 
dent (the charred antelope bones) reached temperatures consistent

8 
with a campfire.” 
Fire, as a tool, separated man from other tool using animals - 
other animals metabolically used the sun’s solar income, sticks, rocks, 
fiber, mud and water - only man had fire. Man tamed fire and used it 
for many thousands of years before he learned how to start and kindle 
it at will. 
Many technologically ignorant peoples, including Homo erectus 
or Java man (before 250,000 years ago), found that starting a fire 
from cold fuel was a difficult and an exasperating task. In the begin- 
ning, when fire was “captured” it was fed and maintained for as long 
as possible, a primitive technique that is still practiced world wide 
yet today. Fire is easy to preserve or transport by the use of wet wood, 
lichens, dry fennel stalks, etc. In some cultures, hearth fires are main- 
tained for generation after generation, oftentimes for the life of the 
house. 
The first evidence we have for fire starting by humans is associ- 
ated with the early Paleolithic Period of Europe (about 25,000 to 
30,000 B.C.). Flints in the classic “strike-a-light” hand held shape 
and chunks of iron pyrite were discovered together in charcoal fire 
beds. However, iron pyrite is only found in very few places on earth 
and therefore, most primitive fire starters fall into the category of the 
fire drill (a bow used to spin an arrow shaft), fire saw and fire plow 
(pointed stick that is rapidly pushed back and forth in a groove cre- 
ated in a log or board). Humans eventually learned that they could 
start fire at will by generating sparks that were then showered into 
“tender” and with the simple generation of heat with some form of 
friction used to ignite tender. 
With fire, humans were able to smelt metal, make tools, fire 
pottery, bake bricks, produce weapons, keep ourselves warm, 
eat food that was otherwise inedible (grain), keep predators at 
bay, destroy our enemies, develop specialties and unwittingly, 
we humans, with the ash byproduct that was generated by fire, 
produced the fertilizer and mineral supplement (“culinary” ashes) 
that would nurture and sustain wé humans for thousands of years 
to come! 
Early humans who had not yet learned how to put their wood

HELV’S KITCHEN 9 
ashes from their heating and cooking fires into primitive gardens 
lived a life that was short, hard, miserable, filthy, fearful, brutish and 
violent. They were often on the verge of starvation. They subsisted 
primarily in family units or family bands. They were driven by an 
internal, intrinsic and relentless salt and mineral hunger as were all 
living creatures. The hunger for salt and minerals didn’t begin with 
the appearance of potato chips, they began with the appearance of 
the first human. 
One isolated group of tiny people, dubbed the “Hobbit”, Homo 
floresiensis, existed on the Indonesian island of Flores, 370 miles east 
of Java. They were the size of a Homo sapiens pygmy or midget only 
three foot tall with the body of a three year old, however, their brain 
was smaller than a grapefruit - a small human with a brain that was 
smaller than the brain of a chimpanzee. 
There is some evidence that these tiny, tribal, and carnivorous 
humans accepted gourds filled with food that the modern human 
Floresians offered to them, until the 1500s, when Dutch traders ar- 
rived. 
The “Hobbit” were clever enough to employ fire, make tools 
(points, blades, awls and tiny barbs) and hunt - cooking fire charred 
bones of the Stegodon (dwarf elephant), Komodo dragon (dinosaur), 
a wide variety of rodents and other animal species were collected 
from garbage dumps. 
Early skeletons of the south Pacific “Hobbit” were dated as far 
back as 95,000 years ago, the more recent ones were dated at 13,000 
- 18,000 years; however, the primitive “Hobbit” crafted stone tools 
dating back to 800,000 B.C. 
The island of Flores was populated by hoards of an extinct dwarf 
elephant (Stegodon), monster rats and giant lizards (including the 
Komodo dragon), all of which were the primary food of the “Hobbit.” 
There is no evidence that the “Hobbit” learned to use the ashes 
from their heating and cooking fires to put in their food, salt or gar- 
dens - they disappeared leaving only a small fossil record. Only six 
skeletons have been found, suggesting the others were mineral poor 
and simply didn’t survive the ravages of the millennia. 
The small stature of the Hobbit demonstrates very clearly that

10 
humans, like other life forms are not exempt from natural laws, in 
this case the “island rule.” The island rule states that, “a combination 
of selective forces - geographic and temporal isolation, scarce resources 
and the absence of predators - will occasionally result in develop- 
ment of giant and dwarf species.” The Hobbit simply followed the 
same historical course as the isolated desert Bushmen of Southern 
Africa. 
Humans were territorial; they hunted and gathered in naturally 
productive and fruitful areas that had quantities small game, edible 
insects, their favorite plants and either clay or salt deposits for a source 
of minerals, a virtual Garden of Eden. There were endless fights be- 
tween competing family bands and tribes over these flora and fauna 
rich Eden’. 
Initially, the small family bands of humans were free of highly 
infectious diseases such as small pox, influenza, tuberculosis and the 
plague because they were living in small bands, isolated from travel- 
ers, strangers and new comers that could be carriers or hosts for 
disease causing viruses and bacteria. 
They were, however, still susceptible to anthrax from the skins of 
contaminated animals, botulism from eating contaminated maggots 
that infested the decomposing carcasses of animals killed by large 
predators,and tetanus and gangrene acquired from soil, manure and 
feces contaminated puncture wounds. 
Their offspring had a very low survival rate, and those that did 
survive were often deformed with congenital birth defects (Down's 
syndrome, heart defects, cleft palate, spina bifida, muscular dystro- 
phy, clubbed hands and feet, etc.) caused by pervasive maternal nu- 
tritional deficiencies. 
Injuries took their toll; fractures healed all askew or didn’t heal at 
all, leaving the victim to remain deformed and dependant upon the 
good graces and surpluses of the rest of the band. If the disabilities 
were catastrophic, the victim was oftentimes eaten by his fellows. 
Revered relatives were often eaten when they died to perpetuate their 
spirit (mortuary cannibalism). 
After 500,000 years ago, the human populations of western 
Eurasia and Africa began to diverge from each other and from east-

HELLS KITCHEN 11 
ern Asian populations in skeletal anatomy. Those that put wood, peat 
or kelp ashes from their cooking and heating fires into their food 
(remember the salt hunger - ashes are the minerals left over when the 
carbon of the fuel is burned away) or primitive gardens, were taller, 
their bones stronger, they were more fertile, delivered healthier ba- 
bies and lived longer. Harder mineralized skeletons resulted in their 
bones surviving the ravages of rodents and scavengers, decomposi- 
tion and the various destructive forces of the millennia - full skel- 
etons of early man were now surviving into the 21st century. 
Between 130,000 and 40,000 years ago the populations of Eu- 
rope and western Asia produced many fossilized skeletons known as 
Neanderthals. They were the first humans known to bury their dead 
and care for their sick and infirm. Their stone tools, however, were 
still crude and lacked the standard shapes that would indicate spe- 
cific functions. 
African fossilized skeletons from 100,000 years ago had more 
modern skeletal features (humans continued to put the ash byproduct 
of fire into their food and gardens) than did their Neanderthal coun- 
terparts - they made the same crude stone tools. The bone evidence 
of their food prey indicated that their hunting skills were minimal 
and they only killed and ate small safely killed animals, including 
rats and rabbits. They had not yet reached the capability of killing 
pigs, buffalo and larger more dangerous species such as the elephant 
(mastodon, mammoth and the dwarf Stegodon). They could not yet 
catch fish - their living sites on the sea coasts and lake shores are 
devoid of fish hooks and fish bones. 
Human developmental history (physical, emotional, spiritual and 
creative) appears to have found it’s footing and gained “traction” about 
50,000 years ago - a time which Dr. Jared Diamond calls “the Great 
Leap Forward.” The first real signals of the human leap include an 
increase in height and the appearance of standardized tools and os- 
trich shell jewelry. 
Similar human developments appeared about 40,000 years ago 
in southwestern Europe, where abundant artifacts are associated with 
skeletons of Cro-Magnons. Soon after, the garbage dumps preserved 
at archaeological sites become more and more complex pointing to

12 
biologically and behaviorally modern humans. 
Cro-Magnon garbage heaps yielded stone tools, bone tools and 
bone fish hooks. Tools were produced in diverse and distinctive shapes 
so modern that their functions as needles, awls and engraving tools 
are straight forward and easily identified. Tools became more com- 
plex, moving beyond simple hand-held scrapers to multi-piece tools 
(stone axes) and weapons such as harpoons, spear throwers and bows 
and arrows. 
These new weapons allowed the human to kill larger and more 
dangerous prey froma safe distance. The creation of ropes and threads 
for nets, twine and string permitted the invention of nets, lines and 
snares which allowed for the addition of small mammals, fish and 
birds to our human diet which promoted the expansion of our hab- 
itable territories. 
Rubble from houses and scraps from sewn clothing point to an 
improved ability to survive frigid weather. Modern jewelry and ten- 
derly buried skeletons show a new aesthetic and spiritual progres- 
sion for humans - humans were now talking to God. 
Of all of the Cro-Magnon’s creations that have been preserved, 
the most remarkable is their artwork, including the famous cave paint- 
ings, statues and musical instruments. The impact of the life sized 
painted bulls and horses in the Lascaux Cave of southwestern France 
or the rhino, lion, horses, mammoth, panther, owl, bear and buffalo 
paintings of the limestone Chauvet grotto in southern France on a 
visitor is dramatic - you realize these ancient people were modern 
not only in their skeletons but also in their abstract minds! 
The limestone caves, located in the Lascaux woods near 
Montignac, France, are in fact ancient museums of natural history: 
they include a main cavern sixty six feet wide and sixteen feet high, 
containing many steep galleries, magnificently decorated with en- 
graved and painted figures. Archeologists date the site back to the 
last Ice Age, between 15,000 and 13,000 B.C. In total there are 600 
painted animals and prehistoric symbols, and 1,500 engravings. 
The Ice Age Chauvet grotto is known as the “Sistine Chapel” of 
prehistoric art. The grotto is found deep within the limestone hills of 
Auvergne, west of the Rhone Alps. The Chauvet grotto contains 447

HELLS KITCHEN 13 
paintings and 4,000 well preserved foot prints of men, children and 
dogs. 
The last Chauvet artist produced a painting between 24,000 to 
32,000 years ago. The artist's work was “signed” with a negative print 
of his hand, which was produced by blowing red ochre onto a non- 
arthritic hand that was held palm down against the cave wall. The 
artists also signed their work with a positive print of their hand by 
putting the ochre on their hand and then placing their print on their 
painting! 
Art historian, Carole Fritz recorded, “I even cried the first time I 
saw it (Chauvet). It's just so intense, like sometimes when you are 
listening to (an) opera, it just moves something in your heart. And 
then you see these finger marks on the walls where they spread the 
pigment and dried their hands and you really have the feeling they 
just left the cave.” 
The Ice Age artists used mainly shades of red, yellow, black and 
brown, using charcoal and rare earth mineral pigments. The most 
dramatic of the paintings depict four sixteen foot long aurochs (large 
wild cattle); other animals include red deer, horses and stags,some of 
which appear to be crossing a river. 
No human bones have been found in either of the caves, how- 
ever, the Chauvet grotto has been a treasure trove of bones from four- 
teen different animal species including rodents, reptiles, birds and 
bears. The bear bones dominate - they have recovered 3,700 of them. 
It is important to note that there were no grains or fruit seeds 
found in the caves, clearly indicating that the Cro-Magnon artists 
were primarily hunters and carnivores and that hunting dominated 
their lives. 
At 40,000 years ago Cro-Magnons “moved into the neighbor- 
hood” of Europe with their modern skeletons (signifying the use of 
the ash byproduct of cooking and heating fires as a supplement mixed 
with salt to extend and cut the more valued salt and in their food as 
“culinary” ash), tools and more clever weapons. 
Classical anthropologists believe that the Cro-Magnons,,used their 
advanced technology, superior weapons to kill, infect or displace the 
Neanderthals - a theorized event that left little or nothing behind in

14 
the form of evidence of hybridization between the Neanderthals and 
Cro-Magnons or a straight forward and ruthless genocide. 
We don’t believe the Cro-Magnons fought, captured, enslaved or 
slaughtered the Neanderthals, because they would have eaten them 
and left their charred, cracked and scraped bones in their garbage 
heaps with the rest of the debris of hunting. They would have cre- 
ated art work that depicted the great heroic battles, heroes, the slain 
enemy and victories. 
We believe that the Neanderthals just failed to learn a basic sur- 
vival technique, and allowed themselves to be “edged out” in a simi- 
lar fashion as the prehistoric Bushmen allowed themselves to be 
“edged out” of the isolated and idyllic Cape of South Africa and into 
the Kalahari Desert, by the more advanced gun toting Dutch explor- 
ers from the Dutch East Indies Tea Company in 1462; and in a simi- 
lar fashion as the island of Flores “Hobbit” allowed themselves to be 
edged out by the 16th century Dutch. 
Neanderthals were primarily hunters and they developed the 
nutritional deficiencies of pure carnivores who had failed to develop 
the habit of using wood, peat or kelp ashes to mix with salt and 
season food and/or failed to put the ashes into their primitive or 
crude “gardens.” One bad hunting year or a draught of several years 
could have wiped them out. 
The number of humans surviving to “old age,” (at least double 
the age of reproductive maturity) quadrupled at about 30,000 years 
ago - something equally as dramatic as the taming of fire happened 
here which we will talk about in due coarse. 
Anthropologists looked at 750 fossilized dental and jaw fragments 
of early humans spanning over millions of years, from the Australo- 
pithecines to Neanderthals to early modern humans. Old age was 
defined as at least double the age which reproductive maturity was 
reached, which is also the point when the third molars usually erupt 
in the teen age years. 
In calculating the ratio of old-to-young hominids, Rachel Caspari 
of the University of Michigan, at Ann Arbor and Sang-Hee Lee of the 
University of California, at Riverside found a trend of increased lon- 
gevity across the human family. With “modern” humans, older adults

HELLS KITCHEN 15 
finally outnumbered younger ones for the first time at the 30,000 
year mark. 
The leap in longevity is thought to have been critical to the de- 
velopment of modern human culture, as elders passed down knowl- 
edge and helped to weld together complex societies. This “hand - me 
- down” knowledge, included, how to cook and preserve meat, how 
to use wood ashes to extend and cut what they perceived as the more 
valuable salt and to employ “culinary” ashes in the preparation of 
certain foods and the value of putting wood ash into their primitive 
gardens. 
The results and benefits of incorporating wood ashes into their 
food either directly as salt extenders or “culinary” ashes or indirectly 
as a garden fertilizer produced stronger, taller, healthier, more fertile, 
more creative and longer-lived humans - we had not yet left the main 
road and humans were in fact accumulating good habits, becoming 
healthier, more fertile, living longer and honing better survival skills.

16

Chapter 2 
“The aim of science is to seek the simplest explanation of complex 
facts. We are apt to fall into the error of thinking that the facts are simple 
because simplicity is the goal of our quest. The guiding motto in the life of 
every natural philosopher should be, seek simplicity and distrust it.” 
- Alfred North Whitehead 
Ash Power 
It is clear and dramatic - something universally uplifting hap- 
pened around 100,000 to 50,000 years ago. Dr. Jared Diamond, pri- 
mary proponent of the “Great Leap” argues for an increase in the size 
and “perfection” of the human voice box, and thus he believes im- 
proved vocal and language skills, was the single event that vaulted 
humans forward in time, while others argued for a universal change 
in function and size of the human brain. 
Our belief is that people learned, between 100,000 and 50,000 
years ago, the value of the ash byproduct of their cooking and heat- 
ing fires in a world wide parallel type of discovery similar to their 
discovery that they could tame fire. They learned the value of mixing 
wood, peat and kelp ashes with salt to extend and dilute the more 
valuable NaCl, employ them as “culinary” ashes and putting their 
wood heating and cooking byproduct (ashes) into a primitive type of 
garden for fertilizer. 
After all, wood ashes were everywhere as a daily byproduct of 
cooking, heating and the enterprise of survival - it was natural to try 
and use ashes in many ways. Certainly with a salt hunger driving

18 
them on a daily basis, humans would have put the ash in their mouth, 
just as a modern child puts everything in their mouth to see if they 
were tasty or usable. 
The universal kitchen byproduct, ashes, were the minerals that 
were left when carbon fuels were burned, They were historically 
thrown out into the garbage piles, tasted and added to food as a spice 
to satisfy a craving as we salt our food today, to satisfy a salt hunger- 
a universal craving shared by all living humans and animals. 
The ashes, initially thrown into the garbage piles, would have 
resulted in adjacent plants growing more verdant and being more 
productive - this seemingly simple observation led to the wood ash 
being routinely placed in the soil around favored food plants, result- 
ing in greater production in terms of tons and bushels per acre and 
even more important, the fertilized gardens remained productive for 
longer periods of time. Modern agriculture was now in its infancy 
and the Great Leap Forward for mankind had begun in earnest! 
While some human cultures flourished in the fertile river valleys 
and flood plains that were rich with mineral laden flood silt (Egypt, 
China, India, Europe) and mineral rich glacial waters (Hunza, 
Vilcabamba, Titicaca, etc.), the vast majority of early humans lived in 
harsh barren or heavily forested mountains, dry semi arid plains and 
seemingly sterile ocean and lake shores. 
The majority of early humans obtained their minerals by eating 
the organs of their prey animals they killed, or directly or indirectly 
from wood, kelp, peat and manure (buffalo “chips”) ash that they 
directly consumed mixed with or without salt or indirectly by plac- 
ing the ashes in their gardens. 
There are primitive peoples yet today who add wood ashes, peat 
or kelp ashes directly into their food to satisfy their salt hunger and 
add the ashes to their gardens to extend the life of the garden and 
make it more productive - thus indirectly, yet regularly adding a con- 
stant stream of supplemental minerals into their food cycle. 
Early man transitioning from hunter gatherer to farmer, “man- 
aged land” in a primitive, inefficient and heavy handed way by burn- 
ing the mature forests and tangled brambles to allow the growth of a 
small number of “volunteer” edible shoots, tubers and seed plants

HELLS KITCHEN 19 
that always follow after the devastation of a wildfire. This process of 
humans transitioning from hunter gatherer to farmers took thou- 
sands of years and is still going on today in parts of the 21st century 
world - the process is expensive in terms of return and at best a 
gamble on the predictable results. 
Investigators have long believed that the first organized gardens 
that we might describe as small “farms” appeared about 11,000 years 
ago - and then village life and civilization soon followed. Before “farm- 
ing” was developed, humans were loose nomadic collections of fam- 
ily bands. 
In June, 2004, archaeologists announced that they had discoy- 
ered the remains of hoards of wild grains, grinding equipment, anda 
stone “oven” at Ohalo II, a 23,000 year old site on Israels Sea of 
Galilee. Ehud Weiss, a paleoethnobotanist at Bar-Ilan University, 
Ramat Gan, Israel says, “his team found traces of 143 kinds of seeds 
including, ancestors of wheat and barley.” 
Weiss feels, “these people gathered volunteer and wild grains in 
the late summer and early fall, ground them into flour and baked 
them (in the oven).” “These findings are a breakthrough in (the un- 
derstanding of) human diet and (food) technology - grinding and 
baking (grains) would have dramatically improved (the efficiency 
and usefulness of grains in) human nutrition,” said Weiss. 
Agriculture, as a science and commercial industry was born in 
the Fertile Crescent of Iraq 10,000 - 11,000 years ago with the early 
and clear domestication of eight “founder” crops - they were the start 
of large scale commercial agricultural systems not only in the local 
region, but also globally. Those eight founder crops were emmer 
wheat, einkorn wheat and barley; the lentil, pea and chickpea le- 
gumes; bitter vetch; and the fiber and oil crop - flax. 
Time budget studies showed that early “farmers” spent more time 
and labor hours per day than hunter gatherers did, however, their 
food supply was eventually more dependable. Archaeologists have 
clearly shown that the first farmers were smaller and more poorly 
nourished, suffered from more diseases and died at a younger age 
than did the hunter gatherers - grain farmers didn’t have access to 
the organ meat (liver, kidney, brains and hearts) that provided a de-

20 
pendable dietary source of vitamins (vitamin B12, Vitamin C, vita- 
min A, vitamin D, etc.), essential fatty acids (omega 3) and minerals 
(selenium, copper, zinc) to the hunters every time they killed an 
animal. 
Many early farmers simply didn’t know the nutritional secret and 
value of their cooking and heating ash byproduct or maybe there 
were just fewer of the essential minerals that are required by humans 
in the soil from which their wood grew. 
Early on, the simple availability of more calories and protein meant 
more people. Among the 250,000 species of plants and animals on 
earth only a relatively small number, several thousand perhaps, are 
edible for humans or are worth the effort to hunt, grow or gather. 
A few hundred (about 800) have been “domesticated” and adapted 
for human use. Even among the several hundred food crops, most 
provide little food in the grand scheme of human diets and wouldn't 
themselves account for the calories required to support the rise in 
human numbers. Just a few dozen varieties of plants account for 80 
percent of the modern agricultural production in the world today. 
These dozen economic and dietary agricultural giants are cereal 
grains that include wheat, corn, rice, barley and sorghum; soybean 
legumes; the roots and tubers potato, manioc and sweet potato; sugar 
cane and sugar beets; and the banana. The four earliest domesticated 
fruits were the olive, grape, fig and date - these four were apparently 
the easiest to domesticate of all of the wild fruits. 
Ancient Egyptians are usually credited with the domestication of 
the cat, however, in 2004 French archaeologists reported they had 
unearthed a large Neolithic village in Cyprus that contain the re- 
mains of a domesticated cat. The investigators reported the discov- 
ery of an intact skeleton of a cat that had been carefully buried beside 
the remains of a carefully buried human, along with a variety of tools 
and ceremonial objects. 
The feline remains were dated back to 9,500 years ago - 5,000 
years before the Egyptians began sculpting cats, or “miu” as the Egyp- 
tians called them. The two sets of remains were found less than 12 
inches apart, positioned symmetrically with their heads facing west. 
The investigators believe that the cats were used in Cyprus to

HELLS KITCHEN 21 
keep rodents out of their grain stores. The cat Felis silvestris lybica, 
somewhat larger than todays domestic cat, was only eight months 
old, was thought to be sacrificed to accompany its owner on the 
journey to the next world. 
In September 2004, Silvia Gonzalez, a geoarchaeologist, reported 
on the radiocarbon-dating of a skull (Penon woman III) that had 
been found near Mexico City in 1959 - the analysis showed that the 
skull was 12,755 years old, considerably older than any previously 
known Native American. 
Gonzalez believes that Penon Woman III was an ancestor of the 
Pericu, peripatetic hunter-gatherers, whose remains were dated back 
to 3,000 years ago and found throughout Baja California. Gonzalez 
suggests, “accounts left by 18th century Spanish missionaries of slim, 
shell-fish eating people in the Baja refer to the Pericu” - she also 
believes that, “the Spanish pushed the Pericu to extinction by forcing 
them into mission labor and simple corn diets.” 
In 4,000 B.C. pottery objects with surface glazes appeared and 
horses were domesticated in Eurasia. 
In 2,500 B.C. the first free-standing glass objects of Egypt and 
Mesopotamia appeared. 
In 1,500 B.C. the first glass vessels appeared. 
The “founder” crops in the eastern zone of North America were 
limited to four plant species domesticated between 2500 and 1500 
B.C. - a whopping 6,000 years after wheat and barley were domesti- 
cated in the Fertile Crescent. A species of North American squash 
was used as a container as well as a source of edible seeds; the bal- 
ance of the three “founder” plants were produced only for their seeds 
- sunflowers, a small daisy called “sumpweed” and goosefoot (a leafy 
relative of spinach). 
For 2,000 years these original North American founder plants 
were only available as less important foods in the total diet of Native 
Americans, while the indigenous Native Americans maintained their 
traditional hunter gatherer traditions and continued to rely on plen- 
tiful wild mammals, waterfowl, wild turkey, fish, shell fish and nuts 
and berries. 
Farming did not contribute to any major part of the Native Ameri-

22 
can diet until 500 to 200 B.C., when three additional seed crops 
appeared - knotweed, Maygrass and little barley. 
All of these native North American founder crops were high in 
protein (17 - 32 %), compared with 8 - 14% in modern wheat, 9 % 
for corn and even lower percentages for barley and white rice. The 
sunflower and the sumpweed were also high in oil (45 - 47%). 
Sumpweed produced 32 percent protein and 45 percent oil. With 
the exception of the sunflower all the founder plants had serious 
downfalls including small yields at one tenth that of wheat, strong 
odors and one, the sumpweed produced hay fever-like allergies. 
In contrast to the pre-founder crops, cereal crops alone account 
for one half of the calories consumed by the world’s human popula- 
tion today, because they are technologically easy to grow and they 
are economically favorable to produce, convert into finished foods 
(bread, pasta, dry cereals, etc.) and store, they have become the “go 
to food.” 
Many potential foods were not useful for humans as they 
transitioned from hunter gather to farmer because they were indi- 
gestible such as twig bark that a deer might eat; poisonous such as 
certain roots, leaves and mushrooms; too much work was required 
to gather and prepare, such as seasonal and or small hard-shelled 
nuts and seeds; or too dangerous such as large game that were armed 
with horns or teeth or protected by aggressive herds. 
Catching and domesticating wild herbivores by contrast was quick 
and easy compared with domesticating wild plants, which required 
clearing the land, sowing seed, irrigation, fertilization, weeding, har- 
vesting, processing and storing. 
To catch wild animals, a simple pen or fence only needed to be 
placed around water, a salt lick or ash dump, when the animals came 
into the pen to drink or lick the salt or ash minerals, a gate was 
pulled closed and the herd was started. (See Table) 
The main reason why Eurasia was the place where most of the 
“founder” animal species were domesticated was that it was the con- 
tinent that had the most potential species for domestication to begin 
with and lost the fewest to extinction over the last 40,000 years. 
Of the world’s 148 wild herbivorous animal species that were

24 
candidates for domestication, only 14 made it to the production line. 
The other 134 species failed (they were doomed to “perpetual wild- 
ness” and to the risks of extinction) because they lacked one or more 
of the six characteristics necessary for successful domestication: 
1. Diet: Every time an animal consumes a plant or another ani- 
mal, the conversion of the food biomass into the eater’s biomass pro- 
duces an efficiency of much less than 100 percent - usually about ten 
percent. It takes 10,000 pounds of corn to grow a 1,000 pound steer. 
If one wants to grow 1,000 pounds of carnivore, you have to feed 
them 10,000 pounds of herbivore fed on 100,000 pounds of corn. 
Many species are too feed specific or too inefficient to lend them- 
selves to domestication. 
The exception is the dog (an omnivore), which was originally 
domesticated and raised as a guard animal and hunting partner, how- 
ever, some breeds of dogs were domesticated and raised specifically 
for food in Aztec Mexico, Polynesia and ancient China (dog is still a 
popular “winter” dish in China). 
2. Growth rate: Domesticated species must grow and mature 
quickly, which eliminates primates (baboons, chimps and gorillas) 
and large herbivorous species such as elephant, rhino and hippo - it 
is more efficient to catch and train wild individuals of large species 
than it is to raise them. 
Rodents and lagomorphs (rabbits and hares), small herbivores 
(goats, sheep and cattle) and pigs grew and matured quickly. 
3. Problems of captive breeding: many animals are secretive 
and will not readily reproduce in confined “factory” like environ- 
ments. 
4. Nasty Disposition: The grizzly bear, onager (wild Asian ass), 
zebra and African Cape buffalo are examples of potentially good can- 
didates for domestication and sources of human food, however, their 
unchangeable and unmanageable dispositions relegates them to the 
“perpetual wildness” category.

HELLS KITCHEN 25 
5. Tendency to panic: Large herbivorous species react to danger 
from predators and humans in different ways. Some species are 
“flighty” and programmed for fast, instant escape by blind flight (deer 
and antelope) if they perceive a danger real or imagined. They will 
run into a fence or wall and literally kill themselves trying to escape 
in a walled enclosure. 
Other species (goats, sheep or cattle) are calmer and tend to bunch 
up in the herd or flock when they feel threatened. 
6. Social structure: Almost all species of large domesticated her- 
bivores that have been domesticated from wild species have three 
social characteristics in common: 1) they live in herds or flocks; 2) 
they exhibit a highly developed dominance hierarchy; 3) herds would 
share overlapping and “movable” home ranges. 
By growing and selecting and nurturing the species of plants and 
animals that humans could cultivate, domesticate and eat, we in- 
creased the density of our dependable food supply from 0.1 percent 
of an acre to more than 90 percent of an acre. An acre of land could 
then support significantly more people. Wandering and the need to 
follow and find herds, flocks and plants in productive and ripe states 
became unnecessary. 
One acre could now feed 10 or 100 times more people than in a 
hunter gatherer society. The power and productive energy of the 
greater density of useable biomass and the shear numbers of farmers 
and shepherds gave them a political strength, business and trade 
power and a military advantage over the less numerous and more 
environmentally vulnerable hunter gatherers. 
In human populations, the possession of livestock instantly added 
to their health, fertility and longevity. The animals provided a steady 
stream of meat, milk, skins, fuel, fertilizer and power to carry loads, 
pull a plow and wagons. Humans no longer had to follow the mi- 
grating herds or wait for the expected annual seasonal gluts of meat, 
fish and birds as the herds, flocks, bands and schools returned to 
breeding or feeding grounds - humans could now control and de- 
pend on a constant year round supply of food - now we had what

26 
seemed to be an unending food supply and fire. 
Some animals such as cattle, sheep, goats, horse, reindeer, water 
buffalo, yak and camels produced significant quantities of milk al- 
lowing for the production of liquid milk, butter, cheese and yogurt - 
producing considerably more calories and protein over their lifetime, 
compared with simply being chased down, killed and eaten. The 
animals organs, including the heart, livers, kidneys and brains were 
rich in stored minerals such as selenium, zinc and copper and vita- 
mins including vitamin B12, vitamin C, vitamin D and vitamin A, 
the bones were rich in calcium and phosphorus and the brains were 
rich in essential fatty acids (Omega-3) extracted from plants and in- 
organic mineral sources that were not easily or metabolically acces- 
sible to humans. 
Animals could eat and create usable food from grass and brush 
that were not directly usable by humans. Crop yields were increased 
by adding manure and manure/ wood ash mixes and composts to 
their gardens and fields. Even in the 21st century, despite the wide- 
spread availability of chemical fertilizer in industrialized nations, 
animal manure and wood ash mixes still make up the bulk of fertil- 
izers for the gardens and fields of the third world’s peasant and rural 
subsistence populations. 
Dried manure was historically used as fuel for cooking and heat- 
ing and is yet still used today (2005) as fuel for cooking and heating 
fires and the mineral rich ash then added directly or as part of a 
compost to the gardens and fields. 
Animals, as a source of draft power were the natural result of 
domestication. They carried packs, loads and humans on their backs 
over long distances, up hills, over rough terrain and they pulled plows, 
carts and wagons. 
At this point in time, human cultures were limited in size and 
complexity by the production limits of the hunting and gathering 
economies. The entire human hunter gatherer population of Europe 
is estimated to have been less than 100,000 people prior to the ap- 
pearance of organized agricultural food production. 
The upper limits of the numbers of individual humans in the 
earth’ total population,.was simply controlled by the availability and

HELLS KITCHEN 27 
quantity of food. Several good years in a row in terms of increases in 
animal populations and successful kills resulted in more babies sur- 
viving and greater numbers of adults living to older ages; and small 
migrations, poor kill efficiency, death of a primary hunter, famine 
and hostile weather would effect a cruel devastation on the human 
population. 
The size of the individual hunter-gatherer bands was set by the 
amount of the available food. The group size rarely exceeded 200 
individuals, while most bands were composed of only four or five 
families. Camps were moved quite frequently and as a result few 
personal belongings of any size or weight were accumulated. 
At 11,000 to 15,000 B.C. village life began. Agricultural success 
beginning 10,000 years ago led to larger and more densely popu- 
lated villages, towns and cities - perfect killing fields for measles, 
rubella, mumps, pertussis, smallpox, plague, influenza and tubercu- 
losis. 
Southwest Asia (Iraq) is credited with the earliest domestication 
of plants at 8,500 B.C. and animals about 8,000 B.C.; the eastern 
zones of the North American continent first domesticated plants and 
animals 6,000 years later. 
The earliest known cultivated emmer wheat came from the “Fer- 
tile Crescent” of Iraq about 8,500 B.C. and only moved west to Greece 
at 6,500 B.C.; the first domesticated chick peas came from Turkey 
and northern Syria about 8,000 B.C. and did not appear in India 
until 5,000 years later. 
The smelting of metal from ore started in the Near East about 
5,000 B.C. Evidence points to copper as the first metal to be ex- 
tracted from ore - copper carbonate, malachite and azurite were his- 
torically and commonly used as pigments, as a result, humans were 
quite familiar with various copper ores and other minerals. 
In 4,500 B.C. the Chinese were the first to discover and record 
the medicinal powers of plants. The Egyptians must be recognized 
for fully developing the physical and chemical properties of aromatic 
essences or plant oils. The natural antiseptic and antibacterial prop- 
erties of essential oils extracted from plants, and in particular cedar 
wood, myrrh and frankincense, made them ideal for preserving a

28 
body for “it’s journey into the next world.” 
Early drawings of Egyptian nobles and slaves depicted them as 
slim and fit - being overweight or obese was certainly not acommon 
human problem during the time of the pharaohs. 
About 3,400 B.C. the first wheel appeared near the Black Sea; it 
turns up over much of Europe and Asia over the next few centuries. 
All of these Old World wheels are of the same simple design - a solid 
wooden disc constructed from three planks fastened together by two 
or more Cross ties. 
As civilizations grew and prospered, and nations fought amongst 
them selves for political power, trees disappeared, they were simply 
gobbled up by hoards of men demanding fuel, timber for building 
and burning and clearing forested land for pasture and growing crops. 
The combination of these ever growing human populations and re- 
lentless demands for wood depleted the same forest stores of plant 
“solar income,” resulting in an energy crisis. 
The numbers and sizes of our fires, our meals, our towns, and 
our economies and ultimately human numbers, were all controlled 
by the limited ability of the woody plants available to us to keep up 
with demand and convert the sun’s energy into a type of energy that 
we could use as food, fuel and building materials. 
Many early craftsmen turned to a “hybrid” fuel that would be a 
significant bridge between wood and coal - charcoal. Charcoal is wood 
that has already been partially burned. For thousands of years char- 
coal has been made by heaping wood into piles that were set on fire 
and then buried causing a slow oxygen depleted smolder that pro- 
duced almost pure carbon. The charcoal burned cleaner and hotter 
than wood, however, the process of making charcoal was energy 
wasteful as it takes four pounds of wood to make one pound of char- 
coal. 
In these early energy crises, coal presented some lucky cultures 
with the stored energy of millions of years of solar income. Solar 
income, that had been deposited in a “solar savings account” of a size 
that no one at the time could fathom. Coal would give these early 
energy seekers the power to change almost every relationship that 
humans had with nature, the earth and the sun itself!

HELLS KITCHEN 29 
At 3,300 B.C., a thirty five year old Neolithic man (“Otzi”) was 
murdered by being shot in the back with an arrow. His body was 
later found in 1991 in a melting glacier in the Tirolean Otztal Alps on 
the Italian-Austrian border. He had well trimmed hair and was clothed 
in a grass cape, the skins of ibex and deer, and leather shoes stuffed 
with grass for insulation. 
Otzi carried a bow made from the wood of the yew tree, a quiver 
filled with arrows that were tipped with flint points, and a copper 
axe. 
Radiographs of Otzi’s well preserved body showed significant lev- 
els of degenerative arthritis of the neck, lumbar region of the lower 
back, and one hip; Otzi had calcifications of his arteries including 
carotid arteries, coronary arteries, aorta and iliac arteries - diseases 
thought to be the results of the dietary sins of modern western man. 
Otzi had the cardiovascular disease (arteriosclerosis) and the de- 
generative bone disease (osteoporosis and osteoarthritis) of modern 
humans, however, he was “fit” in terms of the BMI (Body Mass In- 
dex) which measures body fat versus lean mass. 
“Cardiovascular disease” and osteoporosis and osteoarthritis are 
all multiple vitamin, antioxidant and mineral deficiency disease com- 
plexes, that indicate that the Paleolithic diet was not in of itself per- 
fect. Whatever Otzi was eating, his diet was chemically less than per- 
fect - it certainly was deficient in vitamin C, calcium, magnesium 
and antioxidants - however, Otzi wasn’t obese. 
On December 2001, a Peruvian archaeological site was uncov- 
ered that was documented through carbon dating. to be the oldest 
city in the Americas, at 3,100 B.C. Construction of Caral, one of 
twenty cities in the coastal Peruvian zone of Norte Chico, started 
nearly 400 years before the first pyramid was built in Egypt and when 
most people in the world were still hunters and gatherers. The dis- 
covery was reported in the December 23, 2004 issue of the journal 
Nature. 
The “Andean” society remained basically the same for 1,200 years 
before being overrun by their more warlike neighbors - “that is the 
longest that any civilization is known to have survived,” said Jonathan 
Hass of Chicago’s Field Museum, who led the expedition.

30 
“We are seeing the emergence of centralized decision making, 
government and religion out of pristine conditions,” Hass said. “They 
were not following a pattern established by someone else. They were 
developing it on their own. An Andean culture was being invented 
in this area.” 
It had long been thought that the Americas were behind Europe, 
Africa and Asia in developing civilizations. The new date shows that 
the Americas, separated from the other centers of civilization by then, 
impassable oceans, developed in a parallel way to Europe and Asia. 
The Andean society was built mainly on cotton farming and trade 
with fishing villages along the coast of what is now modern day Peru. 
“Here is a unique system,” Hass said. “There wasn’t anything like this 
in the world.” 
The radiocarbon dating showed that the Supe Valley and the 
nearby Pativilca and Fortaleza valleys, construction began there even 
earlier - about 3,000 B. C. and spread over 700 square miles. 
The attraction for the Andean society for this location was the 
cold Humboldt Current that runs north from Antarctica along the 
Peruvian coast. The current is filled with marine life, which served as 
an “endless source” of high protein food. They also “figured out that 
if you take water out of the rivers and put it on desert land, the desert 
blooms and becomes very productive,” said Hass. 
The Andeans dug canals by hand to bring water to their fields 
and gardens. They grew guava, bean, peppers and fruit trees; how- 
ever, their most important crop was cotton, which was traded to the 
coastal villagers, who used the cotton to make fishing nets - “you 
can’t catch anchovies without nets.” Anchovies were the Andeans 
primary protein supply. 
The Andean diet dominated by small fish, would provide high 
quality protein, minerals (i.e. - calcium, zinc, selenium, magnesium, 
iodine, etc.), vitamins (A, D, E, etc.) and the essential fatty acids. 
There is evidence that the Andeans had communal hearths and 
had feasts that would coincide with successful harvests and fishing 
seasons. The Andeans developed an idyllic society where crime rarely 
occurred and few tried to attack them. Eventually, however, warfare 
was brought to them and these peaceful people were defeated and

HELLS KITCHEN 31 
Norte Chico was abandoned - only scattered subsistence farmers re- 
side there today.

- Michael Faraday 
“Beware of doctors, as they would bring death by medicine!”
