(234 - 149 B.C) 
Codes, Oaths and Medicine 
By 3,000 B.C., the warm and fertile zones in the Middle East, 
irrigated by the Tigris and Euphrates Rivers (calculated by some to 
be the location of the Garden of Eden) had generated many of 
humanity’s first steps into civilization - Ur on the banks of the 
Euphrates (Sumerians), 100 miles up river from the Persian Gulf; 
Babylon, farther up river; Assyria, centered on Assur; and later 
Nineveh on the Tigris, near what is now Mosul in 21st century Iraq. 
All of the Mesopotamian kingdoms (“lands between the rivers”) 
have produced extraordinary archeological artifacts and written lega- 
cies, which have allowed us to understand their civilizations, health, 
infrastructures and governments. 
The Mesopotamian legacy included 30,000 clay tablets, rich with 
cuneiform writings; approximately 1,000 of the tablets found in the 
Library of Assurbanipal (600 B.C.) were devoted to medical diagno- 
sis, therapy and prognosis. 
The main works, “The Treatise of Medical Diagnosis and Progno- 
sis,” are made up of 3,000 reports on forty tablets. It is comprised of 
a list of diseases:

a4 
1) “The patient coughs continuously. What he coughs up is thick 
and frequently bloody. His breathing sounds like a flute. His hand is 
cold, his feet are warm. He sweats easily, and his heart activity is 
disturbed.” -Tuberculosis. 
2) “Stinking disease and distended bellies.” - nutrient deficiency 
diseases (beriberi, pellagra, hypoproteinemia), symptomatic of the 
birth of grain based agricultural societies. 
What was clearly missing in all of these early health and disease 
descriptions was obesity. 
The Xia Dynasty (2205 - 1818 B.C.) fell after the Yie and Lo 
Rivers dried up; the Shang Dynasty fell after the Yellow River went 
dry. Primitive “slash and burn” agricultural techniques were thought 
to be the reason. After the fall of the Shang and Zhou (1122 -255 
B.C.) Dynasties, farming methods changed - wood ashes from heat- 
ing and cooking fires were added to subsistence gardens for fertil- 
izer. Another garden enrichment technique devised by the Chinese 
was to put plant debris and hand pulled weeds into holes and then 
burned to produce ash - the vegetables where then planted on top of 
the ash. 
The sixth king of the first dynasty of Babylon, Hammurabi (1728 
- 1686 B.C.) was a powerful king who brought Babylon to a feared 
and respected position amongst nations. Hammurabi’ greatest legacy 
included mathematical works, dictionaries, astrology and mystical 
writings, however, his finest works were gathered together in “The 
Code of Hammurabi.” 
The Code included 282 laws that deal with the control and regu- 
lation of society in general, family life, trades and professions - in 
effect a legal code. The laws included medical instructions for physi- 
cians. It's guidelines include fee schedules for therapy, with a sliding 
scale based on the patient's social or royal rank. 
There were ruthless fines and punishments “for incompetence or 
failure.” “If a physician has performed a major operation on a lord 
with a bronze lancet and has saved the lord’s life....he shall receive 
ten shekels of silver (a greater amount than the annul pay of a trades-

HELLS KITCHEN 35 
man); but if he caused the death of such a notable, his hand would 
be chopped off. A doctor causing the death of a slave would have to 
replace him.” 
The earliest Egyptian medical writings were produced in 2,000 
Bee: 
In 1,600 B.C., a “Book of Wounds” was produced near Luxor, 
Egypt. The work listed a head to toe inventory of forty eight case 
reports. The surgical cases treated included wounds, fractures, ab- 
scesses, circumcision, etc. Broken bones were set with ox-bone splints 
that were held in place with resin soaked bandages (casts), The pa- 
pyrus lists large numbers of “dressings, adhesive plasters, braces, 
plugs, cleansers and cauteries.” 
The Ebers papyrus (1,550 B.C.), found in Thebes is over sixty 
feet long, it is the oldest surviving medical “book.” The Ebers papy- 
rus contained information on fifteen diseases of the abdomen, twenty 
nine of the eyes, eighteen of the skin, twenty cough treatments. Seven 
hundred drugs and 800 formulae are discussed (herbs, minerals and 
animal parts and extracts). 
Notably missing in all of these Mesopotamian health writings are 
descriptions and treatments of obesity. 
Egyptian medicine credited vegetables and fruit with great heal- 
ing powers; they used tree resins (myrrh, frankincense and manna). 
Egyptians, like Mesopotamians, used plant extracts such as senna, 
colocynth and castor oil. Medicinal recipes included ox spleen, hog 
brains, tortoise bile sweetened with honey, and animal fat. Antimony, 
copper and other minerals were used as astringents and disinfec- 
tants. 
“Multiple” ingredient pills, ointments, poultices, fumigations, 
inhalations, gargles and suppositories contained ingredients ranging 
from leeks, lapis lazuli, garlic, onion, tamarisk, cereals, spices, con- 
diments, resins, gums, dates, hellebore, opium to cannabis were on 
the list of common “medications.” 
By 1,000 B.C. the populations together referred to as “Greeks,” 
were flourishing on the shores of the Aegean Sea, in lonia (the west- 
ern shores of Turkey), the Greek mainland (the Peloponnese) and 
the related islands.

36 
The accumulations of early Greek medical information were re- 
corded in the Homeric epics, dating from before 600 B.C., but also 
certainly offering older data. Greek scholars have documented 147 
battle wounds in the Iliad (106 spear thrust, 17 sword slashes, 12 
arrow hits, and 12 sling shot wounds). 
One of the most famous survivors of arrow wounds was King 
Menelaus of Sparta, whose physician “pulled the arrow from the kings 
flesh, sucked out the blood” and applied a topical salve. 
According to Greek mythology, Asclepius was a heroic warrior 
and “blameless” physician, he was the son of the god Apollo and a 
mortal woman. Homer described Asclepius as a tribal chief and a 
highly regarded wound healer; his sons became physicians who were 
called Asclepiads. As the “father-god” of medicine, Asclepius, was 
usually described as a bearded sage with a staff and snake (the origin 
of the caduceus sign of the modern physician, with it’s two snakes 
intertwined, double-helix like, on a winged staff; the shedding of the 
snake’s skin symbolized the renewal of life). Asclepius was frequently 
pictured with his daughters Hygeia (health or hygiene) and Panacea 
(cure-all). 
The Greeks are legendary for employing religious healing meth- 
ods including exorcisms, diviners, shamans and priests. 
By contrast, Hippocratic medicine, the foundation of Greek writ- 
ten medicine, quite clearly bases the medical healing art on natural 
philosophy rather than the supernatural. The Hippocratic writings 
never attributed disease or cures to the gods. 
Most of what we know about Hippocrates (460 to 377 B.C.) is 
a collection of myth and legend. The earliest of information claims 
that he was born on the island of Cos and that, “he lived a long and 
virtuous life.” The sixty writings that make up Hippocrates’ Corpus 
were in reality derived from many scholars (like the Bible), they were 
dispersed, mixed up, fragmented and then put together again like a 
puzzle. 
Hippocrates’ Corpus, as we know it, was put together about 250 
B.C. in the library at Alexandria; some texts were added at a later 
date - medical historians of modern times still argue over which parts 
of the Corpus are genuine and which have been composed and added

HELLS KITCHEN 37 
by students or admirers. 
In May, 2004, a team of Egyptian and Polish archaeologists, un- 
earthed the ruins of the University of Alexandria - the alma mater of 
Archimedes, that was in it’s peak of glory 2,300 years ago when Alex- 
andria was the center of the scholarly world. 
It was at the University of Alexandria that Archimedes invented a 
water pump design that is still used today; Euclid organized and 
developed the rules of geometry; Hypsicles divided the zodiac into 
360 equal arcs or degrees; and Eratosthenes calculated the diameter 
of the earth. Other scholars edited the works of Homer and pro- 
duced the Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testa- 
ment. 
Hippocrates observed that man is “governed by the same physi- 
cal laws as the cosmos, hence medicine must be an understanding, 
empirical and rational, of the workings of the body in it’s natural 
environment.” 
The profound concept in the Hippocrates Corpus was that “health 
was equilibrium and illness an upset.” 
Followers of Hippocrates specialized in bedside medicine, and 
even at this early date blamed their treatments failures on the patient's 
failure to follow directions: 
“Make frequent visits; be especially careful in your examinations, coun- 
teracting the things wherein you have been deceived at the changes. Thus 
you will know the case more easily, and at the same time you will also be 
more at your ease. For instability is characteristic of the humours (body 
fluids including blood, bile, urine, pus and mucus), and so they may also 
be easily altered by nature and by chance.....Keep a watch also on the 
faults of the patients, which often make them lie about the taking of things 
prescribed. For through not taking disagreeable drinks, purgative or other, 
they sometimes die. What they have done never results in a confession, but 
the blame is thrown upon the physician.” 
The therapeutic methods of the followers of Hippocrates was “ex- 
pectant” - they waited and watched their patients, talking, winning 
trust and giving a helping hand to the “healing power of nature” (vis

38 
medicatrix naturae) highlighted in Hippocratic text On Ancient Medi- 
cine. 
The Hippocratic Oath forbade the use of a knife, even for the 
common kidney and bladder stones (the results of raging osteoporo- 
sis). Additional works referred surgery to those competent in han- 
dling war wounds - surgery was considered an “inferior trade” of the 
hands, while the work of a true physician was a work of the head and 
mind. 
The preferred Hippocratic methods of treatment were steeped in 
dietary regulation. Humans can’t eat forage like herbivores, so a good 
diet was crucial for good health - “The first cook was the first physi- 
cian.” 
Followers of Hippocrates made no claims for “miracle cures,” 
however, they did promise “that they would first and foremost do no 
harm (primum non nocere)” and looked at themselves as friends, ad- 
vocates and representatives of the sick. 
Even though the Hippocratic Oath is taken by modern graduates 
of medical schools, the Oath’s origins are unknown other than it origi- 
nated somewhere around the fifth and third centuries B.C. The Oath 
did not reflect the general medical standards of conduct of the day. 
The Hippocratic reverence for human life (Hippocrates was against 
abortion and infanticide) was not shared by Plato and Aristotle. The 
Hippocratic Oath was the early form of the western style concept of 
a “profession” as a morally self-regulating discipline among those 
sharing a trade and committed to helping others. 
The shortcoming’s of the Hippocratic method of healing included 
a lack of deep knowledge of the internal physiology of the human 
body, however, its departure from contemporary thinking of disease 
was that “sickness was a disturbance in the health of the patient” was 
a landmark shift in thought. 
The preferred Hippocratic therapies were founded in the regula- 
tion of diet. He reasoned that humans, unlike sheep and goats, could 
not eat plant roughage diets and therefore a good and proper diet 
was essential to good health. But the Hippocratic teachings on diet 
(diatetica), meant more than a simple meal of food and drink, his 
view was that “diet” included an entire lifestyle including exercise,

HELLS KITCHEN 39 
sex, bathing, sleep and spirituality - in effect Hippocrates was the 
first physician to consider “fitness” in all of its forms essential to 
health and longevity. 
“Life is short, the art long, opportunity fleeting, experience falla- 
cious, judgment difficult,” offers the first of the Hippocratic apho- 
risms, outlining the arduous, but honorable labor of the physician of 
the time. 
Hippocratic medicine taught two principals that are no longer 
regarded as sacrosanct by the modern physician: 
1) The role of a physician was a lofty and selfless role. 
2) The understanding of sickness requires the understanding 
of nature. 
The Hippocratic Oath 
I swear by Apollo the healer, by Aesculapius, by Health and all 
the powers of healing, and call to witness all the gods and goddesses 
that I may keep this Oath and Promise to the best of my ability and 
judgment. 
I will pay the same respect to my master in the Science as to my 
parents and share my life with him and pay all my debts to him. I 
will regard his sons as my brothers and teach them the Science, if 
they desire to learn it, without fee or contract. I will hand on pre- 
cepts, lectures and all other learning to my sons, to those of my mas- 
ter and to those pupils duly apprenticed and sworn, and to none 
other. 
I will use my power to help the sick to the best of my ability and 
judgment; I will abstain from harming or wronging any man by it. 
I will not give a fatal draught to anyone if I am asked, nor will | 
suggest any such thing. Neither will I give a woman means to pro- 
cure an abortion. 
I will be chaste and religious in my life and in my practice. 
I will not cut, even for the stone, but I will leave such procedures 
to the practitioners of that craft.

40 
Whenever I go into a house, I will go to help the sick and never 
with the intention of doing harm or injury. I will not abuse my posi- 
tion to indulge in sexual contacts with the bodies of women or of 
men, whether they be freemen or slaves. 
Whatever I see or hear, professionally or privately, which ought 
not be divulged, I will keep secret and tell no one. 
If, therefore, I observe this Oath and do not violate it, may I pros- 
per both in my life and in my profession, earning good repute among 
all men for all time. If I transgress and forswear this Oath, may my 
lot be otherwise. 
>, “~~ 
Greek philosophers looked at health and fitness as two of the 
greatest assets of life that a human could have. The earliest philoso- 
pher to write about Hippocrates’ thoughts and methods was Plato 
(427 - 437 B.C.); he divided human nature into three functions - 
reason, spirit and appetites. The three functions, according to Plato, 
were located in the brain, the heart and the liver respectively - and all 
three were constantly in competition. 
Through reason and spirit, the philosopher could conquer a 
human's sordid animal desires. The Republic’s recognition and dis- 
tinctions between reason, appetite, mind and body, was of the great- 
est philosophical and psychological value. 
Platos pupil, Aristotle, additionally theorized significant new views 
on the constitution of human life. A doctors son from Stagira in 
Thrace, Aristotle (384 - 322 B.C.) moved to Athens to become a 
pupil of Plato; he later became the tutor and mentor of Alexander the 
Great (36 =323 BC): 
Aristotle, Alexander’s personal physician and tutor, made and 
recorded many profound physiological observations. 
Aristotle was a promoter for the systematic description of nature 
and he established a method of empirical study into the natural world: 
zoology, botany, and meteorology. He believed that the natural world 
was organized and that nothing occurred by accident - following 
that line of reasoning, anatomical reality was described in terms of 
their function (teleology). Aristotle’s methods of scientific investiga-

HELVS KITCHEN 41 
tion guided medical research for almost 2,000 years. 
Aristotle was the first scientific investigator to advance medical 
and biomedical science through the art of animal dissection. His de- 
scription of a three chambered heart perplexed many of his follow- 
ers, however, reptiles (i.e - snakes, lizards, turtles, etc.) and amphib- 
ians (i.e.- frogs, toads, newts, etc.) classically have three chambered 
hearts, suggesting that Aristotle did his early dissections on reptiles 
and amphibians. 
Aristotle observed chicken embryos developing in eggs and be- 
lieved that that the observable beating heart was the first sign of life 
and therefore was the center of life. He concluded that the heart and 
blood vessels were vessels that contained blood in an organized fash- 
ion as opposed to blood simply being a diffuse pool in the body. He 
believed that blood itself was the “nutrient” that vessels absorbed 
from the intestines concluding, “this explains why the blood dimin- 
ishes in quantity when no food is given, and increases when much is 
consumed.” Thus Aristotle gave the first recognition of anemia’s as- 
sociation with nutritional deficiencies. 
Failing to appreciate the central nervous system and the periph- 
eral nerves as a system separate from the cardiovascular system, 
Aristotle credited the heart as the seat of the soul, and the source of 
movement and sensation further adding to his view the central im- 
portance of the heart. He believed the brain was simply a regulator, a 
cooler that “prevented the blood from overheating and boiling.” 
Aristotle’s presentations of physiology and psychology in, On the 
Soul, On Sleep and Waking, On Sensation and On Memory fascinated 
physicians and scientific philosophers; 2,000 years later his works 
and teleological observations guided physiologists such as William 
Harvey (who finished the puzzle on how blood circulated through a 
closed vascular system, that connected arteries and veins through 
capillaries which Aristotle couldn’ see for lack of a microscope). 
Aristotle’s princely student, Alexander the Great destroyed his 
enemies even as a boy and complained that, “There were no more 
worlds to conquer.” Alexander, mortally wounded, died in 323 B.C. 
- on his death bed he exclaimed, “I die by the help of too many 
physicians.” Following Alexander’ death, basic science gained an

az 
important status in the government of King Ptolemy (323 - 282 B.C.). 
King Ptolemy’s most important cultural contribution, the 
Alexandrian Library and the Museum (sanctuary of the Muses) pro- 
duced a hybrid vigor in scientific thinking as a result of a blending of 
the Greek and Egyptian texts of the day. The library was the “Mecca” 
of the known scholarly world, with an accumulation of more than 
700,000 writings and manuscripts, an observatory, zoological gar- 
dens, 13 massive lecture halls and research laboratories. 
A section of the library was destroyed in 48 B.C. during riots that 
followed Julius Caesar's entrance into Alexandria; sometime later 
Christian zealots called for the destruction of the Temple of the Muses 
and the pagan idols within. According to legends, in A.D. 395, the 
last remaining resident scholar (a female mathematician), Hypatia, 
was dragged from the museum and stoned to death by rioting crowds. 
The Muslim sacking of Alexandria in the 7th century completely 
destroyed the last vestiges of the museum and library. 
Following Alexander's conquest of the “known world,” Hellenis- 
tic thought reached from the Persian Gulf to the Island of Sicily; 
Greek became the status language of royalty. A positive result of the 
“Greeking” of the world was a substantial increase in the accumula- 
tion of scientific information on animals, plants, minerals and “drugs.” 
Alexandria was a magnet for advanced medical and scientific 
thinkers, including Herophilus of Chalcedon (330 - 235 B.C.) and 
his fellow academic, Erasistratus of Chios (330 - 255 B.C.). Herophilus 
practiced medicine in Alexandria and achieved fame by doing hu- 
man dissections in public. He wrote eleven scholarly texts, including 
three on anatomy - it was he that discovered and gave names for the 
prostate gland and the duodenum (Greek for twelve fingers - length 
of the upper small intestine). He wrote extensively on the diagnostic 
value of the pulse, therapeutics, ophthalmology, nutrition and diets, 
and midwifery. 
The personal renderings of Herophilus and Erasistratus were de- 
stroyed by rioters; however, through their later follower’s writings, 
including Cornelius Celsus (A.D. 60), we learned that they experi- 
mented on and dissected living humans. These reports were prob- 
ably correct since the Greeks used their conqueror and occupier sta- .

HELLS KITCHEN $43 
tus to subjugate their “inferiors,” especially condemned criminals. 
Herophilus differentiated between arteries and veins for the first 
time. His observations pointed out that the walls of arteries were 
much thicker than those of veins. His most notable discovery was a 
description of the nerves and their origin in the brain; he correctly 
concluded for the first time that the nerves, not the blood vessels, 
were the carriers of motor impulses from the central nervous system 
to the peripheral tissues including muscle. 
Greek medicine metastasized throughout the Mediterranean, in- 
cluding Italy. The southern Italian cities of Elea, Tarentum and 
Metapontum claimed more of a Greek cultural following than Ro- 
man. Rome was different - the “no-nonsense” Roman thinking stated 
that “one was better off without doctors.” “Romans had no need of 
doctors,” insisted writers such as Cato (234 - 149 B.C.), because 
they, “the Romans were healthier unlike the weaker Greeks.” “Be- 
ware of doctors,” he wrote, “as they would bring death by medicine!” 
“It is our duty, my young friends,” declared Cicero (106 - 43 
B.C.),”to resist old age; to compensate for its defects by a watch- 
ful care; to fight against it as we would fight against disease; to 
adopt a regimen of health; to practice moderate exercise; and to 
take just enough food and drink to restore our strength and not 
overburden it.” Thus Cicero was one of the first individuals of 
royal status to call for a national program of individual responsi- 
bility for health and fitness - and to consciously avoid overeating 
and obesity. 
The medical giant of the Roman era was Galen (129 - 216 A.D.). 
Galen’s importance to medicine over the millennium was due in great 
part to his voluminous writings. More of his writings survived than 
any of those of any other author - he produced more than 350 origi- 
nal works on such wide ranging subjects as the soul to blood letting 
- more than all other Greek writings together. 
Galen was the son of a wealthy architect; as a result, he enjoyed 
an education that was unequalled for his time - when he was but 
sixteen, his father steered him toward medicine. He studied with 
Alexandrian medical scholars, and traveled and studied medicine 
and “drugs” throughout Egypt, India and Africa.

44 
Galen returned home to Pergamon (now known as Bergama, Tur- 
key) in 157 A.D., where he was appointed physician to the gladia- 
tors, a post which allowed him to expand his anatomical knowledge 
and surgical expertise, “as the wounds of battle allowed windows 
into the living body.” 
Pergamon was a rustic town, so in 162 A.D., Galen left for Rome, 
where his public debates against Methodists and well attended pub- 
lic anatomical displays raised his fame to almost royal levels. Galen 
was famous for parlor tricks, which included the severing of nerves 
in the neck of a pig. As the nerves were cut, one by one, the pig 
would continue to squeal until Galen cut the laryngeal nerves - the 
squealing noise stopped even though the pig continued an attempt 
to squeal. 
Galen became the physician to senators and Emperors, and as a 
result he developed the first modern medical ego so typical of M.D.s: 
“I have done as much for medicine as Trajan did for the Roman Em- 
pire when he built bridges and roads through Italy. It is I, and I alone, who 
have revealed the true path of medicine. It must be admitted that 
Hippocrates already staked out this path...he prepared the way, but I have 
made it passable.” 
In the absence of scholarly universities, colleges and degrees and 
the artful nature of medicine, students followed the teachings of in- 
dividual physicians, often sitting at their feet out of respect and fol- 
lowing him on his rounds. Medical authors often publicly debated 
philosophies resulting in a flourishing of rival schools of medical 
thought. 
Ancient scholarly pursuits gained a systematic approach to the 
materia medica, through the writings of two unique philosophers. 
Theophrastus (371 - 287 B.C.), a student of Aristotle, became the 
director of the Peripatetic school of Athens. His two works on plants 
deal with their descriptions and Investigations of Plants (De historia 
planttarum) and their origins (the Causis plantarum) and Explana- 
tions of Plants - through these writings he laid the foundation for the 
field of modern botany.

HELLS KITCHEN 45 
The Investigations classifies plants into the groups of trees, shrubs 
and herbs. All of 550 species and varieties are identified, through 
habitats ranging from the Atlantic to India (his information and ma- 
terial on Indian plants were collected by scholars of Alexander's ex- 
pedition in the 320s B.C.) - his work led to the systematic review of 
medicinal botany and the widespread appearance of botanical gar- 
dens. 
The second most important post Galen scholar was Dioscorides 
(40 - 90 A.D.), a Greek surgeon attached to Nero’ army. His De ma- 
terial medica, published in Greek, however, better known by its Latin 
name appeared as five books: 
BookI - Aromatics and essential oils (i.e. - saffron, 
oils, salves, shrubs and trees). 
Book II - Animals, cereals and herbs. 
Book III - Roots, juices, herbs and seeds. 
Book IV - Roots and herbs. 
Book V - Vines, minerals (including salts of lead and copper). 
His books were designed to list the pharmaceutical values of the 
plants and other “drugs.” 
In the days of the Greeks and Romans, health, fitness, athletics 
and “diet” (philosophy) were everything. Obesity was not an option 
for the Greek or Roman citizen. These cultures were consciously 
putting their wood ashes, dutifully collected each morning from their 
cooking and heating fires, into their gardens, they cut and extended 
their dietary salt (Greek and Roman soldiers, tradesmen and artisans 
were paid “salaries” in the form of salt causing salt to be a substance 
of great value and it was often traded ounce for ounce for gold) with 
wood ashes, and wood ashes were used as “culinary” ashes for the 
preparation of many grain dishes.

“All men dream, but not equally. Those who dream by night in the 
dusty recesses of their minds wake in the day to find that it was vanity; but 
the dreamers of the day are dangerous men, for they may act their dreams 
with open eyes, to make it possible.” 
- T. E. Lawrence 
The Seven Pillars of Wisdom 
Hospitals — 
The Road to Hell 
The appearance of medieval hospitals, were in effect logical ex- 
tensions of religious altruism. Those established in the west were 
initially small and originally erected for the benefit of religious pil- 
grims. Later versions of hospitals were more expansive and provided 
a wider service for more people - St. Leonard's in York had 225 beds 
for the sick and poor in 1287; even larger were the secular hospitals 
of Milan, Siena, and Paris. 
In Florence, a city with a population of 30,000, there were more 
than thirty hospital foundations by the 15th century. Many had only 
ten beds, while some others boasted hundreds of beds. In England 
hospitals and almshouses numbered about 500 by 1400, however, 
only a few were of any significant size. London’s St. Bartholomew's 
dates back to 1123; and St. Thomas from 1215. At Bury St. Edmunds, 
six hospitals were endowed between 1150 and 1260 to “care for lep- 
ers, pilgrims, the infirm and the aged.” 
The Crusades played a large role in hospital expansion; crusad-

48 
ing orders, including the Knights of St. John (later to be called the 
Knights of Malta), the Knights of Templar, and the Teutonic Knights 
- all built hospitals in the Mediterranean and Germanic territories. 
By 1300 non-military brotherhoods, including the Order of the Holy 
Spirit, were supporting clinics from Alsace to Poland; the order of St. 
John of God built insane asylums in 1500 in Spain and about 200 
hospitals in the New World. 
Originally, small hospitals were hostels or hospices that lacked 
medical support. By 1215, physicians were on full time staff at the 
Paris Hotel Dieu, near Notre Dame, and Sta Maria in Florence, from 
12 beds in 1288 “for the sick and the poor,” to a medical staff of ten 
doctors, a pharmacist and several assistants including female sur- 
geons by 1500. 
The Golden Age of hospital building during the 1200s occurred 
at the same time as the founding of universities in Italy, Spain, France 
and England. Paris University was founded in 1110; Bologna in 1158; 
Oxford in 1167; Montpellier in 1181; Cambridge in 1209; Padua In 
1222; and Naples in 1224. 
By the 1230s Montpellier was attracting medical students from 
distant places. Initially medical schools developed in an eclectic fash- 
ion like Topsie. However, the staff eventually formed formal faculties 
for mutual protection and job security. 
There were significant philosophical differences between the cleri- 
cally administered Hospitals of Paris, Cologne, and Oxford, where 
the religious beliefs of the faculty dominated all things and the non 
denominational universities such as Montpellier and Italy, where the 
arts and law faculties dominated. At that time a Bachelor of Medicine 
(MB) required seven years of study and Medical Doctorate (MD) re- 
quired ten years of study. 
Asa result of the stringent requirements for medical degrees, few 
students applied and fewer graduated: Bologna issued sixty five de- 
grees in medicine and only one in surgery between 1419 and 1434; 
Turin only 13 between 1426 and 1462. The one large medical school, 
which was considered a model of superior service and training was 
in Padua, here medical students made up ten percent of the student 
body; its medical faculty was relatively large, boasting 16 teachers in

HELLS KITCHEN 49 
1436, by comparison Oxford had only a single MD on the faculty. 
Degreed physicians were at the top of the “pecking order,” how- 
ever, they were not the only health practitioners. Larger towns and 
cities were magnets for a wide variety of “healers.” In 1400, Florence 
was invaded by medical graduates from Padua and Bologna, bone 
setters from Rome, and families specializing in eye diseases, hernia 
and the stone. Herbalists, midwives, hawkers of home remedies, and 
priests sold religious cures. 
The numbers of health practitioners continued to increase even- 
tually resulting in the appearances of guilds, apprenticeships, exami- 
nations of applicants, locations of pharmacies, supervision of drugs, 
food and herbs. By 1236, Florentine physicians and pharmacists 
joined forces and formed a single guild that was officially recognized 
as one of the cities seven major crafts. 
Medical organizations formed in many different ways. In south- 
ern Europe surgeons and physicians were equals and it was encour- 
aged for physicians to also become surgeons. In Frederick II's regula- 
tions for the Kingdom of Sicily (1231), one could only get a license 
to practice after five years of schooling which included courses and 
qualifications in surgery. 
In Italy, the opportunity to take courses in surgery encouraged 
physicians and surgeons to be cooperative and “happy bed fellows.” 
Outside of Italy, surgery was excluded from the university curricula. 
In northern Europe surgical training and practice were organized as 
guilds with required apprenticeships, and were therefore regarded 
by licensed physicians as “quacks.” 
In Paris, the surgeon’s organization was established in 1210 after 
the College of St. Cosme was chartered. It's members were differenti- 
ated by “long robes” and “short robes.” Only the long robes were 
allowed to perform surgery. Training was primarily practical and the 
college granted three possible degrees: a bachelors degree, a simple 
license, and a masters. A three sided faculty “war” developed be- 
tween the various physicians on the faculty - the physicians, sur- 
geons and barbers. 
The introduction of anatomical dissection to the curriculum fur- 
ther added to the animosity because the dissection was under the

50 
direction of physicians and the knives were wielded by the surgeons. 
The earliest civic contracts for “public” physicians were recorded 
in Reggio, Italy in 1211 and Bologna in 1214, where the contractors 
were responsible for treating soldiers as well as the common citizen. 
Contracts commonly imposed a residence requirement, outlined the 
doctor's private and public duties, and set scales of fees. 
The civic contract system flourished and in 1300 showed up in 
all the large towns and cities in northern Italy. By 1500, civic doctors 
were being hired in northern France, Flanders, several German cities 
- only England dragged their toes and failed to hire civic doctors. 
In 1516, the conflict was resolved by the surgeons agreeing to be 
supervised by the physicians - both quickly became allies and at- 
tacked the barbers. In the Germanys and England, barber-surgeons 
were quite common; in Italy, Spain, and southern France barber- 
surgeons never were accepted. 
In 1518, the College of Physicians was chartered to regulate and 
license the practice of medicine in London. 
Hospitals were the home of surgical teachings and surgeries for 
the poor. In 1780, John Howard had inspected Europe’s hospitals 
and found them dripping with filth, pus, gangrene and infections - 
surgery was better performed at the patient's home. Hospital surgical 
mortality was 300 to 500 percent higher than for surgeries performed 
at home. “It is like a plague,” groaned John Bell, “no operation dare 
be performed, every cure stands still, every wound becomes a sore, 
and every sore is apt to run into gangrene.” At the Paris Hotel Dieu, 
“at the hearing of the word (surgery), the patients gave themselves 
up for lost.” 
Simpson coined the term “hospitalism” defined as “a general 
morbid condition of the building, or of its atmosphere, productive 
of disease.” Simpson believed hospitals should be burnt down every 
once in awhile, 
In England, the Crimean War (1854 - 56) brought to the public 
view the need for sweeping changes in hospital management. Flo- 
rence Nightingale (1820 - 1910) became a heroine; she was from a 
wealthy well educated family, born in Florence during a family vaca- 
tion, she pursued nursing as a mechanism to see the world. After a.

HELLS KITCHEN 51 
short stint with the Daughters of Charity in Paris in 1853, she was 
appointed superintendent in London of the Establishment for Gentle- 
women during illness, eventually becoming superintendent of nurses 
at King’s College Hospital. 
The start of the Crimean War in 1854 was her opportunity to put 
her theories on cleanliness to work. At the English base at Scutari, 
across the Bosphorus from Istanbul, hospital conditions were dread- 
ful. Rumors of the neglect of the sick and wounded were confirmed 
by newspaper stories by, William Russell, a London Times reporter: 
“Not only are there not sufficient surgeons......Not only are there no 
dressers and nurses....there is not even linen to make bandages.” 
The London Times demanded action, England responded; Night- 
ingale offered her services to her friend Sydney Herbert, the Secre- 
tary of War. Herbert confessed, “There is but one person in England 
that I know of who would be capable of organizing and superintend- 
ing such a scheme.” A contingent of thirty eight nurses - ten Roman 
Catholic sisters, eight Anglican sisters, six St John’s House nurses, 
and fourteen from various hospitals were gathered and sent over the 
vigorous objections of the top military leaders. 
On November 4, 1854 Nightingale and her nurses pulled into 
Scutari, where almost 2,000 wounded and sick soldiers lay in filthy 
rat infested wards. Three hundred scrubbing brushes were promptly 
ordered. As the cleanliness crusade was in full flower, the Battle of 
Inkerman was exploding, and the hospital was flooded with armies 
of the wounded. 
Nightingale organized the care of the wounded and ultimately 
called in an additional eighty nurses to bolster her war on filth. She 
prepared and served meals, supplied bedding, and dealt with the 
laundry, “I am a kind of General Dealer in socks, shirts, knives and 
forks, wooden spoons, tin baths, tables and forms, cabbages and car- 
rots, operating tables, towels and soap,” she exclaimed. In six months 
she cut the hospital death rate from forty percent down to two per- 
cent. When she returned to England in 1856, she was surprised to 
find herself heralded as a heroine. 
Nightingale possessed a drill instructor's fortitude and firm hand. 
Her success in promoting the profession of nursing was owed to her

a2 
demand that in her Notes on Nursing (1859) on the necessity for nurs- 
ing applicants to receive an intensive training in nursing theory and 
practice. 
Nightingale was a proponent of facts, referring to statistics “as the 
most important science in the world.” 
Not much has changed in “modern” 20th and 21st century hospi- 
tals since the old days of medicine; sure, there is more chromium, more 
diagnostic machinery (CT scans, MRI, PET scans, ultrasound, etc.), yet 
the mortality statistics are still abysmal. 
In 1990 a survey by the World Health Organization (WHO) and the 
Center for Disease Control showed that America ranked 17th in longev- 
ity when compared with the top thirty two industrialized nations. The 
entire world spent $2.7 trillion for health care; the United States alone 
- spent $1.6 trillion for health care, yet ranked 17th in longevity. Despite 
enormous health care spending that eclipsed the gross national product 
- of most nations, the United States dropped to 24th in longevity ten years 
- later in June of 2000 and 26th in longevity by March 2005! 
In 1996, a Harvard School of Public Health survey showed that 
- 180,000 Americans were killed each year in hospitals alone as a re- 
sult of medical negligence; 1.3 million Americans were physically 
injured each year in hospitals alone as a result of medical negligence. 
In March 11, 1998, the Center for Disease Control reported that 
2,000,000 Americans are infected each year in hospitals alone as a 
result of medical negligence; and despite around the clock nursing 
care and intravenous antibiotics 90,000 died. 
In November 1999, according to the Institute of Medicine, medi- 
cal mistakes and errors ranked as 8th top killer of Americans in hos- 
pitals each year. In December 2002, a Johns Hopkins study elevated 
medical mistakes to the number three cause of death in Americans 
each year killing 250,000. 
In 2004, the Center for Disease Control, listed poly-pharmacy 
(multiple drugs interacting in a negative way) as the cause of unto- 
ward or bad drug reactions in 2.2 million Americans; despite heroic 
efforts, 140,000 to 200,000 died as a result of medical negligence. 
Each year 5.8 million American casualties, killed, wounded 
and infected, are perpetrated against hospital patients by medi-.

HELLS KITCHEN 53 
cal doctors and yet no one seems to care - an unbelievable score 
that dwarfs the horror of all terrorism and wars! 
How can this happen? Many factors are involved: 
1) Americans have a low functioning immune system for the 
lack of raw materials (minerals) necessary to maintain 
and repair itself. 
2) Doctors simply don’t pay attention to details of diet, 
supplementation, treatments, surgeries and prescriptions - 
this environment encourages obesity, diabetes and the 
Metabolic Syndrome. 
3) Doctors don’t wash their hands, change their clothes or 
change shoe covers between patients. 
4) Doctors lie to insurance companies (private and government) 
to get paid for procedures that patients don’t need or get. 
5) According to the insurance industry American doctors are 
responsible for seventy five percent of all health insurance 
and Medicare fraud. 
6) Doctors get kickbacks for prescribing drugs that patients 
didn’t need. 
December 8, 2004, the Los Angeles Times featured a front page 
story that revealed the current state of the “modern” American hos- 
pital system. The story described the terrible conditions at the King/ 
Drew Hospital and “how whole departments fail a hospital’s patients. 
A culture of mismanagement pervades nursing, orthopedic surgery, 
the residency program and the pharmacy. Individual employees’ short- 
comings often made matters worse.” 
“Patients languished unattended by nurses.” 
“There was so much stuff at that place (King/Drew)... You couldn't 
focus on patient care.” 
According to the Los Angeles Times, “Mistakes and lax supervi- 
sion have debilitated King/Drew’s pharmacy and doctor-training pro- 
grams, which affect nearly every patient. The Department of ortho-

54 
pedic surgery was crippled by employee misbehavior, absenteeism, 
profiteering, even the commission of felonies.” 
At King/Drew Hospital it was every doctor, every nurse and ev- 
ery pharmacist for themselves - the patients are relegated to the sta- 
tus of a vehicle that the hospital staff rode to obtain money, drugs, 
sex and power - it was and still is a modern Sodom and Gomorrah. 
To get off of the Road to Hell, to eliminate the weight and obesity 
problems of urban man, we must begin to take control of our own 
health programs (including weight control and anti-obesity programs), 
support our body’s raw material needs and boycott the existing medi- 
cal system of hospitals and surgery - doctors, hospitals and the medi- 
cal system are so busy trying to “get their share” they no longer have 
time for the serious treatment and prevention of the overweight and 
the obese.

“The formulation of a problem is often more essential than its solution,
