The Energy Crisis 
In 1268 Norman castles and great manors had chimneys to draw 
the coal smoke outside, it would be another 200 years before this 
luxury was found in the humble houses of common people. The 
commoners fire sat on a raised stone hearth in the middle of the 
main room to avoid setting the wooden walls on fire. The smoke 
would fill the room until it seeped through gaps in the walls or roof 
- the dusty ashes left over from the wood’s combustion were taken 
out every morning, mixed with salt (three to one or ten to one to cut 
and conserve the salt) for their diets, used as “culinary” ashes to pre- 
pare grain dishes and then they placed the balance of the household 
ash byproduct in the vegetable garden as fertilizer. 
From an indoor air quality standpoint, the majority of English- 
men had not advanced much beyond the conditions suffered by Homo 
erectus, who, over hundreds of thousands of years earlier, had huddled 
over wood fires inside large huts made of sticks and branches. 
The 13th century Englishmen were still getting their daily sup- 
ply of minerals, either directly (mixed with salt and placed on their 
food) or indirectly (placing the ashes in the garden) from their wood 
ashes. 
Beginning in 1285, during the reign of Edward I, various com- 
missions were established in London to address the problem of coal

58 
smoke, which early environmentalists complained, “had infected and 
corrupted” the air. 
During the summer of 1306, bishops, barons and knights from 
all over England ventured from their country estates and manors and 
traveled to London town. They arrived to take part in a new political 
experiment called Parliament. When they arrived in London they 
were met with an awful odor - fumes and smoke from burning coal. 
Blacksmiths and other tradesmen had begun burning coal in place of 
wood, filling London’ air with sooty, smelly smoke. 
By the summer of 1306, enough coal was being burned by black- 
smiths, brewers (20,000 wagon loads of wood were used each year 
to distill and ferment beer and whisky), and others who required 
large quantities of fuel, that there was an organized resistance against 
it leading to a royal ban of coal use in London and other cities. 
Despite new laws threatening steep fines and the destruction of 
furnaces, coal use continued to increase in volume. Some sources 
say that one transgressor of the coal ban law was, “hanged, tortured, 
or decapitated.” 
The reason for London’ rising coal use was obvious. The popu- 
lation of London and of Britain generally, had been increasing, and as 
a result the forests were in a severe state of decline. Forests near 
London were consumed first because of easy access; they were burned 
for firewood, cut for building timber, or simply burned to clear land 
for crop production or pasture for livestock. 
Wood ash supplies for garden fertilizer, cutting salt and culi- 
nary uses declined. People did not like to use coal ashes or “clin- 
kers” for their food, salt or gardens because they imparted a 
burned petroleum smell and taste. The reduced supply of wood 
ashes caused a general decline in the health and immune de- 
fenses in the populations of Europe - “for lack of a nail a king- 
dom was lost.” 
As supplies of energy from living forests declined and became 
scarce, and more costly, the demand for energy from coal rose signifi- 
cantly. The coal merchants, watching the burgeoning cities and the 
shrinking forests surely thought coal was nearing a boom in sales. As 
luck would have it the energy crisis was solved, not by coal, but .

HELVS KITCHEN 59 
rather by a plague that devastated Europe’s populations. 
In October 1347, the crew of a trading ship that docked in Sicily 
brought the Bubonic Plague to Europe. The “Black Death” was al- 
ready wiping out the populations of China, India, and the Middle 
East; in its first European rage, continuing until 1351, it would kill 
almost one third of Europe’s population - 25 million souls were lost. 
Some modern scholars speculated that the plague was particu- 
larly deadly because the general population had already been weak- 
ened by years of bad weather, bad harvests, reduced supplies of wood 
(and therefore ashes) and famine. At the time, most residents of Eu- 
rope viewed the plague as evidence of the wrath of God! After careful 
consideration, the medical staff and faculty of the prestigious Uni- 
versity of Paris determined that the plague was caused by “an un- 
usual alignment of the planets that had occurred on March 20, 1345.” 
In England, the plague would break out again and again and 
again, at least three additional times before 1400, and Europe’s popu- 
lation would continue to be ravaged by disease until 1500. By 1500 
only 3 million people lived in England, about half the number that 
had lived there before the appearance of the plague. As deserted and 
abandoned farms and lands were reclaimed by the forests and trees 
became large enough to provide the needs of the decimated popula- 
tion, coal sales went into the tank - the coal market literally dried up, 
and wood ashes were back! 
In the late 1500s the English suffered an energy crisis when their 
population grew and again because of an increased demand for and 
consumption of wood, the forests shrank. The English learned to 
tolerate what had been thought of as intolerable - they became the 
first western culture to mine and burn coal on a massive preferential 
national scale. The coal burning public and businesses filled the air 
of London and other cities with the most polluted and foul air in the 
history of humanity. 
The English went on to power an industrial revolution with coal 
and transform the world of industry, agriculture, human health and 
the fate of the planet itself - the energy from coal was a double edged 
sword. 
The nobles led popular demonstrations by the populous denounc-

60 
ing the use of coal in the city - King Edward I banned the use of coal 
for political expediency. The original bans were ignored, so more 
desperate laws were passed to punish first offenders with “great fines 
and ransoms.” Second offenders had their furnaces destroyed. 
In 1346 through 1352 the plague (Black Death) killed twenty 
five percent of Europe’s population with some cities recording a loss 
of seventy percent of their populations. 
In 1455 Gutenberg produced a printing press from a wine press, 
and printed his first Gutenberg Bible. 
During the Edo period (1603 - 1867) of Japan, rice straw was 
used to make a variety of products including sandals, hats, mats and 
rope. The manufacture of products from rice straw was a common 
cottage industry used to supplement the meager Japanese cash in- 
come. 
The old and used straw items were burned as fuel in the kitchen 
ovens and heating stoves - farmers and “ash traders” purchased or 
traded for the ash which was then resold for fertilizer. In this way, 
almost all of the 10 million tons of the annual production of rice 
straw was returned to the soil. 
The carbon dioxide produced by burning the straw was used by 
the growing rice plant, along with the sun’s energy (solar income) 
and the photosynthesis process to manufacture the carbon chains 
required by all living things. 
In 1661, John Graunt, a little known London draper theorized 
that by making tables out of London mortality data and analyzing it, 
he could create more useful information. His resulting observations, 
published in 1662, resulted in Charles II, recommending Graunt’s 
election to the prestigious and newly formed Royal Society - adding 
that, “if they found any more such tradesmen, they should be sure to 
admit them all without further ado.” Graunt’s analysis confirmed 
that infant and childhood mortality was higher than earlier believed, 
and that thirty percent of London’s deaths were children under the 
age of four or five years old. The records also showed that lung re- 
lated deaths reached between twenty percent and twenty five per- 
cent of the total deaths. He recorded that, “Seasoned residents lived 
nearly as long in London as elsewhere, yet new-comers, and children.

HELLS KITCHEN 61 
do not, for the Smoaks, Stinks, and close Air are less healthful than 
that of the Country; otherwise why do sickly Persons remove to the 
Country Air?” 
In 1677 a smallpox outbreak wiped out 700 people in Massa- 
chusetts - twelve percent of the population. During the 1702 Massa- 
chusetts outbreak of smallpox, three children of Cotton Mather (the 
Puritan leader who had supported the burning of “witches” during 
the Salem witch hunts) were stricken with the disease - a few years 
latter he was introduced to the practice of smallpox “inoculation” by 
his black slave who had been vaccinated as a child in Africa. When 
he checked with other black slaves who had been brought to Boston 
from Africa, Mather learned that vaccinations were in fact a standard 
practice in Africa. 
Benjamin Franklin 
Benjamin Franklin was born on Sunday, January 17, 1706. Bos- 
ton was by then an incorporated town for 76 years. Boston was no 
longer a simple, isolated religious outpost - it was a bustling, grow- 
ing port city. Boston had more than 1,000 permanent houses, a 1,000 
seagoing ships registered at it’s port and 7,000 permanent residents - 
the population would double every twenty years. 
Even as a child, Franklin was a creative, yet practical inventor. 
One of his first inventions was a set of flippers for his hands and feet 
to improve his swimming speed. He made two oval palettes, with 
holes for his thumbs, and “I also fitted to the soles of my feet a kind 
of sandals.” 
Early in life Franklin became infatuated with kites. While flying 
his kites, Franklin would strip, wade into a pond or lake, floated on 
his back, and used the kite and wind power to pull him across the 
body of water. “Having engaged another boy to carry my clothes 
round the pond,” he recalled, “I began to cross the pond with my 
kite, which carried me quite over without the least fatigue and with 
the greatest pleasure imaginable.” 
Franklin was originally slated by his father to be a minister, and 
to that end he was sent to the Boston Latin School at the age of eight

62 
to prepare for entry into Harvard. Franklin did well at Harvard, mov- 
ing up from the middle of his class to the number one slot, which 
resulted in his being advanced to a higher grade level. Because 
Franklin’s father couldn’t sustain the tuition costs, Franklin was forced 
to drop out of school. 
Young Franklin was described as skeptical, puckish, curious and 
irreverent, “Dr. Franklin, when a child, found the long graces used 
by his father before and after meals very tedious,” his grandson stated. 
“One day after the winter’ provisions had been salted ‘I think, Fa- 
ther, said Benjamin, ‘if you were to say Grace over the whole cask - 
once for all - it would be a vast saving of time.’ “ 
At age ten, with only two years of schooling, Franklin went to 
work full time in his father’s candle and soap shop. It was not the 
most fun job that a ten year old kid could have; he spent his working 
hours skimming rendered tallow from boiling tubs of “stinking” fat; 
trimming wicks, adding wood ashes to the soap and filling candle 
molds was considered a mindless repetitive activity by young Franklin. 
His father took him on long walks to visit various tradesmen to find 
a trade or art that might better interest him. 
As it turned out, his knowledge of many crafts and skills allowed 
him to become a first rate tinker and inventor. Franklin, at age twelve, 
went on to sign on for nine years of indentured service as an appren- 
tice and to acquire the training and the experiences of a publisher, 
printer and newspaperman. 
Franklin was directed to produce poetry based on news stories, 
the stories were well received, to which Franklin wrote, “flattered my 
vanity.” Herman Melville would write that, “Franklin was everything 
but a poet.” 
Benjamin Franklin’s four year old son, Francis died of smallpox 
in 1736, causing him to become an advocate of vaccination for small- 
pox. 
The print industry was a natural course of life for Franklin, “From 
a child I was fond of reading,” he recalled, “and all the little money 
that came into my hands was ever laid out in books.” Books became 
the most important influence in his life. The love and respect of books 
was one of the common traits shared by the Puritanism of Mather.

HELVS KITCHEN 63 
and the Enlightenment of Locke, worlds that would blend in the 
character and personality of Benjamin Franklin. “Often I sat up in 
my room reading the greatest part of the night, when the book was 
borrowed in the evening and to be returned early in the morning, 
lest it should be missed or wanted,” admitted Franklin. 
Franklin ultimately became the most popular writer in Colonial 
America. His original pen name was Silence Dogwood - “a slightly 
prudish widowed woman from a rural area (written by an energetic 
unmarried Boston teenager who had never spent a night outside of 
Boston).” Franklin disguised his handwriting to fool his older brother, 
the publisher of the Courant, who published the first essay of Silence 
Dogwood on Monday, April 2, 1722. 
Franklin wrote and published fourteen Silence Dogwood essays 
between April and October 1722 - all were designed to promote so- 
cial improvements for the misfortunate, especially women. Further, 
in the summer of 1722, Franklin’s older brother James went to jail 
for criticizing the government on their failure to capture pirates, allow- 
ing him at age 16 to print and publish the paper himself for three 
issues - he boasted, “I had the management of the paper, and I made 
bold to give our rulers some rubs in it, which my brother took very 
kindly, while others began to consider me in an unfavorable light as 
a genius that had a turn for libeling and satire.” 
Mrs. Silence Dogwood quoted in full an essay from an English 
newspaper that defended free speech. “Without freedom of thought 
there can be no such thing as wisdom,” it declared, “and no such 
thing as public liberty without freedom of speech.” 
In January of 1723, when Franklin’ older brother James wrote, 
“Of all knaves, the religious knave is the worst” he added, “too much 
of it (religion) is worse than none at all,” James was immediately 
forbidden by the General Court to publish an issue of the Courant, 
without him first submitting each issue to the authorities for approval 
before publication. 
James continued his attacks, which resulted in the General Court 
forbidding James Franklin from further publishing the Courant. On 
Monday, February 11, 1723, there appeared on the Courant mast- 
head - “Printed and sold by Benjamin Franklin.”

64 
James returned to run the newspaper, and in a jealous rage beat 
Benjamin as he woulda truculent apprentice, rather than as a brother 
and fellow author. So, at the age of seventeen years of age, Franklin, 
on the afternoon of Wednesday, September 25, 1723, took a sailing 
ship heading for New York. Franklin, at seventeen years of age was 
almost six foot tall, slim, muscular and barrel-chested. 
While on his journey, Franklin met the only printer in New York, 
one William Bradford. Bradford suggested that Franklin go to Phila- 
delphia instead of New York and apply for a job with his son Andrew 
Bradford who ran the family print shop and weekly newspaper there. 
Philadelphia was the second largest village in America with a 
population of 2,000 people - only Boston was larger. Through a vari- 
ety of adventures Franklin befriended Sir William keith, the gover- 
nor of Pennsylvania. On a false promise of a letter of credit, Franklin 
went to London in November 1724 to buy equipment to set up a 
new print shop back in Philadelphia. 
Franklin found a job with Samuel Palmer's well respected Lon- 
don print shop. It was an exciting time in London with famous writ- 
ers such as Swift, Defoe, Pope, Richardson, Fielding and Chester- 
field. On July 1726 Franklin set sail on his return trip to Philadel- 
phia. 
Franklin became the first printer in America to manufacture type. 
One of the most frequently used contemporary typefaces, a sans- 
serif font dubbed FRANKLIN GOTHIC that is often used in newspa- 
per headlines, was named after Franklin in 1902. Franklin created a 
copperplate press to print paper money for the New Jersey assembly 
so beautifully ornate that the bills could not be counterfeited. 
In 1732, in Philadelphia, Franklin printed the first issue of his 
Poor Richard’s Almanac. Franklin used the pseudonym Richard 
Saunders - in his new almanac he used “pithy maxims” to enliven his 
meteorological forecasts. “Poor Richard” held that, “thrift and hard 
work led to wealth and virtue.” 
Franklin’ publishing business flourished, and his ability to com- 
pete with Philadelphia’ other printer, Andrew Bradford grew more 
aggressive. Through the early 1730s, they exposed each others er- 
rors and bickered over local and national political events. The high _

HELLS KITCHEN 65 
born Bradford, and his American Weekly Mercury newspaper, were 
followers and supporters of Pennsylvania’s “Proprietary faction,” which 
supported the Penn family and their appointed governors. The com- 
mon born Franklin and his Pennsylvania Gazette were vigorously an- 
tiestablishment and supported the rights of the elected Assembly. 
Their politics crossed purposes during the 1733 reelection cam- 
paign of the Assembly's speaker, Andrew Hamilton, an anti-Propri- 
etary leader who had aided Franklin’s bid to take a government print- 
ing job from Bradford. Franklin was fascinated by Hamilton’ anti- 
aristocratic populism. “He was no friend to power,” Franklin wrote. 
“He was the poor man’s friend.” 
Hamilton won the election and in 1736 he was able to get Franklin 
appointed to the post of clerk of the Assembly. Private profit and 
public service were combined. “The clerkship, Franklin agreed, “gave 
me a better opportunity of keeping up an interest among the mem- 
bers, which secured to me the business of printing the votes, laws, 
paper money, and other occasional jobs for the public, that, were on 
the whole, very profitable.” This experience also taught him the use- 
ful skill of winning over enemies. After one high-bred member spoke 
out against him, Franklin decided to hold him close (hold your friends 
close, and your enemies closer): 
“I did not, however, aim at gaining his favor by paying for any servile 
respect to him, but, after some time, took this other method. Having heard 
that he had in his library a certain very scarce and curious book, I wrote a 
note to him, expressing my desire of perusing that book, and requesting he 
would do me the favor of lending it to me for a few days. He sent it imme- 
diately, and I returned it in about a week with another note, expressing 
strongly my sense of the favor. When we next met in the House, he spoke to 
me (which he had never done before), and with great civility; and he ever 
after manifested a readiness to serve me on all occasions, so that we be- 
came great friends, and our friendship continued to his death. This is an- 
other instance of the truth of an old maxim I had learned which says, - He 
that has once done you a kindness will be more ready to do you another, 
than he whom you yourself have obliged.” 
Franklin was able to take away the Philadelphia postmaster job

66 a I i st ee ee eS 
from Bradford when Bradford was exposed for problems with his 
book keeping. The commission was given to Franklin in 1737. “I 
accepted it readily,” Franklin noted, “and found it of great advantage, 
for though the salary was small, it facilitated the correspondence that 
improved my newspaper, increased the number demanded, as well 
as the advertisements to be inserted, so that it came to afford me a 
very considerable income.” 
Between 1740 and 1744, Lord Anson’s (1697 - 1762) round-the- 
world expedition launched with great hope and 1955 men. Before 
the expedition ended, however, 320 died from fever and dysentery 
and 997 had died from scurvy by 1744. Scurvy, characterized by 
swollen, spongy, bleeding gums, large bruises, swollen joints, lassi- 
tude, heart failure and death was a significant danger for sea faring 
crews and passengers. 
Dr. James Lind (1716 - 1791), a Scottish naval surgeon, stead- 
fastly protested against poor shipboard accommodations, spoiled food 
and polluted water. In his written works, Treatise on the Scurvy (1753), 
Lind relied on his personal experience to reason that citrus fruits 
were effective antiscorbutics, supporting his hypothesis by the world’s 
first controlled nutritional clinical trial on board the HMS Salisbury 
Ht: 
“Twelve scurvy patients were chosen, Lind gave two of them a quart of 
cider; two received oil of vitriol; two were given vinegar; two sea water; 
two were given oranges and lemons; and two were given an electuary of 
garlic, radish, Peru balsam, and myrrh. The two given oranges and lem- 
ons were fit for duty in six days, and put to the task of nursing the others, 
who in fact remained sick.” 
Initially Lind did not look at scurvy as a deficiency disease, but 
rather he felt that scurvy was successfully treated with citrus as a 
plant medicine (an herbal medicine) the same way that malaria was 
treated with Jesuit’s bark. 
On February 16, 1741, after several business skirmishes with 
competitors Franklin published General Magazine, his first general 
interest magazine. In the 1700s the term magazine, referred to an .

HELVS KITCHEN’ 67 
eclectic collection of material abstracted from newspapers among other 
sources. Franklin’s version of a magazine was taken from the ten year 
old, Gentleman’s Magazine originating in London: official proclama- 
tions, reports on government proceedings, discussions of paper money 
issues, a few poems and a report on Whitefield’ orphanage. 
After courting several women and fathering an illegitimate child, 
Franklin and Deborah Read moved into a common law relationship. 
In 1743, Franklin proposed the formation of the American Philo- 
sophical Society, which was to be an American version of the Royal 
Society think tank of England. It would be housed in Philadelphia, 
and its members would include illustrious scientists and thinkers 
from other cities. 
The members would exchange their studies by post, and abstracts 
would be distributed to the members quarterly. The subjects to be 
investigated were to be practical rather than theoretical and included: 
“newly discovered plants, herbs, trees, roots, their virtues, uses, 
etc...improvements of vegetable juices, such as ciders, wines, etc.; 
new methods of curing or preventing diseases, ...1mprovements in 
any branch of mathematics... new arts, trades, and manufactures... 
surveys, maps and charts ...methods of improving the breeds of ani- 
mals ...and all philosophical experiments that let light into the na- 
ture of things.” 
By the spring of 1744, the society began meeting regularly; early 
members included Thomas Godfrey, a pedantic mathematician and 
Cadwallader Colden, a scholar and an official from New York, who 
became a life long friend and Franklin supporter. 
In 1747 Franklin proposed a volunteer military force that would 
be independent of Pennsylvania’s colonial government - the Penn- 
sylvania Militia was born. 
In 1747 after the King George’s War (1744 - 1748), Europe's War 
of Austrian Succession and the War of Jenkin’s Ear and the French 
and Indian War in Canada (Franklin’s 16 year old son William 
marched off to fight in this war), Franklin wrote a motivating pam- 
phlet, “Plain Truth,” signed simply “a Tradesman of Philadelphia.” 
Franklin’s depiction of what might happen if Philadelphia was in- 
vaded and sacked by French or Spanish privateers:

68 
“On the first alarm, terror will spread over all... The man that has a 
wife and children will find them hanging on his neck, beseeching him with 
tears to quit the city....Sacking the city will be the first, and burning it, in 
all probability, the last act of the enemy.....Confined to your houses, you 
will have nothing to trust but the enemy’s mercy....Who can, without the 
utmost horror, conceive the miseries of the latter when your persons, for- 
tunes, wives and daughters shall be subject to the wanton and unbridled 
rage, rapine and lust.” 
So, who would save the colony? Franklin called on the new 
“middle class!” “We, the middling people,” he crowed, “The trades- 
men, shopkeepers and farmers of this province and city.” He went on 
to say, “At present we are like separate filaments of flax before the 
thread is formed, without strength because without connection, But 
Union would make us strong.” 
“The pamphlet had a sudden and surprising effect,” Franklin 
wrote. Soon 10,000 men from all over Pennsylvania signed the char- 
ter and formed more than 100 militia companies. Franklin further 
organized a lottery to raise 3,000 British pounds sterling to furnish 
the Militia Association with cannons and equipment. Franklin soon 
led a delegation to New York to urge Governor George Clinton to 
approve the sale: 
“He at first refused us peremptorily; but at dinner with his council, 
when there was great drinking of Madeira wine, as the custom of that 
place then was, he softened by degrees, and said he would lend us six. After 
a few more bumpers he advanced to ten; and at length he very good- 
naturedly conceded eighteen. They were fine cannon, eighteen-pounders, 
with their carriages, which we soon transported and mounted on our bat- 
tery.” 
Franklin, didn’t realize it, but his charter of the Militia Associa- 
tion, in its spirit as well as wording, conceived and preceded the 
Declaration of Independence three decades later: 
“Being thus unprotected by the government under which we live, we do -

HELLS KITCHEN 69 
hereby, for our mutual defense and security, and for the security of our 
wives, children and estates....form ourselves into an Association.” 
Franklin’ print shop grew into a “highly successful, vertically 
integrated media conglomerate.” He boasted a printing press, pub- 
lishing house, newspaper, an almanac series and partial control of 
the postal system. The highly successful books he published included 
Bibles and psalters to Samuel Richardson's novel Pamela (Franklin’s 
1744 reprint of Pamela was the first novel published in America). 
Franklin created a series of profitable partnerships and franchises 
from Newport and New York to Charleston and Antigua - the net- 
work of enterprises provided Franklin a passive stream of income - 
and he invested wisely. 
In 1748, at age forty two, Franklin retired and sold his printing 
business to David Hall, his trusted foreman; the agreement provided 
him with half the businesses profits for eighteen years, which 
amounted to 650 British pounds per year (the average income at the 
time was 25 pounds per year). Now he would have “leisure to read, 
study, make experiments, and converse at large with such ingenious 
and worthy men as are pleased to honor me with their friendship.” 
Franklin gave up his print shop to focus and engage his energies 
on science, then politics, then diplomacy and statecraft. As Poor Ri- 
chard declared in his almanac that year, “Lost time is never found 
again.” His intellectual curiosity and infatuation with the orderliness 
of the greater universe drew him to science. His scientific interests 
would continue through his whole life, including research into the 
Gulf Stream, meteorology, the earth’s magnetism, electricity, heating 
and refrigeration systems. 
Franklin didn’t have any formal academic training in science or 
math to be considered a great theorist of the likes of Einstein; his 
interests of what he fondly called his “scientific amusements” caused 
some contemporaries to dismiss him as a mere tinker. However, even 
during his life he was held up as the most famous scientist alive - as 
Harvard professor, Dudley Herschbach stated, “His work on electric- 
ity was recognized as ushering in a scientific revolution comparable 
to those written by Newton in the previous century or Watson and

70 
Crick in ours.” 
An example of Franklin’ practical application of science was his 
1740s invention of an iron wood-burning stove that could be built 
into fireplaces to maximize heat output into the house while at the 
same time minimizing, fuel requirements, smoke and drafts - using 
his understanding of convection currents and heat transfer, he pro- 
duced an original and unique stove design. The resulting wood ash 
byproduct of minerals were still being mixed with food as “culinary” 
minerals, with table salt and mixed into the soil and/or compost of 
the garden. 
The stove was designed to allow the heat and smoke from the 
wood fire to rise up and warm an iron plate on the top. In 1744, he 
contracted a contemporary to construct the new stove and two of his 
brothers and several friends to market the stove throughout the north- 
east. 
The new “Pennsylvania Fireplaces,” as Franklin called them, were 
initially popular at a price of 5 British pounds. One testimony letter 
in the Boston Evening Post stated, “They (the stove) ought to be 
called, both in justice and gratitude, Mr. Franklin’s stoves.” The let- 
ter writer went on to say, “I believe all who have experienced the 
comfort and benefit of them will join with me that the author of this 
happy invention merits a statue.” 
During a visit to Boston in the summer of 1743, Franklin and 
others were entertained one evening by a traveling scientific show- 
man from Scotland called Dr. Archibald Spencer. Spencer featured 
amazing demonstrations of science that were designed to amuse the 
host's guests. He revealed Newton's theories of light and exhibited a 
device that measured blood flow - both were interests of Franklin's. 
Dr. Spencer performed electricity tricks, such as creating static 
electricity by rubbing a glass tube and drawing sparks from the feet 
of a boy hanging by silk cords from the ceiling. “Being on a subject 
quite new to me,” Franklin recalled, “They equally surprised and 
pleased me.” 
A 100 years earlier, Galileo and Newton had explained and 
demystified gravity, however, another awesome force of the universe 
electricity, was no more understood than it had been by the prehis-

HELLS KITCHEN 71 
toric people who had tamed fire. There were individuals, like Dr. 
Spencer, who used the wonders of electricity as a parlor trick to en- 
tertain admiring audiences; however, no one had as yet put the power 
of electricity to the task of real work. 
The Abbe’ Nollet, court scientist to Frances King Louis XV, con- 
nected 180 soldiers and then 700 monks and “made them jump as if 
one for the court's entertainment by sending a pulse of static electric- 
ity through them.” Franklin, however, was the ideal person to con- 
vert electricity from the status of a simple parlor trick into a func- 
tional, practical and working science. 
Franklin’ initial experiments involved collecting an electric charge 
and then studying its various properties. He employed his friends to 
draw charges from a spinning glass tube and touch each other to see 
if sparks flew. These early experiments resulted in the discovery that 
electricity was “not created by the friction of rubbing the tube, but 
collected only.” 
Many of Franklin’ originally coined terms such as battery, charged, 
neutral, condense, and conductor are still used today in the fields of 
electricity and the greater subject of energy. “He has written equally 
for the uninitiated as well as the philosopher,” the 19th century En- 
glish chemist Sir Humphry Davy noted, “and he has rendered his 
details as amusing as well as perspicuous.” 
Franklin’s discovery that the creation of a positive charge was 
accompanied by the generation of an equal negative charge became 
known as the “conservation of charge” and the “single-fluid theory” 
of electricity. The concepts were easily formulated in Franklin's “book 
keeper” mind. His observations were a scientific breakthrough and 
discovery of historic greatness - “As a broad generalization that has 
withstood the test of 200 years of fruitful application,” Harvard pro- 
fessor I. Bernard Cohen declared, “Franklin’s law of conservation of 
charge must be considered to be of the same fundamental impor- 
tance to physical science as Newton's law of conservation of momen- 
tum.” 
Franklin also discovered a basic feature of electrical charges, “the 
wonderful effects of points” - that shortly would produce his most 
famous practical application. He electrified a small iron ball and

72 eS ee ee ee eee 
dangled a cork next to it, which was repelled to a degree based on 
the size of the ball’s charge. When he brought the tip of a pointed 
piece of metal near the ball, it drew away the charge. By contrast, a 
blunt piece of metal did not draw a charge or spark as easily, and if it 
was insulated instead of grounded, it failed to draw a charge. 
Franklin continued his electricity experiments by collecting and 
storing electricity in an early capacitor called a Leyden jar (named 
after the Dutch town it was discovered in). These jars had a metal foil 
sleeve on the outside; on the inside of the glass jar, separated from 
the foil by the glass insulation, was lead or water or metal that could 
be charged up through a wire. Franklin demonstrated that when the 
inside of the jar was charged, the outside foil had an equal but oppo- 
site charge. He also correctly determined that the electrical charge in 
the Leyden jar was stored in the glass itself. 
In a practical progression of his electrical storage experiments, 
Franklin lined up a series of parallel glass plates separated by metal - 
charged them up, wired them together, and discovered a new electri- 
cal device - “what we called an electrical battery.” 
Franklin was able to use the electrical battery to turn the iron 
fence surrounding his house into the first electrified security fence; 
he created an electrical toy spider that would hop like a live one; he 
electrocuted turkeys with his electrical battery for the entertainment 
of his guests before the birds were roasted for dinner with electricity, 
thus inventing the first electrical execution and cooking devices. 
Franklin commented by letter to a friend that at one of his par- 
ties, “A turkey is to be killed for our dinners by the electrical shock; 
and roasted by the electrical jack, before a fire kindled by the electri- 
fied bottle; while the health’s of all the famous electricians in En- 
gland, France and Germany are to be drank in electrified bumpers, 
under the discharge of guns from the electrical battery.” “The birds 
killed in this manner eat uncommonly tender,” he wrote, thus he 
became a cooking pioneer of electrically roasted turkeys! 
This early Franklin experiment, in which he killed and cooked 
turkeys with electrical devices, was perhaps the very first close 
_ call humanity had with a possible epidemic of obesity, type II 
diabetes and the Metabolic Syndrome, for there were no wood .

HELLS KITCHEN 73 
ash byproducts resulting from this form of cooking! No wood 
ashes to consume with salt, no culinary ashes - no minerals to 
put in the garden! 
Franklin kept a log of his electrical experiments - he noted in 
November 1749, that there were twelve similar properties in electri- 
cal sparks and lightening, including: 
1. Producing light. 
2. Color of the light. 
3. Crooked discharge directions. 
4. Swift motion. 
5. Being conducted by metals. 
6. Crack or noise in exploding. 
9. Destroying animals. 
10. Sulpherous smell. 
He made a mental connection between this surmise about light- 
ening and his earlier experiments on the efficiency of pointed metal 
objects to draw off electrical charges - “Electrical fluid is attracted by 
points. We do not know whether this property is in lightening. But 
since they agree in all particulars wherein we can already compare 
them, is it not probable they agree likewise in this?” He was so en- 
thused by this prospect, he added - “Let the experiment be made.” 
For millennia, lightening was universally considered to be a su- 
pernatural phenomenon or an expression of God's wrath. During 
one thirty five year time line in Germany alone (1700s), “386 churches 
were struck by lightening and an excess of 100 bell ringers were 
killed by heavenly bolts. In Venice, 3,000 people were killed when 
tons of gunpowder stored in a church was struck.” 
Franklin noted that, “The lightening seems to strike steeples of 
choice and at the very time the bells are ringing; yet they continue to 
bless the new bells and jangle the old ones whenever it thunders. 
One would think it was now time to try some other trick.” He con- 
cluded that the “water vapors in a cloud can be electrically charged 
and when these electrically charged clouds pass over high tree, lofty 
towers, spires, masts of ships ... draw the electrical fire and the whole

74 oe ee 
cloud discharges.” His proposal led to some practical advice - “Dan- 
gerous therefore it is to take shelter under a tree during a thunder 
gust.” 
Before he even tried his lightening experiments himself he de- 
tailed his thoughts in two famous letters to Collinson in 1750, which 
were presented to the Royal Society in London and then widely pub- 
lished. The basic concept was to use a tall metal rod to draw some of 
the electrical charge from a cloud, just as he had used a needle to 
draw off the charge of an iron ball in his workshop. In his letter he 
listed specific details: 
“On the top of some high tower or steeple, place a kind of sentry box 
big enough to contain a man and an electrical stand. From the middle of 
the stand, let an iron rod rise....upright twenty to thirty feet, pointed very 
sharp at the end. If the electrical stand be kept clean and dry, a man stand- 
ing on it when such clouds are passing low might be electrified and afford 
sparks, the rod drawing fire to him from the cloud. If any danger to the 
man be apprehended (though I think there would be none) let him stand on 
the floor of his box, and now and then bring near to the rod the loop of a 
wire that had one fastened to the leads; he holding it by a wax handle (.e. 
- insulating him from it). So the sparks, if the rod is electrified, will strike 
from the rod to the wire and not affect him.” 
Franklin’s letters were abstracted in London by The Gentleman’s 
Magazine in 1750 and then published as an eighty six page booklet 
the following year; his letters were translated into French in early 
1752 and quickly became a local public and international sensation. 
Earlier, King Louis XV of France commissioned Comte de Buffon 
and Thomas-Francois D’Alibard to perform Franklin’s proposed ex- 
periment and construct a sentry box with a forty foot iron rod. On 
May 10, 1752, just after 2:00 p.m. a storm cloud passed over the 
village of Marly on the northern suburbs of Paris, a retired soldier, 
drafted to perform the experiment drew sparks as Franklin had pre- 
dicted. An enthusiastic prior grabbed the insulated wire and repeated 
the experiment six times! 
Franklin correctly surmised that “lightning rods” could render |

HELLS KITCHEN 75 
safe one of the greatest natural dangers faced by humans. “Houses, 
ships and even towns and churches may be effectually secured from 
the stroke of lightening by their means,” he predicted. 
Franklin had been waiting for the steeple of Philadelphia’s Christ 
Church to be finished; impatient, he developed the idea of using 
instead a kite. To that end he enlisted the efforts of his son William to 
help fly the silk kite. A sharp wire was attached to the top of the kite, 
and a key was attached near the base of the wet string, so that a wire 
could be brought near it in an effort to draw sparks. 
Initially Clouds passed over to no effect. Franklin almost fell into 
despair until he saw several of the strands of the string straighten out 
and stiffen. He put his knuckle to the key and drew an electrical 
spark. 
Franklin was literally moments away from discovering a com- 
mercially viable source of clean household energy - electrical appli- 
ances and the end of the ash byproduct of cooking and heating fires! 
However, the onset of the American Revolution and the birth of a 
nation siphoned off his creative juices and energies. 
It would take an additional 150 years before Edison’s work pro- 
duced the practical generation of electrical power and the flurry of 
invention of patented electrical appliances that would replace wood 
fueled heating and cooking fires and eliminate once and for all the 
health and life supporting kitchen and heating byproduct of wood 
ash - minerals.

76

Chapter 6 
“With all things being equal, the simplest explanation is normally 
the right one.” 
- Hakim’s Razor 
The Fuel Revolution 
After having tamed fire 1,500,000 years earlier, humans had cre- 
ated many unique ways to use the power of fire to change natural 
materials into everything from metals, pottery, bricks, and glass, to 
salt, soap, and ale. However, in all those hectic and creative millen- 
nia of baking and boiling, melting and smelting, it didn’t quickly 
come to them that fire held another power - the power of movement 
and motion. 
For the average human, the back-breaking work of transporting 
loads from one farm to another, or from one market to another was 
performed only with the power of muscle, water, or wind. This fact 
of work powered only by humans, animals, water or wind limited 
the production and growth that a culture could expect to accomplish 
in each century. There was only so much mechanical lifting, turning 
and pulling that humans and animals could do and only so many 
hours in the day. 
In the 1700s, a machine was invented that allowed humans to 
grow beyond the historical constraints and limits by turning “solar 
income” fuels into enormous amounts of mechanical torque, lift and 
forward motion! The invention was the steam engine and the fuel 
was coal. The steam engine would soon become the center of the 
industrial revolution, causing it to be called, “The most important

(0 2a 
invention in the creation of the modern world.” 
Coal, “motivated” the steam engine and its inventors, who were 
searching for a solution to the problem of coal mine flooding as the 
mine shafts were driven below the water table - because wood had 
become scarce, coal had become a necessity of life and the problem 
of flooding had to be solved. 
In addition to the threat of mine gasses, miners had to worry 
about water. Rainwater poured into the tunnels, when the depth of 
the mines fell below the water tables, ground water seeped into the 
shafts and more frequently than not unearthed underground streams 
and springs quickly filled the shafts. 
Sudden mine floods occurred when diggers broke through their 
shaft into an adjacent abandoned mine that had filled with water. 
The results were catastrophic, drowning the miners immediately, or 
worse trapping them in the mine until they slowly died of oxygen 
deprivation or starved to death. 
To keep the mines dry, operators employed gravity by digging 
tunnels or “watergates” from the valleys up into the mine; others 
used various means of power including wind mills, windlass, chain 
of buckets and simple buckets carried on the backs of diggers. The 
greatest amounts of water in the largest mines was raised by horse 
power, however, the cost of maintaining fifty to sixty horses and their 
teamsters was too burdensome for even the largest of mines. 
By 1700, coal production was ten times greater than it had been 
in 1550. England was now mining five times more coal than all other 
nations combined, she was now totally dependent on coal as a fuel 
for home use and powering industry. Even if England optimized her 
wood fuel production from her forests, ceased the use of wood for 
building and manufacturing, the forests could no longer replace the 
dependency on coal - wood fuels were once and for all gone in En- 
gland. 
The flooding of the mines remained a threat to England’s very 
existence as a world power - as a result, in 1660 Charles II created 
the Royal Society; an independent group commissioned to study and 
promote the theory and application of science. 
The original members were the great philosophers, thinkers and |

HELVS KITCHEN 79 
scientists of the day, including Sir Issac Newton, Gottfried Leibnitz, 
Christopher Wren, Samuel Pepys, John Evelyn, and Robert Boyle - 
“Religion, nationality, profession - all were subordinated to this ulti- 
mate aim of technical mastery, subordinated to a degree perhaps never 
before found in history.” 
One of the more important concerns of the illustrious group was 
coal, yet coal was still a mystery to them. In the 17th century, people 
still believed that coal was a living organism and still alive. The Royal 
Society members, although initially confused about the origin of coal, 
they agreed for certain to one thing - England’s coal supply was threat- 
ened by mine flooding. They focused all of their attention on pump- 
ing technology, and on atmospheric pressure. 
Scientists had only recently begun to appreciate that the atmo- 
sphere had weight and volume and it fills vacuums with a powerful 
force. They learned and appreciated the fact that the force of air op- 
posing a vacuum could be put to work, however, producing and 
controlling the vacuum was problematic. 
Denis Papin, a French Huguenot living in England, found the 
solution - steam! Papin had previously proven his knowledge of steam 
by demonstrating to the members of the Royal Society his steam pres- 
sure cooker which he named the “Digester” or Engine for softening 
bone so they could be completely eaten. 
Between 1690 and 1695, Papin invented a small contraption con- 
sisting of a moveable piston within a brass cylinder. When he heated 
water that filled one end of the cylinder, the resulting steam vigor- 
ously drove the piston upward. When he removed the flame, the 
steam condensed and produced a vacuum - the piston was then vig- 
orously pushed back toward the vacuum by the outside atmospheric 
pressure. 
Papin didn’t believe his first steam piston could be built large 
enough to be of practical use. The inventor who actually put the 
steam piston to work, was not a member of the Royal Society or 
London’ scientific elite, rather it was Thomas Newcomen, a simple 
ironmonger or “tinker” from a small rural town. 
The members of the Royal Society tried to downplay the impor- 
tance of Newcomen’ steam engine, leading many historians to be-

80 
lieve that Newcomen’s lack of credentials was the problem. One his- 
torian went on to say, “to the small scientific world of London, whose 
center was the Royal Society, it seemed inconceivable that such a 
man could excel the brightest intellects of the day in the power of 
invention.” 
Newcomen’s steam driven device was similar to the Papin piston, 
however, it was much larger and sported a separate boiler in which 
to generate the steam. After the steam was let into the bottom of the 
cylinder, cold water was added to condense the steam; the resulting 
vacuum forced the piston in a downward stroke. As the piston moved 
up and down, it rocked an overhead beam. The opposite end of the 
beam moved the rod of a vacuum pump in a similar fashion to those 
driven by horse power. 
The first practical Newcomen steam engine was “quietly and with- 
out ceremony” installed at a coal mine in 1712. The engine was called 
a “fire engine,” and the men who fed coal into the engine’s boiler and 
keep the engine’ fire alive were called “firemen.” 
Newcomen’s steam engine was an immediate success and much 
revered by mine operators - one engine did the work of pumping 
more efficiently and more economically than fifty horses! Those his- 
torians who know Newcomen’ story, write of him and his work with 
an almost religious reverence, calling his steam engine, “the most 
wonderful invention which human ingenuity had yet produced,” and 
credited him with giving “to the whole world.... The art of convert- 
ing fuel into useful power for the benefit and convenience of human- 
ity.” 
In 1761 an English publisher printed an indispensable book for 
the American homemaker - The Colonial Housewife; or, Accomplished 
Gentlewomen’s Companion, which features “every article which can 
add to the knowledge of the housewife.” The Companion offered the 
housewife more than 700 recipes, including such New World dishes 
as West Indian turtle and roast turkey, along with selected recipes 
from France, the Netherlands, Spain and Italy. The author also in- 
cluded 300 home remedies for ailments that range from cancer to 
dropsy. 
By the 1760s, hundreds of Newcomen engines (because they used .

HELLS KITCHEN 81 
atmospheric pressure rather than steam pressure were often called 
atmospheric engines) were pumping water from mine shafts through- 
out England and Scotland; some of Newcomen’ engines were built 
to gigantic proportions for the day, reaching cylinder diameters of 
more than six feet and lengths of ten feet. 
Initially the engine was used only by coal mine operators be- 
cause it was a fuel hog, and coal was for all practical purposes free at 
the mouth of the shaft. 
In 1736, James Watt, the son of a Scottish carpenter was born. 
He was the only one of five children born to his parents to survive to 
manhood; however, he was very frail and suffered from continuous 
migraines that kept him in bed for weeks at a time. According to a 
19th century biographer, Watt's fragile health left him with “an al- 
most feminine delicacy and sensitiveness, which made him shrink 
from the rough play of robust children”; as a result, he was home 
schooled during his early years, he spent his time playing with his 
father’s carpentry tools and intently watching steam pouring from 
tea kettles. 
Watt excelled in math and studied the art of constructing math- 
ematical instruments. It was as an instrument maker, only twenty 
years old, that he was asked to fix a small model of a Newcomen 
engine that had been reverently kept at Glasgow University. 
The Newcomen model, like the full sized engine, was extremely 
wasteful, requiring enormous amounts of coal to keep it going. Watt 
observed that as steam was injected into the system and then cooled 
with water, heat was wasted in the cyclical process of reheating and 
cooling the cylinder. Watt revolutionized the Newcomen steam en- 
gine by installing a separate condenser; the cylinder would be at- 
tached to another container that was surrounded with cool water, 
after the cylinder filled with steam, the steam passed into the cooler 
container where it condensed. In this way, the cylinder itself would 
always remain hot and ready for the next injection of steam! 
According to Dr. Joseph Black, a good friend of Watts, “this capi- 
tal improvement flashed upon his mind at once, and filled him with 
rapture.” 
Watt, after suffering many challenges (“of all things in life, there

82 
is nothing more foolish than inventing”), eventually found the per- 
fect partner, the Birmingham industrialist Mathew Boulton, and one 
of the most successful partnerships in history was started. 
Boulton was a manufacturer and an industrial visionary. Together, 
Boulton and Watt formed the Lunar Society - members included Jo- 
seph Priestly, the scientist who discovered oxygen, sulfur dioxide, 
and carbon monoxide; Joseph Wedgewood, the pottery giant; and 
Erasmus Darwin, inventor, poet and grandfather of Charles Darwin. 
Boulton built his financial empire with buckles, rather than coal, 
iron or textiles. He employed 1,000 workers in his Soho manufac- 
tory, where they made precision crafted goods, with a range from 
buckles, buttons and filigree to classic scientific instruments includ- 
ing clocks, thermometers and telescopes. Boulton’s manufactory be- 
came so famous that it drew tourists, including Catherine the Great. 
Boulton had a serious interest in steam engines because the wa- 
ter wheels that powered the Soho facility could not be depended 
upon for a continuous source of power. He had unsuccessfully tried 
his hand at inventing and designing steam engines, he even exchanged 
letters on the subject of engine design with his good friend Benjamin 
Franklin. 
In 1773 Watt moved to Birmingham and began working on im- 
provements on his steam engine at Soho. Boulton provided skilled 
artisans, financial backing and best of all emotional encouragement 
to Watt. 
The all important cylinder was produced by John Wilkinson, the 
pioneering iron founder. In 1776, while the American Revolution 
was in full swing, James Watt installed his first two engines, one to 
pump water out of a mine shaft and the other to inflate the bellows 
in Wilkinson's iron foundry. 
While Watt improved the steam engine in many ways, his finest 
moment was in getting the Newcomen engine to extract four times 
the work force or horsepower out of a lump of coal. The steam engine’s 
new fuel efficiency moved it out from the darkness of the coal mine 
and into the business world to find a home in the world’s factories. 
The first civilized vaccination was performed in 1796 by Dr. Ed- 
ward Jenner, an English physician. He noted that milkmaids who |

HELLS KITCHEN’ 83 
had contracted the mild disease cowpox never contracted smallpox; 
Jenner removed some material from a milkmaid’ cowpox pustule 
and scratched it into the skin of an eight year old boy. The boy soon 
developed cowpox. Sometime later, Jenner injected the boy with the 
smallpox virus and he failed to contract the disease. During the late 
19th century, research by the French chemist, Louis Pasteur and the 
German bacteriologist Robert Koch produced many new vaccines 
including those designed for anthrax, rabies and tetanus. 
England's coal industry had become relatively large by the 1700s 
just to meet the domestic and non iron industrial requirements of 
the nation; it would expand again tenfold by 1830; and double again 
by 1854. Between 1842 and 1856 the number of English coal mines 
quadrupled again. 
The steam driven industrial revolution’s impact on human en- 
deavor has been compared to the discovery of fire, the invention of 
the wheel, and to the tasting of the apple from the Tree of Knowl- 
edge. 
Not until the 1800s would it be scientifically established, and 
commonly accepted, that coal itself, not only the plant fossils held 
within, was plant material and prehistoric. 
By 1790, “potash fever” hit the young American country, trees 
were burned by the thousands for the ash which was exported to 
Great Britain for fertilizer. The first U.S. Patent Number 1, was for 
“the production and use of wood ash as fertilizer.” 
On April 17, 1790, Benjamin Franklin died at the age of 84. In 
the largest public gathering ever seen in the United States, 20,000 
people attended Franklin’s funeral. By August a national census re- 
vealed there were 3,929,625 Americans living in the United States. 
The census tally showed a vast population growth since the early 
colonial days, when a 1726 census showed a population of only 
32,442 people in New Jersey compared with 200,000 in 1790. New 
Hampshire had a population of 52,700 in 1767 compared with 
200,000 in 1790. 
The census revealed that the United States was made up of many 
ethnic groups. The largest was British, followed by 400,000 Irish. 
There were 260,000 Scottish and 2,000 Jews. Some ethnic groups

84 ee 
were concentrated in particular areas; a third of Pennsylvanians were 
of German decent and New York had 80,000 people of Dutch de- 
cent: 19,000 Swedes and 9,000 French Canadians are scattered 
throughout several states. 
In the mid-1800s Ralph Waldo Emerson wrote this about coal: 
“Every basket is power and civilization. For coal is a portable climate. 
It carries the heat of the tropics to Labrador and the polar circle; and it is 
the means of transporting itself whithersoever it is wanted. Watt and 
Stephenson whispered in the ear of mankind their secret, that a half-ounce 
of coal will draw two tons a mile, and coal carries coal, by rail and by 
boat, to make Canada as warm as Calcutta; and with its comfort brings its 
industrial power.” 
In 1822, Dr. William Beaumont, a U.S. Army surgeon stationed 
at Fort Mackinac on Lake Erie, was called to a remote trading post on 
the Canadian border to treat Alexis St. Martin, a nineteen year old 
trapper who had been the victim of a shotgun wound to the abdo- 
men. He recovered, however he was left with a permanent abdomi- 
nal fistula or tunnel connected into his stomach from the outside (it 
had to be plugged during meals to keep the food from extruding to 
the outside): 
“On August 1, 1825, at 12 o'clock, I introduced through the perfora- 
tion, into the stomach, the following articles of diet, suspended with a silk 
string, so as to pass in without pain: viz. a piece of high seasoned a’ la 
mode beef; a piece of raw, salted fat pork; a piece of raw, salted, lean beef; 
a piece of boiled salted beef; a piece of stale bread; and a bunch of raw, 
sliced cabbage.” 
The cabbage and bread were digested first, then the pork and 
boiled beef; the boeuf a’ la mode took the longest. Beaumont’ stud- 
ies revealed that the digestive processes worked, not by fermentation 
as it was commonly believed, but rather by stomach juices (acid) 
dissolving the food. 
Coal was not just another simple fuel, and “no mere article of

HELLS KITCHEN’ 85 
commerce.” Coal represented man’s victory over the elements of na- 
ture - it became the foundation of civilization itself: 
“With Coal, we have light, strength, power, wealth and civilization; 
without Coal we have darkness, weakness, poverty, and barbarism.” 
For centuries, Britain led the world in coal production, and as a 
result, the industrial revolution started there; Britain became the most 
powerful country on the planet, it created an industrial society, one 
that no one could have imagined - the genie was out of the bottle! 
In the United States, coal transformed an empty wilderness into 
an industrial superpower at warp speed. Without coal we would have 
wallowed longer in the poverty, backbreaking hand labor and the 
ignorance of the pre-industrial world; however, we might have dis- 
covered a more graceful, a more gentle, and a more friendly path to 
modernization, than the one we eventually took. 
Michael Faraday, the scientist acclaimed by all the world, had 
little formal education and had taught himself everything. Since the 
1820s his had been a career of “marvelous discoveries in many dif- 
ferent fields, that of induced electricity appearing as the most re- 
markable of all.” 
“With his horseshoe magnet and disc rotating between it’ poles, 
Faraday, had demonstrated before the Royal Society in 1831 (some 
41 years after Franklin’s death), how mechanical energy could be 
converted into electrical energy - thus presenting to the world, the 
first miniature model of the dynamo (generator. )” 
Faraday published a two volume collection of his experiments 
and theories called, Experimental Researches in Electricity - Thomas 
Edison, as a young boy devoured Faraday’s written works on elec- 
tricity and formed his views on electricity based on the work of 
Franklin and Faraday. 
Faraday’s “dynamo” seemed to provide the answer to the prob- 
lem of a cheap supply of electric current. This early dynamo, pow- 
ered by steam engines, was gradually improved over time by many 
inventers and tinkers - by the 1860s lighthouses along England coast 
were lit by giant arc lights.

86 
In 1876, at the Philadelphia Exposition, Moses G. Farmer exhib- 
ited three arc lights powered by his privately built dynamo. In 1877 
a newspaper reported that Paul Jablochkoff, a former Russian officer 
of engineers had invented “electric candles” which were introduced 
as street lamps in Paris. 
Coal, steam and electricity now had a toe hold in the daily life of 
human culture - it was clear that wood as a fuel for the hearth and 
industry was no longer viable because the maximum rate of re-growth 
of trees could no longer keep up with the growing demand for fuel 
and power. 
Unfortunately at this point, territorial expansion, financial em- 
pires, industrial growth and mass transportation (i.e. - roads, canals, 
railroad, transoceanic ships, etc.) were the driving force in all indus- 
trialized nations - the race was on! As a result of the excitement gen- 
erated by new sources of power and business growth, no one paid 
any attention to the human price that would be paid over the hori- 
zon of time. 
The source of the seemingly endless flow of wood ashes (mineral 
nutrition) that had supported human development in the fields of 
intellect, art, industry, health, longevity and science had begun to be 
pinched off - we had moved from wood to coal as the common fuel, 
we had moved from wind, water and muscle power to steam and 
electricity to run our machines, farms and industries in almost a single 
breath.
