Chapter 7 
“Genius is ninety nine-percent perspiration and one percent inspiration.” 
-Thomas Edison 
Hell’s Fork 
The chemical fertilizer industry began with Justus von Liebig, 
the German chemist, who in 1840 established the beginnings of the 
farm chemical mega-industry. Liebig gained the support of scientists 
of the day and the “practical” farmer looking for quick yields. Liebig 
burned various food crops in the laboratory and analyzed the result- 
ing ash, and he found that the minerals phosphorus and potassium 
were common to all. Liebig created the first NPK or nitrogen, phos- 
phorus and potassium fertilizer - this was the beginning of the NPK 
school of thought in agriculture. 
The new commercial chemical fertilizers were initially used by 
the larger commercial farms of Europe, however, in the 1800s, eighty 
five to ninety percent of all food was still produced by family oper- 
ated subsistence farms and wood ashes from cooking and heating 
fires and animal manure were still the principle source of garden and 
field fertilizers - the general population of the world had not been 
divested of their historical source of minerals just yet. 
Thomas Edison 
The 1830s and 1840s ushered in the “Canal Craze” and the Erie 
Canal carried pioneer families and great quantities of goods into the 
middle of America. Villages, towns and cities bankrupted themselves

90 
to attract the builders of canals that gave the fledgling nation a mass 
transit and heavy freight system. 
In 1847, Thomas Alva Edison was born in Milan, Ohio. Driven 
by the industries supported by the Erie Canal, Milan was a boom 
town. Six hundred wagons arrived each day from a 150 mile radius, 
filling twenty barges with 35,000 bushels of wheat! Fourteen grain 
elevators lined the basin - almost all roofed with shingles manufac- 
tured by Sam Edison, “Little Als” father. 
By the age of eight, “Little Al” was described by one of his teach- 
ers as a little “addled.” Edison recalled, “I was always at the foot of 
the class, I used to feel that the teachers did not sympathize with 
me.” 
Edison missed years of school as a result of chronic upper respi- 
ratory infections, one of which left him with significantly damaged 
hearing (there are many stories on how Edison lost his hearing in- 
cluding being boxed on the ears and having been pulled on a moy- 
ing train by his ears). He was the kind of person who learned by 
actual hands on experience rather than rote learning - perhaps he 
was a victim of ADD. 
One of Al's daily household chores as a small boy was carrying 
the wood ashes produced by the cooking and heating fires to the 
outside and he then put them into the garden - the common kitchen 
chore of the day. 
America, by 1851, had progressed, in the words of Walt Whitman, 
to “a nation of which the steam engine is no bad symbol.” To the 
American people, still in the pioneering stage of the “New World,” - 
“every new method which leads by a shorter road to material com- 
fort, every machine which spares labor and diminishes the cost of 
production....seems the grandest effort of the human intellect.” 
In 1851, the typical American pioneering characteristic included 
ingenuity and the manual skills necessary to produce food in the 
rocky farm land of New England. Subsistence farming in Massachu- 
setts and Connecticut turned to a variety of cottage industries to pro- 
duce additional income streams. 
Americans produced good glass products, pottery and kitchen 
utensils of copper and iron. Shipyards flourished, the art of naviga-

HELLS KITCHEN 91 
tion was advanced, the textile industry grew, gun makers and clock 
makers where amongst the respected trades and tradesmen. 
The American farm boy (the “whittling boy”) such as John Fitch 
and Eli Whitney, constantly dreamed thoughts of tools, machines 
and practical appliances, were free to leave behind the intellectual 
and creative shackles of European restrictions and old ways and try 
new methods of farming, tool making and business in general. This 
pioneering environment engendered the appearance of the “Yankee 
inventor.” 
“Even though their means and (scientific) knowledge were lim- 
ited, and troubles beset them at every step....(they) founded most of 
our important industries.” According to E. L. Bogart, an economic 
historian, “invention became a national habit.” 
By 1851, the revolutionary invention of the cotton gin, sewing 
machine, steamboat and the telegraph which were featured at the 
London Industrial exposition, drew world wide attention. 
According to Tocqueville, “the young American grew up ignor- 
ing the general laws of mechanics, but rather, directed their thoughts 
to the purely practical part of science.” 
The Erie Canal was a route by which the skills and products of 
the Yankee inventors of the American northeast moved toward the 
Midwestern frontiers. European tourists were awed by the unique 
skills of the Americans, including Cyrus McCormick, inventor of the 
mechanical reaper, who “revolutionized” agricultural implements, 
while in Europe, agricultural tools and methods continued to remain 
the same since the time of the Roman Empire. 
Large numbers of American women “liberated themselves from 
the bondage of needle and thread by acquiring Mr. Howe's sewing 
machine; many also utilized new-fangled egg-beaters, apple-peelers 
and clothes-wringers flabbergasting European visitors.” 
Even in far-flung outposts, “Americans had a passion for rapid 
communications and intelligence, reading many newspapers that 
arrived by post, carrying on commerce by means of the newly in- 
vented electromagnetic telegraph.” 
In 1853, Edison at age six, started a fire in his fathers barn, “just 
to see what it would do,” - Little Al received a severe public beating

92 
at the hands of his father as a punishment and as an example for 
other children who might have contemplated mischief. 
Little Al’s father was a jack of all trades, however, he initially made 
his fortune in the shingle manufacturing, lumber and feed business. 
The collapse of the family fortune - “the lake shore railroad came in 
1853, bypassed Milan and drove through Norwalk to Toledo with 
the result that commercial traffic followed the line of the railroad and 
was diverted from Milan, which eventually lost 80 percent of it's popu- 
lation.” 
Sam Edison’ financial collapse resulted in the whole family mov- 
ing north where Sam became a light house keeper in Port Huron, 
Michigan; young Edison’s mother, Nancy, took Little Al out of public 
school and took over his education. She constantly read to him, go- 
ing over the classics such as Gibbon’s, the Rise and Fall of the Roman 
Empire and Sear’, History of the World. 
At age nine, when Little Al asked questions that she could not 
answer she gave him R. G. Parker's, A School Compendium of Natural 
and Experimental Philosophy. The book illustrated basic home experi- 
ments in chemistry and electricity - Little Al carefully ran each ex- 
periment. 
At age ten, Edison began reading Benjamin Franklin’s notes 
on electricity, including the details of generating static electric- 
ity, and the turkey electrocutions. Edison tried the trick of vigor- 
ously rubbing the fur of two huge tomcats, whose tails he had 
attached to wires - the shocked cats severely scratched him. 
Edison, at the age of eleven, partnered up with another boy to 
start his first commercial enterprise. The two friends prepared a large 
garden to grow and sell vegetables. They tilled the soil, laid in wood 
ashes and animal manure for fertilizer, seeded the rows, watered and 
hoed weeds. It didn’t take long - Edison was soon driving a horse 
cart around town selling onions, lettuce, cabbage and peas. The first 
summer's income was $300. 
Edison did not get excited about farming and “hoeing corn in the 
hot sun was unattractive.” He abandoned the fledgling farm. 
At age thirteen, Little Al's first paying job was to sell newspapers 
on the three hour train trip from Port Huron to Detroit selling copies

HELLS KITCHEN 93 
of the Detroit Free Press. He also multitasked by selling fruit, peanuts 
and sandwiches to the passengers - he learned the value of delegat- 
ing by paying other boys to sell his snacks in other rail cars he couldn't 
get to during the trip. 
He developed a habit of hanging around the editorial offices and 
composition rooms of the Free Press to glean early news to accelerate 
his newspaper sales. At the end of the first year in the Civil War, on 
April 6, 1862, his diligence paid off, he hit a home run - the proof of 
the next days headlines featured a story that said as many as 60,000 
soldiers had died in the battle of Shiloh (actual deaths were 24,000). 
He had only enough money to buy 300 papers so he boldly walked 
into the offices of the Free Press’ famous editor, Wilbur B. Storey and 
procured 1,000 copies on credit. 
Edison had already bribed the railroad officials to wire ahead to 
all the station managers that a major battle had taken place. He was 
mobbed at every station and the papers sold out even though he 
raised prices - his net profit was an outrageous $150, six times the 
annual income of most Americans at the time. 
During the following summer, Edison snatched a three year old 
boy from in front of a run away boxcar, and the overjoyed father 
offered him telegraph lessons as a reward. Five months latter, Tom 
(as he grew older Little Al preferred to be called Tom) traveled through- 
out the Midwestern United States as a “tramp” telegraph operator 
with hundreds of other boys. 
The young “tramp” telegraph operators were known for gam- 
bling, cursing, drinking, smoking, playing jokes and hanging out 
with women - they had time, money and all the adult men were off 
fighting the war. Edison was particularly fond of chewing tobacco 
(pica), gambling and played jokes on his fellows; however, he spent 
most of his time reading in deserted boarding houses and tinkering 
with telegraphic devices in railway stations on the night shifts. 
Between 1857 and 1858, young Edison rejected reading and 
writing, when an opportunity arose to play with telegraphs, batteries 
and chemicals - he developed an obsession for learning everything 
that was known about the “new science” of electricity. As he worked 
with his early telegraph constructed of scrap metal, “Edison the boy

94 
approached, unwittingly, the mainstream of electrical experimenta- 
tion since the days of Benjamin Franklin.” 
In 1863, Charles Banting, an obese undertaker, noticed that 
obese people oftentimes died younger than slim people. He gave 
up carbohydrates and promptly lost 100 pounds - he was so ex- 
cited about his rapid weight loss that he published a small pam- 
phlet - How to Eliminate Corpulence (obesity). 
This pamphlet was the first public instruction on how to lose 
excess weight - this sentinel case was the harbinger of our future 
obesity problems and yet physicians failed to take note. 
In 1868, a friend wrote Edison from Boston - he wrote back ask- 
ing if there were any jobs. The response said Edison should come to 
Boston with all haste and appear in the Western Union office in that 
city. 
Edison arrived in Boston in 1868, after having held jobs with the 
Western Union Company and the military. He was driven continu- 
ously to insomnia by his ponderings on electrical and telegraphic 
equipment - magnets, springs, cylinders, rotating gears, armatures, 
batteries and rheostats filled his thoughts. He rented Charles Will- 
iams Jr.'s instrument workshop that had previously been occupied 
by Alexander Graham Bell. Edison, who had become an electrical 
“addict,” modernized the stock telegraph tape printer and collabo- 
rated with other telegraphers to sell his machines and a stock-and- 
gold quotation service. 
About this time, Edison invented an electric rat killer (using the 
same techniques that Franklin had used to electrocute turkeys) that 
consisted of two plates placed on the floor and connected to a stor- 
age battery - when the rats stepped on the two plates they completed 
a circuit and were immediately killed. He later produced a smaller 
version to kill cockroaches - the predecessor of the electrical blue 
light mosquito and fly zapper. 
In October of 1868, Edison filed for a patent for the first electric 
vote recorder - the patent, No. 90,646, was granted in June 1, 1869. 
As a telegraph press reporter, Edison had noticed a great waste of 
time in recording the voice votes during legislative sessions of Con- 
gress, he conceived of a time saving device which he whittled out of

HELLS KITCHEN 95 
wood with his own hands. Edison’ crude vote counter would be the 
fore runner of the 20th century voting machines - in Edison’s words: 
“In front of each member of the House (there were) two buttons, one 
for aye and one for no. By the side of the Speaker’s desk, was erected a 
square frame, in the upper part of which there were two dials, correspond- 
ing to the two classes of votes. Below the dials were spaces in which num- 
bers appeared. When the vote was called for, each member pressed one or 
another of the buttons before him and....the number of votes appeared 
automatically on the record. All the Speaker had to do was to glance at the 
dial and announce the results.” 
Edison immediately took his voting machine to the Massachu- 
setts Legislature, where he presented his idea to a committee chair- 
man - he was sure he would reap the handsome sum of $50,000 
dollars. To his total dismay, the chairman immediately rejected his 
machine saying, “it would infringe on the sacred right of the minor- 
ity to filibuster!” 
Boston failed to meet the financial demographics necessary to 
grow his business so Edison moved to New York. In 1869, Edison 
arrived in Manhattan (now aged 22) and found a job with Dr. Sam 
Law’s Gold Indicator’s wire service as a piecework assistant. 
Almost as soon as he started, the main office device failed and 
broke down - Wall Street came to a screeching halt and hundreds of 
brokers’ runners screamed at the office’s door for information. All of 
the office’s experts failed in their efforts to repair the machine - Edison 
pulled off his second coup by promptly fixing the device which had 
failed because a small spring coil had broken off and jammed the 
drive gears. Edison, at age 22, was now elevated to hero status in the 
financial world and the rapidly expanding growth industry of elec- 
tronics. 
A short time later he went to the directors of a Western Union 
subsidiary to offer a machine that connected stock tickers from out- 
side offices to the main machine in the home office - they paid him a 
blinding fee of $30,000! 
He stretched himself and contracted to provide private telegraph

96 
machines, electrical equipment and 1,200 fast model stock tickers 
for Western Union. He formed a partnership with a Newark machin- 
ist, and by laboring for sixteen hour days and surviving on coffee, 
apple pie and cigars he fulfilled his contract and delivered all his 
machines on time. 
Edison bought out his partner. To keep the cash flow going he 
worked as a foreman, supervising fifty or more pieceworkers - at 
night he built a laboratory equipped with the most up to date scien- 
tific instruments. He slept in his chair for six weeks while he con- 
sumed huge stacks of current scientific literature and he conducted 
experiments day and night. 
In the early 1870s, Edison hired three individuals who proved 
to be crucial to his future successes - Charles Batchelor, a British 
textile machinist; John Kruesi, a Swiss clockmaker; and Edward 
Johnson, a railroad and telegraph engineer. Batchelor could translate 
Edison's crude drawings into a detailed drawing; Kruesi built work- 
ing models that were presented with patent applications; and Johnson 
would complete the detailed patent applications, production con- 
tracts and payroll. 
In 1871, J.B.H. Dumas, proved through a terrible human experi- 
ment that an artificial diet with only the known nutrients of the day 
was missing something and could not by itself support life. 
Responsible for the care of a nursery of starving children and 
having no food, Dumas made an artificial liquid diet. Even though, 
Dumas’ diet contained the known proteins, carbohydrates and fats 
the diet failed to support and nourish the children. Laboratory in- 
vestigations confirmed his concerns, mice could thrive on a milk 
diet; however, the mice could not survive on milk substitutes com- 
posed of fats, proteins and carbohydrates. 
Feeding mice on a mix of compounds found in milk (proteins, 
fats, carbohydrates and salts), Nicolai Ivanovitch Lunin (1853 - 1937) 
demonstrated that the mice died; he concluded that there must be 
some small quantities of unknown factors in milk. 
Edison's 1872 notebook contained more than 100 sketches of 
revolutionary electronic and mechanical devices and that year he won 
34 patents. In 1875 Edison gave his 71 year father a task - find a

HELLS KITCHEN 97 
piece of property on which he could build a laboratory. 
In 1873, the fifty four year old Lydia E. Pinkham, of Lynn, Mas- 
sachusetts became America’ first millionaires. She manufactured and 
marketed, Lydia Pinkham’s Vegetable Compound as a cure for “fe- 
male weakness” - the product was soon being touted as “the greatest 
remedy in the world.” It was favored for dealing with dysmenorrhea, 
PMS and the hot flashes and night sweats characteristic of the “fe- 
male change of life.” 
In March 1876, Edison built his thirty foot by one hundred foot 
laboratory building in Menlo Park. Every room on the first level dis- 
played a banner sporting a quote from an English painter, Joshua 
Reynolds, “There is no expedient to which a man will not resort to 
avoid the real labor of thinking.” Each clock had it’s spring removed, 
the length of the work day would be determined by Edison, who 
frequently would work for 24 hours stealing only cat naps on the 
floor and then sleeping for eighteen hours. 
In 1876, Alexander Graham Bell invented the first telephone. 
Born in Scotland, Bell started is career as an elocution teacher, work- 
ing with deaf children. 
In 1877, Thomas Edison built his first version of the phono- 
graph; he published an article proposing ten uses for his new inven- 
tion. They included preserving the last words of dying people; re- 
cording books for blind people to listen to; announcing clock time; 
and teaching spelling. 
Reproduction of music was not seriously considered as a use of 
the phonograph by Edison. A few years later, Edison complained to 
his assistant that his invention had no commercial value. Within a 
few years he had a change of heart and finally entered into the office 
machine business with his phonograph. 
Other entrepreneurs created “jukeboxes” by arranging for a pho- 
nograph to play popular music for the price of a coin. Twenty years 
later, Edison finally agreed that the main use of his phonograph was 
to record and replay music. 
In August 1878, Edison returned to Menlo Park from a vacation 
(his employees described him as highly animated and excited). He 
had left in July sick and exhausted to the Rockies to view a total

98 
eclipse of the sun with a group of scientists and academics. One aca- 
demic, George Baker of the University of Pennsylvania, had talked 
excitably about a system of lights an inventor, Moses Farmer, had set 
up at an Ansonia, Connecticut foundry. They were known as arc 
lights, because the light was produced by an arch of elongated sparks 
reaching between two carbon rods the diameter of pencils. 
Bright as searchlights, arc lights had been employed since the 
1860s in British and American Lighthouses and certain public meet- 
ing places. The arc lights were too bright, too dangerous and too 
expensive to operate to be practical for private home or small busi- 
ness use. 
It wasn’t the eight large arc lamps and the foundry that generated 
Edison’s high level of excitement, it was the electrical generating sys- 
tem itself - the electric light was produced by a small crude dynamo 
or generator and the current was miraculously carried by wires one 
quarter mile to the foundry! 
Edison observed for the first time that electricity could be gener- 
ated in one place, sent over a distance through insulated wires and 
then efficiently divided and directed to multiple lamps. Edison im- 
mediately saw a potential profit. 
A reporter for the New York Sun who had been invited on the trip, 
recorded Edison’s moment of epiphany, “Edison was enraptured....He 
ran from the instruments to the lights and from the lights back to the 
instrument. He sprawled over a table with the simplicity of a child, 
and made all kinds of calculations. He estimated the power of the 
instrument and of the lights, the probable loss of power in transmis- 
sion, the amount of coal the instrument would save in a day, a week, 
a month, a year, and the result of such saving on manufacturing.” 
Edison’s habit was to reduce the size of machines, to miniaturize 
them, in order to save energy and reduce heat production. Rather 
than sending generated electricity through carbon rods to produce 
an arc light, which was useless for home and small business lighting, 
Edison transmitted it along a wire to a tiny filament in a small incan- 
descent light fixture. He worked for forty eight hours straight at Menlo 
Park, “I discovered the necessary secret, so simple that a bootblack 
might understand it,” he crowed! In his usual bold fashion he went

HELLS KITCHEN 99 
public with his ideas only one week after his visit to Ansonia. 
Edison's “wild” claims were widely published in newspapers, “he 
had not only found the way to create an incandescent bulb, but he 
also would be able to light the entire lower part of New York with 
one engine and 15 or 20 dynamos: | have it now! With a process I 
have just discovered, I can produce a thousand - aye, ten thousand 
(lamps) from one machine. Indeed, the number may be said to be 
infinite....with the same power you can run an elevator, a sewing 
machine, or any other mechanical contrivance, and by means of the 
(electrical) heat you may cook your food.” 
More than 100 years earlier, Benjamin Franklin had killed and 
cooked turkeys with electricity provided by storage batteries. Edison 
saw the future - he envisioned the world run by electrical devices. 
By envisioning a process by which we could cook our food in 
the near future with electricity, Edison ushered in the leading 
edge of our HELLS KITCHEN! How many wood ashes would be 
left over when we heated our homes and cooked our food with 
electricity - none! None! Our historical stream of nutritional 
minerals, used by humans since before recorded history was 
about to be abruptly shut off. 
Unfortunately, there was no one in the forest to hear the tree 
fall - this was the moment of transition for human health and no 
one saw the danger coming - no one offered a replacement for 
the nutritional minerals we were about to lose. 
Edison’s “secret” was a mental creation rather than a reality - a 
thermal regulator to cut off or dampen the electrical current to the 
filament before it melted or burned out. Edison scholars Robert Friedel 
and Paul Israel underline his audacity: “For Edison, the search for a 
practical incandescent light was a bold, even foolhardy, plunge into 
the unknown guided at first more by overconfidence and a few half- 
baked ideas than by science. To suggest otherwise is to rob the in- 
ventive act of its human dimension and thus to miss an understand- 
ing of the act itself.” 
Other electricity hopefuls in arc and incandescent lighting projects 
had transmitted a large amount of current along a heavy wire to a 
low resistance filament. The important discovery that Edison had

100 
made, after arguing it out with Charles Batchelor, was to raise the 
voltage to transmit a small level of current through a thin wire to a 
high resistance filament. His “secret” was the practical employment 
of the law proposed in 1827 by a German physicist by the name of 
George Ohm. 
Edison later said, “At the time I experimented | did not under- 
stand Ohm’s law. Moreover, I do not want to understand Ohms law. 
It would stop me experimenting.” Understanding the relationship 
connecting voltage, current and resistance was all important to the 
creation of the incandescent lamp, and he understood it “intuitively” 
even if he could not illustrate it in a mathematical formula.” 
Academics and scientists from America and England who were 
still caught up in the concept of low resistance and heavier wires, 
dismissed Edison’s project to light New York out of hand as both 
“scientifically stupid and economically hopeless.” 
Edison's immediate need was to find a filament of high resistance 
that he could heat up to incandescence in a clear glass bulb, evacu- 
ated of oxygen to an “airless” state to inhibit oxidation. In 1878 Edison 
was not even close to solving these problems. His friend and lawyer, 
Grosvenor Lowrey quickly raised $300,000 to form Edison Electric 
Light Co. The necessary filament was not as forthcoming as Edison 
had hoped; he had rejected carbon because it burned up very easily. 
Platinum wire produced low resistance but did not oxidize and there- 
fore seemed to fit the need. He started with a design that featured 
long spirals of thin platinum wire, in an effort to increase the filament’ 
resistance, but it was a tedious and extremely hazardous enterprise. 
In mid-April, Lowrey brought a cluster of investors into the dark 
Edison laboratory, where he had set up twelve lamps with platinum 
filaments connected in a series. Edison instructed John Kruesi to turn 
on the current gradually. Francis Jehl, an assistant, noted: “I can see 
those lamps rising to a cherry red and hear Mr. Edison saying, ‘A 
little more juice,’ and the lamps began to glow. A little more.... And 
then one emits a light like a star after which there is an eruption and 
a pulf, and the machine shop is in total darkness.” 
Batchelor replaced the faulty lamp; the same thing happened a 
few minutes later. Only Lowrey’s eloquence and the steadfastness of

HELVS KITCHEN 101 
42-year-old John Pierpont Morgan kept the group together. 
A second challenge was the ability to produce and keep a vacuum 
-no one yet had been able to pull enough air out of the bulb. Edison 
fell back on one of his unique skills, he looked for the right person 
for the job. He placed a classified add in the New York Herald for a 
glass blower, which produced an eighteen year old in a little red 
German student cap. 
The burly mechanics had a hardy laugh at the appearance of the 
relatively small almost feminine Ludwig Boehm; however, his skills 
produced a better bulb more aligned with Edison’s design and he 
helped design and perfect an original technique to evacuate a bulb 
by infusing mercury. The technique was tedious and often times frus- 
trating; however, in September, after almost a month of trial, Edison 
and his team achieved a vacuum that was one hundredth of an atmo- 
sphere. Edison was so overjoyed to learn that at this level of reduced 
oxygen in the bulb that even a carbon stick did not burn up quickly, 
and the carbon gave a brighter light than the platinum had. 
This was the good news. The negative was that resistance to this 
carbon filament was only about two ohms (which would mean more 
current, more copper). Resistance could be raised by creating a fine 
filament in a tight spiral, however, the filament would have to be no 
thicker than fifteen thousandths of an inch. Edison pushed all his 
help to chaos, attempting to convert carbon into “reeds” no thicker 
than thread. Day after Day, night after night, over and over the spiral 
“reeds” would shatter. 
Two hectic, sleepless and fruitless weeks went by before Edison 
decided to change directions; his new thought was to bake the pow- 
dered carbon into a plain cotton thread. On the eighth attempt, on 
October 21, Batchelor carried the small hair-like thread to Boehm’s 
house to insert into a bulb. “Just as we reached the glass blowers 
house, the wretched carbon broke,” Edison recalled. “We turned back 
to the main laboratory and set to work again. It was late in the after- 
noon before we produced another carbon, which was broken by a 
jeweler’s screwdriver falling against it. But we turned back again and 
before nightfall the carbon was completed and inserted in the lamp. 
The bulb was exhausted of air and sealed, the current turned on, and

102 
the sight we had so long desired to see met our eyes.” 
Thread No. 9, lit at 1:30 a.m., and lasted until 3:00 p.m. - 13 full 
hours, after which Edison added a stronger battery to boost the light 
to 30 candles, or three times gas light. They watched the thread-like 
filament react to the severe heat. The light continued for another 
hour. It was a crack in the glass that let air in that resulted in a burst 
of flame and then the lab went dark. Although their carbon-cotton 
filament was not perfect, the excited team had demonstrated to them- 
selves that a carbon filament in a vacuum could work! 
After inspecting the charred filament under the lens of a micro- 
scope, Edison set out to find another organic fibrous material, some 
form of cellulose that would create even more resistance than cotton. 
By November 16th, the team settled on a piece of everyday card- 
board. Edison noted, “None of us could go to bed, and there was no 
sleep for any of us for forty hours. We sat and watched it with anxi- 
ety growing into elation. The lamp lasted about 45 hours, and I real- 
ized that the practical incandescent lamp had been born.” 
Even at this early stage, Edison was setting up the commercial 
electricity business in New York, Paris and London. The laboratory 
team worked day and night, making filaments and assembling bulbs 
by hand, one by one, so that on New Year’s Eve, when Edison held 
his open house in Menlo Park to a public exhibit he had about 300 
bulbs. Over 3,000 people showed up to gawk and ask questions; the 
academics and experts still refused to be impressed. 
Henry Morton of the Stevens Institute, who had been on the 
Rockies expedition to view the eclipse of the sun, claimed that Edison 
was selling “a fraud upon the public,” which spurred Edison to fur- 
ther promise that, “He would erect a statue of Morton at Menlo Park 
and shine an eternal electric light on his gloomy countenance.” 
“There is a wide difference,” Edison noted, “between completing 
an invention and putting the manufactured article on the market,” - 
however, the marketing of the electric light bulb was the least of his 
problems. Edison had to invent the entire electrical industry! He had 
to create an entire system which would include every possible detail 
- and then commercially produce every part necessary. 
Edison had to assemble a central power station; design and manu-

HELVS KITCHEN 103 
facture his own dynamos (generators) to convert steam energy into 
electrical energy; ensure a steady electrical current; connect a 14- 
mile network of underground wiring; insulate the wiring against 
moisture and the accidental discharge of electrical discharges or “short 
circuits,” invent and install safety devices against fire; design com- 
mercially efficient motors to use electricity in the daylight hours to 
run elevators, printing presses, lathes, fans, etc.; design and place 
meters to monitor individual home and business use of the electric- 
ity for billing purposes; invent, manufacture and market a catalogue 
full of switches, breakers, sockets, fuses, distribution systems and 
lamps. 
In 1880 and 1881 Edison established Con Edison and General 
Electric and put their offices in a double brownstone mansion on 65 
Fifth Avenue. For his central power production station, Edison pur- 
chased several deserted and broken down warehouses at 25 - 257 
Pearl Street in full view of the Brooklyn Bridge towers which were 
still under construction. 
In December 1881, Edison began digging up paving stone to lay 
conduit to carry the wires outward from the Pearl Street power gen- 
erating station. Edison frequently was on his hands and knees in the 
trenches to check the connections himself. 
On Sunday, September 3, 1882 Edison spent the day and night 
checking and double checking, rehearsing and double rehearsing the 
completeness and every function of the system for it’s unveiling and 
grand opening on Monday afternoon. “The gas companies were our 
bitter enemies, ready to pounce upon usat the slightest failure,” Edison 
said. 
When John W Lieb, the chief electrician pulled the power switch 
on Monday September 4th at 3:00 p.m. only one of the six dynamo 
sets worked and the steam engine coughed and ran roughly for fail- 
ure to be warmed up early; but Edison, sitting in the distant offices of 
Drexel, Morgan & Co. was ready for the historic moment when he 
would ceremoniously turn on 106 lamps, was not disappointed - 
they all snapped on and brightened the surrounding office. They all 
came on; 100 percent came on! At the offices of the New York Times, 
“In fairy tale style,” fifty two filaments came to life and lit the after-

104 
noon and well on into the night. 
Only a dozen or so people had gathered in the warehouse to 
observe the dynamo open up a new chapter in human enterprise. 
The lights went on in the first district Monday September 4, 
1882 at 3:00 p.m. in the full sunlight of a late summer after- 
noon, which resulted in a “rather feeble effect.” 
The event was anticlimactic - despite the optimistic talk of a cen- 
tral station that would light 2,500 to 10,000 structures by means of 
a single circuit, the Edison Electric Hluminating Company, opened 
for business with only eighty five customer’s fully wired and brag- 
ging only 400 lamps. 
The Pearl Street facility costs to that date came to a total of 
$600,000. The first power station staggered forward with only one 
generator, which worked rather poorly - the advanced engines were 
not to arrive for two more months. 
“Look, the Edison lamps have been turned on,” observers simply 
said, when the first city power station turned on - “and that was 
that.” 
It was only later, after many months had passed that people be- 
gan to realize what Edison had created at the dusty Pearl Street ware- 
house. “It was in fact, nothing less than the marriage of steam and 
electric force,” said T. Comerford Martin, a long time Edison friend. 
The world was now rapidly moving from the steam age of James 
Watt into the electrical age of Thomas Edison. 
It had taken more than 1,500,000 years to move from the 
first taming of fire to steam and then only 100 years to move 
from steam power to electrical power. The simple unassuming 
wood ashes that had carried the health and development of hu- 
mans forward for so long were now becoming “an unnecessary 
drudge.” 
More than fifty years had come and gone since Michael Faraday 
had discovered the “mechanical production of induced electricity 
and had been possessed by a vision of the future (of) electric power.” 
The future needed Edison, however, as well as Ohm, Faraday, Am- 
pere, Arago, Franklin, Newcomen, Watt and others to bring the world 
into the Electrical Age. A London newspaper said it all - “There is but

HELLS KITCHEN 105 
one Edison.” 
In Edison's day, the most revered fertilizer for home gardens was 
an equal mix of wood ash produced in fire places and stoves, bone 
meal and animal blood meal obtained at slaughter houses. The wood 
ashes in this mix would be removed from the stove’s ash pan every 
morning and placed directly on the garden or added to animal ma- 
nure or compost fertilizers. 
Wood ashes were also used as “culinary” ashes and used to cut 
the more valuble salt 10:1 or 100:1 since befor written history - in 
effect providing a constant stream of esssential minerals for the 
growth, health and longevity of humanity 
The fork in the road to Hell that we had been looking for had 
been found; it was at 3:00 p.m. on Monday September 4, 1882 that 
we took the off ramp and exited from the main road and took the 
fork in the road to obesity - the wrong path, the fork in the road to 
Hell. Edison’s technician had loosed the seeds of obesity from 
Pandora’s Box when he loosed commercially generated electricity! 
The road to obesity wouldn't be free, it would be an expensive 
toll road to Hell - it wouldn't be a freeway, we would pay a grave 
price to stay on the road that would bring us to obesity, diabetes and 
the Metabolic Syndrome - $117 billion per year. By 2004, sixty five 
percent of Americans would be overweight, thirty one percent of 
Americans would be obese and 1,000,000 new American diabetics 
each year would become the new metabolic pandemics that plagued 
the industrialized world (of course Americans led the way).

“As one of the greatest of technical geniuses, (Edison) became a 
symbol of our Machine Age, one of the founders of our mechanical 
civilization, for better or for worse.” 
-Dr. Le Compte du Nouy 
HELLS KITCHEN 
In 1761, the Colonial kitchen, with it’s huge stone fireplace, was 
the center of family and community life rather than the church or 
parlor. In the other rooms, the rafters could be bare and the floors 
made of earth or raw hewn boards; however, in the kitchen there was 
room enough near the hearth for finishing chores, and for children 
to play and watch their mother cook and prepare meals in large iron 
pots. 
On the hearth’s back bar were hooks of varying lengths called 
trammels, hakes, pot-claws, potclips and pot crooks. There were also 
lug-poles, job-crokes, recons, gallows-balke and gallows-crooks. On 
these devices the housewife hung pots and kettles. 
In the kettles and pots were hasty puddings and corn meal por- 
ridge, while corn dumplings were baked on the side - “if the colonialist 
travels he takes with him no-cake, or Indian corn, which is (an ear 
of) corn parched in ashes and carried in a leather bag.” 
Since the advent of commercial electrical generation in 1882 by 
Edison, there was a persistent, incessant and accelerating search for 
more uses of electrical power. Some of the more interesting inven- 
tions of progress included electrical, coal, gasoline, oil, natural gas

108 
and propane kitchen appliances. They were marketed as instruments 
of progress “that would free the American house wife from the old 
drudgery and the dirt associated with the starting of the kitchen and 
heating wood or coal fires,” these new stoves were cleaner and more 
efficient that the old wood stoves - “no more soot to clean from your 
pots and pans.”

HELLS KITCHEN 109 
The new electrical technology was proof that things could only 
get better; however, no one even considered the possible side effects 
on the world when we made the conversion from wood and coal 
fuels to electric, gas and propane. 
Where would our nutritional minerals now come from? We still 
needed our essential minerals, yet no one seemed to worry about the 
looming deficit. We had been using the wood ash kitchen byproduct 
for thousands of years as a supplement combined with table salt, 
“culinary ashes” and a fertilizer for our gardens - it would be gone in 
but a few short years and we would be put metabolically naked into 
the ovens of HELLS KITCHEN! 
When one looks at the time line following the 1882 quickening 
of the commercial electric industry, the more frightened we become. 
As we achieved the next plateaus of scientific achievement, includ- 
ing “medical miracles,” nuclear power, space technology, etc., we sped 
up technological progress, but we left our primal human needs for 
mineral nutrients in the empty kitchen ash bin. 
The result of our failure to appreciate our loss, our failure to re- 
place the ash minerals we had depended on for thousands of years 
has become a whirlwind of obesity, diabetes and the Metabolic Syn- 
drome. 
In 1866 Nikolaus Otto invented the first gasoline engine; at this 
time horses had been supplying power for carrying loads, plowing 
and pulling wagons for 6,000 years. As Otto’ first engine was weak 
and seven feet tall it didn’t initially look like it could replace the 
horse. 
In 1874 Eli Whitney invented the cotton gin to end the labor 
intensive job of cleaning the seeds and hulls from the cotton bolls. 
In 1874, an Austrian chemistry student created a new chemical 
compound, dichloro-diphenyl-trichloroethane, that he called DDT. 
On May 10, 1876, the United States opened it’ first centennial 
celebration at the grand Centennial Exhibition in Philadelphia. Over 
all, 9,000,000 people would visit the fairgrounds. One of the most 
popular attractions was a single gigantic coal fired steam engine that 
powered literally thousands of mechanical inventions set in the 
exhibition’s Machinery Hall. At the grand opening, shortly after a

110 
choir sang Handel’ “Hallelujah Chorus” to an enthusiastic crowd of 
over 100,000 people, President Ulysses S. Grant stepped up to the 
giant engine and pulled its control lever. 
With a hiss and cloud of steam, the engine’s cross beam began to 
rock, its two huge pistons began to work, and eight miles of connect- 
ing shafts running through the hall began to turn. A huge cheer rose 
from the crowd as fourteen acres of shiny new machinery sprang 
loudly to life at the same moment spinning, sawing, sewing, pump- 
ing and printing without the need for the control and the muscle 
power of human hands - America became an industrial power, 
America joined the industrial revolution. America was headed faster 
and faster into HELLS KITCHEN. 
In 1885, the gasoline engine increased in efficiency to the point 
where Gottfried Daimler installed an engine on a bicycle to produce 
the first motorcycle; he waited until 1896 to build the first truck. 
In 1877, fingerprints were first invented and used by William 
Hershel; he worked for the Indian Civil Service, he used “thumb 
print signatures” of army pensioners in Bengal to ensure they only 
collected their money once. 
In 1888 the first electric transformer was invented, thus extend- 
ing the distance, that electrical services could be provided. 
In 1895 the first electric locomotive starts commercial operation. 
In 1893 - 1895 recorded the appearance of the first gasoline pow- 
ered automobile. Developed by Charles E. and J. Frank Duryea, and 
incorporated by almost all other automobile manufacturers, they first 
exhibited their automobile to an amazed public in 1900 at the first 
National Auto Show at Madison Square Garden, New York. 
In 1897, British physician, Dr. Ronald Ross, proved that malaria 
was spread by a mosquito of the genus Anopheles. 
In 1897, the Sears Roebuck Catalogue listed iron kitchen stoves 
that burned wood, soft coal or hard coal, gasoline stoves and oil 
stoves - the relentless move away from wood as a kitchen and heat- 
ing fuel had begun in earnest:

proved design, intended to meet the demand for a well made and attrac-

In 1900, Karl Landsteiner, an Austrian physician discovered that 
each human has a slightly different blood “type.” He identified at 
least three “types” or “groups” of blood and called them A, B, and O. 
His discovery explained why people react in different ways when 
given a blood transfusion; if the donor blood does not match the 
recipient blood the red cells can clump together causing blood clots 
and death. 
Yellow fever was proven to be spread by a mosquito of the genus

112 
Aedes in 1900. Studying a yellow fever epidemic in Cuba under the 
watchful eye of U.S. Army surgeon Dr. Walter Reed, British physi- 
cian Dr. James Carrol allowed an infected mosquito that had fed on 
yellow fever victims to bite him - his theory was confirmed when he 
developed yellow fever (he later died of the cardiac complications of 
yellow fever). 
The 1900 Sears and Roebuck Catalogue no longer carried the 
gasoline or oil stoves - the petroleum stoves made food taste like 
gasoline or kerosene and they blew up. The Acme Stoves that used 
wood and coal hung on in the Sears Catalogue, however, the price 
went up to $31.05. 
In 1901 the police of Scotland Yard in London opened their offi- 
cial fingerprint department. Using a simple system of fingerprinting 
techniques, the police collected comprehensive records of convicted 
criminals. The technique was later improved by Sir Henry Galton, 
and developed by Edward Henry, chief commissioner of the London 
Police, who used the technology to offer a sure way of identifying 
criminals. 
In 1901, Dr. Robert Koch, a German bacteriologist proposed a 
theory that it was rats that carried the bubonic plague. 
In 1901, Dr. Gerrit Grijns, a Dutch doctor, has shown that Beri- 
beri, a disease that affects the nervous system and is characterized by 
weakness, numbness, and paralysis is caused by the deficiency of a 
nutrient (thiamine - B1) removed when white rice is polished (re- 
moval of the bran). 
The formal opening of the Texas oil fields in 1901, meant that 
gasoline was plentiful and cheap, making cars the new “king of the 
road.” 
In 1901, Wilhelm Conrad Rontgen received the first Nobel Prize 
for physics for a chance observation that lead to the discovery of the 
X-ray in 1895. His most famous experiment involved placing his 
wifes hand between the X-ray source and a photographic plate, re- 
sulting in the production of the first permanent X-ray of the human 
body. An estimated 3 million X-rays were taken in 1901. 
Dr. George Perthes observed that X-rays can be used to reduce 
the size of cancerous tumors.

HELL'S KITCHEN 113 
In 1901, the McCormick Harvesting Machine Co. Chicago, IIli- 
nois mailed out calendars that still featured a farm family warming 
themselves in their living room with a wood burning open fireplace. 
In 1902, Landsteiner discovers a fourth blood group - AB. 
In 1902, Ivan Pavlov, a Russian physiologist uncovered a funda- 
mental type of nervous system response that he believes is the root of 
many aspects of human behavior - he called it the “conditioned re- 
flex.” Pavlov’s theories are based on experiments in which biologi- 
cally important events, such as feeding time for a dog is accompa- 
nied by a signal, such as the chiming of a bell. After a few days, the 
dog begins to salivate at the sound of the bell, whether or not any 
food is present. 
William Maddock Bayliss and Ernest Henry Starling identified 
the first known hormone - secretin, produced by glands in the small 
intestine (by 1905 they soon called these chemical messengers hor- 
mones from a Greek word meaning to “rouse to activity”). 
In 1903 the American Ford Motor Company sells it’s first “Model 
A” automobile to a doctor in Detroit. 
The German pharmaceutical giant Bayer, marketed a new pain- 
relief drug in 1903 under the name “Aspirin.” The drug was synthe- 
sized by a Bayer chemist, Felix Hoffman, six years earlier, when he 
was searching for a remedy to relieve his father’s arthritis pain. It was 
also suggested that Aspirin (acetylsalicylic acid) could also relieve 
headaches. A related compound, sodium salicylate (extracted from 
willow bark), was used for over twenty five years as a pain reliever 
for arthritis, however, it frequently produced gastric bleeding. 
In 1905, Dr. Albert Einstein, a twenty six year old working in the 
Swiss patent office in Berne, developed the “special theory of relativ- 
ity.” The theory is based on the concept that the velocity of light 
remains constant regardless of the relative motion of it’s source or an 
observer. 
In 1905, German zoologist Fritz Schaudinn and a colleague, der- 
matologist Erich Hoffmann, discovered the bacteria that caused syphi- 
lis; the organism has been named Spirocheta pallida. It had just been 
confirmed that syphilis can be transmitted from mothers to their 
babies and by sexual intercourse.

114 
In 1906, Will Keith Kellogg founded a company to market a 
“healthy” breakfast called “corn flakes.” The rolled and toasted wheat 
and corn “flake” was developed by Will Keith’s brother John Harvey 
Kellogg, an “alternative healer” at the Battle Creek Sanitarium in Michi- 
gan. The Kellogg brothers believed that their revolutionary breakfast 
food would replace the traditional American fried breakfast. 
In 1906, Frederick Hopkins, an English chemist, was the first to 
suggest that many foods contain “accessory factors,” substances apart 
from fats and proteins that are essential for health. Vitamins are chemi- 
cal substances, co-factors that are needed for growth and good health. 
In 1907, the tungsten filament incandescent bulbs went on sale 
and began to replace Edison’s original carbon filament. Tungsten fila- 
ments have an extremely high melting point of 6,170 degrees F 
In 1908, headlines state, “Ford’s production line paves the way 
for autos for all.” Ford, determined to capture the market for auto- 
mobiles that more people can afford, Henry Ford has adapted and 
improved the mass production systems pioneered by Ransom Eli Olds. 
Parts are manufactured in separate locations and delivered into 
the main factory so that technicians could assemble the complete 
auto. It takes just slightly more than twelve hours to assemble each 
car. Ford produces two models of his Model T, selling for $850 and 
$825 respectively. It still wasn’t cheap in terms of 1908 dollars, how- 
ever, Ford continues to develop ways to lower costs. 
French physician, Dr. Claude Mantoux developed a skin testing 
technique for determining if an individual was infected with tuber- 
culosis (consumption) or exposed to it. The technique features an 
injection of a sterile extract of the TB organism, called tuberculin, 
intradermally (between the layers of skin). If redness and swelling 
develop at the site of the injection, it indicates that the individual 
either has an active infection or has been exposed. 
The first filtered coffee maker was manufactured by Melitta Benz, 
a German housewife. She lined a can with absorbent paper, punched 
holes in the bottom of a can, placed fresh ground coffee beans in it 
and then poured boiling water through it, which resulted in per- 
fectly brewed coffee time after time. In 1909, she exhibited her fil- 
tered coffee pot at the Leipzig trade fair.

In 1909 the General Electric Company of Schenectady, New York 
began to market their first electric toaster. The toaster has a heating 
element made of wires that glow when plugged into the wall jack. 
The bread slice was laid on the wire elements, when one side is 
browned, the bread is manually turned over to brown the second 
side. The downside of this original electric toaster was that it had to 
be turned over and removed from the heat manually - left unattended 
the toast would burn.

By 1909, ads began to appear in all magazines and newspapers 
for weight loss programs. The lack of wood ashes in the human diet 
began to have an effect on the modern urbanite. The effect was obvi- 
ous enough that it created the opportunity for a commercial enter- 
prise - the embryonic beginnings of the weight loss industry quick- 
ened. 
Sren Peter Srenson, a Danish chemist, devised a new scale to 
measure acidity and alkalinity of solutions. The scale is called the

116 
“pH” scale and ranges from 0 to 14. Neutral solutions have a pH of 
seven; strong acids such as sulfuric acid have a pH of 0 to 2. At the 
other end of the scale, strong alkalinity such as caustic soda will have 
a pH of 14. The pH ofa solution is a measure of it’s concentration of 
hydrogen (H+) ions. The term pH stands for “potential for hydro- 
gen.” 
By 1910, millions of U.S. homes had telephones. Since Alexander 
Graham Bell had invented the telephone in 1876, many advances 
had been made in the telephone technology. Telephone lines and 
manual exchanges connect distant towns and cities all over North 
America. Some larger cities have automatic exchanges which allow 
the caller to dial the number directly without having to engage an 
operator first. 
In 1912, Sydney Russell invented the first electrical heating pad, 
which a short time later became the electric blanket. 
In 1912, Casimir Funk, a Polish American biochemist, called the 
small essential substances in food vital amines, later called vitamins - 
he isolated thiamine (vitamin B1) from unpolished rice.

In 1913, American chemist Irving Langmuir, of General Electric, 
has developed a way of extending the life of light bulbs and increas- 
ing their brightness. He invented a gas filled, coiled tungsten fila- 
ment lamp bulb. Vacuum tungsten filament bulbs had been manu-

HELLS KITCHEN 117 
factured for several years, however, at high temperature the filament 
gradually evaporates and then condenses on the cooler walls of the 
bulb resulting in the bulb blackening and dimming. Evaporation also 
thins and weakens the tungsten filament, thus shortening the life of 
the bulb. 
Langmuir discovered that when he filled the bulb with nitrogen, 
the process of evaporation slowed down. His tungsten bulb also used 
a coiled filament, which reduces heat loss and evaporation, which 
increase the efficiency and life of the bulb. 
Henry Ford continued to work towards his goal of having every 
American drive a car. He modernized his manufacturing facility in 
Highland Park, Michigan, which included a moving assembly line. 
The automobile bodies slowly move along a conveyer belt and work- 
ers on each side progressively added the appropriate parts. This 
modern process reduced the manufacturing time from twelve hours 
per car to 98 minutes to complete each car, resulting in considerable 
cost savings. Ford was able to reduce his Model T’s (nicknamed the 
“Tin Lizzies”) price to $400 per car! It immediately became the cheap- 
est and fastest selling car on the market. 
In June 1914, the “Modern Kitchen” featured an ad for a six-burner 
natural gas kitchen stove. 
In 1915, Knox Sparkling Gelatin of Johnstown, New York, mailed 
out free issues of Mrs. Knox's, Dainty Dish and Dessert Recipe Book, 
with receipt of 4 cents postage and address. 
In 1916, August Fruehof, a Detroit blacksmith built the first trac- 
tor trailer, a truck with a detachable cab powered by a gasoline inter- 
nal combustion engine. The cab can be attached to a variety of trail- 
ers or other farm equipment. The invention eliminated the need for 
multiple vehicles to operate a farm. 
The first electric washing machines were invented by John Fisher, 
an engineer, who marketed the machine with a promise to “take the 
drudgery out of wash day.” One filled the machine’ tub with the 
laundry to be washed, warm water, soap and turned on the motor 
and the machine did the work. When the wash was done, the water 
was wrung from the clothes using the hand cranked rollers.

In 1919, Charles Strite, an American inventor and mechanic from 
Minnesota patented the pop-up electric toaster. In Strite’s toaster, you

HELLS KITCHEN 119 
push a slice of bread into a slot, where it sits between two heating 
elements. Both sides of the bread are toasted at the same time, and at 
a set time a spring drives the toast popping back out of the slot. The 
toaster has a clockwork timer to ensure the proper toasting time. The 
toaster was sold under the brand name “toastmaster.”

In 1921, Canadian physicians Frederick Banting and Charles Best 
have successfully isolated the pancreatic hormone insulin. Insulin 
was used to treat people with adult onset type II diabetes. They cre- 
ated diabetes in laboratory dogs by tying off their pancreatic ducts. 
They isolated insulin that was produced by the Beta cells of the pan- 
creas, then injecting the hormone into the diabetic dogs, which re- 
sulted in a “cure.” 
In 1923, Albert Calmette, a microbiologist from the Pasteur In- 
stitute in Lille, France, and veterinarian Jean Guerin created and tested 
a vaccine for tuberculosis. The new vaccine, BCG (Bacillus Calmette- 
Guerin) is prepared from a weakened strain of the bovine (cattle) 
tuberculosis bacteria. The vaccine produces a mild infection that in- 
creases the resistance to the human tuberculosis bacteria. 
In 1924, Wilem Einthoven, a Dutch physiologist received the 
Nobel Prize for medicine for inventing the electrocardiograph, a de- 
vice designed to measure the electrical impulses of the heart. It has 
been used to diagnose many different forms of heart disease, irregu- 
lar heartbeat, high blood pressure and thyroid disease. 
The International Harvester Company developed the Farmall trac- 
tor. It is designed to be, as its name implies, an all purpose farm 
tractor that can plow, plant, cultivate and harvest 
During the 1920s, a plethora of electric kitchen appliances ap-

appliances featuring an electric range in a full page Christmas ad in 
The Saturday Evening Post for prices ranging from $2.50 to $65.00.

In 1925, Dr. George Hoyt Whipple, an American pathologist took 
large amounts of blood from dogs to produce anemia. Whipple fed 
the dogs varying diets to learn how different diets affect the production of red blood cells. He recorded that dogs that ate liver, which is 
rich in iron (and copper, selenium, vitamin B12, vitamin A, vitamin 
C, vitamin D, folic acid and essential fatty acids), formed red blood 
cells more quickly than those that did not.

In 1928, Joseph Schick invents the electric razor. 
In 1928, Alexander Fleming, a Scottish bacteriologist working in 
St. Mary’s Hospital Medical School in London, discovers a penicillin 
mold that kills bacteria. One morning, he noticed that a mold had 
contaminated one of his bacterial cultures and was preventing the 
bacteria from growing. He identified the mold as Penicillium notatum, 
and called the antibacterial substance it produced “penicillin.” 
Fleming isolated the mold, he grew a pure culture of it in a broth 
- after a few days he was able to prove that the growth medium had 
acquired the ability to kill a large range of Gram-positive disease 
causing bacteria. 
In 1928, Albert Szent-Gyorgyi, an American biochemist found 
that capsicum or cayenne peppers are rich in vitamin C. 
In 1928, Hugh Hammond Bennett published a U.S. Department 
of Agriculture Circular: 
“Thief of the Fertility of His Soil” 
“Not less than 126,000,000,000 pounds of plant food material re- 
moved from the fields and pastures of the United States every year. Most of 
this loss is from cultivated and abandoned fields and overgrazed pastures 
and ranges. The value of the plant food elements (considering only phos- 
phorus, potash, and nitrogen) in this waste, as estimated on the basis of the 
chemical analysis of 389 samples of surface soil, collected throughout the 
United States, and recent selling prices of the cheapest forms of fertilizer 
materials containing these plant nutrients, exceeds $2,000,000,000 an- 
nually. Of this amount there is evidence to indicate that at least 
$200,000,000 can be charged up as a tangible yearly loss to the farmers of 
the nation. These calculations do not take into account the losses of lime, 
magnesia, and sulfur.”

In 1930, Otto Frederick Rohwedder, an American jeweler per- 
fected a bread slicing and wrapping machine. He had actually in- 
vented the bread slicing machine in 1912, however, the inability to 
wrap the bread mechanically delayed marketing the machine. 
In 1931, Paul Kerrer, a Swiss chemist, establishes the structure 
and function of vitamin A. 
On October 18, 1931 Thomas Edison died at the age of 84 as 
a result of gastric ulcers, Bright’s disease (kidney failure with 
protein in the urine), uremia and diabetes. It is ironic that the 
man whose electrical technology started the downward path of 
the health of Americans and people in the industrialized world, 
should die of diabetes himself. 
We learned that adult onset type II diabetes could be “pre- 
vented and cured” with simple mineral supplement programs in 
1957. The minerals that had sustained us and had prevented 
diabetes, were originally obtained from the wood ash product of 
the kitchen and heating fires - Edison’s electrical world had elimi- 
nated the traditional source of dietary minerals universally used 
by humans. 
In 1932, Charles Glen King, an American chemist isolated vita- 
min C from lemon juice; it had long been known (since James Lind) 
that citrus fruit could prevent and cure scurvy; a vitamin C defi- 
ciency disease. 
In 1933, Tadeus Reichstein has become the first to produce a 
vitamin synthetically in the laboratory, and his “artificial form of vitamin C is identical to the natural one.” 
The first all-electric, automated tea-maker, the Teasmade, is in- 
troduced in Great Britain. 
A carbonated drink, 7 up, formally marketed as Lithiated Lemon 
is introduced in America. 
In 1936, the U.S. Senate entered into the record (as U.S. Sen- 
ate Document No. 264) a reprint of an article from Cosmopoli- 
tan magazine, which stated, “Our physical well-being is more 
directly dependent upon the minerals we take into our systems 
than upon calories or vitamins, or upon the precise proportions 
of starch, protein or carbohydrates we consume.”

124 a 
The document’ authors went on to state: 
“Do you know that most of us today are suffering from certain 
dangerous diet deficiencies which cannot be remedied until depleted 
soils from which our food comes are brought into proper mineral 
balance? 
The alarming fact is that foods (fruits, vegetables and grains) now 
being raised on millions of acres of land that no longer contains 
enough of certain minerals are starving us - no matter how much of 
them we eat! 
This talk about minerals is novel and quite startling. In fact, a 
realization of the importance of minerals in food is so new that the 
text books on nutritional dietetics contain very little about it. Never- 
theless, it is something that concerns all of us, and the further we 
delve into it the more startling it becomes. 
You'd think, wouldn't you, that a carrot is a carrot - that one is 
about as good as another as far as nourishment is concerned? But it 
isn’t; one carrot may look and taste like another and yet be lacking in 
the particular mineral element which our system requires and which 
carrots are supposed to contain. 
Laboratory tests proved that the fruits, the vegetables, the grains, 
the eggs, and even the milk and the meats of today are not what they 
were a few generations ago (which doubtless explains why our fore- 
fathers thrived on a selection of foods that would starve us!). 
No man today can eat enough fruits and vegetables to supply 
his stomach with the mineral salts he requires for perfect health, 
because his stomach isn’t big enough to hold them! And we are 
starting to turn into big stomachs! 
No longer does a balanced and fully nourishing diet consist merely 
of so many calories or certain vitamins or fixed proportion of starches, 
protein and carbohydrates. We know that our diets must contain in 
addition something like a score of mineral salts. 
It is bad news to learn from our leading authorities that 99% of 
the American people are deficient in these minerals, and that a marked 
deficiency in any one of the more important minerals actually results 
in disease. Any upset of the balance, any considerable lack of one or -

HELLS KITCHEN 125 
another element, however microscopic the body requirement may 
be, and we sicken, suffer and shorten our lives. 
We know that vitamins are complex chemical substances which 
are indispensable to nutrition, and that each of them is of impor- 
tance for normal function of some special structure in the body. Dis- 
order and disease result from any vitamin deficiency. 
It is not commonly realized, however, that vitamins control the 
body’s appropriation of minerals, and in the absence of minerals they 
have no function to perform. 
Lacking vitamins, the system can make some use of minerals, 
but lacking minerals, vitamins are useless.” 
Several pivotal things were happening concurrently at this 
point in time regarding the health of Americans and the entire 
industrialized world as well: 
1) Great numbers of people moved off the land and family 
subsistence farms and moved into towns and cities to work 
in factories for cash wages. 
2) Urban families had eagerly shifted their kitchens and 
homes from wood (and coal) as a fuel for cooking and 
heating to electrical, oil, gas and propane appliances - there 
were no longer any ash byproducts to put in our gardens 
and mix with our food and table salt. 
3) Large specialized farms and orchards growing wheat, corn, 
rice, soybeans, citrus and fruit used simple NPK 
(nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium) fertilizers for 
maximum yields - few people put wood ashes in their 
gardens anymore. Gardens had become a toy of the 
serious hobbyist, rather than an important source of food 
and minerals for the average American. 
4) World War II was on the horizon, technology's star was 
rising, infectious diseases were being wiped out by

126 
sewage systems, clean water and improved personal 
hygiene and antibiotics. 
5) Travel was easy, just about every family had a car; 
commercial bus, train and even air travel was cheap and 
widely available; gasoline was 12 cents per gallon. 
In December 1937, the Edison General Electric Appliance Com- 
pany of Chicago, Illinois, placed an ad in the Ladie’s Home Journal to 
market the Hotpoint Electric Range: 
“This year, in homes all over the land, women are going to receive the 
priceless gift of freedom! Freedom from long hours of kitchen toil. Freedom 
from pot-watching, from scouring soot-blackened pans and kettles, and 
from cooking failures. 
Day after day, for years to come, this new Hotpoint Range can be a 
source of joy and contentment in your home! It’s modern - never smudges 
kitchen walls and utensils. And with the help of the amazing Chef's Brain” 
(automatic timer clock) it actually cooks whole meals without any super- 
vision!” 
In 1938 fluorescent lamps were made available in the United 
States, delivering a low voltage to gas-discharge lights - they produce 
approximately four times as much light. The fluorescent lamp is made 
from a glass tube about two feet long and does not contain a classical 
filament typical of an incandescent bulb - instead the inside of the 
tube is coated with phosphor and filled with mercury vapor. This 
new tube produces invisible ultraviolet light in an electrical discharge 
- as a result the phosphor fluoresces, or turns the ultraviolet light 
into visible white light. Thomas Edison's General Electric Company 
had worked on this new development since 1934. 
A new home use steam iron was marketed by Edmund Schreyer 
of Ridgefield, CT. Schreyer’ iron used a jet of steam to dampen the 
clothes as the clothes were pressed. The new iron was fitted with an 
adjustable thermostat - one need only to set the required heat for the 
type of fabric and the iron maintained the temperature.

HELLS KITCHEN 127 
The Swiss company Nestle begins to market a successful instant 
coffee. 
The first hip replacement operation was performed in humans in 
1938. 
In 1939, DDT was rediscovered by Paul Herman Muller, a Swiss 
chemist who discovered DDTS pesticide potential. During World War 
Il, DDT was used to kill fleas and head and body lice. DDT killed 
insects and bugs in two ways - by simple contact on any part of the 
insect and secondly when the insect consumes the chemical. After 
World War Il DDT was employed world wide as a crop spray. 
In November 1940, The American Home displayed a General Elec- 
tric ad for the electric sink: 
General Electric 
Electric Sink 
1. Washes all the dishes! 
2. Disposes of all garbage! 
“It's easy to stay young electrically!” 
By 1940, the electric garbage disposal had all but eliminated 
household kitchen generated compost. 
In 1940, Howard Florey and Ernst Chain, two Oxford University 
scientists have isolated and purified, from a species of mold, an anti- 
bacterial substance that they hope will become a useful drug. The 
drug, “penicillin,” is able to kill infectious bacterium inside the liv- 
ing body. Their discovery of penicillin followed an investigation to 
identify naturally occurring antibacterial substances. They quickly 
came across Alexander Fleming’ 1928 work on penicillin and the 
small scale production of the drug. 
Vincent Du Vigneaud, an American biochemist, identifies the 
sulfur-containing vitamin - biotin. 
In 1941, a revolutionary type of farm was to cover the country- 
side - windmills that generate electricity. For centuries windmills had 
been used to generate power, however, the energy of the wind was 
transformed directly into mechanical energy to grind grain, pump

128 
water and pull loads uphill. In the 1890s, P. LaCour, a Danish engi- 
neer, patents a system of sails that were rigged to a steel tower to 
power an electrical generator. Smith and Putnam, two American en- 
gineers, replaced the sail design with propeller blades similar to those 
that power an airplane - the windmill was first erected at “Grandpa’s 
Knob” in Vermont, ushering in an environmentally friendly, renew- 
able source of electricity. 
On December 7, 1941 the Japanese attacked Pearl Harbor, de- 
stroying battleships of the American 7th Fleet and killing almost three 
thousand sailors, marines and soldiers. We were at war and no one 
was thinking of vitamins and minerals. 
In 1942, the U.S. government set up the Manhattan Project which 
was commissioned to invent the technology required to build an 
atomic bomb before Nazi Germany did. The goal was reached in 
three years, at a cost of $2 billion dollars (today’s dollars would be 
$200 billion). 
In 1943, Selman Waksman, an American biochemist, discovered, 
Streptomycin, a new antibiotic that is highly effective in killing Gram- 
negative bacteria and the tuberculosis bacillus. 
Streptomycin is a natural substance extracted from the mold, Strep- 
tomyces griseus. A mixture of penicillin and streptomycin will be com- 
monly used as a “broad spectrum” antibiotic to kill both Gram-posi- 
tive and Gram-negative bacteria. 
In 1945, the citizens of Grand Rapids, Michigan were subjected 
to an experiment sponsored by the U.S. Surgeon General: fluoride 
was added to the drinking water supply to determine whether or not 
it reduces dental caries or cavities. Earlier, in 1936, Dr. H. Treadly 
Dean, head of the dental hygiene unit at the NIH, speculated that by 
adding fluoride to the drinking water in “safe levels,” one might fight 
tooth decay. 
The most potent weapon known to man, the nuclear bomb, was 
used to end the war in the Pacific. Allied scientists, led by Edward 
Teller, Enrico Fermi, and J. R. Oppenheimer, had been working since 
1941 to develop a nuclear device ahead of the Germans. A small 
plutonium device was exploded on July 17, in the New Mexico desert. 
Following calls for the Japanese to surrender, on August 6, a single

HELLS KITCHEN 129 
uranium bomb “Little Boy,” was air burst 900 feet above the city of 
Hiroshima. Analysts, estimate that half of the cities 350,000 popula- 
tion would be dead by the end of the year as a result of radiation 
exposure. Three days later, an American B-52 bomber air burst a 
plutonium nuclear bomb, nicknamed “Fat Man,” 1,650 feet above 
the city of Nagasaki, causing enormous infrastructure damage and 
killing an estimated 70,000 people. 
Pillsbury introduced it’ first “ready to cook” boxed convenience 
food - a ready mixed pie crust.

In 1946, Linus Pauling, the renowned American chemist came 
up with a theory on how enzymes work - enzymes are proteins that 
do work by causing chemical reactions within living organisms. Ac- 
cording to Pauling, enzyme activity is related to the shape and these 
three dimensional shapes are related to the “activated complexes,” 
that form briefly during chemical reactions during the course that 
the reacting substances were turning into products.

130 
In 1947, the first microwave ovens were marketed in the United 
States, however, sales were initially far less than had been hoped for. 
In 1949, General Mills and Pillsbury began to market packaged 
ready made cake mixes (“Ready-made cake mixes provide home- 
made cakes without the work”) with all of the dry ingredients in a 
box and in various flavors including chocolate, yellow, and white 
fudge. One only needed to add water, eggs, and sometimes butter. 
By the 1950s just about 100 percent of urban kitchens in the 
industrialized nations had an electric, natural gas or propane stove 
and home heating furnace; just about seventy five percent of rural 
homes in industrialized nations had an electric, gas or propane stove 
and home heating furnaces. 
Wood ashes were pretty much an inconvenience of the past, no 
more wood ash dust, no more spilled ash buckets, no more getting 
up early to rekindle the fire. Everyone marveled how wonderful life 
was without ashes. 
The average family was overjoyed to give up the drudgery of deal- 
ing with the ash byproduct of the “old fashioned” kitchen and heat- 
ing furnace or fireplace. However, they had unknowingly and even 
eagerly given up something so basic to human health and life itself - 
it had been hidden in plain sight, the lowly kitchen ashes! 
We were now without the constant stream of nutritional miner- 
als in our diets that wood, peat and kelp ashes had provided for tens 
of thousands of years - we were now rushing headlong, metaboli- 
cally naked toward the pandemics of obesity, diabetes, the Metabolic 
Syndrome, osteoporosis, arthritis and Alzheimer’s disease of the 20th 
and 21st centuries - and no one recognized the insidious loss! No 
One!
