The Vitamin Wars 
Before written history, health care started out as a “family prac- 
tice” - it was the mom, the dad, the grand mother, the grandfather, 
the aunt, the cousin, maybe a neighbor who was a self appointed 
healer, the nun or the pastor that held the childs hand, bandaged 
their wounds, soothed their fevered brow, mixed and dosed the herbs 
and applied the poultices. 
The natural progression from the family dispensing health care 
to its own members to the “angels of mercy” from religious sects 
going out and doing “good works” for the aged, the poor and reli- 
gious pilgrims was a predictable series of events as bands became 
tribes, villages became towns and collections of towns became king- 
doms and nations.

[os 
In the beginning of human development, these concerns about 
health were driven by the individuals desire to survive and true al- 
truistic religious and scholarly motivations. However, as religions 
became entwined with the infrastructure and personalities of gov- 
ernments, kings and empires, public medicine previously driven al- 
most exclusively by scholarly, religious and altruistic motivations, 
became part of the power structure and filled with, intrigue, greed 
and driven by ego. 
Chemistry became a powerful science in the development of clini- 
cal medicine during the 1800s. The pursuit of chemistry and bio- 
chemistry as related to health and disease led to the theory that cer- 
tain diseases could be viewed as deficiency diseases, caused by the 
lack of vital elements in the diet. 
The biochemical theory, now finding it’s way into the medical 
school, that disease could be caused by a “deficiency” was a signifi- 
cant shift in paradigm. “Disease is so generally associated with posi- 
tive (causative) agents - the parasite, the toxin, the materies morbi,” 
noted a report in 1919, “that the thought of the pathologist turns 
naturally to such positive (causative) associations and seems to be- 
lieve with difficulty in causation prefixed with a minus sign.” 
Among the diseases to which this new and novel concept could 
be applied was marasmus or starvation in babies in poor areas (muscle 
wasting, fat loss, and significant loss of body weight). Another was 
Kwashiorkor, found in tropical Africa, characterized by muscle wast- 
ing and edema, enlarged liver, diarrhea and lassitude. 
In 1755, Gaspar Casal (1680-1759), published an account of 
pellagra (a vitamin B3 deficiency), then “a new disease” in Spain. 
Pellagra is characterized by the “Three D’s” including dermatitis (“a 
horrible crust, dry, scabby...crossed with cracks”), diarrhea, dementia and premature death - this disease flared up each Spring. 
Casal noticed that pellegra’s victims were poor and that corn meal 
was their staple food and they had little or no access to meat or milk. 
The same problem of pellagra existed in the poor people of Italy. 
Various theories were offered for the cause of pellagra, the main 
one was the limited diet of corn. “Maize” was a New World plant that 
had been brought back to the Mediterranean area of Europe by the ©

HELL'S KITCHEN 135 
Spanish Conquistadores. A French physician writing in 1845 sug- 
gested that the “predisposing” cause of pellagra was a diet with “too 
few animal products” and that the “efficient” cause was bad maize. 
In 1865, a Mexican doctor, pointed out that pellagra was not a 
problem in Mexico even though maize was the staple food of the 
peasant. He believed the reason pellagra was avoided in Mexico was 
“in the habit of soaking the maize in wood ashes before cooking, 
which had a disinfectant effect.” By contrast the European method of 
baking corn mush to polenta, “left it contaminated with moulds and 
toxins.” 
Some theorized that pellagra was an insect borne disease like 
malaria and yellow fever, carried by flies from the feces of pellegrans 
to the meals of future victims. 
Hungarian-born Joseph Goldfinger (1874-1929), of the U.S. Pub- 
lic Health Service, observed that in orphanages and mental asylums, 
where the “inmates” developed pellagra, the staff (naturally eating a 
wider variety of food) escaped the disease. When eggs and milk were 
added to the diet of the affected individuals, pellagra disappeared. 
Goldfinger started a prisoner volunteer test in which he fed the 
participants only corn meal, when dermatitis developed, he theo- 
rized that pellagra was due to “a lack of dietary factors.” 
In the early 20th century, pellagra was so common in the south- 
eastern part of the U.S., 20,000 cases were reported in Georgia alone 
during it’s peak - the affected patient’s diet’s were all limited to corn 
and pork fat. A system of federal “retardation centers” was estab- 
lished for isolating pellagra patients, who were thought to be infec- 
tious in a similar fashion to tuberculosis. 
In 1849, Thomas Addison reported the existence of a hitherto 
unknown fatal anemia, that was eventually called pernicious anemia 
- it was characterized by a low red blood cell count, but the cells that 
were observed were enlarged red blood cells. Vitamin B12 was eventually identified as the missing essential nutrient, however, there was 
another feature unique to pernicious anemia - the stomach had to 
produce two important substances: 1) stomach acid and 2) an acid 
dependent cofactor known as “intrinsic factor” - without either or 
both vitamin B12 could not be absorbed.

136 
It was learned that vitamin B12 injections could, by bypassing 
the stomach be utilized to prevent and cure pernicious anemia. Stom- 
ach acid substitutes (i.e. - betaine HCl) could be used as a supple- 
ment to activate intrinsic factor and encourage the absorption of vi- 
tamin B12. 
Beriberi (Sinhalese for “I cannot”), was a common disease in the 
Asian rice cultures. Beriberi was characterized by a weakness in the 
legs, hands, arms and congestive heart failure - death resulted from 
pulmonary edema and abdominal “dropsy.” 
In 1882, a Japanese naval vessel was sent on a long training tour 
with a starting crew of 275 sailors. Sixty percent of the crew devel- 
oped beriberi and twenty five died. Dr. Takaki (1849-1915), a Ger- 
man trained naval surgeon noted that the protein content of their 
diet was far below the German standard, he quickly proposed that 
the protein content be increased. 
On a second voyage the next year, Takaki, modified the sailor's 
diet by exchanging half of the rice with barley and he added, milk, 
beef and tofu (soy curd) - beriberi did not appear and no deaths 
occurred. Takakiy reported that he had prevented beriberi by increas- 
ing the protein in the diet. 
In the 1890s, Christiaan Eijkman, a Dutch physician and the 
director of the research laboratory in Batavia, Jakarta, was also over- 
whelmed with beriberi cases. It was theorized that beriberi was due 
to a bacterial infection, so an army of bacteriologists were sent from 
the Netherlands to identify the germ. Eijkman noticed the hospital 
chickens developed polyneuritis (beriberi) when they were fed the 
same white rice that was being eaten by the people with beriberi, 
instead of their standard brown rice chicken feed. 
When Eijkman fed whole brown rice to the affected chickens 
they quickly recovered - he theorized there was a toxin in the pol- 
ished white rice and that the antidote was in the polishings or rice 
bran. 
Eijkman’s colleague, Gerrit Grijns (1865-1944), had a different 
theory - he believed that there was “some essential substance in the 
rice that was being destroyed by heat,” he proposed that, “both dis- 
eases (fowl polyneuritis and human beriberi) were due to the ab- ~

HELVS KITCHEN 137 
sence from the diet of some factor present in the rice polishings.” 
Eijkman mailed a questionnaire to all of Java’s prisons, inquiring 
on the incidence of beriberi, the physical environment and the diet. 
The questionnaire revealed no relationship between the rate of beri- 
beri and the environment, however, the disease was a significant prob- 
lem in seventy one percent of the prisons using white rice and only 
three percent in those using rough rice. 
In 1906, Axel Holst (1860-1931), an Oslo bacteriologist was sent 
to study beriberi, which had become a problem in Norwegian sail- 
ors. In a simple experiment he fed guinea pigs on different kinds of 
_ flower - the entire population of guinea pigs died in thirty days. He 
further found that by simply adding cabbage or lemon juice supple- 
ments to their diet the guinea pigs all flourished and avoided the 
disease. 
The same year, Frederick Gowland Hopkins (1861-1947), of 
Cambridge, fed young rats on casein, lard, starch, sugar and salts 
(minerals) -supposedly everything essential to health. He added milk 
to the diet of one subgroup and found that “only those receiving the 
milk thrived.” “Astonishingly small amounts of certain substances in 
food, accessory food factors, were essential” he concluded, “for the 
body to utilize protein and energy for growth.” The key to nutrition, 
he concluded, “lay in these missing links!” 
In 1912, Casimir Funk (1884-1967), a Polish born biochemist, 
advanced the deficiency theory, by isolating the active substances in 
rice bran that would prevent beriberi. He named these dietary fac- 
tors or missing link “vital amines” (later shortened to vitamins in 
1920 when it was learned that not all vitamins were amines). 
Funk went on to link vitamin deficiencies to beriberi, scurvy, 
pellagra and rickets. The theoretical basis for nutritional supplemen- 
tation had finally been scientifically established. 
The “deficiency” theory was beginning to collect a weight of evi- 
dence and take on a shape that all could understand. All facts were 
pointing to the “likelihood that scurvy, beriberi, pellagra and rickets 
could be caused by dietary deficiencies” of essential factors in the 
diet! 
Rickets was a disease described in the 17th century by Francis

138 
Glisson - it had become wide spread and a significant problem in the 
industrializing cities using coal as a fuel. In 1890, Theobald A. Palm 
reported on rickets - “It is in the narrow alleys, the haunts and play- 
grounds of the children of the poor, that the victims of rickets are to 
be found in abundance” - he recommended sunlight to prevent and 
reverse rickets. 
In 1900, it was estimated that in Boston eighty percent of the 
children “under two, and twenty five percent of the children from 
poor families demonstrated signs of rickets.” Dr. David Paul von 
Hansemann (1858-1920) of Berlin, believed rickets was due to a 
lack of fresh air, sunlight and exercise. 
Edward Mellanby (1884-1955) induced rickets in puppies by 
feeding them diets lacking a factor found in certain animal fats. Supple- 
menting the puppies with cod liver oil prevented and reversed the 
rickets. Mellanby attributed rickets to a deficiency of a fat soluble 
factor he called vitamin A. By then various researchers demonstrated 
that rickets could be prevented and cured by exposing the sufferer to 
sunlight or by supplementing with cod liver oil. 
In April 1912, Professor A. von Wasserman of Berlin, reported in 
Popular Mechanics magazine that he had identified “a chemical sub- 
stance (selenium) that will cure cancer in mice.” He combined sele- 
nium with eosin, a diffusible stain, in order to ensure dispersal of the 
trace mineral and then injected the mixture into the mice - the tu- 
mors were destroyed. 
Wasserman believed the “tumors in mice were so similar to the 
tumors in humans, that it is believed that an important advance had 
been made toward the cure of that veritable scourge (in humans).” 
In 1922, it was established that the original vitamin A consisted 
of two separate factors. The original growth factor retained the name 
vitamin A, while the anti-rachitic factor was designated vitamin D. 
The fortification of milk with vitamin D soon followed in the United 
States. 
In 1922, it was shown that when weanling rats were fed on a 
milk diet, they grew to maturity in a normal way, but they were usu- 
ally sterile - the deficiency of a fertility factor was immediately theo- 
rized. Herbert McLean Evans (1905-1983), of the University of Cali-

HELLS KITCHEN 139 
fornia, and Katherine Scott Bishop (1889-1976), showed that the 
fertility factor was abundant in green leafy vegetables and wheat germ. 
The fertility factor was fat soluble but not related to any other vitamin so it was called vitamin E. 
It was well documented since the 18th century work of Dr. James 
Lind, that several food items could ward off and cure scurvy. The 
19th century pathologist, August Hirsch (1817-1894) identified po- 
tatoes as anti-scorbutic, and then there were the doctors in the Lon- 
don hospitals who had identified scurvy in children, treated them 
with diets of meat, milk and citrus juices. Scurvy was accidentally 
induced in guinea pigs in 1906 by Axel Holst, Theodor Christian 
and Brun Frolich while attempting to produce beriberi by giving an 
all flour diet. 
Funk theorized that scurvy was a vitamin deficiency and a series 
of experiments were set up to isolate and identify it. During the in- 
vestigative process it was found that many foods could prevent and 
reverse scurvy, however, some in particular (apples, lemon juice and 
cabbage) lost their anti-scorbutic effects when they were heated. 
During World War I, scientist, including Harriet Chick (1875- 
1977) at the Lister Institute in London, started a series of studies to 
determine the anti-scorbutic properties of various foods. Arthur 
Harden (1865-1940) was able to isolate citric and other acids from 
lemon juice, including a residue that had an exceptionally high anti- 
scorbutic property that was not found in the other acids isolated 
from lemon juice. 
In 1919, the anti-scorbutic factor had been universally accepted 
as an essential nutrient. Albert Szent-Gyorgyi (1893-1986), isolated 
“hexuronic acid” from the adrenal glands. In 1932, W. A. Waugh and 
Charles Glen King (1896-1988) isolated the “lemon vitamin” and 
demonstrated that it was identical to hexuronic acid. 
In 1932, this vitamin (vitamin C) was synthesized by Szent 
Gyorgyi - in 1933 the vitamin’s name was officially changed to ascor- 
bic acid. 
The concept that certain diseases (i.e. - scurvy, pellagra, beriberi, 
rickets, pernicious anemia, anemia, goiter, etc,) were caused by defi- 
ciencies was initially a novel thought, a really earth shaking thought,

140 
however, it was initially a toy for but a few scholars, military physi- 
cians and philosophers - these beliefs of “deficiency diseases” started 
after Lind in the 1700s, who believed that the healing power of lem- 
ons for scurvy was as an herbal medicine, the same concept as using 
quinine to treat malaria. 
Yet, the concept of “deficiency” diseases, hung on and produced 
a flurry of academic activity and discovery over the next 200 years. 
These 200 years were but a minor blip in the time line of human 
history - 30,000 years had passed since humans had developed the 
basic skills for the survival of modern man. 
These deficiency disease studies set off a new war between 
the various schools of medical thought and theory - a 200 year 
war that continues on into the 21st century, because medicine as 
a professionghas lost it’s altruism for seeking cures and there are 
no laws requiring doctors to cure people even when cures are 
available - so they don’t. 
The “sick man” it is said, “was to have dropped below the radar 
of the medical eye with the advent of the private clinic about 1800 - 
when the individual person was first given the generic label of pa- 
tient,” - a pathological sample or classroom exhibit riddled with dis- 
ease. This Houdini-like “disappearing act” of the sick person acceler- 
ated over the next 200 years, relegating the individual to an algebraic 
“X-factor” in equations of economics, sociology, physicians life styles, 
diagnostic technology, system’ analysis and advertising programs. 
Twentieth century medicine became identified with mind twist- 
ing political and social infrastructures and brick and mortar hospital 
structures and campuses that rivaled the most grand sky scrapers, 
medical universities, professional trade organizations that took on 
the look and functions of trade unions, international insurance con- 
glomerates, pharmaceutical company’s sales that surpassed most in- 
dividual nation’s gross national product, hospitals acting as teaching 
laboratories, research money generators that siphon funds from es- 
tates and large corporations, government departments (Medicare and 
Medicaid), international banking and corporate largess. 
Medicine developing parallel with organized trades and govern- 
ments was predictably connected by an umbilicus to political power, .

HELLS KITCHEN 141 
economics, central and local governments, the law, social services. 
advertising and the mass media (Dr. Marcus Wellby, MD; Dr. Kildare: 
Dr. Ben Casey; E.R., and advertisements for Vioxx, etc.). 
Once medical schools became part of universities, degrees were 
required to get licenses, medical societies, trade associations and guilds 
were formed, the practice of medicine became a toy almost exclu- 
sively for the wealthy. At this point, the industrial privateers and rob- 
ber barons became interested in the “customer base” that patients 
provided. 
During the transition into the 20th century, “scientific medicine” 
rapidly gained political and financial clout in the United States. The 
Federal bacteriological laboratory attached to the Marine Hospital 
on Staten Island, New York (1866), developed prominence and in 
1930 became the National Institute of Health (NIH) and was moved 
to Bethesda, Maryland. 
Medical research was mainly a function of private financing by 
the “robber barons,” industrialists such as Andrew Carnegie (1835- 
1918), John D. Rockefeller (1839-1937) and his son John Jr. (1874- 
1960), who “laundered” their financial empires through “good works.” 
Scottish-born Carnegie grew his fortune through an iron and steel 
empire. Carnegie was a firm believer in the “Survival of the Fittest” 
and ruthless capitalism concepts, however, he also held that, “a man 
who dies rich, dies disgraced” - therefore, he devoted a great deal of 
his time and energy to philanthropy. 
Carnegie was particularly fascinated with medicine and pharma- 
ceuticals, so he committed himself to medical education and spon- 
sored an investigation by Abraham Flexner (1866-1959) - a retired 
university president - into the status of medical education in America. 
American medical schools had developed in a financially com- 
petitive market, initially depending on the student fees for survival. 
From the early 1800s, hundreds of these profit motivated medical 
schools were opened. These proprietary medical schools were par- 
ticularly attractive to students because they were practical and did 
not require a “pre-medical” education prior to application. 
Sixty two of these myriad§ of schools survived between 1802 
and 1876, as well as eleven homeopathic and four eclectic schools.

142 
Flexner’ initial report, Medical Education in the United States and 
Canada (1910), claimed that quality had been sacrificed for quantity. 
Flexner believed that a good medical education could only be 
provided by reducing the numbers of schools and improving their 
standards for selecting students and staff. Educated at Johns Hopkins 
and the University of Berlin, he was committed to making medical 
schools university departments. He demanded that these new medi- 
cal schools be attached to big city universities to “attract better edu- 
cated students and more clinical material for teaching.” 
Prior to the Flexner report only five medical schools were in- 
volved with research - Harvard, Johns Hopkins, and the Universities 
of Pennsylvania, Michigan and Chicago. 
Flexner was a firm believer that the main requirement for true 
medical progress and education was basic science. He was critical of 
the French system, which he viewed as being too clinical, the En- 
glish system he believed was too decentralized and too clinical, and 
he was particularly disturbed by the American system which he viewed 
as being “grossly commercial.” 
Flexner wanted the medical school to be part of a larger univer- 
sity and have a requirement that medical students should first have 
a degree in the natural sciences. He believed that the “medical school 
of the future should have it’s own biomedical departments to encour- 
age research; it had to have a teaching hospital; stiff entrance require- 
ments for all students; and end with a doctoral degree.” 
Finding the existing laboratory facilities “wretched,” Flexner in- 
sisted that the new medical schools he envisioned have scientific fa- 
cilities unequaled by none. 
Flexner, committed to raising medical school standards, suggested 
that the numbers of American schools be reduced to thirty one, and 
he recommended a system in which students were required to have a 
minimum of two years of college education prior to entry; and that 
the staff must be full time and not practicing physicians. 
The majority of medical schools failed to meet Flexner’ gold stan- 
dard litmus test, which resulted in the closure of forty six colleges in 
ten years, including the homeopathic colleges and medical colleges 
for black and female students.

HELLS KITCHEN 143 
In 1910, 4,400 doctors graduated from medical school and ob- 
tained an M.D. degree, by 1920 these numbers of graduating doctors 
dropped to 3,047, resulting in amongst other things,,an increase in 
the social status and incomes of the new graduates. 
The medical schools that did survive got the message, they dis- 
carded their proprietary ways and they reinforced their relationships 
with hospitals and universities. In return, this new American breed 
of “scientific” medical schools was rewarded with enormous amounts 
of money from the philanthropic coffers of Carnegie and Rockefeller. 
Rockefeller, a devout Baptist who made his multi-millions through 
his oil business, at first channeled most of his giving to what he felt 
were “worthy churches,” and their altruistic endeavors, at the urging 
of others he later gave vast fortunes to endow medical schools. 
One of Rockefeller’s closest advisors was Frederick T. Gates, a 
Baptist minister, who had read William Osler’s textbook, The Prin- 
ciples and Practice of Medicine (1892). Gates was mortified by “how 
few (human) diseases medicine could cure.” Gates convinced 
Rockefeller to invest heavily in scientific medicine as a worthy cause 
for mankind. 
The Rockefeller Institute for Medical Research was established in 
1901- it’s mission was medical and pharmaceutical research, clinical 
practice was strictly forbidden. Abraham Flexner’ brother, Simon 
was appointed director of the institute’s laboratories. Simon Flexner 
went on to isolate the Shigella bacteria as a cause of dysentery. 
Not all schools of thought were supportive of the move to “scien- 
tific medicine.” Many mourned the loss of the clinical focus of the 
old medical schools - in 1933 the well respected pathologist Sir An- 
drew MacPhail (1864-1938) complained: 
“I am well aware that in these days, when a student must be converted 
into a physiologist, a physicist, a chemist, a biologist, a pharmacologist 
and an electrician, there is no time to make a physician of him. This con- 
summation can only come after he has gone out into the world of sickness 
and suffering, unless indeed his mind is so bemused by the long process of 
education in those sciences, that he is forever excluded from the art of 
medicine... In that case he is destined for the laboratory, the professor's

144 
chair, or the consultant's office. What would have happened to Sydenham 
had he been put through this machinery is a problem in infinity which no 
human intelligence is competent to solve.” 
Once third party health insurance entered the scene, the patient 
was no longer the primary focus of the physician. The physician now 
seemingly had a bottomless pit of money available to them and all 
they had to do was learn the insurance codes and start sending in the 
bills - it worked! Physicians now had the perfect world - they were 
respected members of the power elite, even without royal blood lines; 
they had an endless stream of money collected from their customers 
in their behalf by the government (Medicare, Medicaid) and by pri- 
vate insurers; and the law treated doctors differently than they treated 
the general public. 
By the 1920s, doctors had a “license to kill,” they could “bury 
their mistakes,” they were to be sued for malpractice rather than 
be charged with manslaughter or murder when they killed a pa- 
tient; and even more importantly, doctors could crush their com- 
petitors socially, politically and financially without fear of re- 
prisals! They were politically vaccinated against commercial 
equality normally provided by the Sherman Antitrust Act - in 
effect and in their own minds doctors had become omnipotent 
gods. 
Standard Oil and “Ma” Bell were broken up, with the justifica- 
tion of the Sherman Antitrust Act to protect free flow of trade and 
promote competition - after all, competition amongst providers that 
stimulates better products, better pricing and better service, is good

In 1951, Ruben Rausing invented the “Tetra Pak” milk carton; 
soon after the glass milk bottle disappeared. 
Linus Pauling, an American chemist advanced the knowledge of 
proteins. He proposed that proteins consist of “polypeptide” chains 
(formed by smaller units called peptides). These chains contain a 
backbone of carbon and nitrogen atoms, to which other chemical 
units, including atoms of hydrogen and oxygen, are attached. Pauling 
showed how a polypeptide chain can coil up into a helix (3-D spiral) 
- weak bonds between the hydrogen and oxygen atoms in “succes- 
sive” turns of the spiral provide stability. 
In 1952, the first commercial jet service was introduced when 
BOAC flew passengers between London, England and Johannesburg, 
Republic of South Africa. 
In 1953, American James Watson and Englishman Francis Crick, 
found that DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) the “super molecule” that 
transmits biological information, has a “double-helical” structure, 
made of two intertwined spiral strands of linked chemical units called 
nucleotide bases. 
The 3-D structure was quickly accepted; Pauling had already pro- 
posed the double-helix structure for proteins two years earlier; it was 
sufficiently complex to store a great deal of information; and it was 
simple enough to copy when cells divide.

146 
In 1954, an electric pressure cooker (the original pressure cooker 
was the precursor of the steam engine in the 1700s) was widely mar- 
keted in the United States. 
Dr. Jonas Salk, head of the virus research laboratory at the Uni- 
versity of Pittsburgh, supervised the mass vaccination of American 
children with a chemically inactivated polio virus vaccine. More than 
200 children developed polio as a result of a production error (all 
cases of polio diagnosed in the U.S. after 1982 were caused by the 
oral polio vaccine). 
In 1955 a team of American surgeons successfully performed the 
first kidney transplant. 
The United States launches the USS Nautilus, the first nuclear 
powered submarine, producing a tactical advantage over the Soviets 
in the Cold War. 
In 1956, Dr. Werner Forssman, a German physician and Ameri- 
can Doctors Andre Cournand and Dickson Richards received the 
Nobel Prize in Medicine for developing a practical cardiac catheter 
method for diagnosing cardiovascular disease. Forssman performed 
the first cardiac catheter experiments on himself (1929) using a fluo- 
roscope to monitor the path of the catheter. 
Birth control pills are tested on a massive scale in Puerto Rico. 
Dr. Willem Kolff, a Dutch physician, developed an “artificial kidney” or dialysis machine, which was first employed in the United 
States. Blood from a patient's artery is diverted into the dialysis machine, which looks like a large rotating cylinder or drum. The blood 
enters a cellulose membrane, which is wrapped around the drum. As 
the drum rotates, the cellulose in submerged in a saline solution. The 
membrane allows metabolic wastes to pass out of the blood. The 
blood cells are too large to pass through the membrane. The cleansed 
blood is then pumped back into the patient.

wrote:

In 1958, BOAC initiated the first transatlantic jet service with

Dr. Ian Donald, a professor of gynecology at the University of

technology originally created to detect submarines. 
In 1959, only twenty years after the production and widespread 
use of antibiotics, some bacteria have begun to show resistance to 
antibiotics. It was discovered that bacteria possess small pieces of 
DNA called plasmids; the plasmids are credited with the resistant 
genes - they replicate themselves and the plasmids pass from one 
bacteria to another when the bacterial cells come into contact. 
In 1959, a Japanese fishing village on the shores of Minamata Bay 
was devastated by 2,500 people being hospitalized and more than 
700 babies being born with catastrophic central nervous system birth 
defects. 
The Minamata Bay disaster was attributed to industrial pollution 
with methyl mercury accumulation in the mud, algae and small fish 
in the bay; the medium sized fish ate the little fish; the large fish ate 
the medium sized fish; and humans ate the large carnivorous fish 
now heavily contaminated with mercury.

In 1966, Dr. Joel Wallach, an American veterinary comparative 
pathologist and conservationist, began performing autopsies for The 
Center for the Biology of Natural Systems. The center was estab- 
lished with a multimillion dollar grant from the National Institutes 
of Health and employed the facilities of the St. Louis Zoological Gar- 
dens, Shaw’s Botanical Gardens and the biology department at Wash- 
ington University, St. Louis. 
The project was brought into being as a direct result of the sud- 
den awareness of a possible world wide environmental meltdown as 
a result of industrial and agricultural pollution (i.e. - Minamata mer- 
cury disaster, DDT, etc.). Wallach was recruited as a comparative 
pathologist to look for captive wild species that might be ultra sensi- 
tive to pollution, species that could be used as “a canary in the mine,” 
that would provide an early warning system for a possible industrial 
or agricultural pollution crises.

The first home microwave ovens go on sale. The ovens work by 
bombarding food with ultra-short microwaves. When the microwaves 
strike the food, they cause the water molecules inside to vibrate millions of times per second which generates friction, which produces 
heat that cooks the food “from the inside out.” 
In 1968, pollution is blamed for the “biological death” of Lake 
Erie. Pollution, produced by industrial wastes, lead from leaded fu- 
els, litter and garbage are becoming more important as environmen- 
tal pollutants. Lake Erie, the fourth largest of the North American 
Great Lakes is listed as a “dead lake.” Industrial and municipal wastes 
had been dumped into the lake for decades, resulting in its waters 
being completely polluted. According to the U.S. Department of the 
Interior, 15,000,000 fish died each year in Lake Erie. 
In 1971, Dr. Linus Pauling, double Nobel Prize winner, has sup- 
ported the benefits of supplementing with large doses of vitamin C 
in his book, Vitamin C and the Common Cold. Pauling supported the 
large doses of vitamin C, both for fighting the common cold, but also 
for preventing cancer and heart disease. Many of the benefits of vita- 
min C arises from it’s role in the manufacture of collagen, which is 
involved in the formation of connective tissue, bone matrix, skin 
and blood vessels. Pauling recommends that individuals take 10 - 12 
grams of ascorbic acid or vitamin C each day.

has produced the “Lithotripter,” a machine that destroys kidney stones

In 1983, Dr. Joel Wallach finishes a 1,000 page reference book, 
The Diseases of Exotic Animals, published by W. B. Saunders, the book 
described therapies and the results of 13,000 autopsies of zoo ani- 
mals - he concluded “every animal and every human being that dies 
of natural causes dies of a nutritional deficiency disease.”

had performed more than 17,500 autopsies in more than 454 spe- 
cies of animals and 3,000 humans - again confirming that “every 
animal and every human being that dies of natural causes dies of a 
nutritional deficiency disease.” 
In 1992, an Earth Summit, the United Nations Conference on 
Environment and Development, was held in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil. 
It's aim was to examine the negative impact of world wide economic 
development. With 117 heads of state and representatives of 178 
nations attending the meeting, it was the largest gathering of world 
leaders in history. Issues discussed included global warming and mass 
destruction of the Amazonian rainforest, habitat for nearly half the 
world’ species of plants. Countries attending this meeting agreed to 
protect endangered species and to reduce carbon dioxide emissions. 
In 1993, the National Cancer Institute reported the results of a 
five year study on the protective value of antioxidants against cancer. 
The study was done in Henan Province of north-central China. 
After selecting 29,000 people between forty and sixty nine years 
of age, the participants were placed in one of several nutrient inves- 
tigation groups. Retinol, zinc, riboflavin, niacin, vitamin C, molyb- 
denum, selenium beta carotene and vitamin E were compared to a.

HELLS KITCHEN 153 
placebo. 
The investigators reported that, “vitamin C, retinol, zinc, riboflavin, molybdenum and niacin had no statistically significant (protective) effect on cancer deaths after five years.” 
The group that took the combination of beta carotene, vitamin E 
and selenium for five years had dramatically positive results: 13 percent fewer deaths from all cancers, 21 percent reduction in deaths 
from stomach cancer and a 9 percent decrease in deaths from all 
causes.

In 1996, a Scottish veterinary technician cloned a sheep; he 
“cloned” or copied a genetically identical sheep from a single cell 
taken from it. He transferred the nuclei from a sheep cell into unfer- 
tilized eggs from which the original nuclei had been removed. The 
eggs were replaced into the uterus of the original sheep, resulting in 
the birth of a lamb, named “Dolly.” This proved that adult mamma- 
lian cells contain functional versions of all the genes required to pro- 
duce an entire animal. 
In December 1996, Dr. Larry Clark, of the University of Arizona 
reported on a randomized double-blind study (the “gold standard” 
in medical rsearch) that followed 1,312 people for seven years. Half 
took 200 mcg of selenium daily, the remainder took a placebo. An 
analysis of the data showed that taking selenium slashed the occur- 
rence of prostate cancer by sixty nine percent; colorectal cancer, sixty 
four percent; and lung cancer, thirty nine percent. 
There are no drugs that will reduce the risk of cancer by one 
percent, yet doctors fail to recommend selenium as a cancer risk 
lowering approach for people with a high cancer risk - there is just 
too little money involved, so recommending selenium is just not worth 
their time.

American Medical Association, that the long held dogma, that high 
dietary fat intake increased the risk of cancer, was false. In a com-

twenty percent of their calories as fat against those that consumed 
more than fifty percent ofjheir calories as fat - the data revealed that 
there was no measurable difference in the rate of breast cancer re-

attributed to Vioxx prescriptions. 
Merck and the FDA should have withdrawn Vioxx from the mar-

Vioxx doubled the risk of heart attacks. Twenty million Americans 
used Vioxx, which racked up $2.5 billion in annual sales.

David Graham, FDA drug safety reviewer of twenty years, says 
that the American public is “virtually defenseless” with regards to

went on to say, “the FDA has an inherent conflict of interest that

2. Crestor, an anti-cholesterol drug. Graham says, “the govern-

To protect the American public from the repressive anti vitamin 
and anti mineral supplement policies of the medical profession and 
the FDA, we decided to change our approach. For decades we had

available at the present time. We decided to test the scope of the First

The Feedlot Syndrome 
The glut of grain produced by world wide peasant agriculture 
and large scale commercial farming has resulted in a series of mixed 
blessings and the opening of a modern “Pandora’s Box” of “new dis- 
eases.” More food has allowed greater numbers of humans to prolif- 
erate, survive and flourish; however, without the necessary system- 
atic programs of essential nutrient supplementation, hundreds of 
nutritional diseases have become epidemic, many are pandemic, 
immune systems are compromised; the appearance of unfamiliar dis- 
eases has produced mass misery; and extended draught has resulted 
in catastrophic famine. 
Even though the industrial revolution and the electrification of 
the world and their subsequent financial and scientific prosperity 
lifted and advanced some human cultures to dizzy heights, there was 
a painful and costly world-wide price. Industrialization has spread 
poverty, unsanitary conditions, pollution, degenerative diseases (i.e. 
- obesity, diabetes, Metabolic Syndrome, osteoporosis, cardiovascu- 
lar diseases, Alzheimer’s disease, etc.) and the appearance of “new” 
infectious diseases (i.e. - HIV, Ebola, Lasa, Marburg Fever, etc.,) that 
were historically isolated in the jungles of Africa and Asia, are now 
efficiently spread by modern jet transportation far beyond the speed 
and distance capabilities of a mosquito’ wings.

166 
One of the unexpected observations that visitors to the United States 
report is that rich people are skinny and poor people are obese. The 
lower your income, the fatter you are! One out of every four of the 
people with below poverty incomes is obese, by contrast in house- 
holds earning more than $67,000 per year show an obesity rate of 
one out of six - minorities show an obesity rate of one out of three. 
Wealthy people tend to be more educated and educated people 
tend to eat more protein and take more supplements when com- 
pared with the economically stressed. 
If one disregards all other considerations, the “bang for the buck” 
when it comes to calories out of the grocery store, is carbohydrates. 
High carbohydrate, high sugar foods are cheap, tasty, they fill you up 
and they are commonly available. 
In 2004, the University of California, Berkeley, reviewed the diet 
of 4,700 adults and found that soft drinks provide more than seven 
percent of the of the average daily adult intake of calories - the largest 
single source of calories in the American diet. 
Together, soft drinks, alcohol, and sweets (candy), including past- 
ries, account for more than twenty five percent of adult’s calories. 
Add fruit drinks and salty, high carb snacks (chips, cake and cook- 
ies) and the numbers rise to thirty percent. 
Twenty eight percent of Americans live in what is referred to as a 
“food desert,” - urban zones where grocery stores are a twenty to 
thirty minute drive away. People simply go to the local convenience 
store, even though prices are higher and the availability of fresh foods 
is lower. 
The city of Philadelphia initiated a $100 million project to build 
ten supermarkets in the “hoods,” because “you have people who are 
literally distanced out of healthy diets,” says Troy Blanchard, a soci- 
ology professor from Mississippi State University. 
The preached and promoted remedies to obesity in America “are 
very middle class,” says Adam Drewnowski, director of the Center 
for Public Health Nutrition at the University of Washington. “They 
tell you, seek a healthy diet and exercise. Well, if you’re working two 
jobs and living in a trailer, you're in no mood to get home and make 
a salad.”

With all of the statistics accumulated, with all of the current busi- 
ness, political and health interest in obesity, how can it be that we 
(Dr. Ma Lan and I) are the only ones who have figured it out! 
On the world stage, we have traded the reputation for being “ugly 
Americans” to being “fat Americans,” people from the other industri- 
alized nations have gone from being “westerners,” to “fat westerners.” 
This process of becoming fat gradually occurred over a fifty to sixty 
year period as we shifted from wood stoves to electrical, gas and 
propane kitchens. 
This fattening process was initially slow and insidious, no one 
paid much attention, after all - we were just well fed Americans. 
However, something much more sinister than simply overeating and 
having made poor food choices had happened. 
Something happened that had guaranteed we were all going to 
get fat - we had been placed in a virtual feedlot. American adults 
weighed on average, twenty five pounds more in 2004 than they did 
in 1960. American children weigh nine to ten pounds more today 
than they did in the 1960s. American teens weigh on the average 
twelve to sixteen pounds more today than they did in the 1960s. 
According to the National Health and Nutritional Examination 
Survey, which is considered the gold standard of assessing American's 
weight, sixty five percent of Americans are overweight and “just 
going on a diet is not going to work.” 
Survey statistics show: 
Women, ages 20 - 74: weighed an average of 164.3 pounds in 2002, 
compared with 140.2 pounds in 1960. 
Men, ages 20 - 74: weighed an average of 191 pounds in 2002, 
compared with 166.3 pounds in 1960.

Girls, ages 12 - 17: weighed an average of 130 pounds in 2002,

Americans were placed in a classical “feedlot” environment, rich 
with a constant stream of dietary carbohydrate calories, low energy 
outputs and a diet almost devoid of minerals (which ensured a rav- 
enous appetite) - the result is what we call “The Feedlot Syndrome.” 
A feedlot is a farm or ranch factory operation designed solely for 
the purpose of intensive growing and fattening of animals for human 
food. Raising prime beef successfully, requires a steady stream of carbohydrate, protein and fat calories in the proper ratios specifically 
designed for fattening. 
The intense weight gain, high carbohydrate diet must be accompanied by optimal mixes of nutrients designed to encourage weight 
gain and drive the animal to eat with an endless and fervent hunger. 
Feed, based on corn or other carbohydrates, is made available in 
a steady stream. Beef cattle will grow from a forty pound calf to 900 
- 1,100 pounds in about a year under optimal feedlot conditions. 
Growth rates slow at about one year of age and attempts to add 
more weight or fat become energy and economically inefficient as 
animals reach maturity. As a result, animals are usually killed for 
meat at young ages; “heavy” beef animals that can reach over 2,000 
pounds at slaughter, produce large prime expensive steaks and are 
inefficient in terms of calories required to add the extra weight. 
Meat produced in a feedlot is specifically designed for “prime” or 
“deluxe” upscale dining. Meat produced in a feedlot is heavily 
“marbled” and thickly covered with fat. Feedlot meat is tender and 
very tasty - it is the form of meat that is demanded by discerning 
dinners. 
In 1920, Americans ate 165 pounds of red meat, poultry and fish 
per person per year; in 2002 we ate 230 pounds of animal protein 
per person per year. 
The calories in the “modern” western diet, especially following 
the Korean War (1950s), primarily came from grain and carbohy- 
drate based meals (i.e. - pasta, bread, dry cereals, potatoes, rice, soft 
drinks, fruits and juices, etc.). Doctors demanded that people eat _

Pica, Cribbing and the Munchies 
At least 30,000 years ago humans, driven by a salt hunger, began 
to mix wood ashes (wood ashes were used to cut the more valuable 
salt 3:1 to as much as 10:1 to extend its volume) with their dietary 
salt, add them directly to food as “culinary” ashes and additionally 
mixed the balance of each days production of ashes into their gar- 
dens as a mineral rich fertilizer, which resulted in greater fertility, 
survival rates of children, longer lives and stronger skeletons that 
survived the millennia. 
The use of wood ashes by humans appeared at about the same 
time amongst all human cultures world wide in a parallel develop- 
ment. Salt and mineral hunger is not a newly acquired drive - it is as 
old as man himself. 
Over the millennia, little changed in the human habit of con-

176 
suming wood ash minerals as “culinary” ash added to their food and 
additionally using them as fertilizer until 3:00 p.m. on Monday Sep- 
tember 4, 1882, when the first commercial electric generating plant 
was brought on line on Pearl Street in New York by a Thomas Edison 
technician. 
By 1950, almost all American urban homes were heated with 
electricity, gas, oil, coal or propane; almost every American home- 
maker cooked on an electric, gas or propane stove; and ninety per- 
cent of rural American homes were heated with or cooked with elec- 
tricity, gas or propane. 
The new kitchen conveniences were successfully marketed by 
trumpeting, “the end of kitchen drudgery,” “no more messy stove or 
furnace ashes to take outdoors.” 
About the same time wood fuels transitioned into electrical, gas 
and propane fuel, the home vegetable and corn garden became a 
labor of love for a few hobbyists as opposed to supplying a signifi- 
cant part of the family food supply as it had for more than 30,000 
years. 
The results of these technological advances and cultural shifts 
were catastrophic: 
1) People moved off the land, left their traditional back yard 
gardens behind and purchased food from large grocery chains 
that obtained their products from commercially grown sources 
that used commercial NPK (nitrogen, phosphorus, potassium) 
fertilizers. 
2) The traditional source of minerals (“culinary” wood ashes) 
necessary to meet our daily need for essential minerals was 
suddenly and even proactively replaced with “clean” fuels that 
left no “waste,” sooty pots or messy byproduct (ashes). 
3) The availability of and rate of consumption of empty sugar 
sweetened calories (i.e. - breads, pasta, potatoes, snacks, soft 
drinks, etc.) rose dramatically.

HELLS KITCHEN 177 
4) Physical activity and personal expenditure of work energy 
was significantly reduced. 
Being overweight and being obese is synonymous with being an 
American, however, other industrialized nations are not far behind 
in their rates of obesity. The “pica,” “cribbing,” and the “munchies” 
classic behaviors are always associated with being overweight and 
obese. These cravings are solely a reflection of mineral deficiencies - 
neither vitamin deficiencies, protein deficiencies, fat deficiencies!or 
calorie deficiencies will produce the munchies behavior - only min- 
eral deficiencies will produce the munchies! 
The snack food and fast food industries are aware of the relation- 
ship between mineral deficiencies and cravings (i.e. - sugar, salt, 
chocolate, carbohydrates, etc.), mineral deficiencies and binging, 
mineral deficiencies and the munchies and they use these behaviors 
to their financial advantage. 
The snack food industry knew that mineral deficiencies produce 
a psychotic eating drive just as the giant tobacco companies knew 
that cigarettes were addictive. 
The munchies behaviors are often extreme in small children 
and teen-agers because these age groups have the highest need 
for energy and minerals to support growth and development. As 
a result, snack food and soft drink companies have paid millions 
of dollars to be the sole suppliers of snacks and soft drinks in 
large county and municipal school districts - the kids pay for the 
advertising by buying the sweetened or salted treats and they 
become addicted to the brand while they are frantically looking 
for minerals. 
Historically, the consumption of salt to satisfy the pica behavior 
was of value because salt contained some traces of other minerals 
and was mixed with wood ashes to extend the more valuable salt. 
Today, salt consumption, although contrary to popular belief, is not 
in of itself harmful; it does, however, present the problem of allow- 
ing our bodies to think we are also getting a wider supply of essential 
minerals than we really are. 
“Cribbing” is a name given to a particular form of pica in domes-

178 
tic animals. A good husbandry man knows when a horse or cow 
“cribs” (chews or gnaws on a wooden feed box, wagon rails, fence, 
hitching post or barn door, or eats dirt); the animal really has a crav- 
ing for minerals that are deficient in their diets. Mineral starved ani- 
mals will at first consume large quantities of supplemental minerals 
until their cravings are satisfied, they will automatically reduce their 
level of consumption to a maintenance level. 
“Salt appetite” is very striking in both pregnant animals and hu- 
mans. Bizarre cravings are legendary in the pregnant human (i.e. - 
corn starch, pickles, dirt, wood ashes, plaster, clay, etc.). From antiq- 
uity, the descriptions of cravings or “pica” are associated with preg- 
nant women. The Hawaiian King Kamehameha’s mother Queen 
Kekuiapoiwa had cravings for eyeballs, although she specifically 
wanted chief's eyes, she was given the eyes of sharks to eat. 
“Geophagia,” or earth eating is very common in mineral deficient 
pregnant humans. 
Pica, or perverted appetite or irresistible cravings is character- 
ized by the eating of bizarre or unsuitable nonfood substances (or 
fattening non foods such as high sugar and high carbohydrate snacks 
and soft drinks) has been a curiosity for philosophers, doctors and 
priests for thousands of years. Pica as a symptom of mineral deficien- 
cies is universal in the world and has been reported in animals and 
humans of all age groups. 
In modern times the easy availability of candy, sugared-high calorie 
snacks, high sugar soft drinks, juices and salted junk foods of all 
types has led to obesity when people feel the obsessive, almost psy- 
chotic deep cravings of pica - “I have to eat and I have to eat it all!” 
People wouldn't crave or consume the empty calories found 
in snack foods if they were properly nourished with the essen- 
tial minerals. No amount of advertising or give away coupons 
and gimmicks would drive humans to constantly consume snacks 
and junk food - when one is well nourished with minerals, junk 
foods and snacks are just not interesting. 
When people are deficient in minerals “the call to eat” is an 
endless incessant obsession that can only be subdued by an 
individual's will for but a short time - eventually the mineral —

HELLS KITCHEN 179 
deficient individual will wake up in the middle of the night and 
eat an entire twenty pound ham or an entire German chocolate 
cake - in the end pica will always win the battle of the binge! 
A doctoral thesis on pica by Agustus Fridericus Mergiletus (1701) 
begins with the definition of pica: 
“The malady pica is called ‘kitta’ in Greek. It’s derivation comes from 
the name of the common magpie because the bird (“pica” is the Latin name 
for “magpie”) itself is believed to suffer from some malady (mineral defi- 
ciencies, yes, even wild animals suffer from mineral deficiencies) because 
it flits from tree to tree constantly seeking food or because it enjoys all sorts 
of foods.” 
A catalogue of bizarre examples of pica is found in Mergiletus’ 
thesis. In men, he recorded one individual who ate leather, wood, 
nestlings and live mice; a second consumed woolen garments, leather, 
a live cat, and some mice; a third ate cattails and decomposed human 
bodies infested with maggots. 
In Mergiletus’ female subjects he recorded women who ate hu- 
man flesh including one particularly horrible lady - “she lured chil- 
dren to her home with sweets, killed them and pickled them for her 
daily fare.” 
The most common description of pica by Mergiletus were of 
women’s desire for mud and mortar scrapped from walls (just like 
modern children who eat caulking and lead paint) - we have often 
said that children who eat lead paint are screaming for minerals for 
their mineral starved bodies - give them minerals and they won't eat 
lead paint. Girls ate their own hair, cotton, thread, raw grain, starch 
and lizards. 
Mergiletus also described pica in animals including cats that ate 
wood ash and pregnant hunting dogs that ate unusual objects. 
Cooper (1957) in her classic report on pica refers to several an- 
cient and medieval writers who emphasized the occurrences of pica 
in pregnant women: Aetios noted that pregnant women crave vari- 
ous and odd foods, some salty and some acid; some, he said, “crave 
for sand, oyster shells and wood ashes.” Aetios recommended a diet

180 
including “fruits, green vegetables, pig’s feet, fresh fish and old tawny 
fragrant wine.” 
Boezo (1638) noted that pica occurred most often in pregnant 
women. Boezo viewed pica as a physiological problem, and is the 
first to mention iron preparations as a treatment for pica - he sug- 
gested “one and one half scruples of iron dross taken for many days 
as wonderfully beneficial for men and women.” He also noted “the 
case of a virgin who was accustomed to devour salt in great quanti- 
ties from which chronic behavior she developed diarrhea and wast- 
ing. 
Christiani of Frankfurt (1691) reported a woman who ate 1,400 
salt herring during her pregnancy. LeConte (1846) suggested, “that 
animals eating earth do so because of a want of inorganic elements.” 
In modern times, the substances frequently reported as eaten as 
a result of pica behavior in humans includes paper, metallic gum 
wrappers, ice, dirt, coal, clay, chalk, starch, baking powder, pebbles, 
wood, plaster, paint, chimney soot, road tar, hair, human and animal 
feces, cloth and carbohydrates! 
Because of social constraints on our public behavior, most people 
under public scrutiny who display pica will eat sugar, snack foods, 
larger servings of food, soft drinks and smoke tobacco when they are 
really craving minerals. 
Commonly, mineral deficient people will stand with the re- 
frigerator door open, blankly staring into the refrigerator after 
consuming a 10,000 calorie, eighteen course holiday or celebra- 
tion meal. When asked what they are doing, they will respond, 
“I don’t know, I’m just looking for something!” It is not more 
calories they are looking for - it’s the minerals that their defi- 
cient body is screaming for that drives them to the refrigerator! 
Many individuals who smoke, drink excess alcohol, abuse pre- 
scription drugs or use street drugs and become addicted to sugar 
laden soft drinks are attempting to satisfy a deep, nagging and un- 
derlying craving that is caused by a mineral deficiency. 
Ice eating (pagophagia) is also commonly observed in iron defi- 
cient children and adults. 
Parasites have been reported as a cause of pica - Ankylostomia-

HELVS KITCHEN 181 
sis, ascariasis, oxyuriasis and others have been blamed. Parasites of 
course can cause anemia and malnutrition by direct blood sucking 
from the host or by competing with the host for food in the gut. The 
resulting anemia is in fact perceived by the body as an iron defi- 
ciency resulting in pica. 
Pica is commonly seen in children, teens, pregnant women and 
the obese; however, David Livingston observed that “both slaves and 
the gentry developed pica” in Africa. In Africa, pica is viewed by 
tribal peoples as a magical superstitious behavior. 
Henrock (1831) attributed pica to a “paucity of good blood anda 
lack of proper nutrition.” Waller (1874) reported that David Livingston 
observed many cases of clay and earth eating (geophagia), a frequent 
form of pica in pregnant women. 
Orr and Gilka (1931) and de Castro (1952) recognized that “ed- 
ible earths might be rich in sodium, iron and calcium.” Gelford (1945) 
reported that pica was common in Kenya amongst African tribes 
(Kikuyu) living mainly on a vegetarian diet; but absent in those people 
eating diets rich in animal flesh, blood and bones (Massai). 
The reduced pica behavior in high animal protein diets, that 
naturally contain iron and phosphorus can partially explain why 
high protein, modest fat and low carbohydrate diets work to re- 
duce weight. We have found that by adding the complete plant 
derived mineral supplement to the weight loss and weight man- 
agement programs, the weight loss will be more dramatic for all 
and will be maintained. 
Dickens and Ford reported that twenty five percent of children 
eat dirt. We believe that mineral deficiency pica occurs in American 
children at a much higher rate than twenty five percent; we believe it 
occurs at eighty to 100 percent, however, because of the social con- 
straints against eating dirt, kids will instead consume more soft drinks, 
more juice, more French fries, more cookies, more cake, more pasta 
and generally more calories as a result of their mineral deficiencies. 
This mineral deficiency driven pica, not playing outdoors, watch- 
ing TV and playing video games, is the cause of obesity in kids. Give 
children and teens more minerals to replace the wood ashes that 
they gave up when their grandparents and parents installed electric,

Cooper (1957) reported a twenty one percent rate of pica in

Lanzkowsky (1959) reported that twelve children with pica had

McDonald and Marshall (1964) reported on twenty five children 
who ate sand. They divided the group in half - they gave one group 
iron and the other group saline (salt solution). After three to four 
months 11 of the 13 children given iron had lost their pica behavior 
compared with only three of the 12 given saline.

Reynolds et al (1968) reported that thirty eight people with ane-

pica; twenty two of twenty five had their symptoms of pica disappear 
after correcting the iron depletion. 
Woods and Weisinger (1970) reproduced pagophagia experimen- 
tally in rats by withdrawing blood. The pagophagia in the anemic 
rats was cured when the anemia was cured. It continues to be of 
interest to note that pica and cribbing behaviors are not produced by 
vitamin deficiencies. 
Iwo thirds of the 153 pregnant women studied by Taggart (1961) 
developed cravings. The most common craving was for fruit, pick- 
les, blood pudding, licorice, potato chips, cheese and kippers. A cray- 
ing for sweets, vegetables, nuts and sweet pickles came in second

Phosphate appetite was described by LeVaillant (1796) as the 
anxious search by cattle in phosphate deficient South African pas- 
tures for discarded dog bones (osteophagia); they also chewed on 
wood (pica) and each others horns. 
Bone chewing (osteophagia, a form of pica) has been reported in 
many wild species of herbivores including reindeer, caribou, red deer, 
camels, giraffe, elephant, and wildebeest. We have seen elephant eat- 
ing limestone road-beds and large termite heaps as ready and avail- 
able sources of minerals. 
It was demonstrated that calcium deficient weanling rats will 
consume large amounts of lead acetate solution (even though they 
may taste bad) when compared with calcium fed controls. The Ori- 
ental diet tends to be low in calcium, leading them to habitually and 
preferentially eat bones (when Ma Lan and I were courting she ate 
the bones and gave me the fillets) partially leached of calcium with 
vinegar. 
Magnesium deficient animals will crave and eat excess salt. Mag- 
nesium tastes bad enough that animals will die of magnesium defi- 
ciency initiated seizures rather than consume a pure magnesium 
supplement. It has been suggested that recovery from a magnesium 
deficiency can be a very unpleasant process, enough so that the bad 
taste of magnesium supplements normally required for recovery ex-

184 
ceeds the unpleasant features of the deficiency. 
A natural potassium deficiency is highly unlikely in nature, as all 
grains, fruits and vegetables tend to be rich in potassium (NPK fertilizer) - a potassium deficiency can be found in individuals given diuretics and individuals whose only vegetable intake is French fries 
and ketchup. 
Potassium deficient rats are hyper alert, hyper active and tend to 
lick everything including their metal cage, lab equipment, each other 
and urine puddles (beer and urine have a similar taste). It is also 
interesting to note that even with a severe potassium deficiency laboratory rats have a strong craving for sodium chloride (salt). 
Chromium and vanadium deficiencies are manifested by thirst 
for liquids (soft drinks, juices, alcohol, etc.), with water being the 
last choice and an obsessive hunger for carbohydrates of all types. 
The “munchies,” cravings for alcohol and candy cravings (Regan’s 
cravings for jelly beans), especially chocolate, are sure signs of a chromium and vanadium deficiency. 
Salt (NaCl) deficiency occurs commonly, and is oftentimes caused 
by ignorant and over zealous doctors who demand that “their” patients reduce or eliminate salt intake. The medical profession must 
think that humans are dumber than cows! The first thing that a farmer 
or rancher puts in the pasture for their livestock is a salt block (and 
they have been doing it for tens of thousands of years) - the animals 
have free choice access to salt and other minerals in uncontrolled 
and unlimited amounts. Aristotle noted in his Historia Animalicem 
VIII, that “sheep are healthier when they are given salt.” 
Where it was rare, salt was traded ounce for ounce for gold, brides 
or slaves. Salt and salt rich clays mixed with wood or kelp ashes were 
the first mineral food supplements consciously employed by man 
since the beginning of time and before recorded history - pica did 
not begin with the first appearance of snacks - pica began with the 
first man. 
The Roman statesman Cassiodorus was quite observant when he 
said, “Some seek not gold, but there lives not a man who does not 
need salt.” 
The production of salt as a food supplement for humans and

HELLS KITCHEN 185 
beasts is as old as civilization itself. Salt was produced by evaporat- 
ing sea water from shallow man made ponds and by mining rock salt 
from large land locked deposits. 
Early villages sprang up around salt springs, humans were at- 
tracted by wild herbivores that were obligated to come daily to the 
springs to satisfy their driving need and hunger for salt and minerals. 
To obtain salt from these dilute springs, hunters kept wood fires con- 
tinuously burning to boil away the water; these salt-boiling peoples 
activities were recorded as far back as the Neolithic Ages. 
Whole forests were burned from Tyrol in the Alps, Moselle, and 
Franche-Count, France; the Soale and Lueburg, Germany, and 
Droitwich, England to support this salt production industry. 
Before the Norman invasion of England, salt pans of Cheshire 
and Worchestershire (famous for it’s sauce) supplied England and 
Gaul, going south to the Thames River with the subsequent estab- 
lishment of London. 
From the 9th to the 14th centuries peat was soaked in sea water, 
dried, burnt and the minerals of the resultant ash were then extracted 
with sea water; many millions of tons of peat were harvested annu- 
ally for this mineral/salt dietary supplement production process for 
the commercial trade. 
Mesopotamian towns specialized in the salt production industry 
and transported salt up the Tigris and Euphrates Rivers. Jericho (8000 
B.C.), near the Dead Sea and the salt mountain of Mo are the oldest 
known agricultural communities that participated in the salt trade. 
The human salt hunger (pica) was also an important force in the 
African slave trade as captured children from warring tribes were 
sold into slavery in exchange for salt. In other parts of salt poor Af- 
rica, humans developed the practice of drinking cattle blood or urine 
to obtain salt. 
The residents of the Sierra Leone coast gave all they possessed, 
including their wives and children in exchange for salt - because salt 
is an absolute requirement for life and because salt is not equally 
distributed on earth and coveted by the have-nots - it is said by Afri- 
can tribesmen that, “He who has salt has war.” 
The modern day human salt hunger is used by the American and

186 
international snack industry to drive their sales. Heavily salted po- 
tato chips, corn puffs, popcorn, salsa, bacon, French fries and pizza 
dominate snack and fast food purchases; heavily sugared snacks, soft 
drinks, cakes, cookies and candy come in a strong second. 
The child, preteen and teen age infatuation with snack foods, 
juices and soft drinks, drugs,smoking and alcohol appear to be the 
result of pica, because kids are so mineral deficient they are driven to 
seek out something; anything to put in their mouth that might sat- 
isfy the irresistible craving. If the available choices taste better (salt or 
sugar), more will automatically be consumed and obesity is guaran- 
teed. 
When people stop smoking they will almost always gain weight 
because their mineral deficiencies were not dealt with, their cravings 
that had been “managed” by the use of tobacco are then no longer 
under control - the resulting cravings and munchies that appear when 
smokers give up tobacco will result in weight gain and obesity. 
We humans happily replaced our historical source of “culi-
