Hunza Land 
Mineral Premix: Culinary Minerals 
Since before written history, wood ashes (plant derived minerals) 
were the most universally available fertilizer. Wood ashes were pre- 
dictable; they were a fine particle sized mineral dust and an immedi- 
ately available source of plant food that was produced everyday in 
the human’ daily life as a byproduct of cooking and warming our 
homes. 
The systematic cultivation and fertilization of land to commer- 
cially produce food was first written about in 4000 B.C. in China. 
Chinese farmers used “organic material, wood ashes and silt reclaimed 
from streams and rivers that had been eroded from the hills and 
mountains to fertilize gardens and field crops.” 
Wood ashes were universally available and contained a wide va- 
riety of plant derived minerals that were easily available to cultivated 
garden plants and directly available to humans as a source of nutri- 
tional minerals when mixed with table salt and applied to our food 
as “culinary” minerals.

190 
“Culinary” ashes were of great and absolute importance to 
human survival. Wood, peat and kelp ashes were added to hu- 
man food since before written history. The “munchies,” pica and 
cravings didn’t begin with the first potato chip or Hershey’s bar - 
the munchies began with the first man. From the first man to 
modern man we were and still are dependant on plant source 
minerals. 
Food preparation, in it’s earliest and simplest form, was raw meat 
or crudely “cooked” in fire. Food was immediately eaten or preserved 
briefly by cooking, drying in the sun or by being salted. 
Salt was a valuable and cherished substance and it was traded 
ounce for ounce for gold and slaves. As a result, salt was not 
squandered by simply eating it, it was mixed, “cut’ and diluted 
with wood, peat or kelp ashes. And so with the simple act of 
“extending” salt with “culinary” ashes, humans, from the begin- 
ning of time, unknowingly acquired essential supplemental min- 
erals. 
Fat Ladies of Malta 
Maltese figurines of “fat ladies” date from the Upper Paleolithic 
era (25,000 years ago) to the earliest evidence of metal-using cul- 
tures in the Neolithic era. Some of these “fat lady” figurines have 
been found in western Europe, however, the greatest numbers of 
these figurines have been found in Egypt, the Levant, Turkey, Greece, 
Cyprus and the Balkans. The most detailed of the “fat lady” figurines 
were found on the islands of Malta dating back to 3000 B.C and 
8000 B.C. in Catalhoyuk, Turkey. 
The most dramatic of the “fat lady” figures is the “sleeping lady” 
of the Hypogeum. This relatively small statue depicts an obese lady 
lying on her side on a highly detailed woven bed. 
These “fat lady” figurines have been interpreted by archaeolo- 
gists to be the subject of religious cults who worshipped “mother 
goddesses” to ask for agricultural and human fertility, In Malta, the 
worship of obese goddesses grew into an all consuming pursuit. 
Geological evidence shows that between 5000 and 7000 years

HELVS KITCHEN 191 
ago, Malta was a heavily wooded island. The first immigrants quickly 
and ruthlessly cleared and deforested the fragile island ecosystem of 
its original plant life. The inhabitants used the wood as fuel to boil 
sea water to produce salt, cook their food and to heat their dwellings. 
Soil erosion soon plagued Malta, stripping the top soil and 
accessable nutritional minerals - crop yields dropped precipitously. 
Prehistoric Maltese agriculture was carried out in an environment 
that had already been deforested. Studies of land snails as early as 
4000 B.C. and fossilized pollen as early as 3600 B.C. confirm that 
Malta was already deforested and treeless - no trees - no wood; no 
wood ashes - no “culinary ashes - no ashes to cut salt and no ashes to 
put in the gardens and fields as fertilizer. 
By the year 3000 B.C., Malta was affected by catastrophic eco- 
logical changes - soil erosion and evironmental degradation effected 
the mineral values of their food grains and vegetables. 
Malta became an island in trouble (i.e. - high population and an 
ecological and agricultural collapse) - village communities struggled 
to maintain earlier standards of living and produce their historical 
cultural foods and diet. 
Wood ashes that had been plentiful in the early days of immigra- 
tion, were no longer avilable for “culinary ashes,” cutting salt or 
ferilizing the gardens. 
The loss of pasture to wind and water erosion resulted in a sig- 
nificant reduction in the consumption of animal protein and an in- 
crease in the consumption of grain as a large portion of their diet. 
Low mineral intakes combined with high carbohydrate intakes threw 
the Maltese into the Feedlot Syndrome and the production of the “fat 
ladies.” 
By 2000 B.C. the entire agricultural industry in Malta vanished. 
The lack of the wood ash byproduct of heating and cooking pro- 
‘duced mineral deficient grains, vegetables, fruit and a loss of culi- 
nary ashes. Wood ashes were the minerals that were left over when 
the carbon of the wood was burned. 
The lack of the nutritional minerals produced pica, cribbing, 
cravings, binge eating and the munchies. The munchies, along with 
the high carbohydrate diet, produces the Feedlot Syndrome - ergo:

192 
obesity! 
Prehistoric Malta was a perfect model for us to understand obe- 
sity, diabetes and the Metabolic Syndrome epidemic in America and 
the pandemic in the industrialized nations of the world. 
Unfortunately the early interpretations of the Maltese “fat ladies” 
were made by archaeologists whose expertise was limited to ancient 
cultures and religions. They failed to recognize the catastrophic re- 
sults (obesity, diabetes and the Metabolic Syndrome) of a wood ash 
deficient culture! 
The interest in substances and materials that would increase crop 
yields dates back to antiquity. The early Greek philosophers pro- 
posed the amazingly modern concept that plants are derived from a 
combination of chemicals. 
Democritus of Abdera (460 - 360 B.C.) proposed, what would 
come to be considered, “the first writings on the atomic theory.” 
“Mother earth, when fructified by rain, gives birth to crops for the 
nourishment of men and beast. But that which came from earth must re- 
turn to earth (from ashes to ashes) and that which came from air (re- 
turned) to air. Death, however, does not destroy matter, but only breaks up 
the union of it’s elements, which are then recombined into other forms.” 
By contrast, Aristotle (384 - 322 B.C.), the tutor and mentor of 
Alexander the Great, believed that all material constituents were de- 
rived from the mutual convertibility of the four elements: earth, wa- 
ter, fire and air - a concept that was believed into the Medieval period 
and beyond. Aristotle assumed that, “plants assimilated organic mat- 
ter through the roots,” the progenitor of what has been known as the 
“humus theory of plant nutrition.” 
The incorrect belief that plants take up organic matter through 
the roots can be understood in light of the ancient observation that 
organic matter, particularly manure, compost and plant residue (ash) 
benefit plant growth. 
Pliny, the Roman wrote, “It is universally agreed by all writers 
that there is nothing more beneficial than to turn up a crop of lu- 
pines, before they have podded, either with the plow or the fork, or

HELLS KITCHEN 193 
else to cut them and bury them in heaps at the roots of trees and 
(grape) vines.” 
At 343 B.C. deserters from Alexander the Great's army established 
the Hunzukut culture in the Hunza Valley (19,500 foot elevation) on 
Mount Rakaposhi (25,550 feet elevation - the third highest moun- 
tain in the world). From the beginning of their written history 2,500 
years ago, the Hunzas employed “culinary” ashes to extend table salt 
and used the remainder as fertilizer in their manufactured soil at an 
elevation of almost three times the timberline level. 
In St. Matthew 13:3, Jesus, spoke in parables to the multitudes 
using the simple language of farmers and shepherds: 
“Behold, a sower went forth to sow; 
And when he sowed, some seeds fell by the wayside, and the fowls came 
and devoured them up: 
Some fell upon stony places, where they had not much earth: and forth- 
with they srung up, because they had no deepness of earth: 
And when the sun was up, they were scorched; and because they had no 
root, they withered away. 
And some fell among thorns; and the thorns sprung up, and choked them: 
But others fell into good ground, and brought forth fruit, some a hundred- 
fold, some sixtyfold, some thirtyfold.” 
Jesus knew that healthy well fertilized “good ground” produced 
good crops and that by giving his message to farmers and shepherds 
as a parable in their simple language, that they would hear and un- 
derstand his deeper message. 
In 10 A.D., the Roman, Lucius Junius Moderatur Columella, 
stressed the need to apply fertilizer so that, “the soil will not be tired 
and doesn’t age when it receives fertilizer, and the farmer can have 
larger yields when the soil is supported by frequent and appropriate 
fertilization.” 
But in Europe, it would not be until the 18th and 19th centuries 
that the idea of using animal and human feces as a source of humus 
was discussed systematically and promoted by German, Austrian and 
French scholars. However, in the early years of the 19th century it

194 
was discovered first by Spiengel and then by Liebig, that it was not 
the humus as such, but rather the minerals in the humus that nour- 
ished plants. 
Liebig demonstrated that potassium is one of the major essential 
nutrients required by plants - potassium had long been known as 
potash, because it was extracted from the ash left in iron pots after 
the burning of wood, straw and other plant materials. 
“Culinary” ashes were used by all humans before the Hunzas, 
and have continually been used by all humans after the Hunzas. So it 
was with the pre-Columbian Indians. In the 13th century, the use of 
“culinary” ashes was recorded in North America by anthropologists. 
In 1588, Thomas Harriat (1560-1621), tutor to Sir Walter Ra- 
leigh, was appointed “geographer” for his second Virginia expedition 
- he soon published in Hakluyt’s Voyages on North Carolina Indians 
and how they raised corn. He described mountain spinach five foot 
high and how the Indians made a “fine broth from the seeds, and 
from the stalks of the herb, they produced a kind of salt by burning 
it to ashes, they then season their broth with it. This is the only salt 
they know, and they enrich their fields by burning weeds after dry- 
ing in the sun. This they do to save themselves the labor of carrying 
it away, rather to enrich better the ground.” 
While the conscious use of manure continued in the Medieval 
times, there were no recorded theoretical contributions to plant nu- 
trition until the Renaissance. Bernard Palissy (1510 - 1589), consid- 
ered by most to be the founder of the study of agricultural chemistry, 
stated: 
“The modern concept that the purpose of manuring was to replace 
substances lost (from the soil) by crop removal. Manure is carried to the 
field for the purpose of restoring to the latter a part of what had been 
removed proceeding, thus you will restore to the soil the same substances 
that have been removed by previous crops and which following crops will 
regain to their advantage.” 
Until the 1700s, scientists believed “that the life of animals was 
dependent on the consumption of food and that plants derived their

HELL'S KITCHEN 195 
nutrition totally from the soil.” 
Jan Baptista van Helmont (1577 - 1644), a Belgian physician of- 
fered the first experimental evidence that soil is not the sole source of 
plant nourishment. Van Helmont grew a small willow tree in an earth- 
enware pot for five years, adding only water to the pot. At the end of 
five years, the willow had increased in weight by 164 pounds, while 
the earth in the pot had decreased in weight by only two ounces. On 
the basis of these results he reasoned (incorrectly) that all the sub- 
stance of the plant was produced from the water and none from the 
soil: 
“I took an earthen vessel in which I put 200 pounds of earth that had 
been dried in a furnace, which I moistened with rain water, and I im- 
planted therein the trunk or stem of a willow tree, weighing five pounds. 
And at length, five years being finished, the tree sprung from thence did 
weigh 169 pounds and about three ounces. When there was need, I always 
moistened the earthen vessel with rain water or distilled water, and the 
vessel was large and implanted in the earth. Lest the dust that flew about 
should be co-mingled with the earth, I covered the lip or mouth of the 
vessel with an iron plate covered with tin and easily passable with many 
holes. I computed not the weight of the leaves that fell off in the four au- 
tumns. At length, I again dried the earth of the vessel, and there was found 
the same 200 pounds wanting about two ounces. Therefore, 164 pounds of 
wood, bark and roots arose out of water only.” (Jan Baptista van Helmont). 
Eighty years later, John Woodward (1605 - 1728) was able to 
show that spearmint sprigs grew better in water containing small 
amounts of soil when compared to those grown in pure rainwater. 
The beginnings of the clear understanding of the relationship of 
chemistry to plant nutrition goes back to two 17th century chemists, 
Johan Glauber (1604 - 1655) and Gabriel Plattes (1600 - 1655) 
through their analysis of “salts” such as wood ash, limestone and salt 
peter (potassium nitrate) and their affect on plant growth. 
To compensate for the lack of manure that resulted from the Thirty 
Year War, Glauber invented a chemical fertilizer he called “philo- 
sophic dung” or “fattening salt.”

196 
Despite these observations on the value of “salts” (the minerals 
found in the ash of burned food plants), the belief that humus (or- 
ganic material) was the basic “food” of plants was upheld well into 
the 19th century and was supported by such renowned chemists as 
Theodore de Saussure (1767 - 1845) and Sir Humphry Davy (1778 
- 1829). 
The source and function of the inorganic elements of plant ash 
was unknown. A prize was offered in Germany, “to resolve the ques- 
tion of whether the inorganic elements found in the ashes of plants 
were constituents produced by the plants or must be absorbed and 
what was their role in the plant.” 
The prize was awarded to A. F Wiegmann and L. Palstroff based 
on experiments comparing the growth of plants using synthetic soil 
compared with plant growth in sand alone. 
Fire, one of the five “elements” of the ancient Greeks, was of 
great interest to the alchemists and the Renaissance chemists who 
followed. What happens when a substance is consumed by fire was a 
favorite subject of study and argument between the learned chemists 
of the 1700s - this one question alone resulted in a revolution in the 
study and understanding of chemistry. The study of plant physiol- 
ogy played an enormous role in the chemistry revolution. 
Joseph Priestley, the English clergyman and chemist, and mem- 
ber of England's Royal Society was extremely interested in the changes 
in matter that resulted from being burned in fire - ashes! 
Priestley, a friend and contemporary of Franklin, was deeply in- 
volved with the problem’s of coal mines in the early days of the in- 
dustrial revolution, including air quality and flooding. 
On August 17, 1771, Priestley “put a (living) sprig of mint into 
air in which a wax candle had burned out and found that, on the 
27th of the same month (ten days later), another candle could be 
burned in the same air.” 
Priestley believed that, “he had accidentally hit upon a method of 
restoring air that had been injured by the burning of candles.” 
The “restorative which nature employs for this purpose,” he stated, 
“is vegetation.” Priestley pushed his observations further and dem- 
onstrated that “air restored by vegetation was not at all inconvenient

to a mouse.” 
When he was presented with a medal for his discovery (of oxy- 
gen), the citation included the statement, “For these discoveries we 
are assured that no vegetable grows in vain... but cleanses and puri- 
fies our atmosphere.” 
It was Jan Ingenhouz (1730 - 1799), a Dutch physician who dis- 
covered that, “plants required light to produce oxygen, and that the 
sun by itself without plants could not produce oxygen.” 
In the 19th century, Justus von Liebig (1803 - 1873), a dominant 
figure in plant nutrition, demonstrated that carbon was supplied by 
the air and not by humus (although he incorrectly believed that the 
carbon was absorbed by the roots). 
Liebig also assumed incorrectly that most nitrogen was absorbed 
from the air (he was unaware of the nitrogen fixation process by soil 
bacteria) and not from the humus. He did conclude correctly that 
the common supplies of wood ash from cooking and heating fires 
were insufficient in volume to support the growing fertilizer needs of 
19th century commercial agriculture. 
At the same time, in 1856, large deposits of potassium were dis- 
covered in Germany. By 1860, the first results from potash field trials 
were published in Germany and throughout Europe. In 1891, the 
German potash industry founded the first agricultural research and 
advisory institute in Stassfurt. By 1910 some 32,000 field trials on 
potash as a fertilizer had been conducted. By 1952, the International 
Potash Institute was established in Berne, Switzerland. 
In addition to carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen, which are obtained 
from water or from air, plants require a number of other elements. 
These elements, including minerals, are obtained from the soil. 
The needs of plants for essential elements such as nitrogen or 
magnesium (for the production of chlorophyll) can be determined 
by an analysis of the molecules of which plant cells are made. Other 
requirements were determined by studying the ability of plants to 
grow in distilled water to which small amounts of various minerals 
are added. 
Comparisons of the mineral content of plant cells with the min- 
eral concentration of the water medium in which it grows, demon-

HELLS KITCHEN 199 
strate that there can be marked differences in the concentrations of 
various nutrients. Thus, we must conclude that substances from the 
soil do not diffuse passively into the root cells of the plant, but rather, 
they are carried across the root cell membranes, against the diffusion 
gradient by an energy requiring event called “active transport.” 
Plants only “need” sixteen essential elements, of which nine 
are minerals, for optimal health, they only “need” three elements 
(N, P, K) for optimal production in terms of tons and bushels per 
acre - humans need a minimum of sixty minerals out of a total of 
ninety essential elements for optimal health - two thirds of the 
essential nutrients necessary for optimal human health and lon- 
gevity are minerals. 
Pure wood ash is not a uniform substance or product. Different 
species of trees are concentrators of select minerals and minerals do 
not occur in a uniform blanket over the crust of the earth - minerals 
occur in veins like chocolate in chocolate ripple ice cream, minerals 
occur in veins like gold and silver making the composition of wood 
ash mineral content highly variable. 
As a general rule, the softer woods contain less potassium and 
the harder woods contain more potassium, ranging from 16 to 40 
percent respectively of the total mineral mix in the ash. 
Wood ashes also have a very favorable physical effect on the soil, 
allowing for more aeration and greater capacity for retention of wa- 
ter. 
Unfortunately, wood ashes are no longer a viable source of hu- 
man mineral nutrition. Wood ashes are no longer a common 
byproduct of the everyday tasks of cooking and heating our homes, 
so we must find an alternative source of plant minerals if we are to 
get obesity, diabetes, the Metabolic Syndrome and other degenera- 
tive diseases under control and yes - even eliminate them. 
Animal Diets Advanced Quicker than Human Diets 
In 1914, there was enough knowledge regarding the need for 
vitamins and minerals for optimal health, maximal production and 
longevity, that nutritional companies sprang up that produced nutri-

200 
ent enriched feeds for livestock. One such company was Purina Mills 
of St. Louis, Missouri. 
The use of vitamins and minerals in animal feeds became com- 
monplace to increase survival, fertility, production and to prevent 
and reverse diseases. Vitamins and minerals were added as a com- 
plete package or “pre-mix” to pellets comprised of alfalfa, corn, soy- 
beans, wheat and oats. 
To improve the efficiency of the enriched feeds and to prevent 
the animals from sorting through the feed and eating only what they 
wanted, the ration was compressed into homogenous pellets. Today, 
there are pellet rations for all laboratory animals, pet animals and 
farm animals. 
Over the years this pellet ration system was so successful, that 
their use resulted in the elimination of as many as 900 different dis- 
eases including obesity (that still afflict humans in 2005) and doubled 
and quadrupled life spans of animals. This miraculous health benefit 
was not the result of the use of wonder drugs, genetically engineered 
proteins or stem cells, or heart transplants - the health benefits were 
simply due to the application of nutritional science. Agricultural sci- 
entists were not going to let the cow’s food choices affect the farmers 
income. 
In 1925, the WT. Rawleigh Company produced a booklet that 
promoted, “Ideal Poultry Raising.” The booklet informed the reader 
on the housing, care, feeding, culling the flock, diseases and their 
symptoms, nutritional prevention and treatment. 
In 1934, Purina, a pioneer animal feed company, advertised in 
The Country Gentleman, “What a difference 90 days make!”

let weighing pounds...all in 90 short days. 
A wonderful change...and only one thing can do it...good feed!

The Navajo Indians 
The diet’s of the Navajo Indians have changed significantly since 
the pre-Columbian times. Archeological data suggests that their early 
diet, was the standard fare of hunter gatherers and consisted only of 
indigenous plants and wild game until 1200, when a northern 
Athabaskan tribe of Navajos migrated to the southwest and learned 
farming from the Pueblo Indians. Corn, laced with “culinary” ashes, 
became a staple in the Navajo diet. Two centuries later, the Spanish 
brought sheep and cattle, and the Navajo became shepherds. 
Today the Navajo Nation numbers more than 151,000 and is the 
largest Native American Tribe. In recent decades, many processed 
foods have been introduced into the Navajo diet as a result of gov- 
ernment rationing, food distribution programs and the growth of 
trading posts and food stores, yet the tradition of “culinary” ashes 
being added to their food remains in the more rural areas.

Juniper ash proves to be a good source of dietary calcium, iron 
and a moderate source of magnesium. One teaspoon of juniper ash 
provides the equivalent calcium levels found in one cup of milk; it 
supplies twenty eight percent of the RDA for iron and nine percent of 
the RDA for magnesium. The woody branches contain more calcium 
and silica, whereas the twigs and needles (the growth part of the 
tree) contained the highest levels of trace minerals. 
Typical recipe that includes “culinary” ashes: 
Shaped Corn Griddle Cakes 
Mix one cup juniper ashes with water and strain through a grass 
brush into basin of corn flour, using about three and one half cups of 
the liquid. Add one half cup clear water to this greenish dough and 
knead until thick. Shape with moist hands into round cakes less than 
an inch thick and four inches in diameter. Bake on a fry griddle, 
rubbing it (the griddle) with a cloth between bakings, and moisten 
cakes on both sides when done. 
Even today, the corn based foods of the Navajo contain “culi- 
nary” ash, which is mixed with boiling water and added after strain- 
ing, to the blue corn flour “to make it blue.” Blue corn contains an 
anthocyanin pigment that turns a distinct greenish-blue color in the 
alkaline medium created by the addition of wood ash. The wood ash 
was also used daily to make a “tea” and was used medicinally for 
stomach and intestinal ailments. 
Of the three major types of plants used to make the culinary 
ashes listed in the Navajo culture, juniper (Juniperus monosperma), 
commonly called cedar is used most frequently. The small branches 
and needles are most favorably used as they produce the finest par- 
ticle ash (the minerals that are left when the wood and needles are 
burned). 
Green juniper branches are gathered, placed on a grill or screen 
and set alight. The ash is collected in a pan placed underneath the 
grill. Juniper being absent, ash was also made from dried tumbleweed 
or greasewood. 
The three sources of wood “culinary” ashes were all good sources 
of essential minerals. Iron concentration was higher in the grease-

HELLS KITCHEN 205 
wood; wood ash, although variable in analysis, tends to be rich in 
potassium, calcium, iron, copper and zinc, trace minerals and rare 
earths. 
In the absence of wood ash, special clay was mixed with the salt 
and/or the Navajo food. Unfortunately in the 1940s, urban Navajo 
cooks began replacing “culinary” ashes with the more available bak- 
ing soda - thus began the transition from the slim wood ash using 
Navajo shepherds to the obese baking soda using urban Navajo. 
The Okinawa People 
In 2003, a small group of our associates, including Blake Gra- 
ham, and Dr. Wallach went to Okinawa to determine where these 
exceedingly long lived people obtained their nutritional minerals. 
We went first to the Department of Agriculture and learned that the 
soil of the entire Okinawa archipelago of 161 islands was made up of 
red, yellow or black clay - no coral calcium. 
Many large studies, including the twenty five year long Okinawa 
Project that was conducted by two Harvard medical doctors, were 
produced in an attempt to determine the Okinawa secrete of longev- 
ity - so we felt we didn’t need to ask all of the detailed questions of 
diet, religion, exercise and professions that had already asked over 
and over again. We decided that we were going to ask only one ques- 
tion - “what did you do with the wood ashes from your cooking and 
heating fires?” 
We visited many of the Okinawa “old ones,” and shared tradi- 
tional meals of boiled pork, steamed sea vegetables, bitter melon and 
squash with them. We asked all of the old men over 100 years, “what 
did you do with the wood ashes from your cooking and heating fires 
each day?” The old men replied, “we don’t know.” 
We asked the Okinawa women of ninety and 100 years or morte, 
“what did you do with the wood ashes from your heating and cook- 
ing fires each morning?” They replied, “Everybody knows that, we 
put the ashes into our gardens and we added them to our rice to 
produce a rich yellow color!” 
The Japanese and the people from Okinawa used “culinary” wood

206 
ash! They preferred the hardwood trees for fuel because they burned 
hotter and burned longer - unknown to them, the hardwood trees 
also contained considerably more minerals than the soft wood trees. 
A recent comparison of the mineral content of the bones from 
the modern Japanese and the 3,000 year old bones from ancient Japa- 
nese monks showed that the ancient bones contained a wider variety 
of minerals and a greater amount of each mineral - a testimony to the 
nutritional value of “culinary” wood ashes. 
The mystery of the Okinawa longevity had been solved by asking 
the question that no one had asked before - “what did you do with 
the wood ashes from the heating and cooking fires each morning?” 
The simple answer was that they “used them in their gardens as fer- 
tilizer and in their rice as ‘culinary’ ashes.” 
It is often said that, “familiarity breeds contempt.” This human 
failing has cost us dearly. Over time, because wood ashes were dirty, 
inconvenient and so “old time,” we lost our respect for, our interest 
in and our understanding of wood ashes - wood ashes that were 
cherished by humans 30,000 years earlier. As a result, we made a 
catastrophic error as we willingly and proactively gave up the “drudg- 
ery” of the wood stove, scorched pots and wood ashes and moved 
almost overnight to electricity, natural gas and propane household 
fuels. 
We have to understand, why we originally used wood ashes be- 
fore written human history. We have to understand what we gave up 
before we can know how to replace it (just like alcoholics have to 
know they are in trouble before they can begin to solve their prob- 
lem). We can’t replace wood ashes with pharmaceuticals! 
We Americans have to get busy, because our window of time for 
a healthful survival is rapidly closing. If we keep doing what we’re 
doing our health care system will bankrupt us even as it fails to give 
us the “bang” for our health buck. Simply said, Obesity is here to 
stay unless we supplement our diets with the plant minerals we have 
so abruptly given up!

Size Does Matter: 
the Key to Cellular Fitness 
We know that if you do nothing else, consuming 1,200 mg of ) 
calcium each day will reduce your risk of being overweight by sixty 
five percent! If you are overweight, give up 500 calories each day 
(one burger), and consume the 1,200 mg of calcium per day and you 
will double your rate of weight loss - even without the need for exercise! 
Supplemental minerals are truly the prevention and cure of 
being overweight and obese. However, all minerals are not cre- 
ated equal - it doesn’t matter how many minerals or how much 
of each mineral you consume - what counts is how many miner- 
als and how much of each mineral you absorb! 
Remember, we require a minimum of sixty minerals to be opti- 
mally healthy, physically fit and to maximize our life spans. Unfortu- 
nately, with the advent of electric, gas and propane cooking stoves

210 
and heating furnaces, we have given up the traditional and ideal source 
of nutritional minerals - plant minerals derived from the wood ashes 
that supported the human need for minerals since before written 
history. 
Because the daily supply of wood, peat and kelp ash generated in 
the home could not fulfill the growth requirements of commercial 
agriculture, agricultural chemistry went another direction. Commer- 
cial fertilizer, NPK, appeared in 1840 to meet the need of a growth 
industry necessary to meet the food demands of the now urbanized 
population of the industrial revolution; shortly thereafter, the first 
commercial electrical generating plant came on line in 1882. 
The urbanized citizen of the industrialized nations demanded 
food and lots of it - they traded the hours they had historically spent 
in their gardens and fields for hours in the factories and cash money. 
They now depended almost totally on commercial agriculture for 
their “daily bread.” 
Unfortunately their bread was not like their fathers bread - yes, 
there was lots of it, however, the new bread was critically short of 
minerals. In 1910, eighty five percent of Americans were still subsis- 
tence farmers or gardeners - and they were still putting wood ashes 
into their gardens and their food. By 1950, almost 100 percent of 
urbanites from all industrialized nations were solely dependent upon 
commercial agriculture for their food. 
We must consciously supplement to make up the difference be- 
tween the high levels of minerals that were in our father’s bread and 
the significantly reduced mineral levels in our commercially prepared 
bread.

Immigrant Statistic 
A simple barometer of the nutritional value of the American diet 
is the health of immigrants. We can look at what happens to people 
who leave their third world birth place and come to the U.S. for a 
new life. In a study published in the Journal of the American Medical 
Association, December 15, 2004, it was reported that, “The longer 
you live in America, the more likely you are to be obese.” 
The study examined the obesity rates among immigrants and 
native born Americans. It found that immigrants, regardless of race 
or country of origin, living in the USA for more than fifteen years 
resulted in an average of a ten pound weight gain - the product of 
being placed in the mineral deficient “Feedlot Syndrome” fostered 
by the American “four food group” mentality, and the loss of their 
historical “culinary” and garden ashes without any concerted effort 
to replace them. 
Minerals Have Personalities 
The most significant development in the trace element field of 
study in the last twenty five years of the 20th century was the recog- 
nition that trace mineral elements have a fundamental role in health 
maintenance for humans and that optimal intakes from food sources 
can no longer be taken for granted. 
The essential role of trace elements in the support and mainte- 
nance of the human immune system against bacteria, viruses, fungi 
and cancer, degenerative diseases and birth defects are well docu- 
mented. The mode of action and the tissue concentrations of trace 
elements varies considerably. The only common denominator of 
trace minerals is that they are all found in exceedingly low amounts 
in the healthy tissue. They are usually found in parts per million 
(ppm); micrograms/gram (u/g); or with some as low as parts per 
billion (ppb) or nanograms per gram (ng/g). 
The concentration of trace elements in tissue or requirement lev- 
els does not represent their relative importance as an essential nutri- 
ent. The concentrations of trace elements and their functional con-

HELLS KITCHEN 213 
centration must be continuously maintained within narrow physi- 
ological limits if the functional and structural fabric of the tissues 
and organs of the animal and human body are to be maintained for 
optimal fertility, development, growth, health and longevity. 
Higher species such as birds, mammals and humans have a “ho- 
meostatic” mechanism that maintains tissue concentrations of trace 
minerals at optimal levels at their physiological sites. 
Almost all trace elements function as catalysts for a cellular or 
sub-cellular enzyme system. Enzymes are simply proteins that do 
work and in their role as catalysts their function ranges from that of 
a weak ionic (ph affects) modulator to highly specific metalloenzyme 
(“metallic finger”) functions for DNA and RNA. 
In metalloenzymes, the metal atom cannot be substituted by any 
other trace element. Although Vallee has demonstrated that Co and 
Cd can replace Zinc in certain enzymes with some changes in sub- 
strate specificity but the enzyme is not deactivated. 
Because of the frequently wide disparity between the absolute 
and dietary requirements (results from different intestinal absorp- 
tion efficiencies for individual elements) the most efficient form of 
trace mineral supplement, such as plant derived colloidal minerals 
should be consumed. 
For the metallic form of iodine the ratio of absorption is almost 
1:1; for iron it’s 1:10 and for chromium it’s 1:100. 
The RDA (Recommended Daily Allowance) and RDI (Recom- 
mended Daily Intake) and other dietary recommendations for trace 
elements are conceived by the National Science Foundation to “meet 
the needs of practically all healthy people” - they are not applicable 
for people with “health challenges” or full blown disease states. 
Initial Depletion Phase 
Initial depletion of trace elements in animals and humans is char- 
acterized by changes only in the metabolism of the element itself in 
response to a suboptimal intake, the individual target tissue com- 
pensating so that no disturbance of biological structure or function 
are detectable by means of a test. Increased intestinal absorption effi-

214 
ciency; and/or reduction in excretion; and gradual diminishment of 
body reserves occurs. Pica occurs in a mild form at this early stage of 
deficiency. This phase of trace mineral deficiency returns to normal 
(including the elimination of the pica behavior) quickly with a re- 
turn of optimal intake. 
Compensated Metabolic Phase 
The compensated metabolic phase is characterized by a reduc- 
tion of certain specific biochemical functions, such as trace mineral 
dependent enzyme reactions or receptor sites. The “homeostatic” 
mechanism becomes ineffective in maintaining the normal trace min- 
eral concentrations at receptor sites causing a reduction in biochemi- 
cal function (i.e. - low blood sugar, irregular heart beat, loss of hair 
color, weight gain, mild obesity, etc.). Pica becomes evident in the 
individuals everyday life - they become “chocoholics” or “cola ad- 
dicts.” Supplementation with minerals will quickly reverse the symp- 
toms and behavior changes including pica and the more extreme 
“munchies.” 
Decompensated Metabolic Phase 
The decompensated phase of trace element deficiency is charac- 
terized by the appearance of serious clinical symptoms or defects in 
the metabolic (i.e. - pre-diabetes, significant weight gain, obesity), 
cognative (i.e. - learning disabilities, ADD, ADHD, etc.), emotional 
(Dr. Jykell/Mr. Hyde, “Bad Seeds,” depression, manic depression, bi- 
polar, anger, rage, anxiety attacks, panic attacks, etc.), developmen- 
tal (dyslexia, gay behavior, spina bifida), structural (muscular dys- 
trophy, obesity), loss of work-stamina and reduction of the longevity 
(average M.D. lifespan is 56 years of age) of a species. Obsessive pica 
is obvious to even the casual observer - the affected individual is 
aware that they can not help themselves in curbing their cravings 
and appetites by means of conscious will alone.

HELVS KITCHEN 215 
Clinical Phase 
The clinical phase of trace mineral deficiency is characterized by 
the onset of full blown disease states and even death (i.e. - cardiomy- 
opathy, diabetes, cancer, aneurysms, arthritis, goiter, morbid obesity, 
etc.). For most trace mineral deficiencies in the clinical phase, diag- 
nosis by the “orthodox” physician is almost nonexistent; the cure 
however, is simply a matter of replacement of the trace element (a 
correct diagnosis can lead to a remarkably rapid positive clinical re- 
sponse ranging from forty eight hours to thirty days for recognizable 
results; including the loss of up to one pound of excess weight per 
day on a proper mineral supplement program); others do not reach 
full return to normal after permanent biochemical, chromosomal or 
gross physical damage has occurred. 
There are three basic types of minerals; they are not of equal 
size or biological value and size does matter! 
Metallic Minerals 
Metallic minerals include egg shell, oyster shell, calcium carbon- 
ate, limestone, coral calcium, dolomite, inorganic mineral salts, sea 
water, Great Salt Lake water, mineral oxides (i.e. - iron oxide, copper 
oxide, etc.), vortex waters, sea bed minerals, “soils” (which are usu- 
ally clay), sea bed clays, clays, “rock flours,” and various antacids 
such as Rollaids and Tums. 
Typically, metallic minerals are found in tablets and powders as 
gluconate (calcium gluconate, zinc gluconate, etc.), lactate, sulphates, 
carbonates and oxides (iron oxide, etc.). 
Metallic minerals, despite wild claims to the contrary are only 
eight to twelve percent biologically available to younger animals and 
humans; after attaining the age of thirty five to forty years, the availability of metallic minerals plummets to three to five percent. 
A typical metallic mineral supplement, either alone or as part of 
a multiple is calcium carbonate. Calcium carbonate can be purchased 
in 1,000 mg tablets or capsules (i.e. - coral calcium, limestone, egg

216 
shell, oyster shell, etc.), which breaks down to 400 mg of metallic 
calcium and 600 mg of carbonate. Two 1,000 mg calcium carbonate 
capsules or tablets do not give you 2,000 mg of calcium - they only 
give you 800 mg of elemental calcium and at a ten percent availabil- 
ity rate you net eighty (80) milligrams of bioavailable calcium. There- 
fore, to get 2,000 mg of usable calcium from a 1,000 mg capsule or 
tablet of calcium carbonate, you would have to take twenty five 25) 
capsules each day! A 100 capsule bottle of calcium carbonate will 
therefore only last four days.

Chelated Minerals 
Chelated (Key - lay - ted) minerals were an invention of the live- 
stock industry. In the 1940s, the livestock industry, to ensure maxi- 
mum availability of dietary and supplemental minerals fed to live- 
stock being fattened for market, created a new form of minerals. 
The original chelating agent used was calcium EDTA, a man- 
made amino acid that was created by the Germans just prior to WWII 
to antidote arsenic and lead exposure in chemical warfare attacks 
(calcium EDTA is used today for intravenous chelation therapy to 
open up obstructed arteries). 
The term chelated, from the Latin, literally means “claw,” but in 
more modern times it is used to describe the process by which an 
amino acid, protein or enzyme (enzymes are proteins that do work) 
is wrapped around the mineral molecule which enhances the

HELLS KITCHEN 217 
bioavailability of the metallic mineral. 
Chelated minerals are approximately forty to sixty five percent 
bio-available, a 400 percent increase over the availability of metallic 
minerals - certainly a significant improvement over the amounts of 
elemental minerals absorbed. 
Chelated minerals are easily recognized on the label of a multiple 
vitamin/mineral supplement or free standing calcium supplement 
such as selenomethionine, chromium picolinate, or sometimes as an 
“amino acid chelate.” 
Colloidal Minerals 
Colloidal (Koal-oid-ul) chemistry while not a new concept, is 
not widely known about or understood by the general public. Sim- 
ply said, a colloid refers to a substance that exists as ultra fine par- 
ticles that are suspended in a medium of different matter. The colloi- 
dal state is the state of a solute (mineral or other substance such as a 
paint pigment) in a solution when its molecules do not separate into 
atoms as with a true solution (sodium chloride or salt separates into 
separate sodium and chloride atoms while in solution), but rather 
the colloidal particle remains grouped together to form solute par- 
ticles. 
The presence of these inorganic colloidal particles, which are 
approximately one hundred-thousandth to one ten-millionth of a 
centimeter in diameter (about 400 thousandths to four millionths of 
an inch), can often be detected by means of an electron microscope. 
Asa result of the grouping of the molecules, a solute in the colloidal 
state cannot pass through a suitable semi-permeable membrane and 
gives rise to negligible osmotic pressure (they will pass through filter 
paper), depression of freezing point and elevation of boiling point 
effects. 
These ultra-fine particles of the colloid are just barely larger than 
most molecules and are so small that they can’t be seen with the 
naked eye - about one billion of these colloid particles would fit into 
0.01 cubic inch. 
The “solution” part of a colloid provides a solid, gas or liquid

218 
medium in which the colloid particles are suspended. The suspended 
particles in a colloid can also be a solid, a gas or a liquid. 
In 1925, H. Freundlich classified solutions into three categories: 
1. True solutions 
2. Colloidal solutions 
3. Emulsions and suspensions 
The four part method of classifying solutions is as follows: 
1. Identify particle size 
Determine presence of Brownian movement (random 
movement of particles suspended in liquids or gasses 
resulting from the impact of molecules of the fluid 
surrounding the particles). 
3. Ability to pass through filter paper 
4. Level of solubility 
In 1975, S.S. Voyutsky (a Russian) wrote the classic text on col- 
loidal chemistry. Voyutsky referred to solutions as “molecular disper- 
sion systems” and “heterogeneous highly dispersed colloidal systems.” 
The exact point between the molecular and colloidal degrees of 
dispersion cannot be established because the transition from mo- 
lecularly dispersed systems to coarsely dispersed systems is a con- 
tinuum. 
A colloidal system must have three basic characteristics: 
1) It must be heterogeneous (consists of dissimilar ingredients 
or constituents). 
2) The system must be multi-phasic (i.e. - solid/liquid; gas/ 
liquid, etc.) 
3) The particles must be insoluble (do not dissolve in the 
solution). 
Each one of these classifications interacts with the others to give 
colloids their unique qualities. The interesting thing about colloids is 
that they remain heterogeneous, multi-phasic and insoluble at differ-

HELLS KITCHEN 219 
ent concentrations as long as a larger number if not all of the par- 
ticles are within the range of sizes of colloids (1n to 100 n). 
The molecular groups or particles of the colloid solute carry a 
resultant electrical charge, generally the same sign (negative) for all 
of the particles. A small percentage of these inorganic colloids will 
pass through the intestine of a living animal or human because a 
natural chelating process takes place in the gut in the presence of 
protein-containing food. 
Inorganic colloidal material which readily passes through filter 
paper may be separated from dissolved substances, such as starch, 
sugar or salt, by placing the mixture of mineral colloid and non-colloid in a parchment shell surrounded by distilled water. The inorganic colloids are “too large” to pass through the membrane, but the 
molecules of salt, starch and sugar or any other dissolved substance 
pass readily through the semi-permeable membrane (they separate 
into individual atoms or very small molecules). This kind of separation process is called dialysis. 
In the process of digestion, the inorganic minerals in food or 
supplements soon become inorganic colloids and as an inorganic 
colloid they cannot penetrate the intestinal wall to enter the blood 
stream. In the presence of amino acids, a small percentage of the 
inorganic colloids form chelated minerals and organic colloids which 
are able to be dialyzed through the mucus membranes of the intesti- 
nal walls into the blood stream - this bio-available mineral state is 
known as a “crystalloid.” 
Crystalloids or organic colloids readily pass through cell walls, 
while inorganic colloids are “too large.” Additionally, we must re- 
member that in the living organism there are other physiological forces 
at work, notably “active transport,” which interferes with or modifies 
the expected osmotic phenomenon. 
Active transport, refers to the act of the intestinal mucosal cell 
reaching out with a pseudopodium, engulfing and transporting the 
particle into itself and then transported into the adjacent blood steam. 
In fact most absorbed food and nutrients are absorbed into the hu- 
man body by active transport rather than by a passive trip through a 
semi-permeable membrane.

220 
Colloidal mineral supplements and commercial colloids are found 
in four different forms: 
1. Unprotected colloids are made of bare “rock flour,” this is the 
form of inorganic metallic colloid found in the sea bed minerals, 
clays, “soils,” and “glacial milk.” This form of inorganic colloid is in 
fact a metallic mineral and is only available to plants when there is a 
healthy soil population of bacteria and fungi. 
2. The second type of mineral colloid is found in living systems 
of bacteria, fungi, green plants (food crops), animals and humans 
and is coated by a water loving (hydrophilic) substance such as gela- 
tin, albumin, albuminoids, or collagen. This coating protects the now 
“organic mineral colloid” and allows it to be a crystalloid for absorp- 
tion, storage and physiological uses and thus maximizing its 
bioavailability up to 98 percent. 
3. The third type of organic mineral colloids has a protective coat- 
ing of carbon with a molecular chain length of 10 to 12 carbon at- 
oms. This type of colloid is also found in bacteria, fungi, plants (in- 
cluding some forms of petrified wood), animals and humans and is 
thought to be the most stable form of natural organic mineral col- 
loid. 
4. The fourth type of mineral colloid is not found in nature, but 
rather is manufactured industrially by coating the metallic colloid 
with sulfated castor oil (lipophyllic or fat or oil loving) to form com- 
mercial detergents. 
Humic Shale 
Humic “shale” is a unique source of plant derived colloidal min- 
erals. Humic shale originated from plants during the Carboniferous 
Era, those lush tropical plants took up some seventy seven plus me- 
tallic minerals available to them from a fertile soil that had as many 
as eighty four minerals. 
The ancient soil was so rich with minerals that trees grew as much 
as twenty five feet per year; the great brontosaurus or “thunder liz-

HELLS KITCHEN 221 
ard” attained a body weight of 140,000 pounds (seventy tons) with a 
mouth no larger than that of a horse - the ability of the brontosaurus 
to attain such a bulk with so little a mouth, meant he was consuming 
plants rich with concentrated mineral nutrients. 
A volcanic eruption covered these mineral rich forests with a thin 
layer of mud and ash, thick enough to create an air-tight “vault” and 
dried or desiccated the plants into a deep accumulation of “hay,” 
plant compost or a “pre-coal” form, but not deep enough or heavy 
enough to pressurize the dried plant material into coal or oil - in 
effect it became a prehistoric compost. | 
The entombed humic shale never fossilized or petrified, in other 
words they never became rocks - just a compressed, dried, prehis- 
toric compost pile that contains a high level of plant derived colloi- 
dal minerals. 
Humic shale can be used as an excellent soil conditioner for or- 
ganic gardens, farms and ranches as it supplies a rich source of hu- 
mus and no less than seventy five plant-derived colloidal minerals. 
Humic shale can also be ground into a fine meal and soaked for 
three to four weeks in filtered spring water, it then provides a high 
quality plant derived colloidal liquid mineral supplement that con- 
tains not less than 19,000 mg of plant minerals per quart. 
It takes seventy eight pounds of humic shale to produce the min- 
erals in one quart of liquid plant minerals. The seventy eight pounds 
of humic shale represents approximately 1,034 pounds of lush pre- 
historic green mineral rich plants. The fluid extract of high grade 
humic shale contains no less than seventy seven plant derived colloi- 
dal minerals and is up to 98 percent available for animals and hu- 
mans.

teoporosis, periodontal disease, anemia, hypothyroidism, bone spurs,

Obesity, Diabetes & the 
Metabolic Syndrome 
Sixty five percent of Americans are overweight and thirty one 
percent are obese (thirty pounds or more overweight); by 2005, obe- 
sity will surpass tobacco as the number one preventable killer of 
Americans; by 2010 seventy five percent of Americans are projected 
to be over weight and forty percent of Americans are projected to be 
obese. 
Obesity, treated as a disease of gluttony, increased by ninety five 
percent between the 1990s and 2000; anti-obesity drugs, diet and 
surgery treatment programs have made 20th and 21st century doc- 
tors wealthy, however, obesity has cut into the bottom line of many 
other industries including the travel industry. 
On December 8, 2004, the Northwestern University Medical 
School of Chicago, Illinois released a report that was published in 
the Journal of the American Medical Association, the report highlighted 
the American obesity epidemic’s effect on the Medicare system - “with 
current trends of increasing overweight and obesity afflicting all age 
groups, urgent preventive measures are required not only to lessen 
the burden of disease and disability associated with excess weight 
but also to contain future health care costs incurred by the aging

226 
population.” 
The report from the Feinberg School of Medicine, “found that 
the annual average Medicare charges for severely obese men were 
$6,192 more than for non-overweight men - 84 percent higher.” 
For severely obese women, “the annual average Medicare charges 
were $5,618 more, or 88 percent higher than for women not over- 
weight.” 
For men, “the total average annual Medicare charges for those 
not overweight were $7,205, for the overweight $8,390, for the obese 
$10,128 and for those men who were severely obese $13,674.” 
The total average annual Medicare charges “for women in the 
same weight groups were $6,224, $7,653, $$9,612 and $12,342.” 
Approximately 12 percent of American adults over the age of 
sixty five years were considered obese in 2004, a figure that is pro- 
jected to increase to 20 percent by the year 2050 - obesity usually 
occurs concurrently with hypertension, diabetes, coronary artery dis- 
ease and the Metabolic Syndrome. 
During the 1990s alone the average American's body weight went 
up ten pounds. Seemingly benign, these ten pounds have crushed 
the American airlines industry with their weight. These extra ten 
pounds caused the airlines industry to spend an extra $275 million 
to consume an additional 350 million gallons of jet fuel to carry the 
additional blubber in the year 2000 alone (American Journal of Pre- 
ventive Medicine). 
“The obesity epidemic has unexpected consequences beyond di- 
rect health effects,” said Dr. Deron Burton of the CDC. “Our goal was 
to highlight one area that had not been looked before.” According to 
the study, the additional fuel burned to carry the additional weight 
also had a devastating environmental impact; an estimated 3.8 mil- 
lion extra tons of carbon dioxide were released into the atmosphere 
in the year 2000 which directly contributes to our earthly problems 
of global warming and desertification. 
The average human has 40 billion fat cells, and they can easily 
and quickly reproduce and duplicate into a population of 100 bil- 
lion fat cells in the obese; and they are tough, resilient and defend 
themselves vigorously almost as if they were directed by a brain that

HELV’S KITCHEN 227 
had a defense plan. 
The most simplistic view of obesity is that, “the fat person 
consumes more calories than they burn.” However, it is quite 
clear that obesity is not that simple. Fat people are driven to eat! 
Their conscious will can only override this command to eat for 
so long and eventually they will succumb to the command and 
eat, and eat, and eat. 
This almost psychotic urgency to eat in spite of being obese, in 
spite of having high blood pressure, in spite of heart disease, in spite 
of having diabetes, in spite of social rejection is a powerful and irre- 
sistible force. 
This force that commands a fat person to eat despite all human 
effort to resist can only be created by one possible condition - min- 
eral deficiencies that result in pica a.k.a. the “munchies.” 
Types and Locations of Fat 
Fat cells are known to function differently in various parts and 
zones of the body. Fat accumulated on the hips and thighs, the “pear 
shape,” is thought to be less dangerous metabolically; this is a double 
edged sword because hip and thigh fat deposits are also the hardest 
to get rid of. 
Visceral fat or abdominal fat inside the body cavity is blamed for 
a higher risk of diabetes, cancer, cardiovascular disease, high blood 
pressure and elevated triglycerides. 
Visceral fat actually produces “inflammation” encouraging the } 
production of free radical compounds that today are thought to be 
the cause of arteriosclerosis and stimulates the production of clot- 
promoting chemicals. Here is where obese and even overweight in- 
dividuals can protect themselves from the free radical damage to their 
arteries by ramping up on their antioxidant intake - it is best to error 
on the side of high intake of antioxidants. The good news is, visceral 
fat is the first fat to be lost as a result of exercise. 
While unsightly and cosmetically undesirable, subcutaneous fat 
tends to be metabolically benign. A 2004 study conducted by Wash- 
ington University in St. Louis showed that removing an average of

228 
twenty two pounds of subcutaneous fat with liposuction did not 
improve the health of the obese person; elevated blood pressure did 
not fall; elevated cholesterol and triglycerides did not drop; and el- 
evated blood sugar did not improve following liposuction procedures. 
There have been uncountable diet plans throughout the centu- 
ries: low calorie diets; low fat diets; low carbohydrate diets, etc. Yet, 
the American obesity epidemic and the world wide pandemic of over- 
weight and obese kids and adults, diabetes and the Metabolic Syn- 
drome (large deposit of visceral abdominal fat, high blood pressure, 
elevated cholesterol and triglycerides, insulin resistance, etc.) con- 
tinue to spiral out of control. 
If improper diet in terms of percentages of carbohydrates, fats 
and proteins was the root cause of obesity, we would have long ago 
eliminated the pandemic and epidemic weight problem. The pur- 
pose of the research that led to the creation of this book, was to 
identify the actual bottom line cause, prevention and cure (in the 
purist sense) of obesity. So we will begin with the successes and fail- 
ures of various peoples. 
The Amish 
The Old Order Amish arrived in North America during the 1700s, 
and yet today in the 21st century, they maintain their old ways. The 
Amish have rejected almost all modern conveniences including elec- 
tricity, natural gas, propane, internal combustion engines and tele- 
phones. 
The Amish still use horses to pull their family carriages and bug- 
gies instead of automobiles, they still use horses to power and pull 
their farm implements, they use kerosene or fireplaces to light their 
homes at night, they use wood fires to cook and heat with, and in the 
21st century they are still adding the wood ash byproduct of their 
kitchen stoves and hearth to their composts and gardens. 
There are 30,000 Old Order Amish in the U.S. today, compared 
with almost 300 million Americans who have willingly and gleefully 
given up the old ways and transitioned their daily lives to electricity, 
automobiles, farm tractors and telephones.

HELLS KITCHEN 229 
The rates of obesity (4 %) and type II diabetes (6 %) in the Amish 
population are only ten percent and fifty percent of those reported 
by their “modern” neighbors living in the same county but on the 
other side of the cultural fence. 
Most casual observers believe the lower rate of obesity and dia- 
betes in the Amish is due to a better, more wholesome diet and the 
physical demands of an 18th century farm life. 
We have spent a considerable amount of time living with the 
Amish in Lancaster County, Pennsylvania and we can assert with 
complete certainty and honesty that the Amish consume more sugar, 
more fat, more red meat, and more dairy products than anyone in 
North America. They are famous for Shoo-fly pie, mince meat pie, 
apple pie, sweet-potato pie, hot oatmeal with brown sugar, potatoes 
of every type, gravy, breads, pancakes, molasses and waffles with 
maple syrup. 
The Amish are heavy consumers of whole milk, butter and cheese; 
they eat pork, beef, goat and mutton three times each day; and they 
are big consumers of poultry and eggs. 
The carriage and draft horses do most of the physical work on an 
Amish farm, so what gives them a significantly lower rate of obesity 
and diabetes? It’s the wood ashes! The wood ashes are the humble 
source of essential minerals that are dutifully put into their gardens 
every morning. Wood ashes are like oxygen, we can’t do without 
them (or their replacement), yet they are hidden in plain sight! They 
don’t get any respect! 
Nez Perce Indians 
Obesity and diabetes are relatively recent disease problems for 
the Native American population of North America - prior to the 1940s, 
obesity and diabetes were rare diseases. An examination of the health 
and morbidity reports compiled by civilian and military physicians 
between the 1830s and the 1930s confirms that diabetes were rare 
diseases in Native Americans. 
Since the 1940s the rate and incidence of obesity and diabetes in 
the Native American population has grown to pandemic and epi-

230 
demic levels. A CDC report estimated 15.3 percent of the Nez pierce 
Indians were diagnosed with diabetes in 2002. Approximately thirty 
three percent of all Native Americans fifty five years of age and older 
were diagnosed with diabetes by 2003. In some tribes, diabetes and 
obesity affects up to fifty percent of the adult members over the age 
of thirty five years. These rates of diabetes and obesity in Native 
Americans are the highest incidence reported in any culture in the 
world. 
Prior to the 1940s, Native Americans still lived on the reserva- 
tion and lived a traditional lifestyle including a traditional diet. Stud- 
ies of photographs of Native Americans, at the end of the 19th cen- 
tury, showed that most Indians were lean and physically fit. Their 
diets consisted of goat, mutton, beef and unrefined grains, including 
corn that was prepared with “culinary” wood ashes - wood ashes 
were still being put into their gardens as fertilizer. 
During the 1960s and 1970s the Indian Health Services began to 
notice a dramatic rise in the rate of diagnosis of adult onset type II 
diabetes and obesity. Unfortunately, the IHS focused on screening, 
early traditional treatment and diabetic drug programs and surgery 
(amputations). The diabetes therapy program developed for Native 
Americans is so drug driven that pharmacists have been designated 
as the “primary” educators and monitors of the diabetic Native Ameri- 
can patient. 
Between 1998 and 2001, respondents in an Idaho, Oregon and 
Washington survey reported that obesity was 250 percent higher in 
Native Americans when compared to all races. 
A 2004 pilot supplement study (Wallach, J.D., MacCoy, B., Henry, 
L. and Schrauzer, G.N.) with the Nez Perce Indians of Idaho, has 
shown that almost all participants lost weight, significantly reduced 
blood pressure and reduced fasting blood sugars by thirty to fifty 
percent. All were able to significantly reduce or wean off of their 
medication. 
The Nez Perce diabetes and obesity program was based on a 
mineral supplement program (plant derived colloidal minerals) that 
replaced the “culinary” and fertilizer wood ashes given up by Native 
Americans as they transitioned into modern electric, natural gas and .

HELLS KITCHEN 231 
propane kitchen and heating fuels. 
Pima Indians 
The Pima Indians are a band of Native Americans who live on the 
invisible border dividing Arizona and Mexico. The Pima band on 
both sides of the border are from the identical genetic pool; yet the 
Pima Indians on the Mexican side of the border have almost no type 
II diabetes and have only a ten percent obesity rate compared with 
the twenty two percent diabetes and seventy percent obesity in the 
Arizona Pimas! 
With a small population of 12,000 individuals, the Arizona Pimas 
ran up a $7 million annual Federal cost for pharmaceutical treat- 
ments; additionally $3 million was spent annually for dialysis charges 
for diabetics. As a result of the epidemic and pandemic rate of diabe- 
tes and obesity, the Arizona Pima Indian’ life span is shorter than the 
average American population by 15 - 19 years. 
The Pima Native Americans from Arizona live in trailer homes 
that use natural gas or propane, electric stoves and microwave ovens 
for cooking and heating - they use automobiles, pickup trucks and 
tractors and they buy food and government Native American “com- 
modities” from the grocery store like everybody else. 
The Arizona Pima’ transition from subsistence farming and wood 
stoves, and their traditional diet to the American lifestyle began in 
the 1940s and by 1960 the epidemic of obesity and type II diabetes, 
seemingly targeting the Arizona Pimas, had started in earnest. 
By contrast the Pima Indians living south of the Arizona border 
in Mexico, are still subsistence farmers, they still cook and heat their 
homes with wood, and, as we go to press they are still putting their 
wood ashes into their gardens every morning. 
What Then is Obesity? 
Obesity, bottom line, is a deficiency disease - a deficiency of an ) 
entire category of essential nutrients - minerals. Certainly the symp- 
toms and clinical presentation of the simple uncomplicated deficien- 
cies are rarely seen anymore, as they are exacerbated or aggravated

232 
by the increased intake of sugars and carbohydrates and reduction in 
Exercise: 
The deficiencies of just about any or all essential minerals will 
result in pica, cribbing, cravings, binge eating and the munchies in 
varying degrees. The deficiency of even one essential mineralyresults 
in a slight increase in food cravings and the “stand in front of the 
open refrigerator door” behavior. 
It is the intersection of multiple mineral deficiencies and the 
excess intake of calories that produce obesity. 
The deficiencies of essential minerals, also explains why women 
gain excess weight during a pregnancy and breast feeding, they lo@se 
minerals to their babies, and if they fail to replace minerals faster 
than the babies steal them, the resulting pica and munchies cause 
the new mom to eat, and eat, and eat. 
The deficiency of two or more essential minerals will produce a significant level of pica, cravings and binge eating. Iron 
and phosphorus deficiencies occurring at the same time will result in an almost psychotic binge eating and soft drink consumption rampage that results in morbid obesity. 
The act of reducing calories from carbohydrate, fat and protein 
can result in weight loss, however, reducing calories alone does not 
solve the systemic effects of mineral deficiencies, which explains why 
individualswho drop off of the highly restricted diets of all types will 
regain the weight that they had lost; it also explains why many indi- 
viduals maintain or gain weight even when they are truly cutting 
back on calories. 
Glucose is an “all-purpose” fuel sugar that can be used as a 
source of energy by the body. However, the overt intake of sug- 
ars is not necessary, as adequate levels of blood glucose can be 
made from dietary or stored fat and protein. Sugars and carbo- 
hydrates are not essential nutrients, they are quick sources of 
energy, they can be fun to eat, however they are not necessary or 
essential to consume. 
A portion of whatever we eat, including bread, rice, or beans is 
converted to glucose. To keep the blood levels of glucose at the level 
each of us requires, the hormone insulin is produced by the Islet’s of .

HELLS KITCHEN 233 
Langerhans, and released to regulate the rate of glucose metabolism. 
Insuling.can’t perform properly without several cofactors includ- 
ing the trace minerals chromium and vanadium. As we consume food, 
insulin is released into the blood from the pancreas to regulate the 
use and storage of sugar and carbohydrate. 
Carbohydrates are fractured into the simple sugar glucose and 
used for an instant energy source, glycogen (animal starch) for stor- 
age and later use or converted to body fat for long term storage. The 
body’s ability to control or manage the use of blood sugar is referred 
to as “glucose tolerance.” 
The absence of or insufficient levels of dietary or supplemental 
chromium and vanadium, results in blood glucose being converted 
into blood lipids and body fat, rather than efficiently being burned 
for energy. The concurrent deficiencies chromium, vanadium and 
other minerals results in ravenous hunger, cravings, binge eating, 
pica, cribbing and the munchies. 
The increased intake of carbohydrate in the form of sugar 
(one half pound per person per day in 2004 compared with one 
half pound per person per year in the 1700s) and the deficien- 
cies of minerals are the basic root causes of obesity. It is an inter- 
esting fact, that as the Asian and Latin American countries “catch 
up to western countries in terms of lifestyles and economics,” 
their rates of obesity catch up as well! 
The reason that the transition from third world cultures to indus- 
trialized cultures results in an almost immediate increase in obesity 
is that they give up using wood for fuel - they convert to electricity, 
natural gas and propane for heating and cooking fuels! There are no 
wood ashes left to put in the gardens or for use as “culinary miner- 
als.” 
The resulting mineral deficiencies produce the munchies, cravings 
and binge eating; add to these deficiencies a significant increase in 
carbohydrates in the diet and only one thing can happen - obesity! 
In 1971, 14.5 % of Americans were obese; in 2004 the rate rose 
to 30.9 %. The number of American deaths attributed to obesity is 
400,000 per year and expected to rise by 2005 to be the highest 
cause of preventable deaths.

From 1996 to 2001, 2 million American teens and young adults 
swelled the ranks of the clinically obese. According to the Journal of 
the American Medical Association, March, 2004, the total cost for this 
level of obesity is a whopping $117 billion per year. 
The rate of obesity in American teens between the ages of 12 and 
19 was five percent in 1980, the rate rose to fifteen percent in 2004.

The adipose or fat tissue is the primary storage organ for energy 
in the human. It is the main reservoir for stored calories ingested in 
excess of basal needs, and it gives up calories when caloric intake is 
delayed (missed meals) or in times of starvation. 
Excess energy is stored in adipose tissue in the form of triglycer- 
ides, when this process becomes excessive obesity occurs.

What is Adult Onset Type II Diabetes? 
Adult onset type II diabetes (insulin resistant diabetes) is a simple 
mineral deficiency disease. Insufficient dietary intakes, or reduced 
absorption efficiency of chromium, vanadium and other minerals 
produces increased blood levels of insulin. Increased blood levels of 
insulin produces hypoglycemia, hyperinsulinemia, narcolepsy, pre- 
diabetes and adult onset type II diabetes (insulin resistant diabetes). 
Adult onset type II diabetics produce more insulin than non- 
diabetics, adult onset type II diabetes is not an insulin problem; rather, 
it is a simple mineral deficiency problem. 
The relationship between chromium, vanadium, insulin and heart 
disease shows why the most common cause of death in diabetics is 
cardiovascular disease. This relationship is described by Walter Mertz, 
M.D., who reported that chromium was an essential nutrient in 1955 
- he was soon appointed to the Chairmanship of the USDA Human 
Nutrition Research Center in Beltsville, Maryland. 
“The most consistent effect of marginal chromium deficiency is 
elevated insulin response, and the first effect of chromium supple- 
mentation is the restoration of a normal response. It appears....that if 
we improve glucose tolerance, restore normal insulin levels and at 
the same time lower cholesterol levels - particularly (LDL cholesterol 
- the bad cholesterol) - we may reduce the risk of cardiovascular 
disease.” 
Richard J. Doisy, Ph.D., professor of biochemistry at the State 
University of New York, Upstate Medical Center, tested the effect of 
supplemental chromium on normal twenty to twenty five year olds. 
He found when healthy individuals were given chromium supple- 
ments, their pancreas did not have to produce as much insulin to 
manage their blood sugar levels. “This could be interpreted that even 
in ‘normal’ subjects the dietary intake of chromium is marginal,” 
Dr. Doisy, concluded. “My contention is that probably one-fourth 
to one-half of the people in this nation are deficient in chromium... 
We have relatively high tissue chromium levels at birth and it’s down- 
hill from there on in. And the result for the health of Americans is 
that millions have maturity (type II) onset diabetes. And millions

236 
suffer from vascular disease,” he reported. 
“You tend to increase (the rate of) your chromium excretion when 
you're eating excessive carbohydrates,” said Dr. Doisy. “And if the 
diet is not providing sufficient chromium (to make up for the in- 
creased rate of loss), then you are going to be in negative balance.” 
We have known since the 1957 reports by Walter Mertz, that the 
deficiencies of chromium and vanadium are the primary causes of 
diabetes, however, the concurrent deficiencies of other essential min- 
erals will “throw gasoline on the fire” of diabetes and produce all of 
the complications associated with the chronic form of the disease. 
Chromium is required for circulating glucose tolerance factor to 
function properly. Chromium is an essential trace element necessary 
for the normal glucose metabolism, It’s function is directly related to 
the function of insulin.

Only the trivalent state of chromium is biologically active and it 
works by forming complexes with organic compounds. Insulin-chromium relationships are not restricted to glucose metabolism. Animal 
studies have shown that chromium stimulates amino acid transport 
and protein synthesis. 
Deficiency of chromium is characterized by glucose intolerance 
(hypoglycemia, insulin resistance, hyperinsulinemia and diabetes), 
poor growth, peripheral neuropathies, negative nitrogen balance (loss 
of muscle mass - sarcopenia) and a decreased respiratory quotient. 
The dramatic drop in serum chromium started when the wood 
ash byproduct of cooking and heating was no longer available for 
“culinary” ash - and, when the ashes were no longer being placed in 
our gardens. 
Signs of Chromium Deficiency in the Human 
¢ Impaired glucose tolerance (hypoglycemia, 
hyperinsulinemia, diabetes) 
¢ Elevated circulating insulin 
¢ Glycosuria (sugar in the urine) 
¢ Fasting hyperglycemia (elevated blood sugar) 
¢ Impaired growth 
¢ Decreased longevity 
¢ Elevated serum cholesterol and triglycerides 
¢ Increased incidence of aortic plaque (arteriosclerosis) 
¢ Peripheral neuropathy 
¢ Brain disorders (dementia) 
* Decreased fertility and lowered sperm count

By 1977, K. N. Jeejeebhoy reported that chromium could eliminate the need for medical insulin in human patients. His intravenous 
use of chromium in human diabetics eliminated peripheral neuropathies and well as eliminated the need for insulin treatments to stabilize blood sugar. 
On August 31, 2004 Harvard investigators released the results of

238 
a fifteen year study on toe nail chromium levels and their relation- 
ship to the rate of diabetes. The Harvard School of Public Health 
scientists analyzed the toenails of 33,000 men ranging from 40 to 75 
years of age. They found that the men with the lower levels of chro- 
mium in their toenails had higher rates of heart disease and diabetes. 
The study was published in the September 2004 issue of Diabetes 
Care, the official journal of the American Diabetes Association. 
In 1991, the University of British Columbia declared that, “the 
trace mineral vanadium could replace insulin for adult onset type II 
diabetics.” 
Vanadium appears to function like insulin by altering cell mem- 
brane function for the ion transport process; therefore, vanadium 
has a very beneficial effect for humans with glucose tolerance prob- 
lems by making cell membrane receptors more sensitive to insulin. 
Vanadium inhibits cholesterol synthesis in animals and humans; 
this is followed by a drop in plasma cholesterol and reduced aortic 
cholesterol deposits. 
The number of Americans with diabetes in 1980 was 5.8 million, 
in 2004 the numbers rose to 13.3 million with an estimated 17 mil- 
lion American diabetics undiagnosed or at the pre-diabetic state. 
In between 1988 and 1994, 45.7 % of Americans over the age of 
eighteen years with diabetes were obese and between 1999 and 2002, 
54.8 % of Americans with diabetes were obese. 
What is the Metabolic Syndrome? 
The Metabolic Syndrome is characterized by insulin resistance 
(hyperinsulinemia, hypoglycemia, narcolepsy, pre-diabetes, etc.), 
hypertension, elevated cholesterol and triglycerides and abdominal 
obesity. 
The cause of the Metabolic Syndrome is not genetic, but rather a 
collection of multiple essential nutrient deficiencies including defi- 
ciencies as illustrated on the table of next page.
