The Nigerian Clue 
On February 1999, the journal Scientific American ran a cover 
article that looked at hypertension in African Americans. The article 
featured a blood pressure study including third world Nigerian tribes 
still living in the remote bush country and African Americans of Ni-

240 
gerian decent in the U.S. 
The tribal Nigerians had an average of seven percent of the indi- 
viduals with hypertension, while there was a thirty three percent rate 
of hypertension in the U.S. urban individuals - obviously, hyperten- 
sion in black people is not genetic, if hypertension was genetic, then 
the blood pressure of both groups would be seven percent and seven 
percent! 
What then causes the difference in the hypertension rates be- 
tween the two groups? The primitive tribal individuals still add the 
wood ashes from their heating and cooking fires into their gardens, 
mix the ashes with their salt and use the wood ashes as “culinary” 
ashes mixed with their food - while, their city cousins use electricity, 
natural gas and propane to cook and heat their homes with, and the 
rate of mineral supplementation in the African American commu- 
nity is very low. 
The Metabolic Syndrome sets up a systemic “inflammation 
crises” that produces arteriosclerosis and atherosclerosis of small 
and medium caliber arteries (cerebral, coronary and carotid ar- 
teries) by causing free radical damage to the single layer of inti- 
mal cells that line arteries, and by damaging (free radical dam- 
age) a portion of the circulating cholesterol to produce a higher 
level of LDL or “bad cholesterol.” 
Total cholesterol is analogous to paint on a house. All of the paint 
on the house came from the same paint can, yet the paint on the 
western exposure often times blisters, powders, oxidizes and flakes 
before the rest of the paint because as the sun sets in the west it 
produces an intense heat damage. Like wise the total circulating cho- 
lesterol comes from the same bucket, however, unprotected by anti- 
oxidants, a portion of the total will be damaged and become LDL. 
LDL is not a separate species of cholesterol, it is just damaged choles- 
terol and damaged cholesterol produces inflammation. 
The number of Americans with “high” cholesterol as defined by 
medical doctors was 37 million in 2004. The most popular drug 
prescribed to lower cholesterol was Lipitor (Pfizer) with 2003 sales 
listed as $5.8 billion resulting in an annual cost per patient of $1,100. 
Side effects of Lipitor include kidney damage, muscle weakness and.

HELLS KITCHEN 241 
thabdomyolysis, a muscle breakdown disease. 
The non drug alternatives that can be used to bring cholesterol 
into the normal range (220 - 270), includesya complete supplement 
program that provides all of the known 90 essential nutrients. In 
particular, chromium, vanadium, essential fatty acids, niacin and 
antioxidants (selenium, green tea, vegetables, dark berries, etc.) and 
a low carbohydrate diet are all known to bring cholesterol into the 
normal range. 
In 2004, the number of Americans with hypertension is listed as 
50 million. The most popular drug prescribed to bring blood pres- 
sure under control is Norvasc (Pfizer). In 2003 sales reached $1.9 
billion for an average annual cost per patient of $500. Side effects 
include dizziness, edema, flushing and palpitations. 
Non drug alternatives for controlling hypertension include a com- 
plete supplement program that would promote healthy blood pres- 
sure levels, including a focus on calcium, weight loss (intense min- 
eral supplement programs), the elimination of sugar, carbonated 
drinks, and caffeine - and the significant reduction of carbohydrates. 
A Brief History of Snacks 
Amelia Simmon’s cookbook, originally published in Hartford and 
then Albany in 1796, is considered the first American cookbook, not 
only because it was published after the Revolution, but because it 
was written by an American for Americans. 
Simmons was famous for huge dessert cakes, including her fa- 
mous “Independence Cake” - the recipe called for twenty pounds of 
flour, fifteen pounds of sugar, ten pounds of butter and twenty four 
eggs. Many of her dessert recipes called for “pearl-ash” (white wood 
ash) or potash. 
Simmons Honey Cake 
6 pound flour 1 oz ginger 
2 pound honey Orange peel (orange zest) 
1 pound sugar 2 tsp pearl ash 
2 oz cinnamon 6 eggs

2AZ 
Dissolve the pearl ash in milk, put the whole together, moisten 
with milk, bake 20 minutes. Amelia Simmons, American Cookery 
1796. 
In 1853, Cornelius Vanderbilt, tired of thick American fries, or- 
dered chef George Crum, of the Moon’ Lake lodge in Saratoga, NY, 
to create the first thin “potato chip.” Crum sliced potatoes paper thin, 
fried them, and the “Saratoga chip” was created and the snack food 
industry was born. 
In 1876, Charles Hires sold his root beer at the great Centennial 
Exposition. It contained sixteen roots and herbs including birch bark, 
spikenard, sarsaparilla and hops. He had sold his drink for seven 
years at his Philadelphia pharmacy after creating it on his honey moon 
in 1870. In 1877, Hires sold his root beer by mail order - a 25 cent 
packet that made five gallons. 
In 1880, Ira Remsen published an article describing his acciden- 
tal discovery of saccharin, a chemical substance he says is, “300 times 
sweeter than an equal amount of sugar.” Saccharin is a white crystal- 
line, aromatic compound that had no nutritive value as it passed 
through the body unchanged. 
Remsen felt that saccharin would have great commercial value 
because, “it could prove quite useful for diabetics, for fat people and 
others that could not use sugar.” 
In 1882, on Monday September 4, at 3:00 p.m. the switch was 
pulled and Edison’ first commercial electric generator hummed into 
action, current flowed and a new era of technological progress was 
joyously welcomed in. By 1950 we were totally captives and victims 
of HELLS KITCHEN and the epidemic of obesity, Metabolic Syn- 
drome and diabetes started in America unopposed. 
Between 1882 and 1950 there was not a single voice raised 
to expose the health dangers of a wood ash (mineral) free soci- 
ety. No matter how lofty our goals were for reaching out to space, 
no matter how many multiple organ transplants we performed 
and artificial hearts we installed and no matter how many politi- 
cally correct dreams and laws we wrote for cultural progress, we

HELLS KITCHEN 243 
ultimately would be held back for the lack of the basic elements 
required for the maintenance and repair of our flesh and blood 
bodies. 
In 1885, a new “tonic brain food and exhilarant” (Dr. Pepper) 
went on sale at Wade Morrison’s Old Corner Drug Store. Morrison 
moved to Waco, Texas from Virginia after his courtship with Miss 
Pepper was ended by her father who also happened to be his boss. 
Morrison headed west and bought the pharmacy, where a clerk named 
Charles Alderton and Morrison concocted a new drink - Morrison 
named it after Miss Pepper's father, Dr. Pepper! 
In 1886, John Styth Pemberton, a Civil War veteran and local 
Atlanta, Georgia pharmacist invented Coca Cola, which he sold for 5 
cents per glass. Sufferer’s of all kinds found “consolation” in the new 
coca-extract brew. It was billed asy“the intellectual beverage and tem- 
perance drink.” 
Coca Cola was said, “to cure dyspepsia (indigestion) and head- 
aches.” Pemberton claimed, “that it lifted the spirits as well.” The 
Atlanta pharmacist formed the Pemberton Chemical Company and 
registered a trade mark for French Wine of Coca, “an ideal nerve 
tonic and stimulant.” To attract the teetotalers he took out the alco- 
hol and added caffeine-rich kola nut extract. 
Coca Cola competed with other drinks including Imperial Inca 
Cola, Coca-coffee and Cocafeine. The original coca drink, vin Mariani, 
had won prizes from organizations all over Europe, one of which 
had called it a “wine for athletes.” 
Experts of the day said that, “cocaine can cure everything from 
head colds, to the mental depression that accompanies the hysteria 
(PMS) in the female to lack of energy.” 
Pemberton also created Globe of flower cough syrup, Indian 
Queen Hair Dye and Triplex Liver Pills. 
Pemberton joined forces with entrepreneur, Asa Chandler, who 
immediately saw the wider commercial value of the drink (Coca Cola) 
and turned a local curiosity into a business. The average sales of the 
original product per pharmacy reached nine bottles per day. 
In 1889, Chandler attributed mythical properties to Coca Cola 
and began to advertise it as a “sovereign remedy.” Many scientists of

244 
the day disagreed - by 1890, 400 clinical cases of acute suffering 
from cocaine abuse had been reported in medical journals. Physician 
Albrecht Erlenmeyer calls the drug “the third scourge on mankind,” 
after morphine and alcohol. Parke Davis sells it in cigarettes, oint- 
ments, tablets and injections. 
Angelo Mariani, whose vin Mariani was the drug's first commer- 
cial application, collected a list of well known users including Tho- 
mas Edison, Sigmund Freud and Pope Leo XIII who was said to keep 
a flask of vin Mariani under his belt. 
In 1892, The Edison General Electric Company merged with 
Thomson-Houston Electric to form General Electric. Edison was said 
to have been bitter because the new company did not bear his name. 
In 1894, Joseph Biedenharng was the first to market Coca Cola in 
a bottle. 
In 1894, Milton Hershey, a Lancaster, Pennsylvania candy maker, 
declared that, “caramels are only a fad, chocolate is a permanent thing.” 
He developed a chocolate product in the shape of a rectangular slab. 
The new chocolate “bar” was priced cheaply so it could become an 
everyday treat. The Hershey Milk Chocolate Bar, which also comes 
with almonds, is “prepared with skimmed milk from Pennsylvania 
COWS.” 
In 1895, the USDA reported that Americans consumed one half 
pound of sugar per person per year. 
In 1898, Caleb Bradham, a New Bera, North Carolina Pharma- 
cist, prompted by the success of Coca Cola, created a similar carbon- 
ated drink. Originally the new fountain beverage was called “Brad’s 
Drink,” but it was later changed to Pepsi Cola and bottled to widen 
sales. 
In 1899, two lawyers, Benjamin E. Thomas and Joseph White- 
head secured exclusive rights to bottle and sell Coca Cola for one 
dollar. 
In 1916, the Root Glass Company of Terre Haute, Indiana cre- 
ated the famous Coca Cola female body shaped bottle. The motto 
was, “You can identify Coca Cola, even in the dark.” 
In 1900 Coca Cola served two bottles per day and by 1920 Coca 
Cola served 1,000 bottles per day.

HELLS KITCHEN 245 
In 1920, brand named and packaged foods hit the market place 
because masses of Americans and peoples in all industrialized na- 
tions had moved off the land into the cities. People no longer had 
their subsistence gardens, people were no longer independent and 
people had begun to switch from wood as the primary fuel for cook- 
ing and heating to coal, oil, kerosene, propane and electricity - the 
wood ashes we had grown up with as humans were becoming “as 
scarce as hen’s teeth.” 
Underwood sardines, La Choy Chinese food, Maxwell House 
coffee (Teddy Roosevelt coined the phrase “its good to the last drop”), 
Sunkist oranges, Campfire marshmallows, Kellogg’s All-Bran cereal, 
Good Humor Ice Cream Suckers, and Baby Ruth bars (named for the 
daughter of former President Cleveland) and Brillo to scour pots and 
pans were the products of the day. 
The “ice box” that one had to fill with block ice to keep food cool 
and keep milk sweet was replaced with the electric Frigidaire. Silk 
stockings are out and the new rayon hosiery from Du Pont Fibersilk 
Company was the rage of the day. The variety of automobiles was 
staggering - Cadillac, Chalmers-Franklin, Chevrolet, Maxwell, 
Mercedes, Milburn Electric, Packard, Peerless, pierce-Arrow, Salient, 
Stephens, Stutz and Ford Model T (the “fliver” is good for the liver). 
In 1923, Robert Woodruff, five years after his father purchased 
the Coca Cola Company, began to distribute the product world wide. 
By 1925, Coca Cola sales were generating $6 million in sales per 
day; four years after Albert Einstein won the Nobel Prize in physics 
for his theory of relativity. 
In 1928, Coca Cola ads followed the American Olympic Team to 
the Amsterdam Olympics; the Coca Cola ads were emblazoned on 
racing dog sleds in Canada and Bull fighting arenas in Spain. 
Coca Cola introduces the “six-pack” and the open topped cooler 
concepts. 
In October 18, 1931 Thomas Edison died at the age of 84 from 
Bright’s disease (kidney disease with protein in the urine), uremia, 
gastric ulcers and diabetes (he was no longer putting wood ashes 
from heating and cooking fires into his garden and his daily fair al- 
ways included sugar in the form of apple dumplings and Coca Cola).

246 
In 1933, the Tennessee Valley Authority (T.V.A.) was created in 
the first 100 days of the FDR administration. It came under constant 
attack by the private power generating sources, including a power 
corporation headed by Wendell Willke, a powerful Wall Street attor- 
ney, who argued, “To take away our market is to take our prosperity.” 
The Supreme Court ruled that the constitution did not give anti- 
competition protection to private utility companies. The T.V.A. was 
formed to bring electrical power and industry to the poorest part of 
America. The states that benefited were seven Southern and border 
states ranging from the Smokey Mountains, coal towns, bleak moun- 
tains and drought ridden cotton farms. T.V.A. promoted flood con- 
trol, produced clean hydroelectric power and developed land and 
forest management programs. 
In the beginning of T.V.A. there were only two out of 100 people 
in the Tennessee Valley who had access to electric power and many 
families lived on less than $100 per year. 
In 1941, Coca Cola’s Woodruff ordered, “Every man in uniform 
gets a bottle of Coke for 5 cents, wherever he is, and whatever it costs 
the company.” 
In 1943, General Dwight D. Eisenhower, sent an urgent cable- 
gram to Coca Cola, requesting the shipment of enough materials to 
build ten bottling plants. Between 1941 and 1960, the number of 
countries bottling Coca Cola doubled. 
In 1957, Walter Mertz, an American chemist discovered that he 
could prevent and cure adult onset type II diabetes in laboratory 
animals, pets and farm animals with a simple supplement program 
that contained chromium and numerous cofactors necessary for the 
chromium to support insulin’s job of getting sugar from the blood 
through the cell membrane into the cell. 
Adult onset type II diabetes is not an insulin problem or insulin 
deficiency, patients with adult onset type II diabetics often times make 
considerably more insulin than non diabetics. Adult onset type II 
diabetes, like obesity, is simply a mineral deficiency problem. 
The obvious question is, if diabetes is a simple mineral deficiency 
problem, how come it still plagues modern man? Unfortunately there 
are no laws requiring doctors to cure patients when there are cures.

Also, urban populations no longer put wood ash minerals into

After seventy five years, Coca Cola began to introduce additional

By 1977, Walter Mertz’ 1957 animal work with chromium and

In 1978 the Coca Cola Company secured the sole rights to mar-

In 1981, Robert E. Goizueta became Chairman of the Board and

In 1991, researchers at the University of British Columbia, 
Vancouver, Canada, stated that the trace mineral vanadium could 
replace prescription insulin for treating and managing type II diabet-

Safe Cooking 
It is quite clear that what one eats and what one doesn‘ eat will 
determine ones health and length of life. It was determined after 
having performed 17,500 autopsies on more than 454 species of 
animals and 3,000 humans - every animal and every human that 
dies of natural causes dies of a nutritional deficiency disease. 
To do everything we can to maximize health and longevity we 
have to guarantee ourselves an optimal stream and proper amounts 
of all essential nutrients each day - we cannot depend on good luck; 
we must be proactive! 
Once we assure ourselves that we are consuming optimal amounts 
of the essential nutrients, we must also protect ourselves from dan- 
gerous choices of foods, food ingredients, storage practices and cook- 
ing methods. So we are going to look at and review some pretty basic 
stuff we all need to be aware of every time we buy, store, cook and 
consume food. 
Free Radicals 
The term free radical is a generic term that covers a broad classi- 
fication of dangerous food chemicals that are produced either di- 
rectly or indirectly by man. Free radicals increase our risk of many of

252 
the dangerous side effects of obesity and weight loss programs, they 
increase our risks of cancer, cardiovascular disease, diabetes, 
Alzheimer’s disease, multiple sclerosis, ALS (Lou Gehrig's disease), 
Parkinson’s disease, macular degeneration, cataracts, fibromyalgia and 
they accelerate the aging process. 
By eliminating the intake of free radicals one can significantly 
reduce their risks of many diseases. A good insurance policy for pro- 
tecting yourself against the accidental or unknowing consumption 
of free radicals is to flood yourself with multiple antioxidants at ev- 
ery meal. The old adage, “An ounce of prevention is worth a pound 
of cure” surely fits here - if taking antioxidants everyday can signifi- 
cantly reduce one’s risk of many terrible diseases, why would a lucid 
person not take antioxidants? Why wait until a disease strikes you 
before you have a motivational concern? 
Additionally, as a person who is overweight or obese loses 
weight, the byproducts of “burning” fat can turn into free radi- 
cals that can increase the risk of many diseases, including in- 
flammation of the intima (the lining of the arteries) which then 
produces arteriosclerosis, cardiovascular disease, cancer, 
Alzheimer’s disease, etc. To reduce the risk of the complications 
of weight loss, people need to flood themselves with antioxidants in order to protect themselves.

Trans Fatty Acids 
Trans fatty acids are a form of free radicals that are universally 
found in margarines, cooking oils (yes, even olive oil), salad dressings and processed foods that contain these ingredients. Trans fatty 
acids are extremely dangerous food chemicals that are formed by 
oxidized (chemically rancid) fat. These types of fats are found in just 
about all processed foods recommended by doctors, and the FDA 
estimates that there are more than 42,000 products containing trans 
fatty acids on grocery store shelves in America. 
Harvard estimates that trans fatty acids directly kill 30,000 Ameri- 
cans each year and many more indirectly. The American romance 
with trans fatty acids began in the 1950s driven by the medical trades 
unrealistic paranoia of saturated animal fats and cholesterol (which 
humans had been eating for thousands of years) and a false belief 
that they were the “boogey man” that caused cardiovascular disease. 
Following the lead of the medical hysteria, food manufacturers 
rushed to find something that could replace animal fat - they grabbed 
at vegetable oils (a product that had been abandoned by the animal 
feed industry because plant oils had produced many new diseases 
including muscular dystrophy, pancreatitis, cardiomyopathy, and

254 
fibromyalgia). 
Oils and margarines were cheaper than animal fat (lard, suet, etc) 
and butter and had a longer shelf life. Chefs could buy vegetable oils 
in large containers that “didn’t need refrigeration,” thus allowing them 
to keep it on the self, however, as the oil sat on the shelf it slowly but 
steadily oxidized and metamorphosed into trans fatty acids. 
Cooks liked the new oils because they could be kept hot all day 
without smoking up the kitchen- they could use the same oil over 
and over, however, the constant heat quickly converted them into 
trans fatty acids - a free radical that few in human medicine knew 
anything about. 
Trans fats were a dream come true for the fast food and junk food 
industries. Trans fatty acids made potato chips, crackers and cookies 
crispier and they allowed manufacturers to add tasty fat in foods that 
had never had fat in them before (Oreo cookie filling, etc.). Trans 
fatty acids are solid at room temperature, oils on the other hand are 
liquid and would have turned the foods into a slimy greasy mess. 
Manufacturers could now “lock” fat into their products. 
“Besides increasing the number of LDL (bad) cholesterol and trig- 
lycerides in the bloodstream, trans fats also lower your HDL (good) 
cholesterol,” says Bruce Holub, Ph.D., a professor of nutrition at the 
University of Guelph, Canada. “Trans fats also increase blood levels 
of acompound called lipoprotein a. The more of this lipoprotein you 
have in your system, the greater your risk of developing heart dis- 
ease,” he said. 
In a current review of the Harvard Nurses Health Study, for 80,000 
nurses over the age of thirty five, found that getting only three (3) 
percent of your total daily calories from trans fatty acids increases 
your risk of heart disease by fifty (50) percent! Three percent of your 
calories from trans fats (7 grams of trans fat) are provided by a single 
order of fries. 
Trans fatty acids increases your risk of diabetes, sarcopenia (muscle 
wasting associated with age) and cancer. When Harvard researchers 
reviewed all the previously published data on trans fats, they found 
that men and women with the highest daily intakes were also the 
most at risk for developing diabetes.

Heterocyclic Amines 
Heterocyclic amines are free radicals that are found in well done 
meat and burnt animal fat. They are extremely dangerous and can 
increase one’s risk of breast, prostate and colon cancer by as much as 
462 percent! 
In a Harvard Nurses Health Study of 90,000 nurses over a twenty 
year period, they compared the rate of cancer between those who 
consumed less than twenty percent of their calories as fat versus those

256 
who consumed more than fifty percent of their calories as fat and 
found there was no difference in the rate of cancer. 
In a 1998 study by the University of South Carolina, they found 
that consuming burnt animal fat increased the risk of cancer by a 
staggering 462 percent! 
It is not the amount of fat in your diet that increases your risk of 
obesity, diabetes and cancer it's the type of fat and how you cook the 
fat! 
Acrylamides 
Acrylamides are free radicals that are generated by “super heat- 
ing” carbohydrates and sugars. Croutons, fried potatoes, crackers, 
cookies, cold cereals, hard crusted breads, browned sugars and pie 
crusts are loaded with acrylamides. These free radicals are known to 
increase the risk of cancer significantly. 
Red Meat 
Red meat is an unequaled source of animal protein and essential 
lipids - the secret is to not eat meat cooked well done or eat the fat 
burnt as they become heterocyclic amines. Medium rare and medium are the safe way to eat beef. Steak Tartar is a half pound of raw, 
freshly ground sirloin, served with chopped onions, pepper corns 
and topped with a raw egg - it doesn’t get any better than this! 
Salt 
Salt your food to taste. Salt does not cause high blood pressure 
and restricting salt will not reduce your risk of high blood pressure 
or add ten seconds to your life - in fact restricting salt will shorten 
your life and make your very short life miserable. 
Salt is the raw material needed to manufacture stomach acid. 
Stomach acid is hydrochloric acid that is necessary to digest food, 
ensure absorption of minerals, activate pepsin (protein digestive enzyme produced by the stomach) and activate the “intrinsic factor”

more than two grams of salt per day. 
Restricted salt intakes will make you heat intolerant and over the 
long term will result in digestive problems including belching, burping, intestinal gas and G.E.R.D. (gastro esophageal reflux disease). 
Restricted salt diets with the resultant hypochlorhydria (low stomach acid) will increase risks of nutritional deficiency diseases including obesity (reduced absorption of minerals increases the munchies),

Reading Labels 
In January 1, 2006, food manufacturers were required to list trans 
fats on the nutrition facts and some supplement facts panels, right 
below saturated fats. Trans fats are formed when manufacturers “hy- 
drogenate” vegetable oils to make them solid at room temperatures. 
Trans fats are found in margarines, fried foods, cooking oils, baked 
cookies and cakes, chips, seasoned popcorn and croutons. 
Label Dictionary 
Fat-Free - Less than 0.5 gm of fat per serving, with no added fat or 
oil. 
Low-Fat - 3 gm of fat or less per serving

260 
Cholesterol-Free - Less than 2 mg cholesterol per serving and 2 gm 
or less saturated fat per serving. 
Reduced-Calorie - At least 25% fewer calories per serving than the 
comparison food. 
Low-Calorie - 40 calories or less per serving 
Light (Fat) - 50% or less of the fat than in the comparison food (.e. 
- 50% less fat than our regular cheese) 
Light (calories) - 1/3 fewer calories than the comparison food 
High-Fiber - 5 gm or more fiber per serving 
Sugar-Free - Less than 0.5 gm of sugar per serving 
Sodium-Free (Salt-Free) - Less than 5 mg of sodium per serving 
Healthy - A food low in fat, saturated fat, cholesterol and sodium, 
and contains at least 10% of the Daily Values for one or more of 
vitamin A, vitamin C, iron, calcium, protein or fiber. 
“High,” “Rich in” or “Excellent Source” - 20% or more of the Daily 
Value for a given nutrient per serving 
“Good Source of,” “contains” or “Provides” - Meal or dish pro- 
vides 10% to 90% more of the Daily Value for a given nutrient than 
the comparison meal or dish.

Dr. Wallach’s See Food Diet 
While there are literally thousands of weight loss programs and 
diets available to everyone, they all have many common denomina- 
tors. All weight loss and diet programs are not guaranteed, they are 
not successful 100 percent of the time, most starters drop off of the 
program over time and most gain all or more of their starting weight 
back again after dropping off the diet. 
The basic reasons for the universal failure of “weight loss diets” 
of any ilk, is that they don’t deal with the basic root cause of obesity 
- the mineral deficiencies. Therefore, the primary difference of the 
Dr. Wallach’s See Food Diet, the principal that sets it apart from the 
others is that the pica, cribbing, cravings, binge eating, the munchies 
and the “stand in front of the open refrigerator” syndrome is dealt 
with and eliminated - these basic failure factors having been removed 
will guaranty success for everyone. 
The diets generally fall into three basic types: 
1. Low calorie - The old standby is the low calorie diet. The think- 
ing is simply that a calorie is a calorie and the fewer you eat the less 
you will weigh and the more weight you can lose. The low calorie 
diet proponents believe if you eat fewer calories than your basal needs,

264 
the more weight you will lose. 
Promoters of the low calorie diets falsely claim that dietary supple- 
ments are not required. You have to count calories, points, or por- 
tion sizes. (Jenny Craig, Weight Watchers). 
In 1963 there were 400 clients registered with weight watchers; 
by 2003 there were 34,600,000 registered clients. 
2. Low fat - The proponents believe that fat is the enemy and 
tend to eat lots of plant based foods rather than animals foods. Un- 
fortunately, the devotees don’t differentiate between good fats and 
bad fats, which results in many health problems, including an in- 
creased risk of stroke, Alzheimer’s disease, seizure disorders and 
asthma. 
The low fat approach to weight loss suggests less than ten per- 
cent of daily calories come from dietary fat. The concept is, by cut- 
ting out fat you can eat one third more total food each day without 
taking in more calories. The claim is that the body only needs four to 
six percent of the calories from fat to maintain health (in actuality, 
thirty five percent of daily calories provided by fat is a more correct 
amount). This diet is very difficult to adhere to and is potentially 
dangerous. (Ornish, Pritikin) 
3. Low Carb - The total amount and the type of carbohydrates 
are limited to reduce the body’ glucose and insulin levels in an effort 
to make weight loss and weight management easier. 
In the diet’s initial phase, one can eat unlimited meat and fat, 
however, carbohydrates must be limited to twenty grams of carbohydrates or less per day (approximately one slice of bread). 
For the first fourteen days, the dieter consumes no fruit, bread, 
pasta, grains, milk or even ketchup. The stated purpose of the low 
carb diet, “is to create a state of ketosis, encouraging your body to 
burn fat for energy rather than carbohydrates.” 
Eugene Fine and Richard Feinman, of the SUNY Downstate Medi- 
cal Center of New York believe that “critics of low-carbohydrate diets 
always invoke the laws of thermodynamics, but they’re not under- 
standing them properly.” Feinman claims there are two laws, not one, .

HELLS KITCHEN 265 
that are involved. 
Opponents of the low carb diets often emphasize the first law of 
thermodynamics - that the total energy in a system is always con- 
served. Feinman considers the second law - that energy tends to dis- 
sipate over time - just as important, it determines what happens to 
energy in food when it is broken down in the body. 
“We know on a molecular level that the body’ pathways are less 
efficient at turning protein calories into glucose,” says Feinman, “That 
means that more energy is lost as heat.” So more of each calorie will 
convert into heat if it comes from protein; more of the calories will 
remain in the body if they come from carbohydrates. 
The idea is to satisfy the taste buds with large amounts of rich, 
sumptuous foods to reduce the cravings. They certainly have forgotten the minerals and an unsupplemented high protein diet can increase the risk of kidney stones. (Atkins, Southbeach, The Zone, Dr. 
Wallach’s See Food Diet) 
In May of 2004, The Valen Group, a consulting firm for the For-

their intake of carbohydrates and an additional 40 million said they 
were considering a low carb diet in the next twelve months. Collec-

Dr. Wallach’s See Food Diet is perhaps the best of the best when

The Principals of a Low-Carb Diet 
In the shortest possible description and purpose of a low carb 
diet, one can say that it is conceived to keep blood sugar levels from 
having wild fluctuations. The purpose is to “steady” blood sugar levels, which will help reduce episodes of ravenous hunger. 
Foods that produce a slower rise in blood sugar after being con- 
sumed are said to have a low glycemic index. Fat has a glycemic 
index of zero because it does not affect blood sugar directly or imme- 
diately. Whole grains, vegetables and proteins are considered low 
glycemic foods because they produce a very slow, gradual, time re- 
lease increase in blood sugar. 
The reality is, low or zero glycemic foods do help you feel full 
longer, however, this theory presumes the reason that a person is 
overweight or obese is just because they can't control their eating 
habits for lack of education, personal will or self control. 
The original Atkins Diet critically slashed carbohydrates (i.e. - 
bread, pasta, rice, sugared soft drinks, potato chips, cookies and fruit.) 
which was driven by the Seven Food Group Pyramid and promoted 
by the USDA, and medical doctors had caused sixty five percent of

HELLS KITCHEN 267 
Americans to be overweight and forty percent to be obese and turn 
adult onset type II diabetes into an epidemic disease. 
Atkins’ followers were allowed large portions of high fat red meat, 
cheese, butter, and oils that traditional low fat diets had prohibited. 
The dieter could eat large bowls of greens and low carb vegetables. 
The South Beach Diet restricts carbs, fruits, sugars, fat laden meat 
and cheese. 
According to many experts, the reason that low carb diets worked 
was that, “they were boring, at some point we become so tired of any 
given thing that we eat, that it has no continuing hedonic effect on 
us,” says Brian Wansink, Ph.D., University of Illinois. Additionally 
he found that, “the more variety in your diet, the more food you'll 
eat, as everyone who has dined at a lavish buffet can attest.” 
Therefore, they theorize, “taking the boredom out of a low carb 
diet with low carb comfort foods, such as ice cream, chips, and beer 
could reduce it’s effectiveness.” “Originally, the Atkins diet was black 
and white,” said Alice H. Lichtenstein, D.Sc., professor of nutrition 
science and policy at Tufts University. “I can have the steak but not 
the baked potato. Now I can have the steak, but I can also have the 
low carb pasta on the side.” 
In summary, experts believe the reason that the Atkins approach 
worked was that blood sugar was more stable and that a low carb 
diet was boring and didn’t encourage over eating of total calories. 
Net Carbs and Sugar Alcohols 
Sugar alcohols and fermentable carbohydrates are safe, although 
their consumption may produce diarrhea and gas in susceptible people 
or following over consumption. There are some studies that show 
that they can aid in blood sugar control, but there is no evidence that 
by consuming these foods weight loss will be easy. 
In low carb labeling, “net carbs” refers to the total grams of car- 
bohydrates per serving minus grams of fiber and sugar alcohols. The 
term “net carbs” has no legal recognition by the FDA as of 2004. 
Sugar alcohols, or polyols include erythritol, hydrogenated starch 
hydrolysates [HSH], isomalt, lactitol, maltitol, mannitol, sorbitol, and 
xylitol.

268 
Polyols are not digested in the small intestine the way that regu- 
lar sugars are. Instead, they pass through to the large intestine, where 
they are digested by bacterial fermentation. Thus they do not elevate 
blood sugar and insulin levels as much or as quickly as table sugar 
does, giving them a low glycemic index. But like sugars, which have 
4 calories per gram, they do contribute calories, ranging from a low 
of 0.2 per gram for erythritol to 3 calories per gram for HSH. 
Fermentable carbohydrates include inulin, resistant starch, and 
polydextrose - this “catchall” refers to smooth, bland-tasting, highly 
processed ingredients (derived from corn) that can be used in place 
of high carb white flour and counted as fiber. Fermentable carbohy- 
drates are digested the way that sugar alcohols are. They have be- 
tween 1.6 and 2.5 calories per gram (compared with 4 calories per 
gram for table sugar and processed starch).

no longer driven by a command to eat.

Burn, Baby - Burn! 
Antoine Lavoisier (1743 - 1794) and his coworker P. S. Laplace 
(1749 - 1827), a mathematician, confined a guinea pig in oxygen for 
ten hours and measured the carbon dioxide produced. He also mea- 
sured the amount of oxygen used by a man active compared with 
him at rest. In these experiments, he was able to show that combus- 
tion of carbon compounds with oxygen to carbon dioxide and water 
is the true source of animal heat and that oxygen consumption in- 
creases during physical work. 
“Respiration is merely a slow combustion of carbon and hydro- 
gen, which is similar in every respect to that which occurs in a lighted 
lamp or candle, and, from this point of view, animals (and humans) 
that breathe are really combustible bodies which burn and are con- 
sumed.”

2i2 
(CH,O) + O, > CO, + H,O + energy (heat) 
Lavoisier was guillotined on May 8, 1794 during the French Revo- 
lution. The judge presiding over Lavoisier’s fate was reported to say, 
“The Republic has no need of savants (scholars).” 
Ingenhousz adopted Lavoisier’s thoughts on gases and theorized 
that the plant was not simply exchanging “good air” for “bad.” In the 
sun, he suggested a plant “absorbs the carbon from carbon dioxide, 
throwing out at that time the oxygen alone, and keeping the carbon 
to itself as nourishment.” 
Nicholas Theodore de Saussure (1767 - 1845) used Lavoisier’s 
theories of quantitative measurements and showed that, “equal vol- 
umes of CO2 and O2 are exchanged during photosynthesis, and that 
the plant does indeed retain the carbon (and store “solar income”).” 
He also showed that, “during photosynthesis the plant gained more 
weight than could be accounted for by the carbon taken in as carbon 
dioxide.” In other words, “the carbon in the dry matter of plants 
comes from carbon dioxide, but, equally important, the rest of the 
dry matter (with the exception of the minerals from the soil) comes 
from water.” 
Now, all of the steps of photosynthesis were identified - carbon 
dioxide, water, and light: 
CO, + H,O + energy (light) > (CH,O) + O, 
Photosynthesis is respiration or combustion reversed! In photo- 
synthesis (which occurs in specific organelles called chloroplasts), 
light energy is converted to chemical energy, and carbon is fixed into 
organic compounds such as sugar, carbohydrates, oils, structural 
polysaccharides (including cellulose), amino acids, enzymes, DNA 
and RNA. 
Humans take in the stored “solar income” chemical energy as 
food and convert it to another form of chemical energy (metabolism) 
or mechanical energy (movement). Excess intake of dietary energy 
above the basal needs for any reason will result in a net weight gain. 
There are many approaches to accelerating weight loss which as

HELLS KITCHEN 273 
a group are referred to as “thermogenics.” They will be discussed 
here becausey humans are impatient and they want immediate re- 
sults from any activity, and it can certainly be appropriate to add 
thermogenics to a weight loss program. 
We like to provide and feature the nutritional program that will 
eliminate pica, cravings, binge eating and the munchies that will re- 
sult in a steady loss of weight and a return to the individual’ ideal 
weight. With this said, we appreciate the emotional support that 
thermogenics can provide. 
Exercise 
Humans are designed for exercise; in an anatomical sense, the 
human’ ability to run separates us from the tree dwelling apes. Ac- 
cording to researchers from Harvard University, there are no less than 
twenty six anatomical and physiological traits that make humans 
capable of long distance running. Over long distances, humans can 
outrun monkeys, dogs and horses. 
While humans cannot run as fast, for short distances, as mon- 
keys, horses, greyhounds or antelopes, humans can keep on going 
and going. 
Benjamin Franklin determined that the best way to measure ex- 
ercise, was not by it’s duration, but rather “by the degree of warmth it 
produces in the body.” Franklin’s theories on exercise were amongst 
the first linking exercise to calories of heat produced and the number 
of calories burnt. For example, Franklin explained, “walking a mile 
up and down stairs produced five times more body warmth than 
walking a mile on a level surface. 
When swinging weights, Franklin calculated, “that this raised his 
pulse from 60 to 100 beats per minute.” He correctly calculated that 
body “warmth generally increases with quickness of pulse.” 
Beside one’s basal requirements for energy, physical exertion or 
exercise is the second greatest influence on the energy requirements 
of humans. Exercise, as a consumer of energy, may be as little as ten 
percent of the total energy requirements (bed ridden patient, com- 
puter technician, executive, etc.) of an individual, or up to fifty per-

Lies 
cent in laborers or athletes. 
A man performing heavy physical activity farmer) may require 
4,800 or more kcalories per day, while an individual of the same 
body build, weight, age and height in the same environment, how- 
ever, performing light work (book keeper) would require only 2,500 
kcalories per day. 
The inactive or sedentary human usually requires thirty percent 
more kcalories in addition to the basal requirement; by comparison, 
a lightly active human may need fifty percent more kcalories beyond 
basal requirements; a moderately active human would require sev- 
enty five percent additional kcalories above basal needs; and a la- 
borer or athlete might require an additional 100 percent more 
kcalories over basal needs.

body weight, the greater the caloric requirement and therefore the 
kcalorie expenditure for an activity. It takes more energy for an obese 
human to walk for ten minutes than it does for a slim individual. 
On December 23, 2004 the Harvard Nurses Health Study showed 
that obese women who exercise for 3 1/2 hours per week will still die 
sooner than women who are toned and trim. Women who were both 
sedentary and obese were almost 2 1/2 times more likely to die. 
“There has been some suggestion that if you are particularly ac- 
tive, you don’t have to worry about your body weight, or about your 
diet. That's very misleading,” says Frank Hu of the Harvard School of 
Public Health, lead author of the study published in the New En- 
gland Journal of Medicine. 
The study of 116,500 women, showed that obese women who 
worked out for at least 3 1/2 hours weekly had a death rate that was 
91 percent higher than lean women who exercised for 3 1/2 hournper 
week. Lean women who exercised less than that increased their risk 
of early death by 55 percent, compared with lean women who worked 
out more frequently - for inactive obese women the premature death 
rate was 142 percent greater. 
Walking or light “express” exercise programs can produce the 
same weight loss result as a heavy weight or machine program in a 
gym after twenty weeks, when combined with a 1,200 to 1,500 calo- 
rie diet. At the end of twenty weeks the individual can lose seven to 
eight percent of their starting weight, after forty weeks their weight 
loss totaled eight to nine percent according to the University of Penn- 
sylvania. 
In 1987 there were 17,351,000 health and fitness club member- 
ships in the USA; in 2003 the numbers climbed to 39,400,000. 
In another study, investigators at the University of Pittsburgh 
monitored the weight loss of fifty nine women who were limited to 
1,200 to 1,500 calories per day, found that behavior modification, 
walking, walking and light strength training, or walking and yoga 
lost twenty two to twenty eight pounds in six months. 
Experts recommended that people try to take 10,000 steps per 
day (about five miles). Walking a mile requires about 2,000 to 2,500 
steps.

Pet Exercise 
Walking one’s dog helped individuals to lose weight and keep it 
off. A study reported in 2004 that when people and dogs were put 
on a diet and exercise weight loss program, they both lost weight and 
kept it off; although dogs did better than their humans and the pro- 
gram did not increase begging. 
Sixty five percent of Americans and twenty five percent of dogs 
are overweight or obese. “If youre looking for motivation and social 
support to lose weight, you probably don’t have to look any further 
than the pet in your own home,” said Dr. Robert Kushner of North- 
western Medical School in Chicago, who led the study. 
The study was funded by Hill’s Pet Nutrition, which makes Sci- 
ence Diet and a prescription diet dog food. The study was made up 
of three groups: 56 people; 53 dogs; and 36 dogs and their owners. 
The participants attended weekly counseling sessions at Northwest- 
ern on diet and exercise, and were encouraged to walk at least twenty 
minutes and limit calories to 1,400 per day - dogs were fed a weight 
loss prescription diet. 
The dog owners did slightly better than the dieters who walked 
and dieted without the dog. Overall, people lost eleven pounds or 
five percent of their starting body weight in the first four months and 
kept it off for the next eight months. The diet and exercise program 
was more enjoyable for the dog owners. 
The weight loss for the dogs was even more effective. They lost 
an average of twelve pounds - fifteen percent of their initial weight - 
one dog actually lost 35 pounds. “Begging behavior did not go up,” 
said Dennis Jewell, a Hills animal nutritionists who did the dog-diet 
part of the study. 
We would be remiss if we didn’t point out that “exercise without 
supplementation is suicide.” There has never been a professional ath- 
lete from a major professional sport who has lived to be 100. The 
reason for this surprise observation is that athletes sweat more than 
the average person. 
Sweat is not just water, sweat is a soup that contains all of the 
known essential nutrients - if the lost nutrients are not replaced prop-

The Knife Diet 
While not classically thought of as a “thermogenic,” bariatric sur- 
gery should be considered as an option for those who are morbidly 
obese. As with all surgery, there are risks associated with bariatric 
surgery. Bariatric surgery has a complication rate of seven (7%) per- 
cent including death. 
In a major government decision that attempted to turn obesity 
from the status of a personal failure to a medical problem, Medicare 
announced that it would remove barriers to covering anti-obesity 
treatments after forty years of stating, “fat was not an illness and not 
covered.” 
The change was anticipated to open the door for government 
funding of various bariatric surgeries and intensive diet and exercise 
programs. The program will “put more pressure (on private insurers) 
in terms of medical and surgical procedures,” says health financing 
researcher John Newman of Georgia State University. 
In 1992, 16,200 bariatric surgeries were performed; by 2002 the 
number had risen to 63,100; and by 2003 the annual numbers reached 
144,000. 
The medical community was overjoyed at this decision, because 
it will cause obese people to preferentially opt for expensive surgery 
without any effort on the part of the individual rather than choose 
the non-surgical programs. With sixty five percent of Americans de- 
scribed as being overweight and thirty one percent of them obese, 
the increase in the financial pressure on Medicare and private insur- 
ance will prove to be catastrophic. 
1. Roux-en-Y gastric bypass: The most popular of the three most 
common “bariatric” surgeries - staples are used to create a small pouch 
in the stomach connected to the bowel by a piece of the small intes- 
tine, bypassing the majority of the stomach. It accounts for up to 
90% of the “bariatric” procedures performed in the USA. 
2. Biliopancreatic diverson: This procedure involves removing a

280 
large portion of the stomach and bypassing much of the small intes- 
tine. 
3. Gastric banding: This procedure uses a band to pinch off a small 
portion of the stomach. The band can be adjusted to allow more or 
less food to enter the stomach. About 9% of the bariatric procedures 
in the USA employ this technique. 
4. Jaw wiring: Jaw wiring is a primitive surgical approach to weight 
control. Yes, by starving the patient they will lose weight, however, 
when the jaw is unwired the weight comes right back because the 
basic problem of mineral deficiencies has not been dealt with. 
HGH 
Human Growth Hormone: Human growth hormone or it’s precur- 
sors are known to conserve body protein and muscle mass while 
encouraging the burning of body fat. 
The reduced production of HGH associated with aging is some- 
times called “somatopause.” Somatopause is characterized by symp- 
toms of aging including wrinkles, gray hair, decreased energy, de- 
creased sexual function, increased body fat (obesity), increased risk 
of cardiovascular disease, osteoporosis, etc.

Anorectic Drugs 
Anorectic drugs: Anorectic drugs include pharmaceuticals and herbs 
that have a profound effect on the central nervous system to sup- 
press the appetite. All anorectic drugs have serious health and life 
threatening side effects. 
Thyroid Hormone 
Thyroid hormone: It is clear that both the caloric content and the 
carbohydrate content of the diet affect thyroid metabolism. The thy- 
roid gland functions as a thermostat for the body’s basal temperature 
and metabolic rate. Increases in calories and carbohydrates increase 
the metabolically active hormones, while extreme restriction decreases 
the levels of active thyroid hormone. 
Pyruvate 
Pyruvate: Is a part of the Kreb Cycle, the cellular metabolic pathway 
that generates energy by burning carbohydrate. When supplemental

HELLS KITCHEN 283 
pyruvate is added to the metabolic system, more energy is burned - 
on a low carbohydrate diet, pyruvate will increase the rate that the 
body converts stored fat into carbohydrate or ATP which is then 
burned. This process takes some time, so there can be a delay of as 
much as six to eight weeks before significant weight loss occurs - 
once weight loss begins, however, it will be steady and predictable. 
Chocolate 
Chocolate is thought of as an indulgence, as something that over- 
weight and obese people gorge themselves on - yet chocolate can be 
one of the great natural herbal weight control products ever known 
to man. 
Studies published in the American Journal of Clinical Nutrition and 
the Journal of the American College of Nutrition, reported that choco- 
late does not raise cholesterol or increase LDL. 
In a 2003 issue of the journal, Hypertension, it was reported that 
the flavonoids in cocoa could help increase blood flow to the heart, 
brain, hands and legs. 
An article in the Journal of Agriculture and Food Chemistry, reported 
that cocoa has a higher antioxidant rating than tea or red wine. 
When people crave chocolate, they are really mineral deficient 
(pica, munchies). In fact, bitter chocolate has a very low glycemic 
index because up to seventy percent of chocolate’s calories come from 
fat, and fat does not raise blood sugar. 
The fat in chocolate, cocoa butter, is comprised of stearic acid 
(34 percent), palmitic acid (20 percent) and oleic acid (30 percent). 
Both stearic and palmitic acids are saturated fats; oleic acid is a 
monounsaturated fat that is also found in olive oil. 
Cocoa powder is made when the fat, or cocoa butter, is pressed 
out of the “liquor” - most cocoa powders have little or no fat. 
Chocolate is a rich source of cocoagin, caffeine, theobromine, 
flavonol and flavonoids. These compounds have thermogenic and 
antioxidant properties that make bitter chocolate a great weight loss 
and free radical neutralizing herb.

284 
Meal-Replacement Diets 
Meal-Replacement Diets: Meal replacement diets are very useful in 
weight loss and weight management programs because calories are 
very accurately controlled and most contain some added nutrients. 
We like to have meal replacements used for one or two meals per 
day; two days per week the meal replacements can be used for all 
meals. The remainder of the meals should contain varying types of 
proteins, green leafy vegetables, steamed vegetables and a serious 
anti-munchies mineral supplement. 
Meal replacement diets should be high protein, moderate fat, low 
carbohydrate and minimal to zero sugar. 
The basic root cause of obesity, it’s symptoms and it’s complica- 
tions is a multi-mineral deficiency - this deficiency must be dealt 
with to eliminate obesity and maintain the individuals optimal weight. 
The benefit of any individual or combination of thermogenic pro- 
grams is to support and accelerate the process of weight loss - used 
as the sole approach to solving a cosmetic or clinical weight prob- 
lem, thermogenics, used alone will always fail over the long haul. 
Thermogenics by themselves cannot safely or permanently pro- 
duce a healthy weight loss program, as these weight loss approaches 
only deal with the symptom of obesity - the fat, and they do not deal 
with the general systemic cause of obesity - the mineral deficiency! 
And they don’t deal with the need for antioxidants to protect the 
individual from the free radical damage that will be generated as a 
result of “burning” body fat.

Recipes to Live For 
Human beings need to consume “90 essential nutrients” on a 
daily basis to promote optimal health, fitness and longevity - fortu- 
nately our food plants (grains, vegetables fruits and nuts) can take 
carbon dioxide from the air, use the sun’s energy or “solar income”, 
and with a process known as photosynthesis manufacture long car- 
bon chains and oxygen. The oxygen is released into the atmosphere 
and the carbon chains are then configured by the plants into starch 
(grains, potatoes, squash, etc.), sugar (sugar cane, fruit, beets, etc.), 
cellulose (plant structure, wood, plant fiber, etc.), vitamins, amino 
acids, fatty acids and phytonutrients (antioxidants, flavonoids, alka- 
loids, glycosides, etc.). 
Unfortunately, plants cannot manufacture minerals and two thirds 
(60) of our ninety essential nutrients are minerals. Plants obtain min- 
erals daily for their own use from the soil. Plants only need nine (9) 
minerals to be optimally healthy, humans need sixty (60) essential 
minerals - typically farmers only add three (3) elements (NPK - ni- 
trogen, phosphorus, potassium) to their fields, because plants will 
produce maximum yields in terms of tons and bushels per acre when 
fed these three elements. 
Unfortunately, mineral nutrients do not occur in a uniform blan-

288 
ket throughout the crust of the earth - minerals occur in veins like 
chocolate in chocolate ripple ice cream and in veins like gold and 
silver. One is more likely to win $1,000,000 a day in Las Vegas than 
they are to get all of their mineral nutrients just by “eating well.” 
The bottom line is, we as humans cannot get all the nutrients we 
need just by eating well - we could spend a million dollars per day 
on exotic food and we still could not get everything we need in opti- 
mal amounts - how many wealthy people live to be 100 years of age? 
We should eat to live rather than live to eat - when one is in the 
mode of “I live to eat” they are mineral deficient (pica, munchies), 
however, with this said, there is no reason that food shouldn't be fun, 
tasty, nutritious and presented well. Food and dining are much more 
than just protein, carbohydrate, fat and fiber - dining is more than 
just putting stuff in our mouth - eating is a family event, a social 
event, a production, an educational experience and on many occa- 
sions, a business event. 
Henry A. Wallace, former Secretary of Agriculture, stated, “Man, 
for 99.9 percent or more of his life here on earth lived without the 
‘benefit’ of the starchy grains like wheat, oats, barély, rice and Indian 
corn.” Man from the beginning was first a carnivore and only be- 
came an omnivore (eats both meat and grains) some 10,000 years 
ago when organized agriculture almost totally replaced hunting in 
the “Fertile Crescent” of Iraq. 
Therefore it is not surprising that people today do better on low 
or moderately low carbohydrate diets. This chapter, “Recipes to Live 
For,” reflects recipes with a relatively low carbohydrate intake con- 
sistent with man’s early low carbohydrate nutritional history and ex- 
pects that each of us will be personally responsible for our daily in- 
take of the supplemental ninety essential nutrients with a special 
emphasis on the sixty essential minerals. 
The application of wood ashes to early gardens, the early use of 
“culinary” ashes that were added to food, either directly or indirectly 
mixed with salt began in a parallel world wide fashion about 30,000 
to 50,000 years ago. In urban communities worldwide today, avail- 
able food no longer contains the wood ash minerals that had sup- 
ported and fulfilled man’s mineral needs for tens of thousands of

HELLS KITCHEN 289 
years. 
From 30,000 B.C. to Monday September 4, 1882 at 3:00 p.m. 
man’s mineral (wood ashes) enriched food provided a steady stream 
of essential minerals - this all ended when Edison's technician pulled 
the lever to start the first commercial electrical generating plant - by 
1950, wood ashes as an essential part of urban mans life and culture 
had gone the way of the buggy whip following the appearance of the 
automobile. 
We surely don’t miss the buggy whip, that was the “accelera- 
tor” for the horse, because we have an accelerator or gas pedal in 
the car; however, we have developed a pandemic and epidemic 
of mineral deficiency diseases in humans including obesity, dia- 
betes and the Metabolic Syndrome because we have given up 
our wood ash source of minerals without replacing them with 
another source of plant minerals. 
The knowledge, recognition and acceptance of food's nutritional 
chemical limits and mineral shortcomings, still allows us to eat well 
and yet recognize that food alone can no longer provide all that hu- 
mans require for optimal health and maximum longevity. 
Nutritional supplements, especially minerals, should then be 
as comfortable on the family and commercial dining table as salt 
and pepper shakers, spices and sauces.

Spinach-cheese omelet 
Nonstick cooking spray 
4 eggs, dash salt 
Dash cayenne pepper 
1/4 cup shredded sharp cheddar cheese (1 oz) 
1 Tblsp snipped fresh chives, flat-leaf parsley or chervil 
1 cup fresh spinach leaves well washed to remove sand 
Red pepper relish 
1. Lightly coat an unheated 8-inch nonstick skillet with flared 
sides with a non-stick cooking spray. Preheat skillet over medium- 
high heat. 
2. In a medium bowl, combine eggs, salt and cayenne pepper. 
Beat with a wire whisk until frothy. Pour into prepared skillet; cook 
over medium heat. As egg mixture sets, run a spatula around edge of 
skillet, lifting egg mixture so that the uncooked portion flows under- 
neath. 
3. Continue cooking and lifting edges until egg mixture is set, 
but is still shiny and moist. Sprinkle with the cheddar cheese and

HELLS KITCHEN 291 
chives, parsley or chervil. Top with 3/4 cup of the spinach and 2 
Tblsp of the red pepper relish. 
4. Using the spatula, lift and fold an edge on the omelet partially 
over filling. Top with remaining spinach and 1 Tblsp of the remain- 
ing red pepper relish. Cut omelet in half for two servings. 
Red pepper relish: in a small bowl, combine 2/3 cup chopped 
red sweet pepper, 2 Tblsp finely chopped onion; | Tblsp cider vin- 
egar; and 1/4 tsp ground black pepper. Makes 2/3 cup of relish. 
French Omelet 
2 eggs 
2 Tblsp water 
1/8 tsp salt 
Dash freshly ground black pepper 
1 Tblsp butter 
Filling (mushroom, cheese, fruit) 
1. In a small bowl, combine eggs, the water, salt (omit salt if 
using cheese filling) and pepper. Beat lightly with whisk until com- 
bined but not frothy. Heat an 8-inch non-stick skillet with flared 
sides over medium-high heat until skillet is hot. 
2. Add butter to skillet. When butter has melted, add egg mix- 
ture to skillet; lower heat to medium. Immediately begin stirring the 
eggs gently but continuously with a wooden spatula until mixture 
looks like very soft scrambled eggs. Stop stirring. Cook for 30 to 60 
seconds more until eggs are set, but still glistening and soft. 
3. Add a filling. With a spatula, lift and fold an edge of the omelet 
about a third of the way towards the center. Remove from the heat. 
Fold the opposite edge of the omelet toward the center; transfer to a 
warm plate. 
Mushroom filling: In a small skillet, cook 1/3 cup sliced fresh mush- 
rooms in | tsp hot butter until tender - spoon across center of om- 
' elet. 
| Cheese filling: Sprinkle 1/4 cup shredded cheddar, Swiss or Monterey

Jack cheese across center of omelet. 
Fruit filling: Spread 2 Tblsp dairy sour cream or yogurt across cen- 
ter of omelet. Fold in two opposite sides. Top with 1/4 cup halved 
fresh or frozen strawberries, blueberries or raspberries. 
Farmer’s breakfast 
1 Thlsp olive oil 
1/2 Cup loose-pack frozen, diced hash brown potatoes 
with onion and peppers (thawed). 
1/2 cup chopped, cooked ham or hamburger meat 
3 Jumbo eggs 
3 Tblsp water 
2 Tblsp shredded cheddar cheese 
1. Ina medium skillet, heat oil over medium heat. Add potatoes 
in a single layer. Cover and cook for 5 to 7 minutes or until potatoes 
are tender, stirring once. Stir in meat. 
2. Ina small bowl, combine eggs and the water; beat with a ro- 
tary beater. Pour over potato mixture. Cook over medium heat, with- 
out stirring, until egg mixture begins to set on the bottom and around 
the edge. Using a spatula or large spoon, lift and fold the partially 
cooked egg mixture so that the uncooked portion flows underneath. 
3. Continue cooking over medium heat until egg mixture cooked 
through, but is still shiny and moist. Sprinkle with cheese. Remove 
from heat. Cover and let stand for 1 to 2 minutes or until cheese is 
melted. Makes two servings.

Roasted tomatoes with shrimp and feta cheese 
5 large tomatoes cut into eighths 
3 Thlsp olive oil 
2 Tblsp minced garlic 
3/4 tsp Kosher salt 
3/4 tsp freshly ground black pepper 
1 1/2 lbs medium shrimp, peeled and deveined 
1/2 cup chopped fresh parsley 
2 Tblsp lemon juice 
1 cup Feta, crumbled 
Preheat oven to 450 degrees F Place the tomatoes in a large bak- 
ing dish. Spoon the olive oil and garlic over the tomatoes. Sprinkle 
with the salt and pepper and toss. Place on top rack of oven and 
roast for 20 minutes. Remove baking dish from oven and stir in the 
shrimp, parsley and lemon juice. Sprinkle the mix with the feta cheese. 
Return dish to the oven for an additional ten to fifteen minutes (until 
shrimp are cooked). Serve warm.

3/4 teaspoon freshly ground black pepper 
4 large tomatoes, cored and chopped (3 cups).

HELLS KITCHEN 297 
1/2 cup chopped sweet onion 
1 large garlic clove, minced 
1/2 cup chopped fresh mint 
1/4 cup red wine vinegar 
Place the tenderloins in a medium bowl. Add the mineral en- 
riched noni juice, 1 Tblsp of the olive oil, 1 tsp of the salt, and 1/2 
tsp of the pepper. Combine these ingredients to coat the pork well - 
cover and set aside. Combine the tomatoes, onion, garlic and mint in 
a medium bowl. Whisk together the red wine vinegar and the bal- 
ance of the olive oil, salt and pepper. Pour over the tomato mixture 
and toss well; cover and set aside. Place a large nonstick skillet or a 
grill pan coated with cooking spray over medium heat. When hot, 
add the tenderloins and cook for three minutes on each side. Reduce 
heat to medium and cook 6 to 8 minutes on each side to brown 
evenly. Remove from skillet to a platter and let stand for five minutes. 
Slice each tenderloin into eight equal pieces, and serve with 1/3 cup 
of the tomato-mint salsa. Serve with sliced avocado and warm flour 
tortillas.

Smoked Salmon Cucumber Rolls

1/2 cup honey mustard 
1 English cucumber, peeled, seeded, trimmed into 2-inch 
julienne strips

cucumber julienne at one end, and roll into a tight bundle. Arrange 
on tray, cover loosely with clear wrap and refrigerate until it is served.

1/4 cup pine nuts, toasted, coarsely chopped

(let ends hang over sides). In a mixing bowl, mash the goat cheese

up. Sprinkle with remaining pine nuts and season to taste with fresh 
ground pepper. Serve with toasted pita chips. 
Steamed Artichokes with Garlic Butter 
For the Artichokes 
2 tsp pickling spices 
4 large fresh whole artichokes 
1/2 lemon, medium 
For the Garlic Butter 
3/4 cup unsalted butter 
1 large clove garlic, minced 
1/4 tsp Kosher salt 
Put one inch of water and the pickling spices into a pot wide 
enough to hold the artichokes. Set a steamer rack into the pot, but 
not touching the water. Bring the water to a simmer over moderate 
heat. Trim artichokes, slice off stems for steaming later, rub exposed 
base with sliced lemon. Remove top one third of artichoke with ser- 
rated knife. Use scissors to trim off points of each artichoke leaf. Rub 
cut surface with sliced lemon. Place artichokes in steamer, stem side 
up. Cover the pot and adjust the heat to bring water to a simmer and 
- steam artichokes until base is tender - about 30 - 40 minutes.

300 
To make garlic butter, melt butter in small sauce pan over low 
heat. Add garlic and salt; warm slowly for one minute. 
To serve, drain artichokes on paper towels, serve warm on plates, 
divide garlic butter into four small side bowls. 
Peppered Shrimp 
1 cup extra virgin olive oil 
2 Tblsp toasted sesame oil 
3 inch ginger root, sliced thin 
1 bunch scallions, coarsely chopped 
1 Tblsp cracked black pepper 
1 Tblsp soy sauce 
1/2 tsp cayenne 
Zest strips from 2 oranges 
40 jumbo shrimp, peeled and deveined 
Kosher salt 
Heat olive oil and sesame oil until smoking, add ginger and scal- 
lions and combine for one minute. Remove from heat, cool for one 
minute and add pepper, soy sauce and orange zest. Cool completely, 
then add raw shrimp - marinate for thirty minutes at room tempera- 
ture. Remove shrimp from marinade, lightly pat dry with paper towel 
and season lightly with salt. Heat the shrimp in a heavy cast iron 
skillet, until hot and opaque throughout. Let cool and serve at room 
temperature on large garnished serving platter. 
Snacks and Desserts 
Nuts 
Nuts are a wonderful low glycemic index snack - their high fat 
content does not raise blood sugar and they are a source of essential 
fatty acids. Nuts are energy dense and nutrient dense - 160 calories 
per ounce. A study done by the Center for Health Research at Loma 
Linda University, California showed that adding 250 calories of nuts 
(36 almonds) to an individual’ diet for six months did not lead to a 
weight gain.

HELLS KITCHEN 301 
Eating a handful of nuts each day without cutting back on carbo- 
hydrates and other sources of fat will translate into a 10 to 20 pound 
weight gain over a year. 
Giving up potato chips and candy will make nut consumption 
safe in terms of simple calorie exchange. If a person is still in the 
clutches of pica or the munchies, and will eat until the container is 
empty, portion control can be used - measure out the daily limit of 
nuts into small zip-lock bags. 
Even the FDA allows, “Scientific evidence suggests, but does not 
prove, that eating 1.5 ounces per day of most nuts, as part of a diet 
low in saturated fat and cholesterol, may reduce the risk of heart 
disease.” 
Dr. Walter C. Willett, author of The Harvard Medical School Guide 
to Healthy Eating, writes, “Contrary to popular opinion, nuts are not 
junk food. They are actually a great source of protein and other nu- 
tritional goodies. One of the more surprising findings from nutrition 
research over the past decade is that people who regularly eat nuts 
are less likely to have heart attacks or die from heart disease than 
those who rarely eat them.” 
Walnuts: English walnuts are a good source of iron, phosphorus, zinc, potassium, and vitamin E; they are the only nut with significant levels of omega-3 fatty acids. One ounce (14 halves) contains 190 calories and 18 grams of fat. 
Almonds: are rich in vitamin E and calcium (6 percent of the 
USDA recommended daily value). One ounce (24 almonds) con- 
tains 160 calories and 14 grams of fat. 
Hazelnuts (Filberts): 24 nuts provide twenty percent of the 
USDA recommended daily value of vitamin E and ninety percent of 
the manganese. One ounce (20 nuts) contain 180 calories and 17 
grams of fat. 
Brazil nuts: can concentrate the trace mineral selenium if it is in 
the soil in which they are growing. One ounce (6 - 8 nuts) contains 
190 calories and 19 grams of fat.

302 
Pistachios: a handful of pistachios gives twenty five percent of 
the USDA recommended daily value for vitamin B6. One ounce (50 
dry roasted nuts) contains 160 calories and 13 grams of fat. 
Pecans: contain a relatively high percentage of fat, however, it is 
“sood fat” (monounsaturated fat) - one ounce (20 halves) contains 
200 calories and 20 grams of fat. 
Macadamias: are “butter smooth” and contain the highest level 
of fat (including saturated fat) of all nuts. One ounce (10 to 20 roasted 
nuts) contains 200 calories and 22 grams of fat. 
Cashews: a handful contains 20 percent of the USDA recommended daily allowance of magnesium, 30 percent of copper, and 
10 percent of iron. One ounce (18 roasted nuts) contains 160 calories and 13 grams of fat.

salt. Serve warm or at room temperature. 
To bring out the maximum flavor of nuts, toast in a preheated 
oven at 350 degrees F for ten to fifteen minutes. 
Dark Berries 
Dark berries are a great source of antioxidants, taste and fun. 
Freezing allows you to buy them during harvest season and have 
them in recipes throughout the year. Strawberries, raspberries, black- 
berries and blueberries can be used as great tasting and healthful 
additions to whey shakes, eaten as a berry salad before or after a

HELLS KITCHEN 303 
meal, they can be used as toppings for no sugar added ice cream or 
just served with heavy cream as a desert. 
The texture of frozen berries is considerably different than that of 
fresh berries. The natural water in the frozen berries forms ice crys- 
tals that cut through the cell walls. When the frozen berries are thawed, 
juices flow out of the damaged cell walls and the character of the 
berry turns from firm to soft. Blueberries tend to maintain their shape 
and firmness better than other berries. Berries that are handled prop- 
erly and frozen in rigid containers or freezer friendly zip-loc plastic 
bags can maintain their taste and eating quality for a year at 10 de- 
grees F or below. 
An important part of the freezing process for berries is the prepa- 
ration: 
Strawberries - put just 3 or 4 strawberries at a time in a quart con- 
tainer of water, swirl briefly to wash off sand and other debris and 
gently remove - do not allow them to soak. Using a sharp knife, 
remove stem and leaves and halve or keep whole. There are two 
good methods for freezing strawberries. 
1. For whole, unsweetened berries, remove berries from the wash 
water, place on several layers of paper towels and gently pat dry. Line 
a cookie sheet with plastic wrap or wax paper, then place the berries 
in a single layer and cover with a paper towel. Place in the freezer. 
When the berries are frozen hard, repack them in freezer containers 
or plastic freezer bags - remove as much air as possible without scuff- 
ing the berries and return to the freezer. 
2. Add a small quantity of pectin and sugar to enhance the reten- 
tion of shape and taste. Wash (using above method) 1 gallon of whole 
berries. Remove stems and leaves and halve berries. Mix together 
1/2 cup granulated sugar, 1/2 cup Sucralose and 2 Tblsp of pow- 
dered pectin. Stir gently into berries; let stand for ten minutes to 
allow the dry ingredients to dissolve and juices to be produced. Place 
in freezer containers or plastic freezer bags - leave 1 inch of space per 
container. This mix will make 6 pints of sweetened frozen berries. 
Caneberries (red raspberries, black raspberries, boysenberries, lo-

304 
ganberries and Marionberries) - place berries in a colander and lightly 
rinse. Drain well and place in a single layer on cookie sheets lined 
with plastic wrap or wax paper. Cover with paper towels. Place in 
the freezer until firm (1 hour). Remove berries and place in freezer 
containers or freezer bags. Caneberries freeze well without added 
sugar, however, if you wish you can add 1/8 to 1/4 cup of Sucralose 
per quart of berries. 
Blueberries - choose blueberries that are a deep blue color and cov- 
ered with a fine white powdery dust (“bloom”) on the skin. Put blue- 
berries in a colander and rinse carefully (washing after thawing is not 
recommended). Drain well and place in freezer containers or plastic 
freezer bags and place in the freezer. 
Grilled Nectarines with Blackberries and Ice Cream 
2 pints blackberries 
1 tsp fresh lemon juice; more to taste 
2 Tblsp unsalted butter, melted 
2 Tblsp honey 
1/2 tsp ground cinnamon 
4 large nectarines 
Vanilla ice cream (no sugar added) 
Crisp butter cookies 
Fresh mint sprigs 
Ina blender, mix I pint of blackberries with 1/3 cup of water and 
the lemon juice. Puree until smooth. Pass the puree through a fine 
sieve, pressing down with the back of a ladle. Adjust the flavor with 
more lemon juice or a little Splenda to taste. Reserve this coulis. 
Heat the grill to medium high. In a shallow bowl or lipped plate, 
stir together the melted butter, honey and cinnamon. Cut each nec- 
tarine in half and remove the pit. Slice a bit off of the skin side so that 
the fruit will lay flat when served. Roll each half in the melted butter 
mix. Grill, pit side down, until the fruit is softened and the pit side 
begins to darkin and caramelize (about 4 minutes). 
Put a nectarine half (or two halves if they are small) pit side up in 
the center of each plate. Drizzle some of the blackberry coulis around

HELLS KITCHEN 305 
the fruit and sprinkle with the blackberries. Top each nectarine while 
still warm with a scoop of the no sugar added vanilla ice cream. 
Garnish with a crisp butter cookie and sprig of mint - serve before 
ice cream melts. 
Creamy Mixed Berry Parfait 
6 oz cream cheese, softened 
1/3 cup Splenda Lite, sifted 
1 cup heavy cream, chilled 
Pinch table salt 
1 1/2 Thlsp fresh lemon juice 
1 tsp finely grated lemon zest 
1 Tblsp finely chopped fresh mint; plus 4 sprigs for garnish 
4 cups fresh or frozen berries (1 1/3 cups each of 
raspberries, strawberries and blueberries). 
Combine the softened cream cheese, Splenda Lite and salt in a 
medium bowl. Using a hand-held electric mixer, beat on low speed 
until the cream cheese is smooth and the Splenda is incorporated. 
Add the heavy cream and beat on low speed until the mixture is 
smooth, stopping to scrape down the sides of the bowl as needed. 
Increase the speed to medium high and beat until the cream is bil- 
lowy and holds medium firm peaks (30 to 60 seconds). Add the 
lemon juice, zest and the mint. Stir briefly to blend. Line up four 
parfait glasses or stemmed wineglasses. Beginning and ending with 
the berries, evenly layer all the berries and all but 2/3 cup of the 
cream into the glasses. Top with a dollop of the remaining cream and 
garnish with a mint sprig.

Grilled everything salad 
2 Tblsp prepared horseradish 
3 Tblsp red wine vinegar 
5 Tblsp extra virgin olive oil 
1 tsp Kosher salt

3 carrots, thinly sliced and grilled (3 cups) 
3 plum tomatoes, halved and grilled

and pepper in a small bowl. Set aside. Arrange the spinach on a

vinaigrette and serve.

Crab and Avocado salad 
1 Tblsp heavy cream 
2 Tblsp buttermilk 
1/2 tsp lemon zest 
2 tsp fresh lemon juice; more for sprinkling

310 
1/2 tsp Dijon mustard 
Kosher salt and freshly ground black or white pepper 
8 oz crab meat (lump) - remove all shell pieces 
1/2 cup finely chopped celery (inner ribs and leaves) 
3 Tblsp chopped fresh chives (1/2 in pieces) 
1 ripe avocado, peeled, pitted - cut into 1/2 in pieces. 
1 head Boston or Bibb lettuce (for lining plates) 
In asmall bowl, whisk the heavy cream, buttermilk, lemon zests, 
lemon juice and mustard. Season with salt and pepper to taste. In a 
medium bowl, mix the crab meat, celery and 2 Tblsp of chives. Fold 
in the dressing gently to avoid breaking up the crab meat chunks.

plates with lettuce leaves, heap a scoop of the crab salad on each, 
garnish with the balance of the chives. Sprinkle each salad with 1/2 
tsp lemon juice and serve. 
Bistro salad with warm goat cheese Dressing 
1/2 cup toasted hazelnuts (skinned) 
4 oz log fresh goat cheese 
1 Tblsp sherry vinegar 
3 Tblsp hazelnut oil 
Kosher salt 
Freshly ground black pepper 
4 hands full of small lettuce leaves (3 varieties) - 6 cups 
Washed and thoroughly dried 
Preheat oven to 400 degrees F and lightly coat a baking sheet 
with a cooking spray. Roughly chop half of the hazelnuts and finely 
chop the remaining half. Slice the goat cheese into 4 equal portions. 
Press the finely chopped hazelnuts into the cheese rounds to coat 
them on all sides. Set the coated rounds on the baking sheet and 
bake until heated (6 - 8 minutes). In a small bowl, whisk together 
the vinegar, oil, a pinch of salt and a sprinkling of freshly ground 
pepper. Taste and adjust seasonings. When the cheese is ready, toss 
the lettuce with the dressing until the leaves are evenly coated. Mound 
the lettuce on individual plates, top with a round of warm goat cheese,

HELLS KITCHEN 311 
scatter the balance of the hazelnuts over all dishes.
