12 sprigs fresh cilantro (1 packed cup); more leaves 
for garnish 
1 small head garlic, cross cut at middle; plus 1 Tblsp 
minced garlic. 
3 Tblsp unsalted butter 
1 large onion, sliced (to yield 1 1/2 cups). 
2 tsp Kosher salt; add more to taste 
1 Tblsp minced fresh ginger 
2 Tblsp minced jalapeno 
1/2 tsp cumin seeds

312 iositigr eee ee eee SS SS 
1/2 tsp coriander seeds 
1/8 tsp cardamom seeds or one green pod 
1 lb zucchini (unpeeled), sliced 1/2 in thick 
1/4 tsp fresh lime juice; more to taste 
For the raita: 
1/2 cup whole-milk yogurt 
1/4 tsp Kosher salt; more to taste 
1 tsp olive oil 
1/2 tsp brown mustard seeds 
1/4 tsp cumin seeds 
To make the soup: Put the cilantro, halved garlic head, and 5 
cups of water in a large saucepan. Bring to a boil, reduce to a simmer, 
and cook uncovered for 15 minutes. Strain the broth. Melt the butter 
in a large heavy saucepan over a medium heat. Add the onion, 2 tsp 
salt, ginger, jalapeno, cumin seeds, coriander seeds and cardamom. 
Cover and cook, stirring occasionally, until the onion is translucent, 
about 10 minutes - don’t let it brown. Stir in the Tblsp minced garlic 
and the zucchini, cover, and cook, stirring occasionally until the 
squash is softened (about 5 minutes). Add 4 cups of the broth; bring 
to a simmer; and cook until the zucchini is tender (about an addi- 
tional 3 minutes). Puree the mixture in a blender and strain through 
a fine sieve. Add the lime juice; taste and add salt if needed. Serve 
warm, garnished with | - 2 Tblsp raita and the cilantro leaves. 
Make the raita: In a small bowl combine the yogurt and salt. Heat 
the oil in a small skillet. When hot, add the mustard and cumin 
seeds. When they start to pop, add the oil and the seeds to the yogurt 
and stir. 
Cucumber soup with avocado 
1 large (8 oz) white sweet onion, peeled and quartered 
1 lb cucumbers, peeled, seeded, and cut into large chunks 
1 medium ripe avocado 
1 1/2 cups plain whole-milk yogurt 
3 Thlsp extra virgin olive oil 
1 - 2 cloves garlic, minced

HELLS KITCHEN 313 
3/4 tsp ground cumin (toasted and freshly ground) 
1 tsp Kosher salt; more to taste 
1/3 cup loosely packed fresh basil leaves 
2 Tblsp fresh mint leaves 
2 -3 Thlsp fresh lemon juice 
Freshly ground black pepper 
Put the onion chunks in the food processor, pulse to chop finely. 
Scoop into a sieve, rinse under cold water, and set aside to drain. Put 
the cucumber chunks in the food processor, pulse to chop finely. 
Add the drained onion and pulse to combine. Set aside 3/4 cup of 
the cucumber onion mix to stir into the soup at the end. Cut the 
avocado in half and remove the pit. Scoop out the pulp and drop it 
into the food processor with the remaining cucumber onion mix- 
ture. Add the yogurt, olive oil, garlic, cumin and salt. Puree until 
smooth. Using a sharp knife, slice the basil and mint leaves into shreds 
and then stir the into the soup along with the lemon juice, 1 cup cold 
water and the reserved cucumber onion mixture. Season to taste with 
salt and pepper. Cover and chill for at least a half hour (several hours 
are preferred). Before serving, check for consistency, gradually add- 
ing another 1/2 to 1 cup cold water to thin the soup if necessary. Add 
more lemon juice and salt if needed and serve. 
Portuguese Canja: Chicken soup 
6 cups homemade or low salt chicken broth 
1/2 tsp Kosher salt; more to taste 
1/2 cup long-grain rice (basmati) 
1 boneless, skinless whole chicken breast, cut into 
1 x 1/2 -inch strips or 1/2 in dice. 
1/4 cup fresh lemon juice 
Fresh ground black pepper to taste 
6 Tblsp chopped fresh mint 
Combine 1/2 cup of the broth, 1/2 cup water, and the salt in a 
small, heavy saucepan. Bring to a boil and stir in the rice. Reduce the 
heat to low, cover the pan and simmer until all the liquid is absorbed 
and the rice is just tender (20 minutes); set aside. In a large, heavy

314 
saucepan, bring the remaining 5 1/2 cups broth to a simmer over a 
minimum heat. Add the chicken, reduce the heat to a gentle simmer, 
and poach until the chicken is firm and just cooked through G - 5 
minutes). If the broth is cloudy, you can remove the chicken, strain 
the liquid through a cheesecloth - lined strainer, and return the 
chicken to the broth. Add the reserved rice and the lemon juice and 
season with salt and pepper to taste. Sprinkle on the mint and serve 
immediately. 
Tomato bisque 
2 Tblsp olive oil 
1 small onion diced 
1 clove garlic, minced 
3 large sprigs fresh thyme 
1 can (28 oz) crushed tomatoes in puree 
1 1/2 cups homemade or low salt chicken broth 
3 Tblsp honey 
1 1/2 tsp Kosher salt; more to taste 
1/4 tsp finely ground pepper; more to taste 
1/3 cup heavy cream 
2 Tblsp chopped fresh flat leaf parsley 
In a medium pot, heat the oil. Add the onion and cook over 
medium heat, stirring frequently, until tender and lightly browned 
on the edges (approximately 7 minutes). Add the garlic and thyme; 
stir until fragrant (1 minute). Add the tomatoes, broth, honey, salt 
and pepper. Bring to a boil over high heat. Reduce the heat and 
simmer, stirring frequently until reduced by a quarter and thickened 
(approximately 15 minutes). Using a stand or immersion blender, 
puree about half the soup; it will be chunky and thick. Return all the 
soup to the pot and stir in the cream. Heat gently and adjust the 
seasoning. Ladle the soup into bowls, sprinkle with the parsley, and 
serve the soup immediately.

2 Tblsp chopped fresh chives 
Melt the butter in a large skillet over medium heat. Add the on- 
ion and cook 5 minutes. Add the snap peas and cook, stirring occa- 
sionally, until bright green and crisp-tender, about 5 minutes. Mix in 
the Sucralose and salt and cook 1 minute more. Stir the half and half 
with 3 cups of water in a large measuring cup. Place half the snap pea 
mixture in a blender or food processor. Pour in half of the water 
mixture and puree until smooth (30 - 60 seconds). Repeat with the 
remaining ingredients. Pour the soup through a strainer and chill for 
at least 4 hours. Sprinkle with the chives just before serving - makes 
6 bowls.

Tomato Marmalade 
1 cup Splenda Lite sugar substitute 
3 cups red wine vinegar 
20 tomatoes, peeled, seeded, and finely diced 
1 Tblsp cumin seed 
4 - 8 jalapenos, seeded and finely diced 
20 black peppercorns 
Zest and juice of 2 large oranges 
Zest and juice of 2 lemons 
1. In a large saucepan, combine the sugar and vinegar and sim- 
mer over medium heat for 5 to 10 minutes, until the mixture reaches 
a syrup texture. 
2. Stir in the tomatoes, jalapenos, cumin seed, peppercorns, or- 
ange and lemon zest and juices. Cook over low heat, stirring fre- 
- quently, until marmalade reaches a thick consistency, 20 to 25 min- 
utes, depending on the juiciness of the tomatoes.

316 
3. Pour into a jar or other container and store in the refrigerator. 
Serve cold. Can be used on breakfast toast or bagel or on a ham- 
burger. 
Picasso Blue Salsa 
2 cups fresh blueberries, divided 
1 - 2 serrano peppers, chopped (with seeds) 
2 Tblsp creamed hot pepper honey 
1/2 tsp grated ginger root 
Juice of 1/2 lime 
1/2 tsp salt 
2 Tblsp minced purple onion 
4 - 6 fresh mint leaves, coarsely chopped 
1. Place 1 cup blueberries, serranos, honey, ginger, lime juice 
and salt in food processor. 
2. Process about 20 seconds until pureed. 
3. Add remaining blueberries, onion and mint leaves, then pulse 
several times. Mixture should be slightly chunky. 
4. Refrigerate for at least one hour before serving. 
This salsa is good on salmon, smoked turkey and pork tender- 
loin. 
Rubs 
As the name implies, rubs are rubbed into the food with your 
fingers prior to cooking to impart an “extra measure of flavor.” 
Cajun Rub 
This rub is especially useful for pork, chicken and seafood. In a 
small bowl, combine | 1/2 tsp ground white pepper, 1 1/2 tsp black 
pepper; 1 tsp cayenne pepper; | tsp dried thyme, crushed; 1 tsp 
onion powder; | tsp garlic powder; and 1 tsp salt. 
To use, sprinkle rub evenly over pork, chicken or fish; rub in 
with your fingers - grill or broil until done. Can store in a tight con- 
tainer for up to three months. Mixture makes seven tsp, enough for

HELL'S KITCHEN 317 
about 3 1/2 pounds of meat poultry or fish. 
Lemon-Herb Rub 
This zesty lemon rub will add a zippy accent to pork or beef. Ina 
small bowl, combine 8 cloves garlic, minced; 1 1/2 tsp fine lemon 
zest; 1 tsp dried rosemary, crushed; 1/2 tsp salt; 1/2 tsp dried sage, 
crushed; and 1/2 tsp ground black pepper. 
To use, sprinkle mix evenly over beef or pork; rub in deeply with 
fingers. Grill or broil until done (medium rare). Makes 2 Tblsp of 
mix - enough for two to three pounds of beef or pork. 
Jamaican Jerk Rub 
Jamaican jerk (barbecue) can be wet, dry, sauced or rubbed, and 
all of these styles have their fans. In a small bowl, combine 2 tsp 
Splenda; 1 1/2 tsp onion powder; 1 1/2 tsp dried thyme, crushed; 1 
tsp ground allspice; 1 tsp ground black pepper; | tsp cayenne pep- 
per; 1/2 tsp salt; 1/4 tsp ground nutmeg; and 1/8 tsp ground cloves. 
To use, sprinkle mix evenly over meat or chicken; rub in with 
fingers. Grill or broil meat (medium rare) and poultry (medium) until 
done. Make 8 tsp or enough for four pounds of meat or chicken. 
Chili Rub 
Chili rub can be used effectively for beef, pork and poultry. In a 
small bowl, combine 1 Tblsp paprika; 1 tsp ground cumin; 1 tsp 
chili powder; 1/2 tsp salt; 1/2 tsp ground coriander; 1/2 tsp ground 
black pepper; and 1/2 tsp ground cayenne pepper. 
To use, sprinkle mix evenly over beef, pork or chicken until done. 
Make six tsp, enough for three pounds of beef, pork or chicken. 
Water 
Water is essential for life, and we are made up of sixty to seventy 
percent water. Good water is essential to any successful weight loss 
and weight control program. Eight to ten glasses of water each day 
will help the absorption of mineral nutrients (which helps to elimi- 
nate pica), water helps to flush out the dangerous byproducts of “burn-

318 
ing” fat and water gives the sensation of fullness. 
Distilled water is 100 percent water with absolutely no contami- 
nants but it tastes “flat” and over time will pull minerals from your 
body. A good compromise is to use distilled or double filtered water 
that has been “enriched” with liquid colloidal minerals at the rate of 
one ounce of the liquid colloidal minerals (19,000 mg of minerals 
per quart) per gallon of distilled or double filtered water. 
We get lots of questions about water, so we looked for and found 
the opinions of professional wine tasters for different brands of bottled 
water. A panel comprised of Rod Smith, wine columnist for the Los 
Angeles Times; Peg Melnick, wine writer for the Santa Rosa Press Demo- 
crat; Jeff Cox, contributing editor for The Wine News (and Men’s 
Health); Bill Traverso, wine buyer and judge for many prestigious 
national wine competitions; and Dan Kosta, wine maker at the Kosta 
Browne Winery in Sonoma County, California. 
The waters were graded ona 1 - 20 scale (20 being the best of the 
best): 
Fiji Natural Artesian Water - Bottled at the source on the main 
island of Viti Levu in Fiji, this slightly alkaline water contains dis- 
solved calcium, magnesium and colloidal silica. Score: 19. Com- 
ments: “Tastes like spring water.” “Fat, round with a hint of flowers.” 
“Clean.” “Sweet and pleasant.” 
Dasani Purified Water - Made by the Coca Cola Company from 
filtered municipal water, which is then “spiked” with minerals. Score: 
17. Comments: “The cleanest tasting water in the bunch.” “Dry, clean 
- even crisp.” “It'S smooth and pure tasting.” 
Ice Age Premium Glacier Water - Glacial meltwater collected at 
Alpine Creek in Toba Inlet, British Columbia. Score: 16. Comments: 
“Fresh and dry.” “Big in the mouth, but not dense.” “Dry.” 
Crystal Geyser Natural Alpine Spring Water - Bottled at the (non- 
erupting) source springs in three mountain ranges - the Sierra Ne- 
vada, Cascades and Blue Ridge. Score: 16. Comments: “It has a pleas- 
ant, fat taste.” “Clean and dry with just a hint of minerals.” 
Aqua Mountain - bottled under the authority of the 7Up Bottling of

HELLS KITCHEN 319 
San Francisco, is purified tap water. Score: 14. Comments: “It tastes 
like the plastic bottle that it comes in.” “A slight creaminess and full 
body.” “Plastic flavor.” 
Alhambra Drinking Water - The water from Mt. Shasta in northern 
California, is subjected to microfiltration and ultraviolet light “for a 
fresh, clean taste,” according to the label. (exposure to ultraviolet 
light is for a germicidal effect). Score: 13. Comments: “Seems like 
tap water.” “A neutral, mineral taste.” “Plump water, round and soft.” ne 
Evian Natural Spring Water - Bottled in the French Alps at Cachat

ter.” “Doesn’t taste softened.” 
Glaceau Smartwater - The name “Glaceau” suggests it comes froma

Aquafina Purified Drinking Water - The label promises “nothing 
but crisp, clean refreshment.” The panel thought otherwise. Score: 
10. Comments: “Bitter edge, tooth-gritting.” 
Tap Water - Only Dan Kosta liked it and thought it was “minerally.” 
Alcohol 
A report released during the summer of 2004 by the National 
Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism concluded that, in gen- 
eral, a single alcoholic drink each day can be beneficial. Wine may 
protect a sixty five year old man from cardiovascular disease, but 
may increase the risk of a car accident in a twenty two year old. 
Two drinks per day can increase the risk of liver cirrhosis, pan- 
creatitis and colon cancer. 
One or two drinks per day can reduce the risk of a heart attack 
by thirty to forty percent about the same as statin drugs. Alcohol is 
thought to protect against gall stones. 
According to a study discussed in the report, “If all current drinkers

320 
were to quite drinking, the USA would suffer from an additional 
80,000 cardiovascular deaths per years.” There is a considerable body 
of evidence which shows that the “benefits of alcohol” are really the 
benefits of the phytonutrients in the alcoholic drink rather than the 
alcohol itself. 
The USDA suggests that moderate alcohol consumption, one drink 
per day for women and up to two per day for men - even these 
suggested limits might be too high when one is planning on driving 
a car or operating machinery. Three drinks in an hour can be ex- 
tremely dangerous while three drinks per day may be alright. The 
amount of alcohol in one twelve oz beer equals the alcohol in four 
ounces of wine and in one ounce of alcohol in spirits. 
Extended or extensive drinking or binge drinking increases the 
risk of dementia and Alzheimer’s disease. Low levels of alcohol con- 
sumption can decrease the risk of senile or vascular dementia caused 
by blood clots and thrombosis type strokes. 
Approximately five percent of American drinkers become addicted 
to alcohol or abuse alcohol. Alcoholism affects 16 million Americans 
and only 1.5 million seek therapy or treatment. 
A single drink can impair judgment, sexual function and sleep 
patterns. 
Research suggests that increased levels of alcohol consumption 
increases the risk for mouth, pharynx, larynx and esophageal cancer. 
Some studies indicate that this risk of cancer goes up twenty six per- 
cent, while other studies indicate an increase of as much as eighty 
three percent. 
Alcohol is known to interact in a negative way with many phar- 
maceuticals. 
One large study indicates that the lifetime risk of breast cancer is 
8.8 percent in nondrinkers and 10.1 percent in those women who 
consume two drinks per day - research also shows that folic acid 
supplementation can reduce the risk of breast cancer for nondrinkers and drinkers alike. 
Alcohol drinking in women who are breast feeding can reduce 
milk production and impairs a baby’s motor development. Pregnant 
women should not drink at all to prevent the fetal alcohol syndrome,

HELLS KITCHEN 321 
where emotional, behavioral and intellectual development are at risk. 
Wine 
The Chinese were consuming fermented beverages, including 
wine, as far back as 7,000 B.C. according to an anthropological dis- 
covery reported in the December 2004 issue of Proceedings of the 
National Academy of Science. “Fermented beverages are central to a lot 
of our religions, social relations, medicine, in many cultures around 
the world,” said Patrick E. McGovern, of the University of Pennsyl- 
vania Museum of Archaeology and Anthropology. 
These alcoholic drinks “have played key roles in the develop- 
ment of human culture and technology, contributing to the advance 
and intensification of agriculture, horticulture and food-processing 
technologies,” he reported. 
McGovern’ team collected pieces of sixteen pottery vessels from 
Jiahu, a Stone Age village in Chinas Henan Province. It’s the same 
site where archaeologists have found the earliest evidence of musical 
instruments, including a flute. The ceramic vessels were dated back 
to 7,000 B.C., and the chemical residue found in the vessels were 
analyzed - the results of the analysis revealed that, “they matched 
residues from modern rice and rice wine, grape wine, grape tannins 
and ancient and modern herbs including hawthorn berries.” 
“The most straightforward interpretation of these data is that the 
Jiahu vessels contained a consistently processed beverage made from 
rice, honey and fruit,” the team concluded. 
The team also reported on an analysis of a 3,000 year old liquid 
found in sealed bronze vessels from the Chinese city of Anyang. These 
vessels contained rice and millet wines, flavored with herbs and flower 
essential oils. 
“It’s similar to the rice wine that’s produced in the area southwest 
of Shanghai” today, McGovern said. “I have never tasted the ancient 
samples, I’ve only smelled it, but it does remind you of these Shaoxing 
rice wines with the floral aromatic scents,” he said. 
Prior to these 2004 findings, the earliest known chemical evi- 
dence of wine, dating to about 3,500 B.C., came from a 1990 discov-

322 
ery in Godin Tepe in the Zagros Mountains of western Iran. That was 
followed two years later with the earliest chemically confirmed bar- 
ley beer, in another vessel from the same room at Godin. 
In 1994, chemical testing confirmed resinated wine inside two 
jars excavated by a University of Pennsylvania archaeological team at 
the Neolithic site of Hajji Firuz Tepe, Iran, dating to about 5,400 
DG! 
Prior to the discovery of wine in China dating back to 7,000 
B.C., evidence for the growth of viticulture or organized commercial 
wine making is connected to the appearance of European villages, 
towns and cities - particularly in the Mediterranean region. It is 
thought that nomadic tribes from Europe were fermenting wild grapes 
and berries into wine and wine-like beverages as far back as 4,000 - 
SAO OO Ma G: 
The establishment of vineyards from the 4th millennium B.C. in 
Egypt and Mesopotamia and in the Aegean in 2500 B.C. followed 
the appearance of crude vineyards in India as far back as the 6th 
millennium B.C. The oldest tools and jugs known to be used for 
wine making are dated to the 5th and 6th millennium B.C. in Geor- 
gia and Armenia and in the 4th millennium in Iran. 
The establishment of agriculture and the appearance of settle- 
ments were soon followed by the cultivation of grape vines, olives 
and figs. 
It is said that, “wine is made up of a thousand substances.” Many 
of these phytochemicals and phytonutrients in wine have not yet 
been analyzed. The greater number of these phytonutrients found in 
wine, including vitamins and minerals come from the grapes, the 
makings of the wine, however, the balance of the wine’s content such 
as ethanol and glycerin are byproducts of the wine making fermenta- 
tion. Some substances that begin in wine, such as sugar and vitamin 
C are either partially or completely removed by the fermentation. 
Water makes up seventy five to ninety percent of the wine vol- 
ume - the fifteen percent difference is made up of varying amounts of 
tannic acid, organic acids, mineral salts and pectins, which form the 
wines extract. 
The second largest constituent of wine is ethyl alcohol. Alcohol

HELLS KITCHEN 323 
levels will vary based on the type of wine and are typically listed on 
the label as a percentage of by volume (% volume). A wine with an 
alcohol level of 11% volume will be light and fun - wine with an 
alcohol content of 13% volume can produce rapid intoxication. The 
determining factor will be the structure of the wine and the ratios of 
the different constituents including plant acids, residual sugar, alco- 
hol, tannins and color compounds. 
The sugar levels of dry wine is typically less than 0.25 ounces per 
gallon (2 grams per liter), while in a botrytized sweet wine it can 
reach almost 67 ounces per gallon (500 grams per liter). 
Over time, wine became known as a medicinal drink and not 
just a luxury. In many countries wine is a basic drink that is included 
in all meals, usually mixed with water to satisfy thirst. Old publica- 
tions called wine the “most hygienic drink,” and it was often safer to 
drink wine than it was to drink the water. 
Serge Renaud, a French scientist observed that, “there was a 
‘French paradox’ - while the French ate as much or more fat than 
Americans, the French had a 2.5 times lower rate of deaths from 
cardiovascular disease than Americans.” The study demonstrated that 
the higher a countries wine consumption the lower was it’s mortality 
rate from cardiovascular disease. 
For those who would like to have the health benefits of wine 
without the alcohol intake, there are juice and juice blends that con- 
tain the same phenolics and antioxidants as wine:

Longevity 
The vast majority of humans that have ever been born died be- 
fore they were ten years of age, primarily from nutritional deficiency 
diseases, exposure and trauma. Over the millennia, we humans have 
increased our life spans to seventy five years of age, give or take a few 
years. This apparent increase in longevity did not happen because of 
medical miracles, but rather, the increase in longevity happened be- 
cause of public works, including sewers, clean water and depend-

328 
able sources of energy and food. Contrary to claims by the medical 
profession, medicine has not added anything to human maximum 
life spans - no, not even a millisecond! 
Experts, including geneticists and gerontologists, agree that all 
humans, regardless of race, religion, gender or creed, BusHans.do not 
have a maximum life span and all have the genetic capacity and the 
genetic potential to live well beyond 100 years of age. The reason 
that we all don’t live to be 100, is not because we can’t, but rather, we 
simply do too many of the bad things and not enough of the good 
things. 
Aubrey de Grey, a biogerontologist at the University of Cambridge 
in England, says, “that, under the right circumstances, humans born 
in the 22nd century could live up to 5,000 years.” De Grey, who 
proposes that we use all the technology available to us to develop a 
“true cure for aging,” is agreeably at the extreme optimistic end of the 
scientific age curve. 
“I think people will someday live substantially longer than to- 
day,” said Steven Austad, a biologist at the University of Texas Health 
Sciences Center in San Antonio. “Living into your 100s will be fairly 
routine, and up to 150 for the outlier (a longer lived person who is 
the exception to the rule). I think this (is possible) because we have 
been so successful at figuring out how to get animals to live longer.” 
People do not die from obesity as most “experts” would have 
you believe - people die from the things that cause obesity - mineral 
deficiencies. Specific mineral deficiencies are the cause of obesity, 
type II diabetes and the Metabolic Syndrome - these same mineral 
deficiencies either directly or indirectly are the cause of Alzheimer’s 
disease, macular degeneration, cataracts, cardiovascular disease, can- 
cer, osteoporosis, arthritis, lupus, fibromyalgia, etc. 
Dr. Dilip V. Jeste, director of the Stein Institute for Research on 
Aging at UCSD says, “The prevention of hypertension, diabetes and 
obesity may help increase life span significantly.” 
The Honolulu Heart Program began in the mid-1960s, for three 
decades it evaluated 6,505 men between the ages of 45 and 68 years 
who were in “good health” when the program began. Of the men 
who survived to the ages of 71 to 95, forty percent remained free

The most reliable predictors of successful aging were low blood

tained humans for thousands of years as a source of dietary minerals 
is gone - we must, without exception, supplement with a new source 
of plant minerals to make up the difference.

In all cultures and races of humans, women live longer than men, 
however, biology alone can’t account for the longer lifespan of women. 
Females tend to eat better than males in terms of eating more veg- 
etables (antioxidants) than men, females tend to nibble all day as 
they prepare food and cook, while men tend to gorge on one, two or 
three meals per day, women tend to smoke fewer cigarettes, con- 
sume less alcohol and their place in a culture or society tends to be 
less dangerous (weaving, gathering, cooking, child rearing, office 
work, etc.) than the pursuits of men (hunting, warfare, farming, truck 
drivers, shepherds, miners, mechanics, lumber jacks, etc.). 
A 1997 survey by the USDA, reported that men consume an av- 
erage of 96 grams of fat each day, which makes up forty four percent 
of their daily calorie intake. Women, on the other hand get thirty two 
percent of their calories from fat. By the year 2000, American men 
had dropped their dietary fat intake to thirty three percent, however, 
their vegetable intake (dietary antioxidant source) was still low.

HELLS KITCHEN 331 
During the 1990s women lived 0.1 years longer than men in 
Bangladesh, 0.6 years longer in India, 6.7 years longer in the US, 5.3 
years longer in the U.K. and 7.8 years longer in France. 
In general, obese people do not live as long as people who more 
closely adhere to their ideal weight. People who are overweight can 
be “fit,” however, when it comes to longevity being overweight is still 
a big negative. 
With the “party line” professed, there is current information that 
recommends that seniors forget dieting unless they are morbidly 
obese: 
P. Don’t go on a weight loss diet unless you are morbidly obese. 
Mild exercise increases muscle mass and bone density well into 
old age. 
Rapid weight loss (five percent over ninety days) can signal the 
onset of dental problems, or serious illness (diabetes, cancer, etc.). 
High protein diets with a serious supplement program are 
essential for optimal health, prevention of sarcopenia (loss of 
muscle mass) and a long life. Seniors should beware of 
extreme heart healthy diets - do not eliminate fat or salt from 
your diet. 
Avoid fried foods, margarines, meat cooked well done, burnt 
animal fats, sugar and carbonated drinks to eliminate direct and 
indirect sources of free radicals. 
With diabetes, eat six to eight small meals per day with animal 
protein, green leafy vegetables, and complex whole grain 
carbohydrates - “the whiter the bread, the sooner you're dead.” 
Provide more finger foods for seniors, roasted chicken wings and 
drumsticks, sandwiches, wraps, etc. 
Serve more of the nutrient dense foods to seniors such as milk, 
cheese, nuts, ground meat, squash, baked potatoes with butter, 
no sugar added ice cream several times each day to avoid 
sarcopenia (loss of muscle mass). 
The Human Scorecard for Longevity 
The September 2000 issue of Scientific American was almost en-

332 
tirely devoted to human longevity and people who lived beyond 100 
years of age. The various articles were written by a wide variety of 
physicians, gerontologists, geneticists, dieticians and nutritionists. A 
summary of the information reported in the September SA pointed 
out that the individuals who followed their doctors advice to the 
letter, didn’t make it to 100 years of age, while people who ate salt, 
eggs, red meat, butter and chicken skin did. 
The special September 2000 human longevity issue of Scientific 
American had a summary, that was, in effect an editorial head scratch- 
ing. The “experts” couldn't figure out why the people who were classed 
as “rebellious” patients lived to be 100 more frequently than ones 
who followed their doctor's advice perfectly. 
Obviously, their doctor’s advice wasn’t very good. To give specific 
numbers to the lack of success by the American doctors when it 
comes to longevity we don’t have to look very hard. In an April 1990 
study, conducted by the World health Organization and the Center 
for Disease Control, of Atlanta, Georgia a comparison was made be- 
tween the top thirty two industrialized nations for industrial produc- 
tion, energy consumption, education, per capita income, birth rate, 
death rate and longevity. 
When the study was presented to the world, everyone was 
shocked to find that the United States ranked 17th in the world for 
longevity with an average life span of 75 years - there were sixteen 
other countries whose peoples lived longer than we did. 
Even though we spent more money for health care than all the 
other countries in the world put together we still only ranked 17th - 
we certainly weren't getting the “bang for our buck” when it came to 
health care. 
In 1990, the Japanese ranked number one in longevity with an 
average life span of 79.1 years, even though western doctors claimed 
the Japanese did everything wrong: 
1. The Japanese were heavy smokers - sixty three percent smoked 
three to five packs of cigarettes per day, yet they had eighty five per- 
cent less cardiovascular disease and eighty five percent less cancer 
than we did.

HELVS KITCHEN 333 
2. The Japanese consumed an enormous amount of salt each day 
compared to Americans. The Americans were admonished by their 
doctors to eat less then one gram of salt per day. The Japanese by 
contrast consumed fifteen grams of salt per day, yet they had eighty 
five percent less cardiovascular disease and hypertension than did 
the Americans. 
3. We dropped two atomic bombs on the Japanese to end World War 
Il in the Pacific - they had more radiation per person than any other 
country in the world, yet they have eighty five percent less cancer 
than did the Americans. 
The Study was rerun ten years later and the results released in 
June 2000. The Japanese remained number one in world ranking for 
longevity, although they dropped in average life span from 79.1 in 
1990 to 74.5 in 2000. 
In the 2000 study, the Americans dropped to an average lifespan 
of 70 and slipped in the world rankings for longevity down to 24th. 
In 2000, the entire world spent $2.7 trillion for health care, out 
of that princely sum, the USA alone spent $1.6 trillion, more than 
half of the world’s total expenditure, yet we only rank 24th in the 
world when it comes to health and longevity. 
It is quite obvious that a lack of money, technology, drugs, doc- 
tors, nurses and hospitals is not the problem. Every country in the 
world sends their brightest young doctors to America to study our 
health and treatment methods. We are the health Mecca to the rest of 
the world. World leaders come to the USA for state of the art treat- 
ments. 
A special 2004 longevity issue of Scientific American pointed out 
that the “oldest old” over ninety five years of age were usually healthier 
than individuals twenty years younger. Fifty percent of ninety year 
olds have Alzheimer’s disease while only thirty three percent of 100 
year olds have the disease. 
Time magazine, August 2004, featured a special cover article, “How 
to Live to be 100 (and not regret it). The authors pointed out that by 
avoiding alcohol, tobacco and caffeine, the clean living (Mormon)

334 
people of Utah live an average of eight years longer than the average 
American - in 2005 we dropped to 26th in the world for longevity! 
The “Eat Less” Myth 
The concept of “under nutrition without malnutrition” goes back 
to the work of Clive M. McKay in the 1920s. McKay, a Cornell Uni- 
versity nutritionist, wrote his landmark papers in 1935 - he reported 
that by restricting carbohydrate calories by thirty percent (while keep- 
ing the fat, protein, vitamins and minerals the same) he could in- 
crease the life span of his test rats by 30 percent; when he cut the 
carbohydrate calories by sixty percent (while keeping the fat, pro- 
tein, vitamins and minerals the same) he could increase life spans by 
sixty to 100 percent. 
Effects of Calorie Restricted Diets

B. Delayed sexual maturity

B. Increased rate of cellular suicide 
C. Longer average life span 
D. Increased maximum life span 
Another way of looking at CR diets is: 
CR slows the rate of cell division. CR is thought to slow the rate of 
cellular metabolism enough so that division occurs less often, effec- 
tively slowing the aging process - this may help explain why labora- 
tory animals on CR diets have a lower rate of late-life cancer. 
CR boosts immunological response and increases levels of protective 
hormones. 
CR reduces mitochondrial damage from free radicals produced by 
metabolizing energy fuel. 
A human study by John O. Holloszy, a professor of medicine at 
Washington University in St. Louis, published in early 2004, noted 
that 18 people who had used a CR diet for three to fifteen years 
showed “dramatically reduced risks of developing diabetes or clogged 
arteries.” 
“It’s very clear that calorie restriction has a powerful protective 
effect against diseases associated with aging,” Holloszy said. “We don’t 
know how long each individual actually will end up living, but they 
certainly have a much longer life expectancy than average because 
they're most likely not going to die from a heart attack, stroke or 
diabetes.” 
This concept of “under nutrition without malnutrition” has been 
touted as a calorie restricted diet (CR), with the emphasis on re- 
stricted calories, by many modern gerontologists; however, they fail 
to give equal weight to an essential part of the program - the “with-

336 
out malnutrition” part. 
A CR diet without a serious mineral intake will feel like a starva- 
tion diet because of the pica or munchies factor and therefore almost 
impossible to stick with. 
Using Clive McKay’s original theory, modern scientists have re- 
peated his work in fruit flies, worms, monkeys and many other labo- 
ratory animals with the same success. Dr. David Sinclair, of Harvard, 
put himself on the same 1,600 calorie diet eaten by members of the 
Calorie Restriction Society - Sinclair was able to maintain the CR diet 
for only a week. “It was too tough,” he said, “My hats off to the calo- 
rie restrictors. Now I’m hoping to find drugs that can give people the 
benefits of a CR (diet) without the (actual) diet.” 
Dr. Roy L. Walford (1924 - 2004), a gerontologist at the Univer- 
sity of California, Los Angeles, kept himself on a 1,600 calorie diet 
for three decades - in April 27, 2004, Walford died of amyotrophic 
lateral sclerosis (Lou Gehrig's disease) at the age of seventy nine years. 
Walford continually preached the gospel of CR diets, however, he 
paid little attention to the “without malnutrition part” and as a result 
he died an unnecessarily early death.

Essential Nutrients and Antioxidants 
Our dietary requirements for essential nutrients, goes up as we 
get older because our ability to assimilate them decreases with age. 
We like to error on the side of excess when it comes to daily intakes 
of our essential nutrients, especially when it comes to the needs of 
seniors - vitamins, amino acids, essential fatty acids and minerals - 
and don’t wait until you're obese or old to take antioxidants, you 
should start before conception, during pregnancy and from the mo- 
ment of birth. 
While it is easy to produce deficiencies of essential nutrients and 
wind up with debilitating and life threatening diseases simply by not 
supplementing, conversely, it is difficult to overdose and damage 
yourself. 
Supplement properly with plant minerals and you can avoid obe- 
sity, type II diabetes and the Metabolic Syndrome - if you have these 
chronic longevity stealing diseases, supplement properly and you 
can reverse these post September 4, 1882 diseases. 
It is easy to forget that we humans supplemented with minerals 
for tens of thousands of years with the simple household byproduct 
of wood ashes (the minerals that are left when you burn the carbon 
in wood) from our heating and cooking fires - we put them in our 
gardens, we used them as “culinary” ashes and we used them as “salt” 
or we cut the more valuable table salt with them. 
Most experts agree that Albert Einstein was one of the more in- 
telligent human beings that have ever lived. He won the Nobel Prize 
in physics in 1921 “for his services to theoretical physics and espe- 
‘ Cially for his discovery of the law of the photoelectric effect.” Yet he 
‘ died in 1955 at the age of 76 years of age of a ruptured aortic aneu-

338 
rysm, something that even a turkey wouldn't die from (remember, 
we put everything that turkey’ need nutritionally into their little al- 
falfa pellet) - ten years earlier we had eliminated ruptured aneurysms 
in turkey’s (a cause of devastating losses) by simply increasing the 
copper levels in their food pellets. 
We are sure that Einstein had the very best doctors that money 
could buy and that medical science could provide - yet Einstein died 
of a simple copper deficiency - a deficiency that he had warnings of 
(grey hair and deep skin wrinkles) for decades before he died. 
Next time someone says, “Why should I supplement, they didn’t 
supplement hundreds of years ago!” you now have the proper an- 
swer - yes they did, and here’s how they did it - it was the wood 
ashes. 
We believe that antioxidants will be added to the list of essential 
nutrients - without them we cannot protect ourselves from dietary 
and environmental free radicals and attain maximum life spans. Antioxidants are found in substantial quantities in: 
Fruit - blueberries, cherries, kiwis, pink grapefruit, oranges, plums, 
prunes, raisins, raspberries, red grapes, strawberries, boysenberries, 
pomegranate, noni and mangostein. 
Vegetables - alfalfa, beets, broccoli, Brussels sprouts, eggplant, kale, 
tomatoes, red bell peppers, spinach, tomatoes. 
Green tea - EGCG 
Antioxidants are essential to a long and healthy life and they 
are especially important to the obese human and the obese per- 
son who wants to loose weight. When body fat is burned, waste 
products (trans fats, oxidized fats and ceroid lipofucsin) are pro- 
duced. These fat metabolism waste products are free radicals 
and they significantly increase the risk of cancer, heart disease, 
vascular disease (arteriosclerosis, atherosclerosis), thrombotic 
disease (stroke, deep vein thrombosis, pulmonary embolism, and 
coronary thrombosis), macular degeneration, cataracts and 
Alzheimer’s disease.

HELLS KITCHEN 339 
Unfortunately, we have seen numerous people who were very 
good at losing weight, but they refused to take adequate levels of 
antioxidants and six months to a year later wound up with some 
terrible disabling or fatal disease that could have been prevented with 
a serious antioxidant program. 
It is predicted that by 2025, 14 million Americans will die of 
Alzheimer’s disease, a disease that is caused by oxidative damage to 
the myelin of the brain. Myelin makes up about seventy five percent 
of the human brain weight and myelin is almost 100 percent choles- 
terol. 
The byproducts of oxidized cholesterol and fat includes, trans 
fatty acids and ceroid lipofucsin, which builds up and occupies space 
in all cells. Typically, thirty five year olds have as much as fifty per- 
cent of their neuron volume filled with ceroid, fifty year olds have 
sixty percent of their neuron volume filled with ceroid and 100 year 
olds have as much as eighty five percent of their neuron volume 
filled with ceroid. 
Externally ceroid shows up as liver spots and age spots - if you 
have a dozen obvious deposits on the outside, you have millions of 
them on the inside. Fortunately, by eliminating the multiple sources 
of free radicals and significantly ramping up on your intake of anti- 
oxidants you can remove the ceroid from your body and slow the 
aging process. 
In 1992, the University of California, San Diego and the Salk 
Institute jointly released a study that was published in Biochemical/ 
Biophysical Research Communications, the study showed that the simple 
antioxidant vitamin E can ease memory loss in Alzheimer’ patients. 
In March of 1996, Johns Hopkins University released a 14-year 
study of 2,065 elderly people who were tracked to determine long 
term effects of Ibuprofen use for arthritis pain - they were surprised 
to learn that those individuals taking Ibuprofen had a 60 percent 
reduction in the risk of Alzheimer’s disease (the effect was attributed 
to a unique antioxidant property of Ibuprofen). 
On April 24, 1997, the University of California, San Diego re- 
leased a study showing that vitamin E could “slow the devastating 
decline for some Alzheimer’ patients, significantly delaying the need

340 
for nursing home care.” “This is the first study showing there are 
(antioxidants) that can delay important milestones of deterioration 
in the life of a patient with Alzheimers,” said Dr. Leon Thal, chair- 
man of the department of neurosciences at the UCSD School of Medi- 
cine. 
On November 22, 2001, Dutch researchers at the University of 
the Netherlands released the two year study of 6,989 patients who 
took Ibuprofen for pain. The study showed that those taking Ibuprofen 
had a reduced Alzheimer’s risk of 80 percent. 
On November 18, 2003, researchers at Tufts University reviewed 
the fatty acid levels of blood collected from 1,137 patients on the 
Framingham Heart Study, their diets were assessed and those free of 
dementia were followed for a mean of ten years. During the study 
they were assessed for the onset of new dementia including Alzheimers 
disease. Those with, “diets rich in Omega-3 fatty acids had a significant 48 percent reduction in the risk for the onset of dementia.” 
On January 20, 2004, researchers from the Johns Hopkins University School of Public Health, released the results of a study on 
4,740 men and women over the age of sixty five that showed, those 
taking vitamin C and vitamin E had a reduced risk of Alzheimer’s 
disease by 78 percent. 
In summing it all up, we can confidently say that the simple in- 
take of antioxidants and Omega-3 essential fatty acids can reduce the 
onset of Alzheimer’s disease by as much as forty eight to eighty per- 
cent. 
Life Spans of the Rich and Famous 
To our knowledge, with the exception of one professional bowler, 
there has never been a professional athlete live to be 100; to our 
knowledge there has never been a billionaire ever live to be 100; 
with the exception of Katherine Hepburn, Bob Hope and George 
Burns, there has never been a movie star ever live to be 100; with the 
exception of the Queen Mother of England who died at 101 and 
Madam Jiang Jie Shi of Taiwan who died at 104, there has never been 
any political figures live to be 100; and there never has been a Nobel

HELLS KITCHEN 341 
Prize winner live to be 100. 
One would think, that if money, political power, connections, 
physical fitness, or intelligence had anything to do with healthful 
longevity that the notables in each category would have the longev- 
ity advantage - however, statistics point out that there are more 100 
year olds and that there are more people over the age of 105, 110 
and 114 from simple families and third world countries than from 
the industrialized nations.

30,000 years ago in the latter century’s of the last Ice Age; he deter- 
mined that humans were keeping monthly calendars 25,000 years 
before the appearance of writing and almost 20,000 years before the 
appearance of agriculture - his findings were published in Science, 
November 1964; because of his discovery he was hired as a research 
associate at the Peabody Museum of Archaeology and Ethnology at 
Harvard in 1963. Heart failure. 
The Longevity Realities 
We like to look at birthday announcements in the newspapers as 
well as obituaries so we know what the longevity possibilities are - in 
reality, what is our reward in terms of health and longevity if we eat 
right, supplement properly and maintain a proper weight. In other 
words, bottom line, is it all (the goal of healthy longevity) in the final 
analysis worth the conscious effort. The answer turned out to be a 
resounding - YES!

366 
We will share with the reader a few of our favorite birthday and 
death announcements because they are a well traveled roadmap of 
how to do things right and how to get there from here. 
On January 1995, Masoomeh Dousti, died at her home in the 
Iranian village of Kalatenbala in Khorasan province at the age of 161 
years - she was survived by six living children ranging in age from 
120 to 128 years of age. According to her son, she never visited a 
doctor or took any chemical medications - she only used herbs for 
medication. 
On June 27, 1995, Dorah Ramothibe of South Africa turned 114 
years of age - she was born in 1881 when Queen Victoria was on the 
throne of England. Her diet consisted of locusts (grasshoppers), pump- 
kin seeds, tortoise meat, wild herbs, dried fruit and coffee. 
On April 1998, Bir Narayan Chaudhuri, a cattle farmer from a 
town outside of Katmandu, Nepal died at the age of 141 years. 
On October 1999, Ralph Charles, of Somerset, Ohio turned 100 
years of age - what made him interesting to us was that he was still 
piloting commercial charter flights, and his pilot’s license had no re- 
strictions. 
On March of 2003, Maria Etelvina Dos Santos, a Brazilian, died 
at the age of 124 years. According to her birth certificate, she was the 
descendent of African slaves and was born on July 15, 1878 - she 
enjoyed a healthy life throughout her years. 
On October of 2003, Sek Yi, a Cambodian tiger hunter and mar- 
tial arts expert died at the age of 122 - his surviving widow, Long 
Ouk, was 108 years of age. 
On September 1, 2004, a 93 year old woman who has six chil- 
dren, thirty five grandchildren, seventy five great grand children and 
ten great great grandchildren performed a sky dive from an airplane 
to celebrate her 93rd birthday- “Because it’s a new adventure,” ex- 
plained Luree Kohtz, of Burley, Idaho. 
A feisty Etna Powers rode in a horse drawn carriage during the 
2003 Portland, Oregon Rose Parade on her 100th birthday as she 
had done six earlier times. 
Sarah Clark Knauss, was born September 24, 1880, she turned 
120 years of age in 2000 - older than the Eiffel Tower. She was born

HELVS KITCHEN 367 
in Hollywood, Pennsylvania. 
On May 1, 2001, Harold Stilson, of Deerfield Beach, Florida, 
turned 101 years of age and went to play golf for his birthday - he hit 
his sixth hole in one - a 108 yard par three hole! 
On January 2001, the Guinness World Records, listed Antonio Todd, 
a shepherd from Sardinia as the worlds oldest man at 112 years of 
age. His secret, “drink a good glass of red wine every day.” 
Jeanne Calment of France was born on February 21, 1875 - she 
died at the age of 122 years and 164 days August 4, 1997 which was 
documented by the Guiness World Book of Records. 
The world’s oldest man Shigechiyo Izunis of Japan lived to be an 
authenticated 120 years and 237 days. He was born June 29, 1865. 
He was six years old when Japan had its first census in 1871. He 
worked until he was 105 years of age, drank shochu (liquor distilled 
from barley) and took up smoking at the age of 70 - he died Febru- 
ary 21, 1986. 
The oldest dwarf was Hungarian born Susana Bokonyi, alias “Prin- 
cess Susana,” of New Jersey until she died at the age of 105 on Au- 
gust 24, 1984. She was three foot four inches in height. 
The oldest person to fly as a passenger was Charlette Hughes of 
Redcar, England who was given a flight on Concorde from London 
to New York as a 110th birthday present in 1987. She flew again in 
1992 at the age of 115. 
The world’s oldest athlete, Boba Joginder Singh was 105 years of 
age when he got the Gold Medal for his winning the discus throw at 
the 1998 Indian National Meet for Veterans, held in Thane, Bombay 
(Mumbai) - he was the only competitor over the age of 100 years. He 
represented India in a 1910 World Championship event held in Ber- 
lin, Germany. 
The oldest person to ever have an operation was James Henry 
Brett, Jr., whose hip was operated on in Houston, Texas on Novem- 
ber 7, 1960 at age 111 years and 105 days. 
On August 6, 2004, Verona Johnson turned 114, and was docu- 
mented as the oldest living American - there have been older Ameri- 
cans before her, and there will be older Americans after her. At 114, 
she “still remembers names pretty well.” Her mind is still sharp enough

368 
to solve crossword puzzles and remember joke punch lines - she can 
still rattle through the names of her thirty six grandchildren and great- 
grandchildren. 
Johnson’s daily snack consists of orange juice, one cracker, one 
cinnamon drop and one cashew - “That’s enough,” she claims. 
Johnson gave up traveling by herself at age 100 and she gave up 
playing bridge at age 110. Johnson once exclaimed, “Electricity was 
the most important thing that happened to us.” She is famous for 
saying, “You can get too old to enjoy life - (but) I never got that old!” 
Johnson was quite correct, electricity was one of the more impor- 
tant things that happened to us, however, it was a two edged sword. 
Electricity provided a type of universal power that could be gener- 
ated using a variety of fuels (i.e. - wood, coal, oil, nuclear, etc.) and 
could be used to drive everything from kitchen appliances to com- 
puters. 
Unfortunately, when electricity was brought on line at 3:00 pm, 
Monday September 4, 1882 - few, if any knew that the genie had 
been let out of the bottle. The wood ash byproduct of cooking and 
heating was not replaced with a complete mineral supplement and 
the resultant consequences produced obesity, adult onset type II dia- 
betes, the Metabolic Syndrome and hundreds of other mineral defi- 
ciency diseases. 
Living healthfully beyond 100 years of age is in fact as easy as 
making a pie. There are many recipes for making pies and most cook 
books show a picture of what the pie will look like if one but follows 
the recipes. 
In a similar fashion, we know what the typical seventy five year 
old human will look like if they follow the instructions of the average 
western doctor. We know what the average obese thirty five year old 
looks like and we know their reduced life span statistics. 
To maximize ones health, fitness and longevity requires a signifi- 
cant level of personal responsibility of each individual. We must make 
sure we give our body all of the raw materials that it needs to main- 
tain and repair itself - all 90 essential nutrients with a special empha- 
sis on the sixty essential minerals. 
We must eliminate the bad stuff that directly or indirectly gener-

HELLS KITCHEN 369 
ates free radicals - no fried foods, margarines, cooking oils, carbon- 
ated drinks, caffeine, excessive alcohol, sugar, excess carbohydrates, 
burnt animal fats and meat cooked well done. By eliminating these 
dangerous foods and substances we can add a healthful eight to 20 
years to our lives; add in the 90 essential nutrients one can add an- 
other 10 to twenty years; add in the antioxidants you add more health- 
ful years and significantly reduce the risks of cancer, cardiovascular 
disease and Alzheimer’s disease. 
Doing the correct things will allow us to avoid obesity, adult on- 
set type II diabetes and the Metabolic Syndrome - these diseases are 
not inevitable, genetic or hopeless, they are in fact preventable and 
curable as they are caused by simple mineral deficiency diseases. 
Remember, humans have the genetic capacity to live to be be- 
yond 100 years of age - if we accept death at the age of sixty five as 
having had a “good life” when we could have lived to a healthful 100 
years of age - we squandered and gave up thirty five years of a health- 
ful and useful life that was given to us for free. 
We fight hard to ensure that we get the full measure of our car, 
dishwasher, and television warranty - why not fight hard and intelli- 
gently to get the full measure of the healthful life that we are equipped 
to get.
